Gravity driven traveling bore wave solutions to the free boundary incompressible Navier-Stokes equations Thanks: N. Stevenson was supported by an NSF Graduate Research Fellowship Thanks: I. Tice was supported by an NSF Grant (DMS #2204912).
Noah Stevenson, Ian Tice

Abstract
. We give the first mathematical construction of two-dimensional traveling bore wave solutions to the free boundary incompressible Navier-Stokes equations for a single finite depth layer of constant density fluid. Our construction is based on a rigorous justification of the formal shallow water limit, which postulates that in a certain scaling regime the full free boundary traveling Navier-Stokes system of PDEs reduces to a governing system of ODEs. We find heteroclinic orbits solving these ODEs and, through a delicate fixed point argument employing the Stokes problem in thin domains and a nonautonomous orbital perturbation theory, use these ODE solutions as the germs from which we build bore PDE solutions for sufficiently shallow layers.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary 35Q35, 35C07, 76D33; Secondary 35B40, 35J66, 76A20, 76L05, 34C37, 35A24

1.
Introduction

A fluid bore is a special type of traveling wave, observed on the surfaces of canals and rivers, that possesses distinct asymptotic heights in the upstream and downstream limits. It has been understood since at least the work of Rayleigh
[
73
]
, over a century ago, that a single layer of inviscid homogeneous incompressible fluid cannot sustain bores. Rayleigh’s original argument, which has since been reconfirmed and generalized in modern proofs, is that inviscid conservation laws are incompatible with a bore-like change of states, and he deduces from this that including viscous effects is essential to understand the phenomenon. Therefore, in a mathematical study of the existence of bores it is reasonable to account for more robust fluid mechanical effects, such as viscosity and other sources of friction.

The mathematical search for bores then leads to the realm of traveling
viscous
surface waves, a topic relatively unexplored by rigorous analysis, in stark contrast with the classical, centuries-spanning study of traveling waves on the free surface of an
inviscid
fluid. The vast majority of the latter consists of investigations of irrotational water waves, with important effects such as vorticity only recently receiving rigorous attention. The typical inviscid surface wave handled by this theory is a traveling profile sustained purely by a constant gravitational force. On the other hand, all known traveling waves with viscosity are generated by gravity acting in conjunction with a spatially varying force or surface stress (e.g. localized external pressure) that perturbs the equilibrium. Thus, a construction of viscous bore solutions would not only stand as the first mathematical verification that single layers can support a profile with differing asymptotic heights, but would also show for the first time that gravity alone can sustain nontrivial viscous surface waves.

In this paper we prove that within the realm of shallow fluids, modeled by the free boundary incompressible Navier-Stokes equations over an inclined plane, bores not only exist but are, in a sense, common steady states. Due to the shallow assumption, the dynamics are described by a viscous Saint-Venant system of ordinary differential equations, which we show admit special heteroclinic orbits that then give an effective approximation of our Navier-Stokes solutions. Moreover, we produce both small and large bores, as measured by the size of the jump in velocity across the bore’s transition front. Furthermore, our construction, which is general and modular, opens the door to further searches for other types of gravity driven viscous surface waves.

1.1.
Traveling bore wave solutions to the free boundary Navier-Stokes system

In this paper we study two-dimensional finite-depth layers of incompressible, viscous fluid with free boundaries. We posit that the fluid sits atop a flat rigid surface and meets an atmosphere of constant pressure along its free boundary. The dynamics of the fluid are governed by the incompressible Navier-Stokes equations, but we focus our attention on traveling wave solutions, which are a special type of solution that is time-independent when viewed in a coordinate frame moving at a constant speed parallel to the fluid bottom. Within the traveling frame the fluid occupies a static, but unknown, set of the form

(1.1.1)

\Omega_{\zeta}=\{{(x,z)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\;:\;0<z<\zeta(x)}\}

where
\zeta:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}^{+}
is an unknown function, which we assume is continuous in order to ensure
\Omega_{\zeta}
is open and connected. The graph of
\zeta
,

(1.1.2)

\Sigma_{\zeta}=\{{(x,z)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\;:\;z=\zeta(x)}\},

describes the free boundary along the upper part of the fluid, while the set
\Sigma_{0}=\mathbb{R}\times\{{0}\}
describes the rigid bottom boundary of the fluid.

We assume that the only bulk force acting on the fluid is gravity, which has both a standard vertical component as well as a horizontal component. The latter can be thought of as corresponding to the fluid being situated on an inclined plane, after a coordinate transformation. The fluid configuration is described by the aforementioned
\zeta
in addition to the unknowns
(v,q):\Omega_{\zeta}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}
, the velocity field and pressure profiles in the traveling frame. These must satisfy the traveling free boundary Navier-Stokes equations:

(1.1.3)

\begin{cases}(v-\overline{\gamma}e_{1})\cdot\nabla v+\nabla q-\mu\Delta v=\kappa e_{1},\quad\nabla\cdot v=0&\text{in }\Omega_{\zeta},\\
-(q-\mu\mathbb{D}v)\mathcal{N}_{\zeta}+(\overline{g}\zeta-\overline{\sigma}\mathcal{H}(\zeta))\mathcal{N}_{\zeta}=0,\quad\overline{\gamma}\partial_{1}\zeta+v\cdot\mathcal{N}_{\zeta}=0&\text{on }\Sigma_{\zeta},\\
v_{2}=0,\quad\mu\partial_{2}v_{1}=\overline{a}v_{1}&\text{on }\Sigma_{0},\end{cases}

where
\mathcal{N}_{\zeta}=(-\zeta^{\prime},1)
is a (non-unit) outward normal and
\mathcal{H}(\zeta)=\zeta^{\prime\prime}/(1+\char 12\relax{\zeta^{\prime}}^{2})^{3/2}
is the mean curvature of the free surface. The roles of the parameters are as follows:
\mu>0
is the fluid viscosity,
\overline{\gamma}>0
is the speed of the traveling wave frame in the
e_{1}
direction,
\overline{g}\geqslant 0
is the vertical gravitational acceleration,
\kappa>0
is the horizontal gravitational acceleration,
\overline{\sigma}\geqslant 0
is the surface tension coefficient, and
\overline{a}>0
is the slip parameter (with units of speed so that
\mu/\overline{a}
is the slip length). For the sake of brevity, we have chosen to state the system (
1.1.3
) directly in traveling form rather than record its derivation from the time-dependent Navier-Stokes system; we refer to
[
47
,
41
]
for details on how to perform the derivation. The first equation in (
1.1.3
) corresponds to the balance of momentum, the second to the conservation of mass, and the third to the balance of surface stresses on the free boundary, while the fourth is the kinematic equation for the free surface. The final two equations are known as the Navier slip boundary conditions, as they forbid fluid transport through the rigid interface but allow horizontal slip at the cost of an oppositional tangential stress. First proposed by Navier in
[
61
]
, this boundary condition generalizes the usual no-slip condition (recovered formally by sending
\overline{a}\to\infty
) to allow the modeling of slip along the boundary, a phenomenon that has since been experimentally observed: see
[
63
]
and references therein.

For any continuously differentiable map
w:\Omega_{\zeta}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
satisfying
\nabla\cdot w=0
in
\Omega_{\zeta}
and
w_{2}=0
on
\Sigma_{0}
we may compute

(1.1.4)

\partial_{1}\int_{0}^{\zeta(x)}w_{1}(x,z)\;\mathrm{d}z=w_{1}(x,\zeta(x))\zeta^{\prime}(x)-\int_{0}^{\zeta(x)}\partial_{2}w_{2}(x,z)\;\mathrm{d}z=-w(x,\zeta(x))\cdot\mathcal{N}_{\zeta}(x).

From this we readily deduce that the kinematic boundary condition in (
1.1.3
) is equivalent to

(1.1.5)

\Phi=-\overline{\gamma}\zeta(x)+\int_{0}^{\zeta(x)}v_{1}(x,z)\;\mathrm{d}z=\int_{0}^{\zeta(x)}(v_{1}(x,z)-\overline{\gamma})\;\mathrm{d}z\text{ for }x\in\mathbb{R}

for a constant
\Phi\in\mathbb{R}
measuring the relative flux across all vertical line segments
(0,\zeta(x))
. We will use this reformulation of the kinematic boundary condition, as the flux constant will play a crucial role in our analysis.

The horizontal gravitational acceleration term
\kappa e_{1}
in (
1.1.3
) allows the system to support equilibrium shear flow solutions. These are special solutions of constant height and pressure with velocities that depend only on the vertical variable:

(1.1.6)

\zeta(x)=h\in\mathbb{R}^{+},\;q(x,z)=\overline{g}h,\text{ and }v(x,z)=s(z)e_{1}\text{ with }s(z)=-(\kappa/2\mu)z^{2}+(\kappa h/\mu)z+(\kappa h/\overline{a}).

Note that this constitutes a one-parameter family of solutions indexed by the constant height variable,
h\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
. However, if we assume that a given fluid configuration is subject to the relative flux condition (
1.1.5
) for a fixed constant
\Phi\in\mathbb{R}
, then we can significantly reduce the parameter space. Indeed, plugging the shear flows from (
1.1.6
) into (
1.1.5
) yields the identity

(1.1.7)

\Phi=(\kappa/3\mu)h^{3}+(\kappa/\overline{a})h^{2}-\overline{\gamma}h,

and we learn from trivial algebra that there are at most three possible values of
h\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
compatible with the constraint (
1.1.7
). In fact, we can say quite a bit more. First, if
\overline{\gamma}<0
then there exists a solution with
h\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
if and only if
\Phi>0
, and in this case the solution is uniquely determined by the flux. Second, if
\overline{\gamma}>0
and
\Phi\geqslant 0
then again there is a unique solution
h\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
. Third, if
\overline{\gamma}>0
there exists
\Phi_{\mathrm{min}}<0
such that if
\Phi\in(\Phi_{\mathrm{min}},0)
then there exist two distinct solutions
0<h_{-}<h_{+}
, which then give rise to two distinct shear flows
(\zeta_{\pm},v_{\pm},q_{\pm})
determined by
h_{\pm}
as in (
1.1.6
); see Figure
1
for a graphical depiction.

Figure 1
.

On the left we have the plot of
\Phi(h)
from (
1.1.7
) when
\overline{\gamma}>0
, in which case choosing a negative flux constant,
\Phi_{\ast}\in(\Phi_{\mathrm{min}},0)
, allows for two positive solutions,
0<h_{-}<h_{+}
, that give rise to distinct shear flow solutions
\zeta_{\pm}=h_{\pm},

q_{\pm}=\overline{g}h_{\pm}
, and
v_{\pm}=s_{\pm}e_{1}
. On the right we have cartoons of these two shear flow solutions, with the
h_{+}
solution above the dotted line and
h_{-}
below. The arrows plot the velocities with the dashed line indicating a plot of the shear profiles
s_{\pm}
. Evidently, these have very different profiles but the same relative flux
\Phi_{\ast}
.

The above discussion raises an intriguing question: if the flux is chosen with
\Phi\in(\Phi_{\mathrm{min}},0)
do there exist solutions to (
1.1.3
) that converge to one of the shear flow states
(\zeta_{\pm},v_{\pm},q_{\pm})
as
x\to-\infty
and the other state as
x\to\infty
? Our principal task in this paper is to answer this question in the affirmative. We will define a solution of this form to be a
bore
, a term borrowed from the natural phenomenon of tidal bores, which are fluid configurations found in channels and rivers that behave in a similar manner, with different fluid heights upstream and downstream. As we will see, bore solutions can also be thought of in more mathematical terms as traveling fronts or viscous shocks and rarefaction waves.

A precise statement of our main result can be found in Section
1.5
, but an informal summary is as follows. We show that bore solutions to (
1.1.3
) exist, provided that we assume that the fluid is sufficiently shallow. This shallow condition is quantified in terms of a dimensionless depth parameter
0<\varepsilon\ll 1
and the various physical parameters appearing in (
1.1.3
), some of which we need to assume scale in particular ways with
\varepsilon
. Nevertheless, we prove that bores are ubiquitous within this shallow regime of physical parameter space. The solutions we construct fall into two broad qualitative categories, for which we continue to borrow tidal terminology: solutions that transition from
h_{+}
at
-\infty
to
h_{-}
at
+\infty
, which we refer to as
surging bores
(or viscous shocks); and solutions that transition from
h_{-}
at
-\infty
to
h_{+}
at
+\infty
, which we refer to as
ebbing bores
(or viscous rarefactions). The eager reader may jump ahead to Figure
3
to see numerical simulations of the surging and ebbing bores, though the plots will make more sense after some further notation is developed. All of the bores we construct are surging when the traveling wave speed
\overline{\gamma}
is sufficiently small and ebbing otherwise; see Corollary
4
for a more precise statement.

1.2.
Survey of previous work

The mathematical study of traveling surface waves on free boundary fluids has spanned centuries, and as such, we cannot hope to give an extensive review here. Instead, we content ourselves with considering only the results most closely related to those of this paper. In particular, unless stated otherwise, we will restrict our attention to two-dimensional fluid configurations of a single layer.

The study of the inviscid, non-tilted analog of system (
1.1.3
) (the traveling free boundary Euler equations) is also known as the traveling water wave problem. In this situation, the existence of various species of traveling waves was established long ago, starting with the work of Stokes
[
82
]
. The first mathematical construction of two-dimensional periodic water waves is due to Nekrasov
[
62
]
and Levi-Civita
[
49
]
. These are small amplitude and irrotational waves. The existence of large amplitude, irrotational, and periodic water waves of various types, including those that solve the Stokes conjecture, was later established by Krasovskiĭ
[
43
]
, Keady and Norbury
[
40
]
, Toland
[
85
]
, Amick, Fraenkel, and Toland
[
3
]
, Plotnikov
[
71
]
, McLeod
[
58
]
, and Plotnikov and Toland
[
72
]
. The incorporation of the effects of vorticity into the periodic water wave problem is a more recent development, beginning with the work of Constantin and Strauss
[
24
]
. Wahlén
[
87
,
88
]
added the effects of surface tension to the rotational problem, while Walsh
[
89
,
90
,
91
]
considered density stratification.

We now turn our attention to the case of solitary water waves. The first rigorous proof of small amplitude, solitary, and irrotational water waves is due to Lavrentiev
[
45
]
, and this theory was then extended by Friedrichs and Hyers
[
31
]
, Beale
[
8
]
, Mielke
[
59
]
, and Pego and Sun
[
69
]
. Large amplitude solitary waves came with the work of Amick and Toland
[
4
,
5
]
. For generalizations with vorticity and other effects, we refer to the work of Hur
[
38
]
, Groves and Wahlén
[
35
]
, Wheeler
[
92
]
, Chen, Walsh, and Wheeler
[
18
,
19
,
22
]
, Haziot and Wheeler
[
37
]
, and Ehrnström, Walsh, and Zeng
[
28
]
. There have also been recent works that study the effects of wind or localized pressure sources in the classical water wave equations: see the papers of Bühler, Shatah, and Walsh
[
16
]
, Bühler, Shatah, Walsh, and Zeng
[
17
]
, and Wheeler
[
93
]
.

The proof of the
nonexistence
of traveling bore wave solutions to the single-layer inviscid and irrotational water wave equations goes back to Rayleigh
[
73
]
. More modern proofs, which incorporate vorticity, can be found in the work of Wheeler
[
93
]
and Haziot and Wheeler
[
37
]
. Nevertheless, traveling inviscid internal fronts are possible within the context of a two-fluid system, as first proved in a small amplitude context by Amick and Turner
[
6
]
. See also the work of Mielke
[
60
]
, Makarenko
[
53
]
, and Chen, Walsh, and Wheeler
[
21
]
. The first construction of large bores for a two-fluid Euler systems is due to Chen, Walsh, and Wheeler
[
20
]
.

This review of the inviscid water wave problem has barely scratched the surface. We refer the reader looking for a deeper dive to the survey articles of Toland
[
86
]
, Dias and Iooss
[
26
]
, Groves
[
34
]
, Strauss
[
83
]
, and Haziot, Hur, Strauss, Toland, Wahlén, Walsh, and Wheeler
[
36
]
.

Juxtaposed with the extended history of inviscid water waves are the more recent developments on the traveling viscous surface wave problem. The objects of study in this realm are free boundary fluids in which the effects of viscosity are taken into account and are modeled by Navier-Stokes systems similar to (
1.1.3
). Due to the dissipative nature of viscosity, one might expect that the generation of nontrivial traveling waves in viscous free boundary fluids necessitates a balancing source of power; to date, all proofs of the existence of such waves have required such a power source in the form of a spatially localized and moving source of force or stress acting on the fluid in addition to gravitational or capillary effects. For example, this could take the form of a moving, localized pressure disturbance.

Leoni and Tice
[
47
]
provided the first construction of traveling solutions to the free boundary incompressible Navier-Stokes equations. This result was generalized by Stevenson and Tice
[
77
,
79
]
to incompressible fluids with multiple layers and to compressible free boundary flows, and by Koganemaru and Tice
[
41
,
42
]
to periodic or inclined fluids and flows obeying the Navier slip boundary condition. Nguyen and Tice
[
65
]
constructed traveling wave solutions to free boundary Darcy flow and, in the periodic case, performed a stability analysis. For technical reasons, these results all required a nontrivial traveling wave speed and did not address the stationary problem. The existence of solitary stationary waves was then proved by Stevenson and Tice
[
80
]
, though only in three spatial dimensions. All of the previously mentioned papers on stationary and traveling viscous surface waves produce small amplitude solutions. Large amplitude traveling wave solutions have yet to be found for the free boundary Navier-Stokes equations; however, they were produced for Darcy flow by Nguyen
[
64
]
and Brownfield and Nguyen
[
15
]
and for the shallow water equations by Stevenson and Tice
[
81
]
.

While the constructions mentioned in the previous paragraph are mathematical, solitary viscous traveling waves generated by moving, localized disturbances have also been observed in several experimental studies. We refer to the papers of Akylas, Cho, Diorio, and Duncan
[
23
,
27
]
, Duncan and Masnadi
[
57
]
, and Cho and Park
[
67
,
68
]
and the references therein for details. On the other hand, the experimental results of Russel
[
75
]
, Cornish
[
25
]
, and Favre
[
29
]
suggest that gravitational forces alone may suffice for sustaining viscous surface waves, which is more akin to the typical setup in the classical theory of inviscid surface waves.

To the best of the authors’ knowledge, this work stands as the first construction of nontrivial traveling viscous surface waves driven solely by gravity as well as the first verification that viscous fluids can support single-layer traveling front solutions, which is in stark contrast with the inviscid case.

1.3.
Shallow nondimensionalization and flattening

We now endeavor to quantify the shallowness of the fluid layer, nondimensionalize the fluid equations, and reformulate them in a form more convenient for analysis. To begin, we introduce a small dimensionless parameter
0<\varepsilon<1
and make the following five ansätze, which are motivated by the theory of the shallow water equations and place (
1.1.3
) into a certain
\varepsilon
-shallow regime. First, we posit that the height of the free surface
\zeta
is proportional to
\varepsilon
. Second, the slip parameter is proportional to
\varepsilon
: there exists
a>0
for which
\overline{a}=\varepsilon a
. Third, the surface tension is at most of order
\varepsilon
: for some
\sigma\geqslant 0
we have
\overline{\sigma}=\varepsilon\sigma
. Fourth, the normal component of gravity is of order at most
1/\varepsilon
: for some
g\geqslant 0
we have
\overline{g}=g/\varepsilon
. Fifth, we assume that the traveling frame speed decomposes according to
\overline{\gamma}=\gamma+\varepsilon^{2}\widetilde{\gamma}
with
\gamma>0
and
\widetilde{\gamma}\in\mathbb{R}
; here the parameter
\gamma
is arbitrary, whereas
\widetilde{\gamma}
is a correction factor that will later be determined as a function of the other parameters. To summarize the parameter scaling:

(1.3.1)

\overline{a}=\varepsilon a,\;\overline{\sigma}=\varepsilon\sigma,\;\overline{g}=g/\varepsilon,\;\text{and }\overline{\gamma}=\gamma+\varepsilon^{2}\widetilde{\gamma}.

We now embark on the task of nondimensionalizing system (
1.1.3
). For reasons that will only become clear later, we select units with the effective normalization
\gamma\mapsto 4
and
\kappa/a\mapsto 4
. Define the length and velocity scales
\mathrm{L}=a\gamma/\kappa
and
\mathrm{U}=\gamma/4
; these determine the nondimensional free surface
\boldsymbol{\zeta}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
, velocity
\boldsymbol{v}:\Omega_{\varepsilon\boldsymbol{\zeta}}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
, and pressure
\boldsymbol{q}:\Omega_{\varepsilon\boldsymbol{\zeta}}\to\mathbb{R}
via

(1.3.2)

\zeta(x)=\varepsilon\mathrm{L}\boldsymbol{\zeta}(x/\mathrm{L}),\quad v(x,z)=\mathrm{U}\boldsymbol{v}(x/\mathrm{L},z/\mathrm{L}),\text{ and }q(x,z)=\mathrm{U}^{2}\boldsymbol{q}(x/\mathrm{L},z/\mathrm{L})\text{ for }(x,z)\in\Omega_{\zeta}.

We also introduce the following non-dimensional physical parameters:

(1.3.3)

\upmu=4\kappa\mu/a\gamma^{2},\quad\mathrm{a}=4a/\gamma,\quad\mathrm{g}=16ga/\kappa\gamma,\quad\upsigma=16\sigma/\gamma^{2},\text{ and }\overline{\upgamma}=4\widetilde{\gamma}/\gamma.

In terms of (
1.3.2
) and (
1.3.3
), the system (
1.1.3
) is rewritten as

(1.3.4)

\begin{cases}(\boldsymbol{v}-(4+\varepsilon^{2}\overline{\upgamma})e_{1})\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{v}+\nabla\boldsymbol{q}-\upmu\Delta\boldsymbol{v}=4\mathrm{a}e_{1},\quad\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{v}=0&\text{in }\Omega_{\varepsilon\boldsymbol{\zeta}}\\
-(\boldsymbol{q}-\upmu\mathbb{D}\boldsymbol{v})\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon\boldsymbol{\zeta}}+(\mathrm{g}\boldsymbol{\zeta}-\varepsilon\upsigma\mathcal{H}(\varepsilon\boldsymbol{\zeta}))\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon\boldsymbol{\zeta}}=0,\quad\varepsilon(4+\varepsilon^{2}\overline{\upgamma})\partial_{1}\boldsymbol{\zeta}+\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon\boldsymbol{\zeta}}=0&\text{on }\Sigma_{\varepsilon\boldsymbol{\zeta}},\\
\boldsymbol{v}_{2}=0,\quad\upmu\partial_{2}\boldsymbol{v}_{1}=\varepsilon\mathrm{a}\boldsymbol{v}_{1}&\text{on }\Sigma_{0}.\end{cases}

The first difficulty encountered when analyzing (
1.3.4
) is that the domain
\Omega_{\varepsilon\boldsymbol{\zeta}}
itself is one of the unknowns of the problem. This is inconvenient, and our task now is to reformulate system (
1.3.4
) in a fixed slab domain of height
\varepsilon
. The tool that allows us to do so is the diffeomorphism
\mathfrak{F}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}:\overline{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}\to\overline{\Omega_{\varepsilon\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}
defined by
\mathfrak{F}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}(x,y)=(x,y\boldsymbol{\zeta}(x))
for
(x,y)\in\overline{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}
. Associated with
\mathfrak{F}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}
are the matrix quantities
\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}},\mathcal{M}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}:\Omega_{\varepsilon}\to\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}
defined by

(1.3.5)

\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}(x,y)=\nabla\mathfrak{F}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}(x,y)^{-\mathrm{t}}=\begin{pmatrix}1&-y\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{\prime}(x)/\boldsymbol{\zeta}(x)\\
0&1/\boldsymbol{\zeta}(x)\end{pmatrix}\text{ and }\mathcal{M}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}(x,y)=\boldsymbol{\zeta}(x)\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}(x,y)^{\mathrm{t}}=\begin{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{\zeta}(x)&0\\
-y\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{\prime}(x)&1\end{pmatrix},

along with the flattened velocity
\boldsymbol{u}=\boldsymbol{v}\circ\mathfrak{F}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}:\Omega_{\varepsilon}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
and pressure
\boldsymbol{p}=\boldsymbol{q}\circ\mathfrak{F}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}:\Omega_{\varepsilon}\to\mathbb{R}
.

Next, we work out the analog of (
1.1.5
) in this flattened reformulation. The flattened velocity
\boldsymbol{u}
satisfies
\nabla\cdot(\mathcal{M}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}\boldsymbol{u})=0
, and upon integrating this over
y\in(0,\varepsilon)
and substituting the flattened kinematic boundary condition
\varepsilon(4+\varepsilon^{2}\overline{\upgamma})\partial_{1}\boldsymbol{\zeta}+\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\mathcal{M}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}^{\mathrm{t}}e_{2}=0
, we find that

(1.3.6)

0=\partial_{1}\Big(\boldsymbol{\zeta}\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{1}(\cdot,y)\;\mathrm{d}y\Big)+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\mathcal{M}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}\boldsymbol{u}\cdot e_{2}=\partial_{1}\Big(\boldsymbol{\zeta}\Big(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{1}(\cdot,y)\;\mathrm{d}y-(4+\varepsilon^{2}\overline{\upgamma})\Big)\Big).

Therefore, we deduce the existence of a constant
\hat{\mathrm{A}}\in\mathbb{R}
such that the right hand argument of
\partial_{1}
above is identically
-\hat{\mathrm{A}}
, a sign convention more convenient for constructing bores. We will think of
\hat{\mathrm{A}}
as another given physical parameter in the problem. We thus arrive at the equivalent flattened reformulation of system (
1.3.4
):

(1.3.7)

\begin{cases}(\boldsymbol{u}-(4+\varepsilon^{2}\overline{\upgamma})e_{1})\cdot\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}\boldsymbol{u}+\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}\boldsymbol{p}-\upmu\Delta^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}\boldsymbol{u}=4\mathrm{a}e_{1},\quad\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}\cdot\boldsymbol{u}=0,&\text{in }\Omega_{\varepsilon},\\
-(\boldsymbol{p}-\upmu\mathbb{D}^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}\boldsymbol{u})\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon\boldsymbol{\zeta}}+(\mathrm{g}\boldsymbol{\zeta}-\varepsilon\upsigma\mathcal{H}(\varepsilon\boldsymbol{\zeta}))\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon\boldsymbol{\zeta}}=0&\text{on }\Sigma_{\varepsilon},\\
\boldsymbol{u}_{2}=0,\quad\upmu\partial_{2}^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}\boldsymbol{u}_{1}=\varepsilon\mathrm{a}\boldsymbol{u}_{1}&\text{on }\Sigma_{0},\\
\big(4+\varepsilon^{2}\overline{\upgamma}-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{1}(\cdot,y)\;\mathrm{d}y\big)\boldsymbol{\zeta}=\hat{\mathrm{A}}&\text{on }\Sigma_{\varepsilon},\end{cases}

where
\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}=\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}\nabla=(\partial_{1}^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}},\partial_{2}^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}})
,
\Delta^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}=\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}\cdot\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}
, and
\mathbb{D}^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}\boldsymbol{u}=\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}^{\mathrm{t}}+\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}\nabla\boldsymbol{u}^{\mathrm{t}}
.

1.4.
Bores, parameter tuning, and a final reformulation

As we have now reformulated the original equations (
1.1.3
) into the form (
1.3.7
), it is important to recompute the equilibrium shear solutions and give a mathematically precise definition of bores. We do this now and then give one final technically convenient reformulation of the equations.

The shear solutions to (
1.3.7
) are
\boldsymbol{\zeta}=H\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
,
\boldsymbol{p}=\mathrm{g}H
, and

(1.4.1)

\boldsymbol{u}(x,y)=b(y)e_{1}\text{ for }b(y)=4H+4(\mathrm{a}/\upmu)H^{2}(\varepsilon y-y^{2}/2),

and the relative flux identity (
1.1.7
) then becomes

(1.4.2)

(4+\varepsilon^{2}\overline{\upgamma})H-4H^{2}-\varepsilon^{2}(4\mathrm{a}/3\upmu)H^{3}=\hat{\mathrm{A}}.

With these calculations in hand, we can now give a mathematically precise definition of bores as well as their surging and ebbing forms.

Definition 0

(Bore waves)
.

Given
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
,
\upmu,\mathrm{a}>0
,
\mathrm{g},\upsigma\geqslant 0
,
\overline{\upgamma}\in\mathbb{R}
, and
\hat{\mathrm{A}}>0
, we say that a tuple
(\boldsymbol{\zeta},\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{p})\in H^{5/2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})\times H^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega_{\varepsilon};\mathbb{R}^{2})\times H^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})
is a bore wave solution system (
1.3.7
) if the following are satisfied.

(1)

We have
\boldsymbol{\zeta}(x)>0
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
and
(\boldsymbol{\zeta},\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{p})
is a strong solution to the free boundary Navier-Stokes system (
1.3.7
).

(2)

There exists
0<\mathrm{H}_{-}<\mathrm{H}_{+}
such that
(4+\varepsilon^{2}\overline{\upgamma})\mathrm{H}_{\pm}-4\mathrm{H}^{2}_{\pm}-\varepsilon^{2}\frac{4\mathrm{a}}{3\upmu}\mathrm{H}^{3}_{\pm}=\hat{\mathrm{A}}
.

(3)

There exists
\iota\in\{{-1,1}\}
such that for every
y\in(0,\varepsilon)
we have that

(1.4.3)

\lim_{x\to\pm\infty}\boldsymbol{\zeta}(\iota x)=\mathrm{H}_{\pm},\quad\lim_{x\to\pm\infty}\boldsymbol{u}_{1}(\iota x,y)=4\mathrm{H}_{\pm}+\frac{4\mathrm{a}}{\upmu}\mathrm{H}^{2}_{\pm}\Big(\varepsilon y-\frac{1}{2}y^{2}\Big),\text{ and }\lim_{x\to\pm\infty}\boldsymbol{u}_{2}(\iota x,y)=0.

If
(\boldsymbol{\zeta},\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{p})
is a bore wave, then we say that it is surging if
\iota=-1
and ebbing if
\iota=1
.

We show that both surging and ebbing bore solutions to system (
1.3.7
) exist. The roots of the polynomial in (
1.4.2
) determine the asymptotic heights of the bore waves, and in general, these roots are nontrivially
\varepsilon
-dependent. However, for technical reasons in our construction it is crucial that the roots
\mathrm{H}_{\pm}>0
from the second item of the definition are actually independent of
\varepsilon
; this can be achieved via the judicious selection of
\overline{\upgamma}
and
\hat{\mathrm{A}}
, as we now show. We first make the ansatz that
\hat{\mathrm{A}}=\mathrm{A}+\varepsilon^{2}\overline{\mathrm{A}}
with the parameter
\mathrm{A}\in(0,1)
freely chosen but the parameters
\overline{\mathrm{A}},\overline{\upgamma}\in\mathbb{R}
determined in terms of the other physical parameters as follows. When
\varepsilon=0
, the cubic in (
1.4.2
) reduces to a quadratic equation with roots
\mathrm{H}_{\pm}=\mathrm{H}_{\pm}(\mathrm{A})>0
satisfying

(1.4.4)

\mathrm{H}_{\pm}-\mathrm{H}^{2}_{\pm}=\mathrm{A}/4,\text{ and hence }\mathrm{H}_{\pm}=(1\pm(1-\mathrm{A})^{1/2})/2.

Observe that
0<\mathrm{H}_{-}<1/2<\mathrm{H}_{+}<1
and that
\mathrm{H}_{+}+\mathrm{H}_{-}=1
. We then define
\overline{\upgamma}
and
\overline{\mathrm{A}}
in such a way that both
\mathrm{H}_{+}
and
\mathrm{H}_{-}
are solutions to the cubic (
1.4.2
) for all
\varepsilon>0
, which in light of (
1.4.4
) is equivalent to
\overline{\upgamma}
and
\overline{\mathrm{A}}
solving

(1.4.5)

\overline{\upgamma}\mathrm{H}_{\pm}-(4\mathrm{a}/3\upmu)\mathrm{H}_{\pm}^{3}=\overline{\mathrm{A}}\text{ and hence }\overline{\upgamma}=(4\mathrm{a}/3\upmu)(\mathrm{H}_{+}^{2}+\mathrm{H}_{+}\mathrm{H}_{-}+\mathrm{H}^{2}_{-})\text{ and }\overline{\mathrm{A}}=(4\mathrm{a}/3\upmu)(\mathrm{H}_{+}^{2}\mathrm{H}_{-}+\mathrm{H}_{+}\mathrm{H}_{-}^{2}).

We refer to the above as the parameter tuning identities; they play an essential role in our analysis. Notice that
\mathrm{H}_{\pm}
are only functions of the parameter
\mathrm{A}\in(0,1)
(thanks to our choice of units in (
1.3.2
)). As
\mathrm{A}\to 0
, we have
(\mathrm{H}_{-},\mathrm{H}_{+})\to(0,1)
whereas when
\mathrm{A}\to 1
we have
(\mathrm{H}_{-},\mathrm{H}_{+})\to(1/2,1/2)
. Therefore,
\mathrm{A}
is a measurement of the separation of the asymptotic bore heights, and we will occasionally refer to
\mathrm{A}\sim 1
as the small bore regime and
\mathrm{A}\sim 0
as the large bore regime.

We conclude this subsection by making one further reformulation of the Navier-Stokes system (
1.3.7
). The current form of the final equation is somewhat unacceptable in that the regularity count of the free surface function relative to that of the velocity is suboptimal. This can be fixed by employing the following trick. The final equation is written

(1.4.6)

\boldsymbol{d}=\mathrm{A}+\varepsilon^{2}\overline{\mathrm{A}}\text{ for }\boldsymbol{d}=\Big(4+\varepsilon^{2}\overline{\upgamma}-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{1}(\cdot,y)\;\mathrm{d}y\Big)\boldsymbol{\zeta}.

In our functional framework the quantity
\boldsymbol{d}
will always be a tempered distribution (in fact, a bounded function), so the above equation is equivalent to
\boldsymbol{d}-\partial_{1}^{2}\boldsymbol{d}=\mathrm{A}+\varepsilon^{2}\overline{\mathrm{A}}
. A computation similar to (
1.3.6
) shows that

(1.4.7)

\partial_{1}\boldsymbol{d}=(4+\varepsilon^{2}\overline{\upgamma})\partial_{1}\boldsymbol{\zeta}+(\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon\boldsymbol{\zeta}})/\varepsilon,

and hence
\boldsymbol{d}-\partial_{1}^{2}\boldsymbol{d}=\mathrm{A}+\varepsilon^{2}\overline{\mathrm{A}}
is equivalent to the final identity in our next, and final, reformulation of the free boundary Navier-Stokes system:

(1.4.8)

\begin{cases}(\boldsymbol{u}-(4+\varepsilon^{2}\overline{\upgamma})e_{1})\cdot\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}\boldsymbol{u}+\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}\boldsymbol{p}-\upmu\Delta^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}\boldsymbol{u}=4\mathrm{a}e_{1},\quad\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}\cdot\boldsymbol{u}=0&\text{in }\Omega_{\varepsilon},\\
-(\boldsymbol{p}-\upmu\mathbb{D}^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}\boldsymbol{u})\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon\boldsymbol{\zeta}}+(\mathrm{g}\boldsymbol{\zeta}-\varepsilon\upsigma\mathcal{H}(\varepsilon\boldsymbol{\zeta}))\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon\boldsymbol{\zeta}}=0&\text{on }\Sigma_{\varepsilon},\\
\boldsymbol{u}_{2}=0,\quad\upmu\partial_{2}^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}\boldsymbol{u}_{1}=\varepsilon\mathrm{a}\boldsymbol{u}_{1}&\text{on }\Sigma_{0},\\
\big(4+\varepsilon^{2}\overline{\upgamma}-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{1}(\cdot,y)\;\mathrm{d}y\big)\boldsymbol{\zeta}-(4+\varepsilon^{2}\overline{\upgamma})\partial_{1}^{2}\boldsymbol{\zeta}-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\partial_{1}(\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon\boldsymbol{\zeta}})=\mathrm{A}+\varepsilon^{2}\overline{\mathrm{A}}&\text{on }\Sigma_{\varepsilon}.\end{cases}

1.5.
Statement of the main results and discussion

In terms of the nondimensional parameters (
1.3.3
), there are no restrictions on
\upmu,\mathrm{a}>0
or
\upsigma\geqslant 0
for our bore construction, but we must constrain the parameters
\mathrm{g}\geqslant 0
and
\mathrm{A}\in(0,1)
to belong to certain sets, which we need to describe now in order to precisely state our main results. Define the function
\uprho_{-}:(0,1)\to\mathbb{R}
via
\uprho_{-}(\mathrm{A})=\log\mathrm{H}_{-}(\mathrm{A})
, where
\mathrm{H}_{-}
is given in (
1.4.4
). We then define the function
\uprho_{\star}:(0,1)\to\mathbb{R}
via the condition that
\uprho_{\star}(\mathrm{A})>\uprho_{-}(\mathrm{A})
is the unique number such that

(1.5.1)

\int_{\uprho_{-}(\mathrm{A})}^{\uprho_{\star}(\mathrm{A})}\Big(1-e^{x}-\frac{\mathrm{A}}{4}e^{-x}\Big)\;\mathrm{d}x=0.

The rigorous construction of
\uprho_{\star}
is handled in Lemma
2.1
. Using the functions
\uprho_{-}
and
\uprho_{\star}
, we define the sets

(1.5.2)

\mathfrak{C}_{1}=\{{(\mathrm{g},\mathrm{A})\in[0,\infty)\times(0,1)\;:\;\mathrm{g}<\mathrm{A}^{2}e^{-3\uprho_{\star}(\mathrm{A})}}\}\text{ and }\mathfrak{C}_{-1}=\{{(\mathrm{g},\mathrm{A})\in[0,\infty)\times(0,1)\;:\;\mathrm{g}>\mathrm{A}^{2}e^{-3\uprho_{-}(\mathrm{A})}}\},

each of which is nonempty (see Lemma
2.2
for further details). A graphical depiction may be found in Figure
2
.

Figure 2
.

The blue and red shaded regions in
(\mathrm{g},\mathrm{A})
-space depicted above, labeled
\mathfrak{C}_{\pm 1}
and defined in (
1.5.2
), correspond to the parameter values for which we are able to produce traveling bore waves in Theorem
1
. The waves coming from the blue
\mathfrak{C}_{1}
region are ebbing bores, whereas the waves coming from the red
\mathfrak{C}_{-1}
region are surging bores. The parameter
\mathrm{A}\in(0,1)
on the vertical axis measures the separation of the equilibrium heights, with
\mathrm{A}
close to
1
being the ‘small bore’ regime and
\mathrm{A}
close to
0
being the ‘large bore’ regime. Notice that the only value of
\mathrm{g}
not covered by these regions is
\mathrm{g}=8
.

We can now state our main theorem, the proof of which is found in Section
5.3
. In what follows, the space
W^{\infty,\infty}
consists of smooth functions with all derivatives bounded, while
H^{\infty}\subset W^{\infty,\infty}
enforces that the function itself and all of its derivatives are square summable. See Section
1.6
for the precise conventions of notation.

Theorem 1

(Proved in Section
5.3
)
.

Fix
\upmu,\mathrm{a}>0
,
\iota\in\{{-1,1}\}
,
(\mathrm{g},\mathrm{A})\in\mathfrak{C}_{\iota}
, and
\upsigma\geqslant 0
. There exist
\mathsf{H}\in W^{\infty,\infty}(\mathbb{R})
,
C,\delta\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
, and
\epsilon_{\star}\in(0,1)
with the property that for all
\varepsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{\star})
there exists
\overline{\eta}\in H^{\infty}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})
,
\overline{u}\in H^{\infty}(\Omega_{\varepsilon};\mathbb{R}^{2})
, and
\overline{p}\in H^{\infty}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})
such that the following hold.

(1)

We have the estimate
\lVert\overline{\eta}\rVert_{H^{5/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})}+\lVert\overline{u}\rVert_{(L^{\infty}\cap H^{2})(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}+\lVert\overline{p}\rVert_{H^{1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}\leqslant C\varepsilon
.

(2)

The function
\mathsf{H}
is everywhere positive, solves the ODE

(1.5.3)

4\upmu\mathrm{A}\Big(\frac{\mathsf{H}^{\prime}}{\mathsf{H}}\Big)^{\prime}=4\mathrm{a}\Big(1-\mathsf{H}-\frac{\mathrm{A}}{4\mathsf{H}}\Big)-\Big(\frac{\mathrm{A}^{2}}{\mathsf{H}^{2}}-\mathrm{g}\mathsf{H}\Big)\mathsf{H}^{\prime}\text{ in }\mathbb{R},

and obeys the limits

(1.5.4)

\lim_{x\to\pm\infty}\mathsf{H}(\iota x)=\mathrm{H}_{\pm},

where
\mathrm{H}_{\pm}
are defined in (
1.4.4
). Moreover,
\mathsf{H}^{\prime}\in H^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
.

(3)

Upon defining
\boldsymbol{\zeta}\in W^{\infty,\infty}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})
,
\boldsymbol{u}\in W^{\infty,\infty}(\Omega_{\varepsilon};\mathbb{R}^{2})
, and
\boldsymbol{p}\in W^{\infty,\infty}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})
via

(1.5.5)

\boldsymbol{\zeta}=\mathsf{H}+\overline{\eta},\quad\boldsymbol{u}=\Big(4\mathsf{H}+\frac{4\mathrm{a}}{\upmu}\mathsf{H}^{2}\Big(\varepsilon y-\frac{1}{2}y^{2}\Big)\Big)e_{1}+\Big(4-\frac{\mathrm{A}}{\mathsf{H}}-4\mathsf{H}\Big)e_{1}+\overline{u},\text{ and }\boldsymbol{p}=\mathrm{g}\mathsf{H}-2\upmu\mathrm{A}\frac{\mathsf{H}^{\prime}}{\mathsf{H}^{2}}+\overline{p},

the triple
(\boldsymbol{\zeta},\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{p})
is a classical bore wave solution to system (
1.3.7
) in the sense of Definition
0
with
\hat{\mathrm{A}}=\mathrm{A}+\varepsilon^{2}\overline{\mathrm{A}}
and
\overline{\upgamma}
,
\overline{\mathrm{A}}
determined by the parameter tuning identities (
1.4.5
). Moreover,
\inf(4-\boldsymbol{u}_{1})\geqslant\delta
.

(4)

If
\iota=-1
then
(\boldsymbol{\zeta},\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{p})
is a surging bore, and if
\iota=1
then
(\boldsymbol{\zeta},\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{p})
is an ebbing bore.

Several remarks about the theorem are in order. First, we refer to Figure
3
for numerical simulations of the bore wave solutions produced by our main result. These highlight the striking differences between surging and ebbing bores and also demonstrate that each appears to come in both monotone and oscillatory forms. Second, while the set
\mathfrak{C}_{1}
is bounded, the set
\mathfrak{C}_{-1}
is unbounded and of infinite measure, which gives a sense in which surging bores are more common than their ebbing counterparts. Third, we are only able to guarantee that bore waves exist with
(\mathrm{g},\mathrm{A})\in\mathfrak{C}_{-1}\cup\mathfrak{C}_{1}
, and we make no claim as to whether or not bores, or other types of traveling waves for that matter, exist for parameter values outside this set; our results simply do not address this region (the white part of Figure
2
). Fourth, the entirety of the result applies in the case
\upsigma=0
, the regime in which surface tension is entirely neglected. In particular, this confirms that, indeed, gravitational forces alone can generate bores.

Figure 3
.
Here we present four numerical simulations of the graphs of
\mathsf{H}
, the leading order parts of the free surface components corresponding to our rightward traveling bores constructed by Theorem
1
(with
\upsigma=0
). Aspects of the fluid velocity are depicted in the subgraph. The streamlines shown are that of the flow seen by an observer
moving with the wave
. The color indicates the variation in the curl, which is negative throughout, with violet and red indicating the regions of largest and smallest magnitudes, respectively. The top row has solutions that appear to be monotone, while the bottom row has solutions that appear oscillatory. On the left we show ebbing bores, corresponding to
(\mathrm{g},\mathrm{A})\in\mathfrak{C}_{1}
, whereas on the right we show surging bores, corresponding to
(\mathrm{g},\mathrm{A})\in\mathfrak{C}_{-1}
. The sets
\mathfrak{C}_{\pm 1}
are defined in (
1.5.2
) and sketched in Figure
2
.

The following corollaries unpack specific consequences of our main result that are worth emphasizing. To contextualize the first we recall that the parameter tuning (
1.4.5
) forces the
\varepsilon
-independent end state heights
\mathrm{H}_{\pm}
(given by (
1.4.4
)) of any bore wave to satisfy
0<\mathrm{H}_{-}<\mathrm{H}_{+}<1
, but these states are very close for large portions of the parameter space
\mathfrak{C}_{-1}\cup\mathfrak{C}_{1}
. Our first corollary underlines the fact that Theorem
1
yields bores for which
\mathrm{H}_{+}-\mathrm{H}_{-}
is arbitrarily close to
1
and the jumps in the velocity and pressure are also of unit order.

Corollary 2

(Existence of large shallow bore waves)
.

Let
0<\alpha<1
,
\iota\in\{{-1,1}\}
,
\upmu,\mathrm{a}>0
, and
\upsigma\geqslant 0
. There exists
(\mathrm{g},\mathrm{A})\in\mathfrak{C}_{\iota}
and a smooth traveling bore wave solution
(\boldsymbol{\zeta},\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{\eta})
to (
1.3.7
) produced by Theorem
1
with end state heights satisfying
\lim_{x\to\pm\infty}\boldsymbol{\zeta}(\iota x)=\mathrm{H}_{\pm}
with
\mathrm{H}_{+}-\mathrm{H}_{-}>1-\alpha
, and end state velocity and pressure satisfying

(1.5.6)

\iota\cdot\lim_{x\to\infty}[{\boldsymbol{u}(x,y)-\boldsymbol{u}(-x,y)}]=4(\mathrm{H}_{+}-\mathrm{H}_{-})\Big(1+\frac{\mathrm{a}}{\upmu}\Big(\varepsilon y-\frac{1}{2}y^{2}\Big)\Big)e_{1}\text{ and }\iota\cdot\lim_{x\to\infty}[{\boldsymbol{p}(x,y)-\boldsymbol{p}(-x,y)}]=\mathrm{g}(\mathrm{H}_{+}-\mathrm{H}_{-}),

for
0<y<\varepsilon
.

Our second corollary emphasizes that the bore waves produced by Theorem
1
become increasingly well-approximated by solutions to one-dimensional shallow water equations as
\varepsilon\to 0
.

Corollary 3

(The shallow water limit for bore waves)
.

Under the hypotheses of Theorem
1
, let
0<\varepsilon<\epsilon_{\star}
and

(1.5.7)

\mathsf{H}\in W^{\infty,\infty}(\mathbb{R}),\quad\boldsymbol{\zeta}\in W^{\infty,\infty}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon}),\text{ and }\boldsymbol{u}\in W^{\infty,\infty}(\Omega_{\varepsilon};\mathbb{R}^{2})

be components of any bore wave solution to system (
1.3.7
) produced by Theorem
1
. Define
\mathsf{U}=4-\mathrm{A}/\mathsf{H}\in W^{\infty,\infty}(\mathbb{R})
. Then we have the estimates

(1.5.8)

\lVert\boldsymbol{\zeta}-\mathsf{H}\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})}+\lVert\boldsymbol{u}-\mathsf{U}e_{1}\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}\lesssim\varepsilon

for an implicit constant independent of
\varepsilon
. Moreover,
\mathsf{H}
and
\mathsf{U}
are a traveling wave solution to the one-dimensional inclined viscous shallow water equations with laminar drag:

(1.5.9)

((4-\mathsf{U})\mathsf{H})^{\prime}=0,\quad\mathsf{H}(\mathsf{U}-4)\mathsf{U}^{\prime}+\mathrm{a}\mathsf{U}-4\upmu(\mathsf{H}\mathsf{U}^{\prime})^{\prime}+\mathrm{g}\mathsf{H}\mathsf{H}^{\prime}=4\mathrm{a}\mathsf{H}.

The final corollary demonstrates that we can undo the flattening and nondimensionalization procedure of Sections
1.3
and
1.4
to obtain bore wave solutions to the Eulerian traveling Navier-Stokes system (
1.1.3
).

Corollary 4

(Dimensional, Eulerian bores)
.

Fix the following dimensional parameters: viscosity
\mu>0
, horizontal gravitational acceleration
\kappa>0
, slip parameter
a>0
, vertical gravitational acceleration
g\geqslant 0
, and surface tension
\sigma\geqslant 0
. Let
0<\gamma<\infty
be a traveling frame speed such that
\gamma\neq 2ga/\kappa
, and let
\iota\in\{{-1,1}\}
be such that
\iota(\gamma-2ga/\kappa)>0
.

The set of
\mathrm{A}\in(0,1)
such that
(16ga/\kappa\gamma,\mathrm{A})\in\mathfrak{C}_{\iota}
is nonempty; for each such
\mathrm{A}
there exists
\epsilon_{\star}\in(0,1)
with the property that for all
\varepsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{\star})
there exists
\zeta\in W^{\infty,\infty}(\mathbb{R})
with
\zeta>0
,
v\in W^{\infty,\infty}(\Omega_{\zeta};\mathbb{R}^{2})
, and
q\in W^{\infty,\infty}(\Omega_{\zeta})
such that upon setting

(1.5.10)

\overline{\gamma}=\gamma+\varepsilon^{2}\frac{a^{2}\gamma^{2}}{3\kappa\mu}(\mathrm{H}_{+}(\mathrm{A})^{2}+\mathrm{H}_{+}(\mathrm{A})\mathrm{H}_{-}(\mathrm{A})+\mathrm{H}_{-}(\mathrm{A})^{2}),\quad\overline{g}=g/\varepsilon,\quad\overline{\sigma}=\varepsilon\sigma,\text{ and }\overline{a}=\varepsilon a

we have that
(\zeta,v,q)
is a smooth bore wave solution to the free boundary Navier-Stokes system (
1.1.3
) obeying the limits

(1.5.11)

\lim_{x\to\pm\infty}\zeta(\iota x)=\varepsilon\frac{a\gamma}{\kappa}\mathrm{H}_{\pm}(\mathrm{A})=\zeta_{\pm},\quad\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\to\pm\infty\\
0<z<\varepsilon(a\gamma/\kappa)\mathrm{H}_{\pm}(\mathrm{A})\end{subarray}}q(\iota x,z)=\frac{ga\gamma}{\kappa}\mathrm{H}_{\pm}(\mathrm{A})=\overline{g}\zeta_{\pm},

and

(1.5.12)

\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\to\pm\infty\\
0<z<\varepsilon(a\gamma/\kappa)\mathrm{H}_{\pm}(\mathrm{A})\end{subarray}}v(\iota x,z)=\Big(\gamma\mathrm{H}_{\pm}(\mathrm{A})+\varepsilon\frac{a\gamma}{\mu}\mathrm{H}_{\pm}(\mathrm{A})z-\frac{\kappa}{2\mu}z^{2}\Big)e_{1}=\Big(\frac{\kappa\zeta_{\pm}}{\overline{a}}+\frac{\kappa\zeta_{\pm}}{\mu}z-\frac{\kappa}{2\mu}z^{2}\Big)e_{1}.

In particular, if
\iota=-1
then
0<\gamma<2ga/\kappa
and the solution is a surging bore, while if
\iota=1
then
\gamma>2ga/\kappa
and the solution is an ebbing bore. Moreover, the relative velocity flux
\Phi<0
(see (
1.1.5
) and (
1.1.7
)) of these solutions takes the form

(1.5.13)

\Phi=-\varepsilon\frac{a\gamma^{2}}{4\kappa}\mathrm{A}-\varepsilon^{3}\frac{a^{3}\gamma^{3}}{3\kappa^{2}\mu}(\mathrm{H}_{+}(\mathrm{A})^{2}\mathrm{H}_{-}(\mathrm{A})+\mathrm{H}_{+}(\mathrm{A})\mathrm{H}_{-}(\mathrm{A})^{2}).

We emphasize that Corollary
4
establishes our previous ubiquity claim for shallow bores. Indeed, for any given set of fixed physical parameters
\mu,\kappa,a>0
and
g,\sigma\geqslant 0
the system (
1.1.3
) is guaranteed to admit traveling bore wave solutions for every leading order choice of wave speed
\gamma>0
, excepting the sole choice
\gamma=2ga/\kappa
, and every
\varepsilon>0
sufficiently small. An important consequence of the corollary is that we produce surging bores if and only if
\gamma\kappa/2ga<1
, and this dimensionless quantity can be understood as a sort of averaged Froude number. Indeed, if we average the end state heights and bottom speeds, we get
\zeta_{\mathrm{avg}}=\overline{a}\gamma/2\kappa
and
v_{\mathrm{avg}}=\gamma/2
, so if we define a Froude number,
\mathrm{Fr}
, from these we obtain

(1.5.14)

\mathrm{Fr}^{2}=\frac{v_{\mathrm{avg}}^{2}}{\overline{g}\zeta_{\mathrm{avg}}}=\frac{\gamma\kappa}{2ga}=\frac{8}{\mathrm{g}}.

Thus, our surging bores all satisfy the subcritical Froude number condition
\mathrm{Fr}<1
, while the ebbing bores satisfy the supercritical condition
\mathrm{Fr}>1
, and we fail to construct any bores at the critical value
\mathrm{Fr}=1
.

Corollary
4
also shows that, while the bore waves we construct are small amplitude in physical units (as seen by the first limit in (
1.5.11
)), the pressure and velocity differentials are made large in physical units when the parameter
\mathrm{A}\in(0,1)
is near zero. In the dimensional form of Corollary
4
it is also easier than in Theorem
1
to single out the most important physical parameters (and thus effects). Viscosity, slip, and horizontal gravity are essential to our result (i.e.
\mu,a,\kappa>0
), as it is the ‘competition’ between these three that leads to bore generation. On the other hand, capillarity plays no essential role and may be neglected
(\sigma=0)
, and while vertical gravity can also be neglected
(g=0)
this results only in ebbing bores.

Before turning to the discussion of the difficulties and techniques used to prove Theorem
1
, we pause to highlight several related lines of inquiry that are beyond the scope of this work but that we believe are important and interesting. First, is it necessary to have
\kappa>0
in order to produce bores, or can they somehow be sustained purely with vertical gravity? Second, do bore-like solutions exist with
\kappa>0
but
\overline{\gamma}<0
, i.e. waves traveling upstream? Third, what happens in other scaling regimes when capillarity is no longer perturbative?

We now turn to a discussion of our techniques for constructing bore waves, which will also serve as an outline of the paper’s structure. As is clear in the statement of Theorem
1
, our strategy hinges on the assumption that the fluid is shallow, as this allows us to make connections to the viscous shallow water equations (sometimes called the viscous Saint-Venant equations). This is a system of simplified equations and variables that emerge as the effective limit of the Navier-Stokes system (
1.1.3
) (and its dynamic form, as well) in both the vanishing depth and long wavelength limit. Taking this limit necessitates introducing the parameter
\varepsilon>0
and making a number of assumptions about the
\varepsilon
-scaling of various parameters and unknowns. Our selection of assumptions in this paper is made as in (
1.3.4
) to ensure that viscous, slip, and horizontal gravitational effects survive in the limit, but there are, in fact, many other possibilities. Indeed, the literature on the shallow water and related equations is varied and vast; we refer, for instance, to the works
[
52
,
66
,
48
,
32
,
9
,
10
,
11
,
54
,
13
,
56
,
44
,
78
,
76
]
and the references therein for further exploration.

The vanishing depth limit of (
1.1.3
) is known (see, for instance, Appendix B in Stevenson and Tice
[
81
]
) to be described by two simple unknowns,
(\mathsf{H},\mathsf{U}):\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}
, the effective fluid height and horizontal velocity, respectively. These satisfy the (nondimensionalized) traveling wave viscous shallow water system (
1.5.9
)
in which the Navier-Stokes forcing terms manifest as
4\upmu(\mathsf{H}\mathsf{U}^{\prime})^{\prime}
for shear viscosity,
\mathrm{a}\mathsf{U}
for slip,
\mathrm{g}\mathsf{H}\mathsf{H}^{\prime}
for vertical gravity, and
4\mathrm{a}\mathsf{H}
for horizontal gravity. Mirroring what we did for Navier-Stokes, we integrate the first equation in (
1.5.9
) and introduce a shallow water flux parameter:
(4-\mathsf{U})\mathsf{H}=\mathrm{A}\in\mathbb{R}
. This allows us to solve for
\mathsf{U}
in terms of
\mathsf{H}
and thereby reduce to a second order ODE for
\mathsf{H}
alone, namely (
1.5.3
). The appearance of the quantity
(\mathsf{H}^{\prime}/\mathsf{H})^{\prime}
in (
1.5.3
) and the importance of
\mathsf{H}>0
both suggest the swap to the new unknown
\rho:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
determined via
e^{\rho}=\mathsf{H}
. This yields a reformulation of (
1.5.3
) in terms of
\rho
as the Liénard-type equation

(1.5.15)

\rho^{\prime\prime}=F(\rho)-G(\rho)\rho^{\prime},

where
F,G:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
are the smooth functions given by

(1.5.16)

F(x)=\frac{\mathrm{a}}{\upmu\mathrm{A}}\Big(1-e^{x}-\frac{\mathrm{A}}{4}e^{-x}\Big)\text{ and }G(x)=\frac{1}{4\upmu}\Big(\mathrm{A}e^{-x}-\frac{\mathrm{g}}{\mathrm{A}}e^{2x}\Big).

There are two equilibrium states for (
1.5.15
), corresponding to the roots of
F
and given by
\uprho_{\pm}=\log\mathrm{H}_{\pm}
with
\mathrm{H}_{\pm}\in(0,1)
as defined in (
1.4.4
). This fact reveals a key point about how we have selected the parameter tuning in (
1.4.5
): this has been done specifically to make the positive roots of the cubic relative flux equation (
1.4.2
) match up with the equilibrium states of
F
as above, and to allow the parameter
\mathrm{A}
to serve as a relative flux measurement in both the Navier-Stokes and shallow water systems. In turn, this provides a tantalizing first indication of the existence of bore waves since the ODE (
1.5.15
) can in principle support heteroclinic orbits between
\uprho_{\pm}
, which upon swapping back to the
\mathsf{H}
and
\mathsf{U}
variables would yield a shallow water approximation of a bore wave.

To pursue this line of reasoning, we need more refined information about the equation (
1.5.15
), and in particular the functions
F
and
G
. The function
G
is strictly decreasing and converges to
\mp\infty
as
x\to\pm\infty
, while
F
is strictly concave and satisfies
F(x)>0
if and only if
\uprho_{-}<x<\uprho_{+}
. The structure of (
1.5.15
) suggests introducing the potential for
F
, the smooth function
V:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
, which we will zero at the
\uprho_{-}
state:

(1.5.17)

V(x)=-\int_{\uprho_{-}}^{x}F(s)\;\mathrm{d}s.

Then
V
has exactly two critical points, namely
\uprho_{\pm}
, but there is a third important state,
\uprho_{\star}>\uprho_{+}
, which we have already seen appear in (
1.5.1
) and is selected such that
V(\uprho_{\star})=0
. This means that
\uprho_{-}
and
\uprho_{\star}
are equipotential states bookending the lower energy equilibrium
\uprho_{+}
: see Figure
4
for a graphical depiction.

Figure 4
.
We plot the graph of the potential energy
V
(
1.5.17
) of the Liénard system (
1.5.15
). The states
\uprho_{-}
and
\uprho_{+}
are the critical points of
V
. The top dashed line indicates
V=0
and intersects the equipotential states
\uprho_{-}
and
\uprho_{\star}
. The lower dashed line shows
V=V(\uprho_{+})<0
. See also Figure
5
for associated phase space portraits.

This conjunction of special properties of
V
and
G
then provides a mechanism for constructing heteroclinic orbits between
\uprho_{\pm}
. Indeed, as we prove in Section
2.1
, when
G
has a sign between the equipotential states, meaning
|G(x)|>0
for all
x\in[\uprho_{-},\uprho_{\star}]
, we can combine energy bounds and topological arguments to prove that the ODE (
1.5.15
) admits a global solution over
\mathbb{R}
whose far field limits are the equilibria
\uprho_{-}
and
\uprho_{+}
. Since
G
is decreasing there are exactly two ways it can have a sign in
[\uprho_{-},\uprho_{\star}]
:
G(\uprho_{\star})>0
, in which case the
G
term in
2.1
acts dissipatively, driving the orbit from
\uprho_{-}
to the lower potential state
\uprho_{+}
(an ebbing bore); and
G(\uprho_{-})<0
, in which case
G
acts as an amplifier, driving the orbit up the potential from
\uprho_{+}
to
\uprho_{-}
(a surging bore). The sets
\mathfrak{C}_{\pm 1}
, which are defined in (
1.5.2
) and depicted in Figure
2
, precisely encode the values of the parameters
(\mathrm{g},\mathrm{A})
for which these sign conditions hold, with
\mathfrak{C}_{1}
corresponding to
G>0
and
\mathfrak{C}_{-1}
corresponding to
G<0
. A crucial feature of this construction is that the state
\uprho_{-}
is always hyperbolic, while
\uprho_{+}
is a sink when
G>0
and a source when
G<0
. This means that while the actual solutions to (
1.5.15
) are not unique due to translation invariance, the orbit itself (viewed as a curve in phase space) is uniquely determined.

With the heteroclinic orbit solutions to (
1.5.15
) in hand, the aim is to return to the Navier-Stokes system and attempt to construct bore wave solutions as perturbations of the shallow water bores. It turns out that there is not a unique way to attempt this, but our strategy (and most other plausible ones) for doing so involves introducing a model Stokes problem in the
\varepsilon
-thin domain
\Omega_{\varepsilon}
with non-constant coefficients determined by a given function
h:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}^{+}
: see (
3.2.1
) for the precise statement. As we will discuss in greater detail later, the goal is to show that this
\varepsilon
-Stokes problem induces an isomorphism between certain Banach spaces and build the bore waves via a fixed point argument that crucially employs this isomorphism. The
\varepsilon
-Stokes system is elliptic (in the sense of Agmon, Douglis, and Nirenberg
[
2
]
), so it is perhaps unsurprising that it induces an isomorphism, but the key point here is that in order for this to be useful in a fixed point argument we need to understand how the operator norms of this map and its inverse depend on
\varepsilon
and the function
h
. This, it turns out, is a nontrivial task.

The principal technical tool for obtaining estimates to Stokes problems with applied stress conditions such as (
3.2.1
) is the Korn inequality. Thus, in some sense, the main task then reduces to having a good understanding Korn’s inequality in
\varepsilon
-thin domains, and here fortune favors us somewhat. Indeed, Chapter 3 of Lewicka
[
50
]
provides a detailed treatment of the Korn inequality, including an analysis in thin domains based on earlier work of Lewicka and Müller
[
51
]
. Unfortunately, the exact result we require does not appear in either of these works. Nevertheless, the ideas and techniques developed there are adaptable, and we use them as the foundation on which we build our specialized thin domain Korn inequality for the
\varepsilon
-Stokes problem (
3.2.1
). This, other
\varepsilon
-thin functional inequalities, and the
\varepsilon
-Stokes problem are our focus of study in Section
3
.

Finally, we turn to a discussion of the principal part of our construction. This is quite technical if presented in full detail, so our aim here is to give a schematic, abstract overview and emphasize the connections to the aforementioned tools. The precise details are recorded in Sections
4
and
5
. In broad strokes, the idea is to mimic the derivation of the shallow water system from Navier-Stokes by positing that the full solution is decomposed into a leading order part that is close to the shallow water bore in some sense, and a small residual term that can be constructed as a fixed point, at least when
\varepsilon
is sufficiently small. However, actually executing this strategy turns out to be significantly more delicate than what is suggested by the shallow water derivation.

In the following discussion, the solution to the Navier-Stokes system (
1.4.8
) we seek will be denoted
\boldsymbol{X}=(\boldsymbol{\zeta},\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{p})
. The first task is to specify the structural relationship between
\boldsymbol{X}
and our shallow water heteroclinic orbit,
(\mathsf{H},\mathsf{U})
, which we do by making the schematic ansatz

(1.5.18)

\boldsymbol{X}=\mathscr{P}(X,\varepsilon^{2}x)+\varepsilon^{2}x.

In the above
X=(H,U,\dotsc)
is a tuple of smooth, but not necessarily square summable, scalar functions over
\mathbb{R}
referred to as the
ODE unknowns
; these should be thought of as approximations
H\approx\mathsf{H}
and
U\approx\mathsf{U}
, with the other terms in the tuple playing auxiliary roles. The tuple
x=(\eta,u,p)
constitutes the
residual unknowns
; these encode the difference between the Navier-Stokes and shallow water solutions, and we will assume these belong to
L^{2}
-based Sobolev spaces. The
ansatz prefix

\mathscr{P}(\cdot,\cdot)
dictates the leading order contribution to the Navier-Stokes solution
\boldsymbol{X}
as a function of the ODE unknowns and a scaled down version of the residual unknowns. The precise form of
\mathscr{P}
is chosen to satisfy three mandates: first, it should be close to the shallow water bore solutions; second, it should yield the correct shear flow equilibria in the far field; and third, the dependence on
\varepsilon^{2}x
should act to minimize the complexity of the resulting equations for
X
and
x
.

Once we select the prefix operator
\mathscr{P}
, we plug the ansatz into the Navier-Stokes system (
1.4.8
). Due to the increased number of unknown terms in the ansatz, this obviously results in an underdetermined system for
x
and
X
, so we must impose enough auxiliary equations to make the system determined. In our case, all of the new equations will be ODEs, which explains the naming convention for the new
X
variable. A good guiding principle for finding these ODEs is to sort the terms in the system according to their size relative to
\varepsilon
and impose equations to make the highest order terms vanish. We emphasize that there is no unique way to do this and that it can be advantageous to shift terms in the sorting hierarchy to make analysis easier.

The most obvious choice of auxiliary ODEs decouples the
X
unknown from
x
and yields a nonlinear autonomous system

(1.5.19)

\mathscr{N}_{\mathrm{ODE}}(X)=0,

which is precisely solved by our distinguished shallow water solution
X=\mathsf{X}=(\mathsf{H},\mathsf{U},\dots)
. Plugging
X=\mathsf{X}
into the ansatz then provides a nonlinear PDE system that must be satisfied by the residual unknown
x
in order for
\boldsymbol{X}
to solve (
1.4.8
); we write this schematically as

(1.5.20)

0=\mathscr{N}_{\mathrm{PDE}}^{\varepsilon}(\mathsf{X},x)=\mathscr{L}^{\varepsilon}(\mathsf{X},\varepsilon^{2}x)x+\mathscr{A}^{\varepsilon}(\mathsf{X})x+\mathscr{F}^{\varepsilon}(\mathsf{X},\varepsilon x),

with the nonlinear operator
\mathscr{N}^{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{PDE}}
decomposed into three distinct pieces. The first and most important of these,
\mathscr{L}^{\varepsilon}(\mathsf{X},\varepsilon^{2}x)x
, is based on a version of the
\varepsilon
-Stokes operator discussed above that is quasilinear because its coefficients depend on both
\mathsf{X}
and
\varepsilon^{2}x
. The second term consists of the
adversarial linear operator

\mathscr{A}^{\varepsilon}(\mathsf{X})
applied to
x
; the moniker adversarial is to emphasize that this map generically spoils the invertibility of the sum of linear maps
\mathscr{L}^{\varepsilon}(\mathsf{X},\varepsilon^{2}x)+\mathscr{A}^{\varepsilon}(\mathsf{X})
when we know the first summand is invertible. The third term is the
residual source term
, which encompasses a large number of nonlinear and remainder terms. Crucially, its structure allows us to guarantee it is contractive in
x
for
\varepsilon
small. To execute our fixed point strategy for solving (
1.5.20
) we would first establish the invertibility of
\mathscr{L}^{\varepsilon}(\mathsf{X},\varepsilon^{2}x)
for all
x
in some complete metric space and then study the map

(1.5.21)

x\mapsto-[{\mathscr{L}^{\varepsilon}(\mathsf{X},\varepsilon^{2}x)}]^{-1}(\mathscr{A}^{\varepsilon}(\mathsf{X})x+\mathscr{F}^{\varepsilon}(\mathsf{X},\varepsilon x)).

Unfortunately,
\mathscr{A}^{\varepsilon}(\mathsf{X})
remains adversarial in this role as well, as it prevents this map from being contractive. We thus arrive at the conclusion that the obvious choice of auxiliary equations (
1.5.19
) does not work due to the obstructions caused by the adversarial linear operator.

The decoupling approach above is an obvious choice only insofar as it presents minimal technical complexity. If we are to salvage our construction, we must employ a more intricate approach that embraces a coupling of
X
and
x
in the ODEs for the sake of weakening the adversarial linear operator. To understand this approach schematically, we introduce the notion of a
coupling operator

\mathscr{C}^{\varepsilon}(\cdot,\cdot)
as well as the
coupling response operator

F[\mathscr{C}^{\varepsilon}](\cdot,\cdot)
, which together encode how the unknowns
X
and
x
couple in the systems of PDEs and ODEs:

(1.5.22)

\mathscr{N}^{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{PDE}}(X,x)+\mathscr{C}^{\varepsilon}(X,x)=0\text{ and }\mathscr{N}_{\mathrm{ODE}}(X)+F[\mathscr{C}^{\varepsilon}](X,\varepsilon^{2}x)=0.

These operators can thought of as an abstract expression of the sorting hierarchy resulting from plugging the ansatz (
1.5.18
) into (
1.4.8
). The ansatz (
1.5.18
) is chosen to make the residual contribution to
\boldsymbol{X}
negligible relative to that of the ODE unknown, and this scale difference is inherited by
\mathscr{C}^{\varepsilon}
and
F[\mathscr{C}^{\varepsilon}]
, as can be seen through size comparisons: if
|\mathscr{C}^{\varepsilon}(X,x)|\lesssim 1
then
|F[\mathscr{C}^{\varepsilon}](X,\varepsilon^{2}x)|\lesssim\varepsilon^{2}
. The upshot is that we have the freedom to select
\mathscr{C}^{\varepsilon}
to influence the PDEs at unit scale, paying only the price of a small perturbation to the ODEs.

The above observations suggest a mechanism to pacify the adversarial operator: we choose
\mathscr{C}^{\varepsilon}(X,x)\approx-\mathscr{A}^{\varepsilon}(X)x
to cancel the harmful contribution in (
1.5.21
). We cannot take equality here due to the constraint that
\mathscr{C}^{\varepsilon}(X,x)
must be smooth and independent of the vertical coordinate. Nevertheless, we identify a choice that, in a sense, converges to the adversarial operator in the limit of vanishing
\varepsilon
(see the latter half of Section
4.3
for the precise estimates on this convergence).

At first glance, it might seem that introducing the coupling response term to the ODE in (
1.5.22
) is a disastrous choice, as it breaks the autonomous structure, which plays a key role in the construction of the heteroclinic orbits. However, the same structure in
\mathscr{N}_{\mathrm{ODE}}
that we exploit to show the existence of heteroclinic orbits offers a resilient defense to this threat: the configuration of its equilibria renders the system stable under small nonautonomous perturbations. In Section
2.2
we isolate this stability mechanism and prove a general result showing the persistence of heteroclinic orbits under small and nonautonomous perturbations, akin to an implicit function theorem for such orbits. This grants us the existence of a perturbation operator
\tau^{\varepsilon}(\cdot)
, which can be thought of as the implicit function for solutions near
\mathsf{X}
, such that for all
x
in an appropriate space

(1.5.23)

\mathscr{N}_{\mathrm{ODE}}(\mathsf{X}+\tau^{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon^{2}x))+F[\mathscr{C}^{\varepsilon}](\mathsf{X}+\tau^{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon^{2}x),\varepsilon^{2}x)=0.

Parameterizing solutions to the ODE in (
1.5.22
) as in (
1.5.23
) provides the principal tool to defeat the adversarial linear operator. Indeed, we solve for the ODE unknown in terms of
\mathsf{X}
and
x
via
X=\mathsf{X}+\tau^{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon^{2}x)
, check that
\mathscr{L}^{\varepsilon}(\mathsf{X}+\tau^{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon^{2}x),\varepsilon^{2}x)
is invertible for all
x
in an appropriate complete metric space, and reformulate the PDE in (
1.5.22
) as the fixed point problem

(1.5.24)

x=-[{\mathscr{L}^{\varepsilon}(\mathsf{X}+\tau^{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon^{2}x),\varepsilon^{2}x)}]^{-1}(\mathscr{A}^{\varepsilon}(\mathsf{X}+\tau^{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon^{2}x))x+\mathscr{C}^{\varepsilon}(\mathsf{X}+\tau^{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon^{2}x),x)+\mathscr{F}^{\varepsilon}(\mathsf{X}+\tau^{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon^{2}x),\varepsilon x)).

In contrast with what we saw for (
1.5.21
), the operator on the right hand side of (
1.5.24
) is contractive for small enough
\varepsilon>0
, precisely because we can take
\mathscr{C}^{\varepsilon}
to approximate
-\mathscr{A}^{\varepsilon}
. The contraction mapping theorem then applies, and we obtain the existence of a residual
x
that solves (
1.5.24
), which in turn defines the ODE solution
X=\mathsf{X}+\tau^{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon^{2}x)
. Together these identities imply that
\boldsymbol{X}
given by (
1.5.18
) solves the free boundary Navier-Stokes system (
1.4.8
). Our results guarantee that
x
and
\tau^{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon^{2}x)
vanish at infinity and that
\varepsilon^{2}x
is small, so the solution
\boldsymbol{X}=\mathscr{P}(X,\varepsilon^{2}x)+\varepsilon^{2}x
is close to the shallow water heteroclinic orbit
(\mathsf{H},\mathsf{U})
and has far field limits of distinct shear flow equilibria. We have therefore found the desired bore wave solutions. The final component of our argument is an a posteriori elliptic regularity promotion to show that the bores are, in fact, smooth.

1.6.
Notation conventions

The naturals are the set
\mathbb{N}=\{{0,1,2,\dots}\}
while
\mathbb{N}^{+}=\mathbb{N}\setminus\{{0}\}
. The positive real numbers are
\mathbb{R}^{+}=(0,\infty)
. For
d\in\mathbb{N}
the bracket
\langle\cdot\rangle:\mathbb{C}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{+}
is the function with action defined by
\langle x\rangle=(1+\char 12\relax{x_{1}}^{2}+\cdots+\char 12\relax{x_{d}}^{2})^{1/2}
.
The characteristic function of a a set
E_{0}\subseteq E_{1}
is denoted by
\mathds{1}_{E_{0}}:E_{1}\to\{{0,1}\}
. The notation
\ell_{0}\lesssim\ell_{1}
will mean that there exists a constant
C\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
, depending only on the parameters mentioned in context, such that
\ell_{0}\leqslant C\ell_{1}
; we add emphasis to the dependence on
C
to one or more particular parameters
p_{0},\dots,p_{1}
by writing
\ell_{0}\lesssim_{p_{0},\dots,p_{1}}\ell_{1}
. If
\{{X_{r}}\}_{r=1}^{\ell}
are normed spaces with product space
X
endowed with any product norm
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{X}
, then we write
\lVert x_{1},\dots,x_{\ell}\rVert_{X}=\lVert(x_{1},\dots,x_{\ell})\rVert_{X}
. If
E
is a subset of any topological space, we denote its closure by
\overline{E}
and its interior by
\mathrm{int}E
.

The Fourier transform and its inverse on the space of tempered distributions
\mathscr{S}^{\ast}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{C})
, which are normalized to be unitary on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{C})
, are denoted by
\mathscr{F}
and
\mathscr{F}^{-1}
, respectively. For measurable functions
\chi:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{C}
we write
\chi(D)
to be the Fourier multiplication operator with symbol
\xi\mapsto\chi(\xi)
.

The
\mathbb{R}^{d}
gradient is denoted by
\nabla=(\partial_{1},\dots,\partial_{d})
and the divergence of a vector field
\Psi:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is
\nabla\cdot\Psi=\partial_{1}\Psi_{1}+\cdots\partial_{d}\Psi_{d}
. If
E\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
is any open subset,
k,\ell\in\mathbb{N}
,
t\in[1,\infty]
, we write
W^{k,t}(E;\mathbb{R}^{\ell})
to denote the usual Sobolev space of
\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
-valued functions with weak derivatives of order at most
k
in
L^{t}(E;\mathbb{R}^{\ell})
; these are endowed with a standard choice of norm
\lVert f\rVert_{W^{k,t}(E)}=(\lVert f\rVert_{L^{t}(E)}^{2}+\cdots+\lVert\nabla^{r}f\rVert_{L^{t}(E)}^{2})^{1/2}
. When
t=2
we write
H^{k}(E;\mathbb{R}^{\ell})
in place of
W^{k,t}(E;\mathbb{R}^{\ell})
to emphasize that the space is Hilbert. We also denote
W^{\infty,t}(E;\mathbb{R}^{\ell})=\bigcap_{k=0}^{\infty}W^{k,t}(E;\mathbb{R}^{\ell})
. The set of
k
-times continuously differentiable
\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
-valued
(compactly supported) functions on
E
is
C^{k}(E;\mathbb{R}^{\ell})
(resp.
C^{k}_{c}(E;\mathbb{R}^{\ell})
). Finally, we denote
C^{\infty}(E;\mathbb{R}^{\ell})=\bigcap_{k=0}^{\infty}C^{k}(E;\mathbb{R}^{\ell})
and
C^{\infty}_{c}(E;\mathbb{R}^{\ell})=\bigcap_{k=0}^{\infty}C^{k}_{c}(E;\mathbb{R}^{\ell})
.

2.
Analysis and perturbations of shallow water heteroclinic orbits

The method by which we produce bore wave solutions to system (
1.4.8
) passes through an analysis of the shallow water equations. In a sense to be made explicit later in Sections
4
and
5
, solutions to the
\varepsilon
-shallow free boundary Navier-Stokes equations (
1.4.8
) are, up to a remainder of order
\varepsilon
, approximated by solutions to a corresponding system of ODEs that emerge in the limit as
\varepsilon\to 0
(see also Corollary
3
). These are what we refer to as the distinguished shallow water equations. In Section
2.1
, via the combination of energy estimates and a topological argument, we prove the existence of heteroclinic orbit solutions to these shallow water equations.

As was discussed in Section
1.5
, the overarching strategy is to find a Navier-Stokes solution ‘nearby’ these shallow water solutions; however doing so turns out to be rather subtle and we end up requiring persistence and stability of our shallow water heteroclinic orbits under a general class of nonautonomous perturbations. This perturbation theory is developed in a rather general setting in Section
2.2
.

2.1.
Distinguished shallow water bore solutions

The one-dimensional viscous shallow water equations with gravity, laminar drag, and prescribed relative velocity flux, which in a certain sense are the limit of (
1.4.8
) as
\varepsilon\to 0
, are a coupled system of ODEs in the unknown height and (depth-averaged) velocity
\mathsf{H},\mathsf{U}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
given by

(2.1.1)

(4-\mathsf{U})\mathsf{H}=\mathrm{A},\quad\mathsf{H}(\mathsf{U}-4)\mathsf{U}^{\prime}+\mathrm{a}\mathsf{U}-4\upmu(\mathsf{H}\mathsf{U}^{\prime})^{\prime}+\mathrm{g}\mathsf{H}\mathsf{H}^{\prime}=4\mathrm{a}\mathsf{H}.

The parameters are taken to satisfy:
\upmu,\mathrm{a}>0
,
\mathrm{g}\geqslant 0
, and
\mathrm{A}\in(0,1)
. We are only interested in solutions to (
2.1.1
) that satisfy
\mathsf{H}>0
and
\mathsf{U}<4
. Under these assumptions, we can use the first identity in (
2.1.1
) to solve for
\mathsf{U}
as a function of
\mathsf{H}
, namely
\mathsf{U}=4-\mathrm{A}/\mathsf{H}
. Then this expression can be substituted into the second identity of (
2.1.1
) to derive the closed second order ordinary differential equation for the surface height
\mathsf{H}
given by (
1.5.3
) from the second item of Theorem
1
.

We make two more reformulations of these shallow water equations. Since we require that
\mathsf{H}>0
, it is convenient to swap unknowns to the function
\rho:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
such that
e^{\rho}=\mathsf{H}
. Then we calculate that
\rho
obeys the Liénard equation (
1.5.15
) for
F
and
G
defined in (
1.5.16
). Finally, our methods in this subsection rely on phase space analysis and thus (
1.5.15
) is written as the first order system of ODEs in the unknown vector
X:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
with vector field
\Phi:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
,

(2.1.2)

X^{\prime}=\Phi(X)\text{ with }\quad\Phi(x)=(x_{2},F(x_{1})-x_{2}G(x_{1})).

Note the correspondence between (
1.5.15
) and (
2.1.2
) is given by
X=(\rho,\rho^{\prime})
.

The equilibrium solutions to (
2.1.2
) correspond to the zeros of
F
which, in turn correspond to the states
\mathrm{H}_{\pm}
of (
1.4.4
). We use the notation

(2.1.3)

\uprho_{\pm}=\log\mathrm{H}_{\pm}\text{ so that }\Phi(\uprho_{\pm},0)=0.

The points
\uprho_{\pm}
are functions of
\mathrm{A}
alone and we will find it occasionally useful to write
\uprho_{\pm}(\mathrm{A})
to emphasize this dependence.

The following lemma builds a special region related to the potential energy (
1.5.17
) associated with
F
from (
1.5.16
) and the equilibria (
2.1.3
), which we dub the trapping region.

Lemma 2.1

(Preliminary facts)
.

The following hold.

(1)

F>0
on
(\uprho_{-},\uprho_{+})
with
\pm F^{\prime}(\uprho_{\pm})>0
.

(2)

There exists a unique
\uprho_{\star}>\uprho_{+}
such that
\int_{\uprho_{-}}^{\uprho_{\star}}F(s)\;\mathrm{d}s=0
.

(3)

The function
v:[\uprho_{-},\uprho_{\star}]\to[0,\infty)
given by

(2.1.4)

v(x_{1})=\Big(2\int_{\uprho_{-}}^{x_{1}}F(s)\;\mathrm{d}s\Big)^{1/2}=(-2V(x_{1}))^{1/2}

is well-defined and continuously differentiable and satisfies
v(x_{1})>0
if
\uprho_{-}<x_{1}<\uprho_{\star}
and
v(\uprho_{-})=v(\uprho_{\star})=0
.

(4)

The region
R=\{{x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\;:\;\uprho_{-}\leqslant x_{1}\leqslant\uprho_{\star},\quad|x_{2}|\leqslant v(x_{1})}\}
is nonempty and compact.

(5)

The map
G:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
is strictly decreasing and, if
\mathrm{g}>0
, there exists a unique
\uprho_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
, such that
G(\uprho_{0})=0
.

Proof.

The function
F
from (
1.5.16
) is strictly concave,
F(x_{1})\to-\infty
as
|x_{1}|\to\infty
, and
F(x_{1})=0
if and only if
x_{1}=\uprho_{\pm}
; therefore, the first item must hold. The existence and uniqueness of the second item follows by the intermediate value theorem and the fact that
x_{1}\mapsto\int_{\uprho_{+}}^{x_{1}}F(s)\;\mathrm{d}s
is strictly deceasing to
-\infty
as
x_{1}\to\infty
. The third and fourth items now follow from simple calculations. The fact that
G
is strictly deceasing following from the observation that
G^{\prime}(x_{1})=-(\mathrm{A}e^{-x_{1}}+(\mathrm{g}/\mathrm{A})e^{2x_{1}})/4\upmu<0
. If
\mathrm{g}>0
we compute directly that
\uprho_{0}=\log(\mathrm{A}^{2}/\mathrm{g})/3
. This gives the fifth item.
∎

Figure 5
.
Shown here are a streamline plots of the vector field
\Phi
from (
2.1.2
) superimposed on the trapping region
R
(from the fourth item of Lemma
2.1
) in green, with the boundary of
R
shown in red. Both plots obey
(\upmu,\mathrm{a})=(2,1)
. The plot on the left corresponds to parameter values
(\mathrm{g},\mathrm{A})=(0.125,0.770)\in\mathfrak{C}_{1}
whereas the plot on the right has
(\mathrm{g},\mathrm{A})=(25.0,0.855)\in\mathfrak{C}_{-1}
. The sets
\mathfrak{C}_{\pm 1}
are defined in (
2.1.5
). The curves in pink are the global unstable/stable manifolds for the left equilibrium intersected with
R
; we construct these trajectories in the second and fourth items of Proposition
2.4
.

We note that like the points
\uprho_{\pm}
, the point
\uprho_{\star}
is a function of
\mathrm{A}
alone and so we will also write
\uprho_{\star}(\mathrm{A})
, when necessary. The next lemma studies the sign of the function
G
on the interval
[\uprho_{-},\uprho_{\star}]
. Notice that from the fifth item of Lemma
2.1
, we learn that
G
is signed on
[\uprho_{-},\uprho_{\star}]
if and only if
G(\uprho_{\star})>0
or
G(\uprho_{-})<0
. This is what motivates the forthcoming definition (
2.1.5
).

Lemma 2.2

(Conditions for a sign)
.

Let

(2.1.5)

\mathfrak{C}_{1}=\{{(\mathrm{g},\mathrm{A})\in[0,\infty)\times(0,1)\;:\;G(\uprho_{\star}(\mathrm{A}))>0}\}\text{ and }\mathfrak{C}_{-1}=\{{(\mathrm{g},\mathrm{A})\in[0,\infty)\times(0,1)\;:\;G(\uprho_{-}(\mathrm{A}))<0}\}.

The following hold for
(\mathrm{g},\mathrm{A})\in[0,\infty)\times(0,1)
.

(1)

We have
(\mathrm{g},\mathrm{A})\in\mathfrak{C}_{1}
if and only if
\mathrm{g}<\mathrm{A}^{2}e^{-3\uprho_{\star}(\mathrm{A})}
and
(\mathrm{g},\mathrm{A})\in\mathfrak{C}_{-1}
of and only if
\mathrm{g}>\mathrm{A}^{2}e^{-3\uprho_{-}(\mathrm{A})}
. In particular, for
\iota\in\{{-1,1}\}
we have that

(2.1.6)

\{{(\widetilde{g},\mathrm{A})\;:\;\widetilde{g}\in[0,\infty)}\}\cap\mathfrak{C}_{\iota}\neq\varnothing.

(2)

For
\iota\in\{{-1,1}\}
, we have the inclusion
\mathfrak{C}_{\iota}\subset\{{(\widetilde{g},\widetilde{A})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\;:\;\iota(\widetilde{g}-8)<0}\}
.

(3)

If
\mathrm{g}<8
, there exists
\widetilde{\mathrm{A}}\in(0,1)
such that
(\mathrm{g},\widetilde{\mathrm{A}})\in\mathfrak{C}_{1}
. If
\mathrm{g}>8
, there exists
\widetilde{\mathrm{A}}\in(0,1)
such that
(\mathrm{g},\widetilde{\mathrm{A}})\in\mathfrak{C}_{-1}
. If
\mathrm{g}=8
, then no matter the choice of
\widetilde{\mathrm{A}}\in(0,1)
, we have
(\mathrm{g},\widetilde{\mathrm{A}})\not\in(\mathfrak{C}_{-1}\cup\mathfrak{C}_{1})
.

Proof.

If
\uprho\in\{{\uprho_{-},\uprho_{+}}\}
then a direct computation from the definition of
G
shows that
\pm G(\uprho)>0
if and only if
0<\pm(\mathrm{A}^{2}e^{-3\uprho}-\mathrm{g})
. The asserted equivalence of the first item now follows. The assertion (
2.1.6
) follows by taking
\mathrm{g}
sufficiently small in the case
\iota=1
and by taking
\mathrm{g}
sufficiently large in the case
\iota=-1
.

For the second item, consider first the case that
\iota=1
. If
\mathrm{g}\geqslant 8
, then
