\uprho_{0}\leqslant\frac{1}{3}\log(\mathrm{A}^{2}/8)<\log(1/2)<\uprho_{\star}
and hence
G(\uprho_{\star})<0
and so
(\mathrm{g},\mathrm{A})\not\in\mathfrak{C}_{1}
. On the other hand, if
\iota=-1
and
(\mathrm{g},\mathrm{A})\in\mathfrak{C}_{-1}
, then

(2.1.7)

\mathrm{g}>\mathrm{A}^{2}e^{-3\uprho_{-}(\mathrm{A})}=8(1+(1-\mathrm{A})^{1/2})^{3}/\mathrm{A}\geqslant 8.

The third item’s positive inclusions follow by taking
\mathrm{A}\in(0,1)
sufficiently close to
1
and using that

(2.1.8)

\lim_{\mathrm{A}\to 1}\mathrm{A}^{2}e^{-3\uprho_{\star}(\mathrm{A})}=\lim_{\mathrm{A}\to 1}\mathrm{A}^{2}e^{-3\uprho_{-}(\mathrm{A})}=8.

The claimed negative inclusion follows from
\mathrm{g}=8
implying that
\uprho_{0}=\log(\widetilde{\mathrm{A}}^{2/3}/2)
(from the fifth item of Lemma
2.1
). Then
\uprho_{+}(\widetilde{\mathrm{A}})=\log\mathrm{H}_{+}(\widetilde{\mathrm{A}})>\log(1/2)\geqslant\uprho_{0}
which, by
\uprho_{\star}>\uprho_{+}
, implies that
G(\uprho_{\star})<0
. Conversely, we have
\uprho_{-}(\widetilde{\mathrm{A}})=\log\mathrm{H}_{-}(\widetilde{\mathrm{A}})<\log(\widetilde{\mathrm{A}}/2)\leqslant\uprho_{0}
which implies that
G(\uprho_{-})>0
.
∎

See Figure
2
for a plot of the regions (
2.1.5
). Next, we study the zeros
(\uprho_{\pm},0)
of the map
\Phi
from (
2.1.2
).

Lemma 2.3

(Linear character of equilibria)
.

Define the maps
\lambda_{\pm}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{C}
via

(2.1.9)

\lambda_{\pm}(x_{1})=(-G(x_{1})\pm\sqrt{(G(x_{1}))^{2}+4F^{\prime}(x_{1})})/2,

where
F
and
G
are as in (
1.5.16
). The following hold.

(1)

We have that
\mathrm{spec}(D\Phi(\uprho_{\pm},0))=\{{\lambda_{-}(\uprho_{\pm}),\lambda_{+}(\uprho_{\pm})}\}\subset\mathbb{C}
.

(2)

If
G(\uprho_{+})>0
, then
(\uprho_{+},0)
is an asymptotically stable equilibrium for
\Phi
forward in time (a sink). On the other hand, if
G(\uprho_{+})<0
, then
(\uprho_{+},0)
is an asymptotically stable equilibrium for
\Phi
backward in time (a source).

(3)

We have
\lambda_{\pm}(\uprho_{-})\in\mathbb{R}
and
\lambda_{-}(\uprho_{-})<0<\lambda_{+}(\uprho_{+})
. In particular,
(\uprho_{-},0)
is a hyperbolic equilibrium for
\Phi
.

(4)

The stable manifold at
(\uprho_{-},0)
is tangent to
(1,\lambda_{-}(\uprho_{-}))
, while the unstable manifold at
(\uprho_{-},0)
is tangent to
(1,\lambda_{+}(\uprho_{-}))
.

(5)

If
G(\uprho_{-})>0
we have the bounds

(2.1.10)

\lambda_{-}(\uprho_{-})<-v^{\prime}(\uprho_{-})\text{ and }\lambda_{+}(\uprho_{-})<v^{\prime}(\uprho_{-}),

while if
G(\uprho_{-})<0
, we have the bounds

(2.1.11)

\lambda_{-}(\uprho_{-})>-v^{\prime}(\uprho_{-})\text{ and }\lambda_{+}(\uprho_{-})>v^{\prime}(\uprho_{-}).

Proof.

The first item is a standard calculation. The second and third items follow from
F^{\prime}(\uprho_{-})>0
and
F^{\prime}(\uprho_{+})<0
(see first item of Lemma
2.1
). The fourth item is now a standard calculation and application of the (un)stable manifold theorem (see, for instance, Section 2.7 in Perko
[
70
]
).

For the fifth item consider first the equality

(2.1.12)

(-G(\uprho_{-})+2\sqrt{F^{\prime}(\uprho_{-})})^{2}=(G(\uprho_{-}))^{2}+4F^{\prime}(\uprho_{-})-4G(\uprho_{-})\sqrt{F^{\prime}(\uprho_{-})}.

If
G(\uprho_{-})>0
, then the final term above is negative and so we deduce that

(2.1.13)

-G(\uprho_{-})+2\sqrt{F^{\prime}(\uprho_{-})}<\sqrt{(G(\uprho_{-}))^{2}+4F^{\prime}(\uprho_{-})}.

Since
v^{\prime}(\uprho_{-})=\sqrt{F^{\prime}(\uprho_{-})}
we deduce from the above that
-v^{\prime}(\uprho_{-})>\lambda_{-}(\uprho_{-})
.

On the other hand, if we suppose that
G(\uprho_{-})<0
, then the last term in (
2.1.12
) is positive and so we find

(2.1.14)

-G(\uprho_{-})+2\sqrt{F^{\prime}(\uprho_{-})}>\sqrt{(G(\uprho_{-}))^{2}+4F^{\prime}(\uprho_{-})}

which implies that
\lambda_{-}(\uprho_{-})>-v^{\prime}(\uprho_{-})
. Similar arguments will verify the right hand inequalities in (
2.1.10
) and (
2.1.11
).
∎

Recall that the region
R
is defined in the fourth item of Lemma
2.1
and the map
\Phi
is defined in (
2.1.2
). The following result says, in particular, that if
G>0
on the region
R
, then this region is invariant forward in time for the flow of
\Phi
whereas if
G<0
on
R
then the region is backwards in time invariant. See Figure
5
for a depiction of the flow of
\Phi
over the region
R
in either of these two cases.

Proposition 2.4

(Trapping region)
.

The following hold.

(1)

Assume
G(\uprho_{\star})>0
. For all initial data
X_{0}\in R
there exists a unique smooth
X:[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
solving the initial value problem
X(0)=X_{0}
and
X^{\prime}(t)=\Phi(X(t))
for all
t\in[0,\infty)
. Additionally,
X(t)\in R
for all
t\in[0,\infty)
and, if
X(0)\neq(\uprho_{-},0)
, then
X(t)\to(\uprho_{+},0)
as
t\to\infty
. Moreover,
X
obeys the bounds

(2.1.15)

\sqrt{G(\uprho_{\star})}\lVert e_{2}\cdot X\rVert_{L^{2}((0,\infty))}+\lVert e_{2}\cdot X\rVert_{L^{\infty}((0,\infty))}\leqslant 2\max_{[\uprho_{-},\uprho_{\star}]}v.

(2)

Assume
G(\uprho_{\star})>0
. The global stable manifold at
(\uprho_{-},0)
intersects
R
precisely at
(\uprho_{-},0)
, and the global unstable manifold at
(\uprho_{-},0)
intersected with
R
consists of a heteroclinic orbit from
(\uprho_{-},0)
to
(\uprho_{+},0)
.

(3)

Assume
G(\uprho_{-})<0
. For all terminal data
X_{0}\in R
there exists a unique smooth
X:(-\infty,0]\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
solving the terminal value problem
X(0)=X_{0}
and
X^{\prime}(t)=\Phi(X(t))
for all
t\in(-\infty,0]
. Additionally,
X(t)\in R
for all
t\in(-\infty,0]
and, if
X(0)\neq(\uprho_{-},0)
, then
X(t)\to(\uprho_{+},0)
as
t\to-\infty
. Moreover,
X
obeys the bounds

(2.1.16)

\sqrt{-G(\uprho_{-})}\lVert e_{2}\cdot X\rVert_{L^{2}((-\infty,0))}+\lVert e_{2}\cdot X\rVert_{L^{\infty}((-\infty,0))}\leqslant 2\max_{[\uprho_{-},\uprho_{\star}]}v.

(4)

Assume
G(\uprho_{-})<0
. The global unstable manifold at
(\uprho_{-},0)
intersects
R
precisely at
(\uprho_{-},0)
, and the global stable manifold at
(\uprho_{-},0)
intersected with
R
consists of a heteroclinic orbit from
(\uprho_{+},0)
to
(\uprho_{-},0)
.

Proof.

We will only prove the first two items, working forward in time. The third and fourth items follow from similar arguments, working backward in time. Assume, then, that
G(\uprho_{\star})>0
in what follows.

We begin with some simple observations. Let
\tau_{\pm}=(1,\lambda_{\pm}(\uprho_{-}))
for
\lambda_{\pm}(\uprho_{-})
as in Lemma
2.3
. The fourth item of the lemma tells us that
\tau_{\pm}
are tangent to the unstable
(+)
and stable
(-)
manifolds at
(\uprho_{-},0)
while the fifth item of the lemma tells us that the line generated by
\tau_{-}
is contained in a closed cone that intersects
R
precisely at
(\uprho_{-},0)
and that
r\tau_{+}\in R^{\circ}
for
0<r
small.

The set
R
is compact, so we may choose a time horizon
T_{R}>0
such that for any data
X_{0}\in R
the ODE
X^{\prime}(t)=\Phi(X(t))
,
X(0)=X_{0}
admits a unique solution on the interval
t\in[0,T_{R}]
. Recall from Lemma
2.1
that the function
G
is decreasing and that when
\mathrm{g}>0
it has a unique zero at
\uprho_{0}>\uprho_{\star}
. In light of this strict separation and the compactness of
R
, we may assume without loss of generality that
T_{R}>0
is chosen so that

(2.1.17)

G(e_{1}\cdot X(t))\geqslant G(\uprho_{\star})/2\text{ for all }t\in[0,T_{R}]\text{ and }X_{0}\in R.

Fix arbitrary data
X_{0}\in R
; we will show that its solution trajectory satisfies
X(t)\in R
for
t\in[0,T_{R}]
by way of an energy argument. To this end, define the map
V:[\uprho_{-},\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
via (
1.5.17
) and note that
v
and
V
(where the former is defined in (
2.1.4
)) are related via
v(x_{1})=\sqrt{-2V(x_{1})}
for
x_{1}\in[\uprho_{-},\uprho_{\star}]
. Moreover,
V(\uprho_{\star})=0
and
V(x_{1})>0
for all
x_{1}>\uprho_{\star}
.

Now let
\rho:[0,T_{R}]\to\mathbb{R}
be defined via
\rho=e_{1}\cdot X
. Then, by the definition of
\Phi
(see (
2.1.2
)),
\rho
is a solution to the second order differential equation

(2.1.18)

\rho^{\prime\prime}(t)=F(\rho(t))-G(\rho(t))\rho^{\prime}(t)\text{ for }t\in[0,T_{R}].

We multiply the above equation by
2\rho^{\prime}(t)
and use the definitions of
v
and
V
together with (
2.1.17
) to deduce that

(2.1.19)

[(\rho^{\prime})^{2}]^{\prime}=-2[V(\rho)]^{\prime}-2G(\rho)(\rho^{\prime})^{2},

and hence

(2.1.20)

(\rho^{\prime}(t))^{2}+G(\uprho_{\star})\int_{0}^{t}(\rho^{\prime}(s))^{2}\;\mathrm{d}s\leqslant-2V(\rho(t))+[{(\rho^{\prime}(0))^{2}-v(\rho(0))^{2}}]\text{ for }t\in[0,T_{R}].

As
X_{0}=(\rho(0),\rho^{\prime}(0))\in R
the final term in square brackets above is nonpositive and hence we deduce that

(2.1.21)

(\rho^{\prime}(t))^{2}\leqslant-2V(\rho(t))\text{ for all }t\in[0,T_{R}].

Since
-V(x_{1})<0
for
x_{1}>\uprho_{\star}
we deduce from this that
\rho(t)\leqslant\uprho_{\star}
for all
t\in[0,T_{R}]
. On the other hand, if there exists an
s\in[0,T_{R}]
for which
\rho(s)=\uprho_{-}
then
V(\uprho_{-})=0
and (
2.1.21
) implies that
\rho^{\prime}(s)=0
as well and thus
X(s)=(\uprho_{-},0)
is the left equilibrium point, which is only possible if
X
is constant. If there were
t\in(0,T_{R}]
for which
\rho(t)<\uprho_{-}
, then the intermediate value theorem implies the existence of
s\in(0,t)
for which
\rho(s)=\uprho_{-}
, but we have just argued that this is inconsistent with
\rho(t)<\uprho_{-}
. Upon combining these observations, we deduce that

(2.1.22)

\uprho_{-}\leqslant\rho(t)\leqslant\uprho_{\star}\text{ for all }t\in[0,T_{R}].

With this in hand, we may return to (
2.1.21
) and recall that
v(x)^{2}=2V(x)
to conclude that

(2.1.23)

X(t)\in(\rho(t),\rho^{\prime}(t))\in R\text{ for all }t\in[0,T_{R}].

Since the time horizon
T_{R}>0
is valid for all initial data in
R
, we may now iterate this argument to deduce that the solution exists and remains in
R
for
[0,kT_{R}]
for arbitrary
k\in\mathbb{N}
. The estimate (
2.1.15
) is now immediate from (
2.1.20
), which continues to hold for all
t\in[0,\infty)
.

Since
\rho
is smooth, the bound (
2.1.15
) implies that
\rho^{\prime}(t)\to 0
as
t\to\infty
. Thus, if we write
\omega_{+}(X_{0})
for the forward limit set, then

(2.1.24)

\omega_{+}(X_{0})\subseteq R\cap\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\;:\;y=0\}.

On the other hand, the form of the vector field
\Phi
prevents the set on the right from containing cycles or limit orbits from
(\uprho_{-},0)
to
(\uprho_{+},0)
, so the Poincaré-Bendixson theorem (see, for instance, Section 3.7 in Perko
[
70
]
) requires that either
\omega_{+}(X_{0})=\{(\uprho_{-},0)\}
or
\omega_{+}(X_{0})=\{(\uprho_{+},0)\}
. Since
R
is compact, we deduce from this that
X(t)\to(\uprho_{\pm},0)
as
t\to\infty.
If
X_{0}=(\uprho_{-},0)
, then obviously
\omega_{+}(X_{0})=(\uprho_{-},0)
. Assume, then, that
X_{0}\neq(\uprho_{-},0)
and
X(t)\to(\uprho_{-},0)
as
t\to\infty
. The stable manifold theorem shows that the trajectory must be tangent to
\tau_{-}
at
(\uprho_{-},0)
, but we saw above that
\tau_{-}
is contained in a cone outside
R
, so this is impossible. Hence, if
X_{0}\neq(\uprho_{-},0)
then
X(t)\to(\uprho_{+},0)
as
t\to\infty
. This completes the proof of the first item.

We now turn to the proof of the second item. The convergence result from the first item shows that the stable manifold for
(\uprho_{-},0)
intersects
R
precisely at
(\uprho_{-},0)
. On the other hand, the same convergence result and the initial observation about
\tau_{+}
shows that the intersection of
R
with the unstable manifold to
(\uprho_{-},0)
consists of a heteroclinic orbit from
(\uprho_{-},0)
to
(\uprho_{+},0)
.
∎

We now say more about the heteroclinic orbits produced by the previous result.

Theorem 2.5

(Distinguished heteroclinic orbits)
.

The following hold.

(1)

If
G(\uprho_{\star})>0
, there exists a smooth
\rho:\mathbb{R}\to[\uprho_{-},\uprho_{\star}]
solving the ODE (
1.5.15
) and satisfying
\lim_{t\to\pm\infty}\rho(t)=\uprho_{\pm}
. Moreover, there exist constants
\alpha,C_{m}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
for
m\in\mathbb{N}
such that

(2.1.25)

\sup_{t\leqslant 0}e^{\alpha|t|}\char 12\relax{\rho(t)-\uprho_{-}}+\sup_{t\geqslant 0}e^{\alpha|t|}\char 12\relax{\rho(t)-\uprho_{+}}\leqslant C_{0}\text{ and }\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}e^{\alpha|t|}\char 12\relax{\rho^{(m)}(t)}\leqslant C_{m}\text{ for }m\geqslant 1.

(2)

If
G(\uprho_{-})<0
, there exists a smooth
\rho:\mathbb{R}\to[\uprho_{-},\uprho_{\star}]
solving the ODE (
1.5.15
) and satisfying
\lim_{t\to\pm\infty}\rho(t)=\uprho_{\mp}
. Moreover, there exist constants
\alpha,C_{m}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
for
m\in\mathbb{N}
such that

(2.1.26)

\sup_{t\leqslant 0}e^{\alpha|t|}\char 12\relax{\rho(t)-\uprho_{+}}+\sup_{t\geqslant 0}e^{\alpha|t|}\char 12\relax{\rho(t)-\uprho_{-}}\leqslant C_{0}\text{ and }\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}e^{\alpha|t|}\char 12\relax{\rho^{(m)}(t)}\leqslant C_{m}\text{ for }m\geqslant 1.

Proof.

We only prove the first item, as the second follows by mirrored arguments. Suppose then that
G(\uprho_{\star})>0
.

We know from the second item of Proposition
2.4
that the global unstable manifold for the flow of
\Phi
intersected with
R
consists of a heteroclinic orbit from
(\uprho_{-},0)
to
(\uprho_{+},0)
. Pick a point
X_{0}\in\mathrm{int}R\setminus\{{(\uprho_{+},0)}\}
on this orbit. Then the proposition provides a unique global smooth solution
X:\mathbb{R}\to R
to the initial value problem
X^{\prime}(t)=\Phi(X(t))
for
t\in\mathbb{R}
with
X(0)=X_{0}
, and the solution satisfies
X(t)\to(\uprho_{\pm},0)
as
t\to\pm\infty
. Write
\Gamma=\overline{X(\mathbb{R})}=X(\mathbb{R})\cup\{(\uprho_{-},0),(\uprho_{+},0)\}
, and define
\rho:\mathbb{R}\to[\uprho_{-},\uprho_{\star}]
via
\rho=e_{1}\cdot X
.

Let
r>0
be such that
R\subset B(0,r)
and pick
\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
such that
\varphi=1
in
B(0,2r)
. We then define
\Psi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})
via
\Psi=\varphi\Phi
, and let
\psi:\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
denote its flow map, i.e.
\partial_{t}\psi(t,x)=\Psi(\psi(t,x))
and
\psi(0,x)=x
for
t\in\mathbb{R}
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Standard ODE theory guarantees that
\psi(t,\cdot):\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
is a smooth diffeomorphism for
t\in\mathbb{R}
, and that
\psi(t,\psi(s,x))=\psi(t+s,x)
for
s,t\in\mathbb{R}
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Since
\Phi
and
\Psi
agree in
B(0,2r)
, we have that
X(t)=\psi(t,X_{0})
. Additionally, since
\Psi
is Lipschitz, we can choose a constant
L>0
such that

(2.1.27)

\left|\psi(t,x)-\psi(t,y)\right|\leqslant e^{\left|t\right|L}\left|x-y\right|\text{ for all }t\in\mathbb{R}\text{ and }x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}.

Next, we define the smooth diffeomorphism
f=\psi(-1,\cdot):\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Theorems 6.9 and 6.17 in Irwin
[
39
]
then provide
\chi>0
and constants
A_{0},A_{1}>0
such that

(2.1.28)

\left|f^{n}(x)-f^{n}(y)\right|\leqslant A_{0}e^{-A_{1}n}\left|x-y\right|\text{ for all }x,y\in\Gamma\cap\overline{B((\uprho_{-},0),\chi)}\text{ and }n\in\mathbb{N},

where
f^{n}
denotes the
n-
fold iteration of
f
. Pick
T>0
large enough such that
X(t)\in\overline{B((\uprho_{-},0),\chi)}
for
t\leqslant-T
. Now let
t\leqslant-T
and write
t=-T-n-\tau
for
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\tau\in[0,1)
. Then for
y=(\uprho_{-},0)
we can combine (
2.1.27
) and (
2.1.28
) to bound

(2.1.29)

\left|X(t)-y\right|=\left|\psi(t,X_{0})-\psi(t,y)\right|=\left|\psi(-n,\psi(-T-\tau,X_{0}))-\psi(-n,\psi(-T-\tau,y))\right|\\
=\left|f^{n}(\psi(-T-\tau,X_{0})-f^{n}(\psi(-T-\tau,y))\right|\leqslant A_{0}e^{-A_{1}n}\left|\psi(-T-\tau,X_{0})-\psi(-T-\tau,y)\right|\\
\leqslant A_{0}e^{-A_{1}n}e^{L(T+\tau)}\left|X_{0}-y\right|=A_{0}e^{A_{1}t}e^{(L-A_{1})(T+\tau)}\left|X_{0}-y\right|\leqslant A_{0}e^{(L-A_{1})(T+1)}e^{A_{1}t}\left|X_{0}-y\right|.

From this we deduce that there exists
M_{0}>0
such that

(2.1.30)

\sup_{t\leqslant 0}e^{-A_{1}t}\left|X(t)-(\uprho_{-},0)\right|=M_{0}<\infty.

On the other hand, we know from Lemma
2.3
that the point
(\uprho_{+},0)
is an asymptotically stable equilibrium point for
\Phi
. Consequently, there exists
M_{1},A_{2}>0
such that

(2.1.31)

\sup_{t\geqslant 0}e^{A_{2}t}\left|X(t)-(\uprho_{+},0)\right|=M_{1}<\infty.

The left hand bound in (
2.1.25
) now follows from (
2.1.30
) and (
2.1.31
) upon setting
\alpha=\min\{A_{1},A_{2}\}>0
.

We now prove the right hand bound in (
2.1.25
). Since

(2.1.32)

X^{\prime}=\Phi(X)=\Phi(X)-\Phi(\uprho_{\pm},0)

and
\Phi
is Lipschitz in
\overline{B(0,r)}
, the bounds (
2.1.30
) and (
2.1.31
) imply that
\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}e^{\alpha\left|t\right|}\char 12\relax{X^{\prime}(t)}<\infty
. In turn, we may differentiate the ODE
X^{\prime}=\Phi(X)

m
times and apply the Faá di Bruno formula to bound

(2.1.33)

\char 12\relax{X^{(m+1)}(t)}\lesssim_{\Phi}\sum_{k=1}^{m}\sum_{n_{1}+\cdots+n_{k}=m}\prod_{j=1}^{k}\char 12\relax{X^{(n_{j})}(t)}.

This and a simple induction argument reveal that
\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}e^{\alpha\left|t\right|}\char 12\relax{X^{(m)}(t)}<\infty
for all
1\leqslant m\in\mathbb{N}
, which then yields the desired result.
∎

Remark 2.6

(Nonuniqueness of heteroclinic orbit solutions)
.

The maps
\rho:\mathbb{R}\to[\uprho_{-},\uprho_{\star}]
produced by Theorem
2.5
are not uniquely determined by the properties: 1)
\rho
is a solution to the Liénard equation (
1.5.15
) and 2)
\rho
has the limits
\uprho_{\pm}
at
\pm\infty
. There is, in fact, a translation invariance and we find that for all
t_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
the function
\rho(\cdot-t_{0})
also satisfies these two properties.

2.2.
Perturbation theory for heteroclinic orbits

In contrast with the analysis of the specific ODEs of Section
2.1
, our aim here is to consider in abstract what happens when we have heteroclinic orbit solutions to a certain autonomous ODE and then make a small nonautonomous perturbation to the equations. We will show that if the original heteroclinic connects a hyperbolic point to an asymptotically stable point, then under a general class of perturbations the nonautonomous equation admits a nearby global solution; the dependence of the family of solutions on the perturbation is also studied. Of course, the dynamical systems literature is ancient and vast and may contain a version of our main result here, Theorem
2.10
; as such, we make no claim that this material is entirely novel. We refer, for instance, to Barreira and Valls
[
7
]
for related results on nonautonomous perturbation theory near hyperbolic equilibria. However, we are unaware of a reference with the precise form of the result we need.

Definition 2.7

(Hypotheses for heteroclinic orbit perturbation theory)
.

We define the following for use in this subsection.

(1)

Let
\Lambda
be a complete bounded metric space with metric
\mathrm{dist}_{\Lambda}
; suppose that
\mathrm{diam}\Lambda\leqslant 2
.

(2)

Let
\Psi:\Lambda\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a continuous function admitting the following decomposition

(2.2.1)

\Psi(\lambda,t,x)=\Psi_{0}(x)+\Psi_{1}(\lambda,t,x)

where
\Psi_{0}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is smooth and for each
\lambda\in\Lambda
we have that
\Psi_{1}(\lambda,\cdot,\cdot):\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is smooth for all
k_{1},k_{2}\in\mathbb{N}
the partial derivative
D_{\mathbb{R}}^{k_{1}}D_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}^{k_{2}}\Psi_{1}:\Lambda\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is continuous. Here we are using
D_{\mathbb{R}}
and
D_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
to indicate differentiation with respect to the
\mathbb{R}
and
\mathbb{R}^{d}
factors in the domain of
\Psi_{1}
.

(3)

We suppose that
\Psi_{0}^{-1}\{{0}\}\supseteq\{{\mathrm{x}_{\mathrm{h}},\mathrm{x}_{\mathrm{a}}}\}
with
\mathrm{x}_{\mathrm{h}}\neq\mathrm{x}_{\mathrm{a}}
and denote

(2.2.2)

M_{\mathrm{h}}=D_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\Psi_{0}(\mathrm{x}_{\mathrm{h}})\text{ and }M_{\mathrm{a}}=D_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\Psi_{0}(\mathrm{x}_{\mathrm{a}}).

We posit further

(a)

The matrix
M_{\mathrm{a}}
is asymptotically stable in the sense that its spectrum has only negative real part.

(b)

The matrix
M_{\mathrm{h}}
is hyperbolic in the sense that there exists a direct sum decomposition of nontrivial
M_{\mathrm{h}}
-invariant subspaces
\mathbb{R}^{d}=E^{\mathrm{s}}\oplus E^{\mathrm{u}}
with
M_{\mathrm{h}}E^{\mathrm{s}}\subseteq E^{\mathrm{s}}
,
M_{\mathrm{h}}E^{\mathrm{u}}\subseteq E^{\mathrm{u}}
such that
M_{\mathrm{h}}\restriction E^{\mathrm{s}}
has only spectrum with negative real part and
M_{\mathrm{h}}\restriction E^{\mathrm{u}}
as only spectrum of positive real part. Associated to this direct sum decomposition, we have the projection operators
\Pi_{\mathrm{s}}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to E^{\mathrm{s}}
and
\Pi_{\mathrm{u}}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to E^{\mathrm{u}}
.

These spectral hypotheses on the matrices
M_{\mathrm{h}}
and
M_{\mathrm{a}}
provide constants
K,\alpha\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
such that for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and all
t\in\mathbb{R}
we have the following semigroup estimates

(2.2.3)

|e^{tM_{\mathrm{a}}}x|\leqslant Ke^{-\alpha t}|x|,\quad|e^{tM_{\mathrm{h}}}\Pi_{\mathrm{s}}x|\leqslant Ke^{-\alpha t}|\Pi_{\mathrm{s}}x|,\text{ and }|e^{tM_{\mathrm{h}}}\Pi_{\mathrm{u}}x|\leqslant Ke^{\alpha t}|\Pi_{\mathrm{u}}x|.

(4)

We give quantitative measures of the functions
\Psi_{0}
and
\Psi_{1}
appearing in the decomposition of
\Psi
(
2.2.1
). For fixed
R\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
large enough so that
\mathrm{x}_{\mathrm{h}},\mathrm{x}_{\mathrm{a}}\in\overline{B(0,R/2)}
, define, for
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\lambda,\widetilde{\lambda}\in\Lambda
(with
\lambda\neq\widetilde{\lambda}
), and
t\in\mathbb{R}

(2.2.4)

\text{\char 23\relax}_{n}=\sup_{x\in\overline{B(0,2R)}}\sum_{k=0}^{n}|D_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}^{k}\Psi_{0}(x)|,\quad\text{\char 107\relax}_{n}(\lambda,t)=\sup_{x\in\overline{B(0,2R)}}\sum_{k_{1}+k_{2}\leqslant n}|D_{\mathbb{R}}^{k_{1}}D_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}^{k_{2}}\Psi_{1}(\lambda,t,x)|,

and

(2.2.5)

\text{\char 19\relax}_{n}(\lambda,\widetilde{\lambda},t)=(\mathrm{dist}_{\Lambda}(\lambda,\widetilde{\lambda}))^{-1}\sup_{x\in\overline{B(0,2R)}}\sum_{k_{1}+k_{2}\leqslant n}\char 12\relax{D_{\mathbb{R}}^{k_{1}}D_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}^{k_{2}}\Psi_{1}(\lambda,t,x)-D_{\mathbb{R}}^{k_{1}}D_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}^{k_{2}}\Psi_{1}(\widetilde{\lambda},t,x)}.

We also define the values

(2.2.6)

\overline{\text{\char 107\relax}}_{n}=\sup_{\lambda\in\Lambda}\lVert\text{\char 107\relax}_{n}(\lambda,\cdot)\rVert_{(L^{\infty}\cap L^{2})(\mathbb{R})},\quad\overline{\text{\char 19\relax}}_{n}=\sup_{\lambda,\widetilde{\lambda}\in\Lambda,\;\lambda\neq\widetilde{\lambda}}\lVert\text{\char 19\relax}_{n}(\lambda,\widetilde{\lambda},\cdot)\rVert_{(L^{\infty}\cap L^{2})(\mathbb{R})}

and suppose that
\overline{\text{\char 107\relax}}_{n},\overline{\text{\char 19\relax}}_{n}<\infty
for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

Our interest lies in the study of smooth solutions
X:I\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
to the family of ODEs

(2.2.7)

X^{\prime}(t)=\Psi(\lambda,t,X(t)),\quad X(T)=X_{\mathrm{i}}

with
T\in I
,
I\subseteq\mathbb{R}
an interval,
X_{\mathrm{i}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and
\Psi
as in the second item of Definition
2.7
. Since
\Psi
is smooth and hence locally Lipschitz, standard ODE theory implies that solutions to (
2.2.7
) are always unique and defined in a maximal open interval containing
T
, which we denote by
\mathcal{J}_{\mathrm{max}}^{\lambda,T}(X_{\mathrm{i}})\subseteq\mathbb{R}
in order to indicate the dependence on the parameter
\lambda\in\Lambda
and the data
X_{\mathrm{i}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. This allows us to uniquely define the flow
\psi_{\lambda}
associated with (
2.2.7
) as the map
\psi_{\lambda}(\cdot,T,X_{\mathrm{i}}):\mathcal{J}_{\mathrm{max}}^{\lambda,T}(X_{\mathrm{i}})\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
via
\psi_{\lambda}(t,T,X_{\mathrm{i}})=X(t)
. We will also use the notation

(2.2.8)

\Psi_{\mathrm{q}}(\lambda,t,x)=\Psi_{0}(\mathrm{x}_{\mathrm{q}}+x)-M_{\mathrm{q}}x+\Psi_{1}(\lambda,t,\mathrm{x}_{\mathrm{q}}+x)

for
\mathrm{q}\in\{{\mathrm{h},\mathrm{a}}\}
,
\lambda\in\Lambda
,
t\in\mathbb{R}
, and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

We are now ready to state our first result, which concerns the behavior of the hyperbolic equilibrium
\mathrm{x}_{\mathrm{h}}
. This is a generalization of the classical unstable manifold theorem for dynamical systems. A somewhat related general theory containing results on nonautonomous stable manifold theory can be found in the work of Barreira and Valls
[
7
]
.

Proposition 2.8

(Unstable manifolds with parameter and nonautonomy)
.

Assume Definition
2.7
. There exist
0<\delta_{1},\theta,\sigma\leqslant 1
, depending on
R
,
\text{\char 23\relax}_{2}
,
K
, and
\alpha
, such that if
\overline{\text{\char 107\relax}}_{1}\leqslant\delta_{1}
then for each
T\leqslant 0
there exists a Lipschitz continuous map

(2.2.9)

\boldsymbol{V}_{T}:\Lambda\times(E^{\mathrm{u}}\cap\overline{B(0,\sigma\theta R)})\to E^{\mathrm{s}}\cap\overline{B(0,\theta R)}

and the following hold for
\lambda\in\Lambda
,
T\in(-\infty,0]
.

(1)

The set of terminal conditions with bounded and square summable backward trajectories for (
2.2.7
), namely

(2.2.10)

\mathcal{I}(\lambda,T)=\{X_{\mathrm{i}}\in\overline{B(\mathrm{x}_{\mathrm{h}},\theta R)}\;:\;|\Pi_{\mathrm{u}}(X_{\mathrm{i}}-\mathrm{x}_{\mathrm{h}})|\leqslant\sigma\theta R,\;(-\infty,T]\subseteq\mathcal{J}_{\mathrm{max}}^{\lambda,T}(X_{\mathrm{i}}),\\
\text{ and }\lVert\psi_{\lambda}(\cdot,T,X_{\mathrm{i}})-\mathrm{x}_{\mathrm{h}}\rVert_{(L^{\infty}\cap L^{2})((-\infty,T))}\leqslant\theta R\},

is nonempty. In fact, we have the equality

(2.2.11)

\mathcal{I}(\lambda,T)=\{{\mathrm{x}_{\mathrm{h}}+x+\boldsymbol{V}_{T}(\lambda,x)\;:\;x\in E^{\mathrm{u}}\cap\overline{B(0,\sigma\theta R)}}\}

(2)

The induced solution map

(2.2.12)

\Lambda\times(E^{\mathrm{u}}\cap\overline{B(0,\sigma\theta R)})\ni(\lambda,x)\mapsto\psi_{\lambda}(\cdot,T,\mathrm{x}_{\mathrm{h}}+x+\boldsymbol{V}_{T}(\lambda,x))-\mathrm{x}_{\mathrm{h}}\in(C^{1}\cap W^{1,\infty}\cap H^{1})((-\infty,T];\mathbb{R}^{d})

is Lipschitz continuous with constant depending only on
R
,
\text{\char 23\relax}_{2}
,
K
,
\alpha
, and
\overline{\text{\char 19\relax}}_{0}
.

(3)

There exists a constant
C\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
, depending only on
R
,
\text{\char 23\relax}_{2}
,
K
, and
\alpha
, such that we have the estimate

(2.2.13)

|\boldsymbol{V}_{T}(\lambda,x)-\boldsymbol{V}_{T}(\widetilde{\lambda},x)|\leqslant C\overline{\text{\char 19\relax}}_{0}\mathrm{dist}_{\Lambda}(\lambda,\widetilde{\lambda})

for all
\lambda,\widetilde{\lambda}\in\Lambda
and
x\in E^{\mathrm{u}}\cap\overline{B(0,\sigma\theta R)}
.

Proof.

We divide the proof into several steps.

Step 1 – Auxiliary linear problem:
We first embark on the study of the auxiliary linear inhomogeneous equation

(2.2.14)

X^{\prime}(t)=M_{\mathrm{h}}X(t)+f(t)\text{ for }t\in(-\infty,T],

where
T\in(-\infty,0]
and
f:(-\infty,T]\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a given bounded and continuous function, seeking bounded and continuously differentiable solutions
X:(-\infty,T]\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Note that standard linear ODE theory implies that any solution to (
2.2.14
) is necessarily global in time (but not necessarily globally bounded). We claim the following equivalence: for such
f
fixed,
X:(-\infty,T]\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a bounded and continuously differentiable solution to (
2.2.14
) if and only if there exists
x_{\mathrm{u}}\in E^{\mathrm{u}}
such that

(2.2.15)

X(t)=e^{(t-T)M_{\mathrm{h}}}x_{\mathrm{u}}-\int_{t}^{T}e^{(t-\tau)M_{\mathrm{h}}}\Pi_{\mathrm{u}}f(\tau)\;\mathrm{d}\tau+\int_{-\infty}^{t}e^{(t-\tau)M_{\mathrm{h}}}\Pi_{\mathrm{s}}f(\tau)\;\mathrm{d}\tau\text{ for }t\leqslant T.

Note that this equivalence determines the terminal data
X(T)
in terms of
x_{\mathrm{u}}
and
f
via

(2.2.16)

X(T)=\Pi_{\mathrm{u}}X(T)+\Pi_{\mathrm{s}}X(T)\text{ with }\Pi_{\mathrm{u}}X(T)=x_{\mathrm{u}}\text{ and }\Pi_{\mathrm{s}}X(T)=\int_{-\infty}^{T}e^{(T-\tau)M_{\mathrm{h}}}\Pi_{\mathrm{s}}f(\tau)\;\mathrm{d}\tau.

One direction of the claim is easy: due to (
2.2.3
) we readily check that the formula (
2.2.15
) defines a bounded and continuously differentiable solution to (
2.2.14
). It remains to establish the converse. The Duhamel formula ensures that for any choice of
r,t\leqslant T
and bounded solution
X
to (
2.2.14
) we have

(2.2.17)

X(t)=e^{(t-r)M_{\mathrm{h}}}X(r)+\int_{r}^{t}e^{(t-\tau)M_{\mathrm{h}}}f(\tau)\;\mathrm{d}\tau.

We apply
\Pi_{\mathrm{s}}
to (
2.2.17
) and send
r\to-\infty
; since
X
is bounded and we have the semigroup bounds (
2.2.3
), the first term on the right hand side of (
2.2.17
) vanishes, and we are left with the formula for the stable part of the solution:

(2.2.18)

\Pi_{\mathrm{s}}X(t)=\int_{-\infty}^{t}e^{(t-\tau)M_{\mathrm{h}}}\Pi_{\mathrm{s}}f(\tau)\;\mathrm{d}\tau.

Similarly, we can take
r=T
in (
2.2.17
) and apply the operator
\Pi_{\mathrm{u}}
to deduce that the unstable part of
X
is given by

(2.2.19)

\Pi_{\mathrm{u}}X(t)=e^{(t-T)M_{\mathrm{h}}}\Pi_{\mathrm{u}}X(T)-\int_{t}^{T}e^{(t-\tau)M_{\mathrm{h}}}\Pi_{\mathrm{u}}f(\tau)\;\mathrm{d}\tau.

We sum (
2.2.18
) and (
2.2.19
) to deduce formula (
2.2.15
), which completes the proof of the converse and of the claimed equivalence.

Step 2 – Solution operator and fixed point formulation:
By using (
2.2.3
) with the previous equivalence and formula (
2.2.15
), we deduce the following estimate on any bounded solution to (
2.2.14
):

(2.2.20)

\sup_{t\leqslant T}\char 12\relax{X(t)}\leqslant K\char 12\relax{\Pi_{\mathrm{u}}X(T)}+(2K/\alpha)\sup_{t\leqslant T}\char 12\relax{f(t)}.

If we suppose further that
f
is square summable, then any bounded solution to (
2.2.14
) is also square summable and from (
2.2.15
) and Young’s inequality we may also deduce the estimate

(2.2.21)

\Big(\int_{-\infty}^{T}|X(t)|^{2}\;\mathrm{d}t\Big)^{1/2}\leqslant\frac{K}{(2\alpha)^{1/2}}\char 12\relax{\Pi_{\mathrm{u}}X(T)}+\frac{2K}{\alpha}\Big(\int_{-\infty}^{T}|f(t)|^{2}\;\mathrm{d}t\Big)^{1/2}.

The estimates (
2.2.20
) and (
2.2.21
) allow us to define the bounded linear operator

(2.2.22)

\boldsymbol{L}_{T}:E^{\mathrm{u}}\times(C^{0}\cap L^{\infty}\cap L^{2})((-\infty,T];\mathbb{R}^{d})\to(C^{0}\cap L^{\infty}\cap L^{2})((-\infty,T];\mathbb{R}^{d})\text{ via }\boldsymbol{L}_{T}(x_{\mathrm{u}},f)=X

where
X
is given by (
2.2.15
).

Next, we consider the differential equation in the left of (
2.2.7
). Suppose that we are given a bounded, square summable, and continuously differentiable function
X:(-\infty,T]\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
X+\mathrm{x}_{\mathrm{h}}
is a solution to these equations for some value of
\lambda\in\Lambda
. Then we also have a bounded solution to the linear inhomogeneous equation (
2.2.14
) with

(2.2.23)

f(t)=\Psi_{\mathrm{h}}(\lambda,t,X(t)),\quad t\in(-\infty,T],

where
\Psi_{\mathrm{h}}
is as in (
2.2.8
). The finiteness of (
2.2.4
) and (
2.2.6
) ensure that the boundedness and square summability of
X
implies the boundedness and square summability of
f
in (
2.2.23
). In fact, we calculate directly that

(2.2.24)

\lVert f\rVert_{L^{\infty}\cap L^{2}}\leqslant\text{\char 23\relax}_{2}\lVert X\rVert^{2}_{L^{\infty}\cap L^{2}}+\overline{\text{\char 107\relax}}_{0}

Hence the previous equivalence (
2.2.15
) forces
X
to obey the fixed point identity

(2.2.25)

X=\boldsymbol{L}_{T}(\Pi_{\mathrm{u}}X(T),\Psi_{\mathrm{h}}(\lambda,\cdot,X)).

Step 3 – Fixed point argument:
With the above considerations in mind, we will now set up a fixed point argument that produces solutions to (
2.2.25
) (and hence, by adding
\mathrm{x}_{\mathrm{h}}
, solutions to (
2.2.7
)). Given
0<\theta,\sigma\leqslant 1
, define the operator

(2.2.26)

\boldsymbol{N}_{T}:\Lambda\times(\overline{B(0,\sigma\theta R)}\cap E^{\mathrm{u}})\times(\overline{B(0,\theta R)}\cap(C^{0}\cap L^{\infty}\cap L^{2})((-\infty,T];\mathbb{R}^{d}))\to(C^{0}\cap L^{\infty}\cap L^{2})((-\infty,T];\mathbb{R}^{d})

via
\boldsymbol{N}_{T}(\lambda,x_{\mathrm{u}},X)=\boldsymbol{L}_{T}(x_{\mathrm{u}},\Psi_{\mathrm{h}}(\lambda,\cdot,X))
.

Due to the bounds on
\boldsymbol{L}_{T}
from (
2.2.20
) and (
2.2.21
), along with (
2.2.24
), we deduce that

(2.2.27)

\lVert\boldsymbol{N}_{T}(\lambda,x_{\mathrm{u}},X)\rVert_{L^{\infty}\cap L^{2}}\leqslant K(1+(2\alpha)^{-1/2})\sigma\theta R+(2K/\alpha)(\text{\char 23\relax}_{2}(\theta R)^{2}+\overline{\text{\char 107\relax}}_{0})

and

(2.2.28)

\lVert\boldsymbol{N}_{T}(\lambda,x_{\mathrm{u}},X)-\boldsymbol{N}_{T}(\lambda,x_{\mathrm{u}},Y)\rVert_{L^{\infty}\cap L^{2}}\leqslant(2K/\alpha)(\text{\char 23\relax}_{2}\theta R+\overline{\text{\char 107\relax}}_{1})\lVert X-Y\rVert_{L^{\infty}\cap L^{2}}.

Therefore, if we take

(2.2.29)

\theta=\min\{{1,\alpha(6R\text{\char 23\relax}_{2}K)^{-1}}\},\quad\sigma=\min\{{1,(3K(1+(2\alpha)^{-1/2}))^{-1}}\},\quad\overline{\text{\char 107\relax}}_{1}\leqslant\alpha\min\{{1,\theta R}\}(6K)^{-1}

then
\boldsymbol{N}_{T}(\lambda,x_{\mathrm{u}},\cdot)
maps
\overline{B(0,\theta R)}
into itself with a Lipschitz constant at most
2/3
. We also check that
\boldsymbol{N}_{T}
is Lipschitz in its first and second arguments as well, as it obeys

(2.2.30)

\lVert\boldsymbol{N}_{T}(\lambda,x_{\mathrm{u}},X)-\boldsymbol{N}_{T}(\widetilde{\lambda},y_{\mathrm{u}},X)\rVert_{L^{\infty}\cap L^{2}}\leqslant K(1+(2\alpha)^{-1/2})|x_{\mathrm{u}}-y_{\mathrm{u}}|+(2K/\alpha)\overline{\text{\char 19\relax}}_{0}\mathrm{dist}_{\Lambda}(\lambda,\widetilde{\lambda})

for all
\lambda,\widetilde{\lambda}\in\Lambda
,
x_{\mathrm{u}},y_{\mathrm{u}}\in E^{\mathrm{u}}\cap\overline{B(0,\sigma\theta R)}
, and
X\in\overline{B(0,\theta R)}\cap(C^{0}\cap L^{\infty}\cap L^{2})
. The Banach contraction mapping principle with parameter (see, for example, Theorem C.7 in Irwin
[
39
]
) thus applies, and we acquire a Lipschitz continuous map

(2.2.31)

\boldsymbol{F}_{T}:\Lambda\times(\overline{B(0,\sigma\theta R)}\cap E^{\mathrm{u}})\to\overline{B(0,\theta R)}\cap(C^{0}\cap L^{\infty}\cap L^{2})((-\infty,T];\mathbb{R}^{d})

with the property that
X=\boldsymbol{F}_{T}(\lambda,x_{\mathrm{u}})
is the unique (in the ball
\overline{B(0,\theta R)}\subset(C^{0}\cap L^{\infty}\cap L^{2})((-\infty,T];\mathbb{R}^{d})
) solution to
X=\boldsymbol{N}_{T}(\lambda,x_{\mathrm{u}},X)
and hence, after adding
\mathrm{x}_{\mathrm{h}}
, is the unique bounded solution to (
2.2.7
) with
\Pi_{\mathrm{u}}(X_{\mathrm{i}}-\mathrm{x}_{\mathrm{h}})=x_{\mathrm{u}}
.

Step 4 – Conclusion:
We may now see that the set (
2.2.10
) is indeed nonempty, since for every choice of
\lambda\in\Lambda
and
x_{\mathrm{u}}\in E^{\mathrm{u}}
we have the inclusion
\mathrm{x}_{\mathrm{h}}+[\boldsymbol{F}_{T}(\lambda,x_{\mathrm{u}})](T)\in\mathcal{I}(\lambda,T)
. On the other hand, for any
X_{\mathrm{i}}\in\mathcal{I}(\lambda,T)
we have, by definition, an associated bounded and square summable function
X=\psi_{\lambda}(\cdot,T,X_{\mathrm{i}})-\mathrm{x}_{\mathrm{h}}
such that
X+\mathrm{x}_{\mathrm{h}}
solves (
2.2.7
). By the previous calculations, the fixed point identity (
2.2.25
) holds, so the uniqueness assertion of the contraction mapping principle implies that
X=\boldsymbol{F}_{T}(\lambda,\Pi_{\mathrm{u}}X(T))
.

We have just argued that

(2.2.32)

\mathcal{I}(\lambda,T)=\{{\mathrm{x}_{\mathrm{h}}+[\boldsymbol{F}_{T}(\lambda,x_{\mathrm{u}})](T)\;:\;x_{\mathrm{u}}\in E^{\mathrm{u}}\cap\overline{B(0,\sigma\theta R)}}\}.

From (
2.2.25
) we also see that
\Pi_{\mathrm{u}}[\boldsymbol{F}_{T}(\lambda,x_{\mathrm{u}})](T)=x_{\mathrm{u}}
. Hence we may define the map (
2.2.9
) via

(2.2.33)

\boldsymbol{V}_{T}(\lambda,x_{\mathrm{u}})=\Pi_{\mathrm{s}}[\boldsymbol{F}_{T}(\lambda,x_{\mathrm{u}})](T)

for
\lambda\in\Lambda
and
x_{\mathrm{u}}\in E^{\mathrm{u}}\cap\overline{B(0,\sigma\theta R)}
, and conclude that the first item holds. The third item is a simple calculation that follows from the Lipschitz estimate (
2.2.30
) and the fact that
\boldsymbol{N}_{T}
is
2/3
-contractive in its final argument. In fact, we may take
C=6K/\alpha
.

It remains to prove the second item. The map in (
2.2.12
) is none other than
\boldsymbol{F}_{T}
, and we therefore know it to be Lipschitz in the target space (
2.2.31
) of one fewer derivative. For any
\lambda
and
x_{\mathrm{u}}
belonging to the domain of
\boldsymbol{F}_{T}
we have established that
\boldsymbol{F}_{T}(\lambda,x_{\mathrm{u}})
solves (
2.2.25
) and is therefore continuously differentiable. We then calculate that its derivative obeys the identity

(2.2.34)

[{\boldsymbol{F}_{T}(\lambda,x_{\mathrm{u}})}]^{\prime}=M_{\mathrm{h}}\boldsymbol{F}_{T}(\lambda,x_{\mathrm{u}})+\Psi_{\mathrm{h}}(\lambda,\cdot,\boldsymbol{F}_{\mathrm{T}}(\lambda,x_{\mathrm{u}})),

so it is then a simple matter to use the above to promote the Lipschitz continuity of the map (
2.2.31
) to the claimed (
2.2.12
).
∎

Our second result studies behavior near the asymptotically stable equilibrium
\mathrm{x}_{\mathrm{a}}
and gives sufficient conditions for the existence of a family of forward in time attracting trajectories for (
2.2.7
). This is a generalization of the classical result on the stability of a sink equilibrium (see, for instance, Theorem 1 in Section 2.9 of Perko
[
70
]
).

Proposition 2.9

(Generalized attractor stability)
.

Assume Definition
2.7
. Let
T\in(-\infty,0]
and suppose that
\mathsf{X}:[T,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a given continuously differentiable solution to the equation
\mathsf{X}^{\prime}(t)=\Psi_{0}(\mathsf{X}(t))
for
t\geqslant T
, satisfying
\lim_{t\to\infty}\mathsf{X}(t)=\mathrm{x}_{\mathrm{a}}
,
\mathsf{X}(T)=\mathsf{X}_{\mathrm{i}}\in\overline{B(0,R)}
, and
\lVert\mathsf{X}-\mathrm{x}_{\mathrm{a}}\rVert_{L^{\infty}\cap L^{2}}\leqslant R/2
.

There exist
0<\delta_{2},\theta,\sigma\leqslant 1
, depending only on
R
,
\text{\char 23\relax}_{2}
,
K
,
\alpha
,
T
,
\Psi_{0}
, and
\mathsf{X}
, such that if
\overline{\text{\char 107\relax}}_{1}\leqslant\delta_{2}
then there is a Lipschitz continuous map

(2.2.35)

\boldsymbol{U}_{T}:\Lambda\times\overline{B(0,\sigma\theta R)}\to(C^{1}\cap W^{1,\infty}\cap H^{1})([T,\infty);\mathbb{R}^{d})

such that the following hold.

(1)

For all
(\lambda,x)
in the domain of (
2.2.35
) we have that
X=\mathsf{X}+\boldsymbol{U}_{T}(\lambda,x)
is a solution to the ODE (
2.2.7
) in the time interval
[T,\infty)
with
X_{\mathrm{i}}=\mathsf{X}_{\mathrm{i}}+x
.

(2)

For all
(\lambda,x)
in the domain of (
2.2.35
) we have
\boldsymbol{U}_{T}(\lambda,x)\in\overline{B(0,\theta R)}\subset(C^{0}\cap L^{\infty}\cap L^{2})([T,\infty);\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

(3)

The Lipschitz constant of
\boldsymbol{U}_{T}
depends only on
R
,
\text{\char 23\relax}_{2}
,
K
,
\alpha
,
\overline{\text{\char 19\relax}}_{0}
,
T
,
\Psi_{0}
, and
\mathsf{X}
.

Proof.

We begin by noting that any bounded and continuously differentiable solution
X:[T,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
to equation (
2.2.7
) satisfies

(2.2.36)

X(t)=e^{(t-T)M_{\mathrm{a}}}X_{\mathrm{i}}+\int_{T}^{t}e^{(t-\tau)M_{\mathrm{a}}}(\Psi(\lambda,\tau,X(\tau))-M_{\mathrm{a}}X(\tau))\;\mathrm{d}\tau\text{ for }t\geqslant T.

A similar identity is satisfied by
\mathsf{X}
if we replace
\Psi
above with
\Psi_{0}
and
X_{\mathrm{i}}
by
\mathsf{X}_{\mathrm{i}}
. By subtracting the latter from the former and writing
X=\mathsf{X}+Y
and
X_{\mathrm{i}}=\mathsf{X}_{\mathrm{i}}+Y_{\mathrm{i}}
, we deduce that
Y:[T,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
obeys the equation

(2.2.37)

Y(t)=e^{(t-T)M_{\mathrm{a}}}Y_{\mathrm{i}}+\boldsymbol{P}(Y)(t)+\boldsymbol{R}_{\lambda}(Y)(t)\text{ for }t\geqslant T,

where the linear map
\boldsymbol{P}
is defined by

(2.2.38)

\boldsymbol{P}(Y)(t)=\int_{T}^{t}e^{(t-\tau)M_{\mathrm{a}}}(D_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\Psi_{0}(\mathsf{X}(\tau))-M_{\mathrm{a}})Y(\tau)\;\mathrm{d}\tau

and the nonlinear map
\boldsymbol{R}_{\lambda}
is given by

(2.2.39)

\boldsymbol{R}_{\lambda}(Y)(t)=\int_{T}^{t}e^{(t-\tau)M_{\mathrm{a}}}[{\Psi_{0}(\mathsf{X}+Y)-\Psi_{0}(\mathsf{X})-D_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\Psi_{0}(\mathsf{X})Y+\Psi_{1}(\lambda,\cdot,\mathsf{X}+Y)}](\tau)\;\mathrm{d}\tau.

We now aim to establish that
\boldsymbol{P}
is a bounded and compact operator on the space
(C^{0}\cap L^{\infty}\cap L^{2})([T,\infty);\mathbb{R}^{d})
. In light of (
2.2.3
), it’s clear that
\boldsymbol{P}
is actually a bounded linear map with values in the space
(C^{1}\cap W^{1,\infty}\cap H^{1})([T,\infty);\mathbb{R}^{d})
, with operator norm depending only on on
K
,
\alpha
, and
\text{\char 23\relax}_{2}
.
The theorems of Rellich–Kondrachov and Arzelà-Ascoli establish the compactness of the embedding

(2.2.40)

(C^{1}\cap W^{1,\infty}\cap H^{1})([T,\widetilde{T}];\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow(C^{0}\cap L^{\infty}\cap L^{2})([T,\widetilde{T}];\mathbb{R}^{d})

for every
\widetilde{T}\geqslant T
. The compactness of
\boldsymbol{P}
now follows from this by way of a standard argument that stitches together this compactness on bounded intervals with the uniform decay of the tails granted by the fact that
\lim_{t\to\infty}(D_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\Psi_{0}(\mathsf{X}(t))-M_{\mathrm{a}})=0
.

With the compactness of
\boldsymbol{P}
in hand, we now know that the operator
I-\boldsymbol{P}\in\mathcal{L}((C^{0}\cap L^{\infty}\cap L^{2})([T,\infty);\mathbb{R}^{d}))
is Fredholm with index zero. If
Z\in\mathrm{ker}(I-\boldsymbol{P})
then a simple application of the Grönwall inequality implies that
Z=0
. Therefore, the operator
I-\boldsymbol{P}
is actually invertible and so we may apply
(I-\boldsymbol{P})^{-1}
to identity (
2.2.37
) to see that the perturbation
Y
obeys the fixed point identity

(2.2.41)

Y=\boldsymbol{Q}Y_{\mathrm{i}}+(I-\boldsymbol{P})^{-1}\circ\boldsymbol{R}_{\lambda}(Y).

where
\boldsymbol{Q}Y_{\mathrm{i}}
denotes
(I-\boldsymbol{P})^{-1}
applied to the function
[T,\infty)\ni t\mapsto e^{(t-T)M_{\mathrm{a}}}Y_{\mathrm{i}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Next, we set up a contraction mapping argument that produces solutions to (
2.2.41
). Given
0<\theta,\sigma<1
we define the map

(2.2.42)

\boldsymbol{N}_{T}:\Lambda\times\overline{B(0,\sigma\theta R)}\times(\overline{B(0,\theta R)}\cap(C^{0}\cap L^{\infty}\cap L^{2})([T,\infty);\mathbb{R}^{d}))\to(C^{0}\cap L^{\infty}\cap L^{2})([T,\infty);\mathbb{R}^{d})

via
\boldsymbol{N}_{T}(\lambda,Y_{\mathrm{i}},Y)=\boldsymbol{Q}Y_{\mathrm{i}}+(I-\boldsymbol{P})^{-1}\circ\boldsymbol{R}_{\lambda}(Y)
. We directly estimate that

(2.2.43)

\lVert\boldsymbol{N}_{T}(\lambda,Y_{\mathrm{i}},Y)\rVert_{L^{\infty}\cap L^{2}}\leqslant\lVert\boldsymbol{Q}\rVert\sigma\theta R+\lVert(I-\boldsymbol{P})^{-1}\rVert(K/\alpha)(\text{\char 23\relax}_{2}(\theta R)^{2}+\overline{\text{\char 107\relax}}_{0}).

Therefore, if we take

(2.2.44)

\theta=\min\{{1,\alpha(3R\text{\char 23\relax}_{2}K\lVert(I-\boldsymbol{P})^{-1}\rVert)^{-1}}\},\quad\sigma=\min\{{1,(3\lVert\boldsymbol{Q}\rVert)^{-1}}\},\quad\overline{\text{\char 107\relax}}_{1}\leqslant\alpha\min\{{1,\theta R}\}(3K\lVert(I-\boldsymbol{P})^{-1}\rVert)^{-1},

then
\boldsymbol{N}_{T}(\lambda,Y_{\mathrm{i}},\cdot)
maps
\overline{B(0,\theta R)}
into itself and

(2.2.45)

\lVert\boldsymbol{N}_{T}(\lambda,Y_{\mathrm{i}},Y)-\boldsymbol{N}_{T}(\lambda,Y_{\mathrm{i}},Z)\rVert_{L^{\infty}\cap L^{2}}\leqslant\lVert(I-\boldsymbol{P})^{-1}\rVert(K/\alpha)(\theta R+\overline{\text{\char 107\relax}}_{1})\lVert Y-Z\rVert_{L^{\infty}\cap L^{2}}\leqslant(2/3)\lVert Y-Z\rVert_{L^{\infty}\cap L^{2}}

The latter estimate shows that
\boldsymbol{N}_{T}
is a contraction in its final argument, but we can also check that
\boldsymbol{N}_{T}
is Lipschitz in its first and second arguments as well:

(2.2.46)

\lVert\boldsymbol{N}_{T}(\lambda,Y_{\mathrm{i}},Y)-\boldsymbol{N}_{T}(\widetilde{\lambda},Z_{\mathrm{i}},Y)\rVert_{L^{\infty}\cap L^{2}}\leqslant\lVert Q\rVert\char 12\relax{Y_{\mathrm{i}}-Z_{\mathrm{i}}}+(K/\alpha)\overline{\text{\char 19\relax}}_{0}\mathrm{dist}_{\Lambda}(\lambda,\widetilde{\lambda}).

The Banach contraction mapping principle with parameter, see e.g. Theorem C.7 in Irwin
[
39
]
, then provides a Lipschitz continuous map

(2.2.47)

\boldsymbol{U}_{T}:\Lambda\times\overline{B(0,\sigma\theta R)}\to(C^{0}\cap L^{\infty}\cap L^{2})([T,\infty);\mathbb{R}^{d})

with the property that
Y=\boldsymbol{U}_{T}(\lambda,Y_{\mathrm{i}})
is the unique (in the ball
\overline{B(0,\theta R)}
) solution to
Y=\boldsymbol{N}_{T}(\lambda,Y_{\mathrm{i}},Y)
. We can undo the sequence of reductions made at the beginning of the proof to deduce that the function
X=\mathsf{X}+Y
solves (
2.2.7
) with
X_{\mathrm{i}}=\mathsf{X}_{\mathrm{i}}+Y_{\mathrm{i}}
.

We have now established the first and second items, and it remains only to show that
\boldsymbol{U}_{T}
is a Lipschitz map into the target space given in (
2.2.35
). This is achieved via a similar strategy to that used at the end of the proof of Proposition
2.8
. Since
\mathsf{X}+\boldsymbol{U}_{T}(\lambda,x)
is a solution to (
2.2.36
) and
\mathsf{X}
is continuously differentiable, we deduce that
\boldsymbol{U}_{T}(\lambda,x)
is continuously differentiable as well. Hence, its derivative obeys the identity

(2.2.48)

[\boldsymbol{U}_{T}(\lambda,x)]^{\prime}(t)=\Psi_{0}((\mathsf{X}+\boldsymbol{U}_{T}(\lambda,x))(t))-\Psi_{0}(\mathsf{X}(t))+\Psi_{1}(\lambda,t,(\mathsf{X}+\boldsymbol{U}_{T}(\lambda,x))(t))

for
t\geqslant T
, and it is then a simple matter to use the above to promote the Lipschitz continuity of the map (
2.2.47
) to the claimed (
2.2.35
).
∎

Our third result combines the analysis of Propositions
2.8
and
2.9
to yield a certain type of stability for heteroclinic orbits.

Theorem 2.10

(Perturbations of heteroclinic orbits)
.

Assume Definition
2.7
. Suppose
\lambda_{0}\in\Lambda
is such that
\Psi_{1}(\lambda_{0},t,x)=0
for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Further suppose that
\mathsf{X}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a given continuously differentiable solution to the equation
\mathsf{X}^{\prime}(t)=\Psi_{0}(\mathsf{X}(t))
for
t\in\mathbb{R}
, satisfying
\lim_{t\to\infty}\mathsf{X}(t)=\mathrm{x}_{\mathrm{a}}
,
\lim_{t\to-\infty}\mathsf{X}(t)=\mathrm{x}_{\mathrm{h}}
, and
\max\{{\lVert\mathsf{X}-\mathrm{x}_{\mathrm{h}}\rVert_{(L^{\infty}\cap L^{2})((-\infty,0))},\lVert\mathsf{X}-\mathrm{x}_{\mathrm{a}}\rVert_{(L^{\infty}\cap L^{2})((0,\infty))}}\}\leqslant R/2
.

There exists
0<\updelta\leqslant 1
, depending only on
R
,
\text{\char 23\relax}_{2}
,
K
,
\alpha
,
\Psi_{0}
, and
\mathsf{X}
, with the property that if
\max\{{\overline{\text{\char 107\relax}}_{1},\overline{\text{\char 19\relax}}_{0}}\}\leqslant\updelta
then there exists a Lipschitz continuous map

(2.2.49)

\boldsymbol{B}:\Lambda\to(C^{1}\cap W^{1,\infty}\cap H^{1})(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R}^{d})

possessing the following properties.

(1)

We have
\boldsymbol{B}(\lambda_{0})=0
, and for all
\lambda\in\Lambda
the function
X=\mathsf{X}+\boldsymbol{B}(\lambda)
is a solution to the ODE
X^{\prime}(t)=\Psi(\lambda,t,X(t))
for
t\in\mathbb{R}
.

(2)

The Lipschitz constant of
\boldsymbol{B}
as in (
2.2.49
) depends only on
R
,
\text{\char 23\relax}_{2}
,
K
,
\alpha
,
\Psi_{0}
, and
\mathsf{X}
.

Proof.

Let
\updelta=\max\{{\overline{\text{\char 107\relax}}_{1},\overline{\text{\char 19\relax}}_{0}}\}
. The hypotheses on
\mathsf{X}
ensure that for each
0<\varepsilon\leqslant R
, there exists a
T_{\varepsilon}\in(-\infty,0]
with the property that

(2.2.50)

\sup_{t\leqslant T_{\varepsilon}}\char 12\relax{\mathsf{X}(t)-\mathrm{x}_{\mathrm{h}}}+\Big(\int_{-\infty}^{T_{\varepsilon}}\char 12\relax{\mathsf{X}(\tau)-\mathrm{x}_{\mathrm{h}}}^{2}\;\mathrm{d}\tau\Big)^{1/2}\leqslant\varepsilon.

Assume that
0<\updelta\leqslant\delta_{1}
, where
\delta_{1}
is the parameter from Proposition
2.8
. The hypotheses of the proposition are thus satisfied, so its third item provides
0<\varepsilon_{1}\leqslant R
and, for every
T\leqslant 0
, a Lipschitz continuous map

(2.2.51)

\boldsymbol{A}_{T}:\Lambda\times(\overline{B(0,2\varepsilon_{1})}\cap E^{\mathrm{u}})\to(C^{1}\cap W^{1,\infty}\cap H^{1})((-\infty,T];\mathbb{R}^{d})

such that for all
\lambda
and
x_{\mathrm{u}}
belonging to the domain of (
2.2.51
) the function
X=\mathrm{x}_{\mathrm{h}}+\boldsymbol{A}_{T}(\lambda,x_{\mathrm{u}})
is a solution to (
2.2.7
) on the interval
(-\infty,T]
with
X(T)\in\mathcal{I}(\lambda,T)
the unique element with
\Pi_{\mathrm{u}}(X(T)-\mathrm{x}_{\mathrm{h}})=x_{\mathrm{u}}
.

The definition (
2.2.50
) of
T_{\varepsilon_{1}}
ensures that
\mathsf{X}(T_{\varepsilon_{1}})\in\mathcal{I}(\lambda,T)
. Let us define
\overline{x}_{\mathrm{u}}=\Pi_{\mathrm{u}}(\mathsf{X}(T_{\varepsilon_{1}})-\mathrm{x}_{\mathrm{h}})
. Since
\Psi_{1}(\lambda_{0},\cdot,\cdot)=0
we have that on
(-\infty,T_{\varepsilon_{1}}]

(2.2.52)

\mathrm{x}_{\mathrm{h}}+\boldsymbol{A}_{T_{\varepsilon_{1}}}(\lambda_{0},\overline{x}_{\mathrm{u}})=\psi_{\lambda_{0}}(\cdot,T_{\varepsilon_{1}},\mathsf{X}(T_{\varepsilon_{1}}))=\mathsf{X}.

We are now in a position to define the map

(2.2.53)

\boldsymbol{B}^{-}:\Lambda\to(C^{1}\cap W^{1,\infty}\cap H^{1})((-\infty,T_{\varepsilon_{1}}];\mathbb{R}^{d})\text{ via }\boldsymbol{B}^{-}(\lambda)=\mathrm{x}_{\mathrm{h}}-\mathsf{X}+\boldsymbol{A}_{T_{\varepsilon_{1}}}(\lambda,\overline{x}_{\mathrm{u}}).

Since
\mathsf{X}-\mathrm{x}_{\mathrm{h}}\in(C^{1}\cap W^{1,\infty}\cap H^{1})((-\infty,T_{\varepsilon_{1}}];\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
\boldsymbol{B}^{-}
is well-defined. Moreover, this map inherits the Lipschitz continuity of
\boldsymbol{A}_{T_{\varepsilon_{1}}}
, and the calculation (
2.2.52
) shows that
\boldsymbol{B}^{-}(\lambda_{0})=0
. Finally, since
X^{-}=\mathsf{X}+\boldsymbol{B}^{-}(\lambda)=\mathrm{x}_{\mathrm{h}}+\boldsymbol{A}_{T_{\varepsilon_{1}}}(\lambda,\overline{x}_{\mathrm{u}})
, we have that
(X^{-})^{\prime}(t)=\Psi(\lambda,t,X^{-}(t))
for
t\leqslant T_{\varepsilon_{1}}
for every
\lambda\in\Lambda
.

The goal now is to use Proposition
2.9
to extend the definition of
\boldsymbol{B}^{-}
to all of
\mathbb{R}
. To achieve this we assume in addition that
\updelta\leqslant\delta_{2}
, the parameter from the proposition. This then grants us
0<\varepsilon_{2}\leqslant R
and a Lipschitz continuous map

(2.2.54)

\boldsymbol{U}_{T_{\varepsilon_{1}}}:\Lambda\times\overline{B(0,\varepsilon_{2})}\to(C^{1}\cap W^{1,\infty}\cap H^{1})([T_{\varepsilon_{1}},\infty);\mathbb{R}^{d})

such the for all
\lambda
,
x
in the domain of (
2.2.54
) we have that
X=\mathsf{X}+\boldsymbol{U}_{T_{\varepsilon_{1}}}(\lambda,x)
is a solution to (
2.2.7
) on
[T_{\varepsilon_{1}},\infty)
with
X_{\mathrm{i}}=\mathsf{X}(T_{\varepsilon_{1}})+x
.

Further decreasing
\updelta
if necessary, the third item of Proposition
2.8
combined with the facts that
\mathrm{diam}\Lambda\leqslant 2
and
\boldsymbol{B}^{-}(\lambda_{0})=0
allows us to arrange for
[{\boldsymbol{B}^{-}(\lambda)}](T_{\varepsilon_{1}})\in\overline{B(0,\varepsilon_{2})}
for all
\lambda\in\Lambda
. In turn, we may define the map

(2.2.55)

\boldsymbol{B}^{+}:\Lambda\to(C^{1}\cap W^{1,\infty}\cap H^{1})([T_{\varepsilon_{1}},\infty);\mathbb{R}^{d})\text{ via }\boldsymbol{B}^{+}(\lambda)=\boldsymbol{U}_{T_{\varepsilon_{1}}}(\lambda,[{\boldsymbol{B}^{-}(\lambda)}](T_{\varepsilon_{1}})).

\boldsymbol{B}^{+}
is a composition of Lipschitz maps and hence Lipschitz as well. For all
\lambda\in\Lambda
, the map
X=\mathsf{X}+\boldsymbol{B}^{+}(\lambda)
solves (
2.2.7
) on
[T_{\varepsilon_{1}},\infty)
with
X_{\mathrm{i}}=\mathsf{X}(T_{\varepsilon_{1}})+\boldsymbol{B}^{-}(\lambda)(T_{\varepsilon_{1}})
. By uniqueness and the identity
\boldsymbol{B}^{-}(\lambda_{0})=0
, we find that
X=\mathsf{X}
when
\lambda=\lambda_{0}
, which in turn implies that that
\boldsymbol{B}^{+}(\lambda_{0})=0
.

To complete the proof and construct the map of (
2.2.49
), we concatenate
\boldsymbol{B}^{-}
and
\boldsymbol{B}^{+}
; indeed, for
\lambda\in\Lambda
we define

(2.2.56)

[{\boldsymbol{B}(\lambda)}](t)=\begin{cases}[{\boldsymbol{B}^{-}(\lambda)}](t)&\text{if }t\leqslant T_{\varepsilon_{1}},\\
[{\boldsymbol{B}^{+}(\lambda)}](t)&\text{if }t>T_{\varepsilon_{1}}.\end{cases}

It is then a simple matter to check that

(2.2.57)

\lim_{t\to T_{\varepsilon_{1}},\;t<T_{\varepsilon_{1}}}[{\boldsymbol{B}(\lambda)}](t)=\mathrm{x}_{\mathrm{h}}-\mathsf{X}(T_{\varepsilon_{1}})+\boldsymbol{A}_{T_{\varepsilon_{1}}}(\lambda,\overline{x}_{u})=\lim_{t\to T_{\varepsilon_{1}},\;t>T_{\varepsilon_{1}}}[{\boldsymbol{B}(\lambda)}](t),

and hence
\boldsymbol{B}(\lambda)
as defined in (
2.2.56
) is continuous on
\mathbb{R}
. By design, we also know that
X=\mathsf{X}+\boldsymbol{B}(\lambda)
solves
X^{\prime}(t)=\Psi(\lambda,t,X(t))
for
t
in each of the intervals
(-\infty,T_{\varepsilon_{1}})
and
(T_{\varepsilon_{1}},\infty)
. Therefore, (
2.2.57
) implies that
\boldsymbol{B}(\lambda)
is continuously differentiable in all of
\mathbb{R}
. Finally, the Lipschitz continuity of
\boldsymbol{B}
as a map between the spaces of (
2.2.49
) is inherited from the Lipschitz continuity of
\boldsymbol{B}^{-}
and
\boldsymbol{B}^{+}
.
∎

Our final result regarding perturbations of heteroclinic orbits studies higher regularity of the solutions to the perturbed problem.

Corollary 2.11

(Improved regularity)
.

For each
\mathbb{N}\ni n\geqslant 2
the map
\boldsymbol{B}
of (
2.2.49
) produced by Theorem
2.10
is actually a Lipschitz continuous between the spaces

(2.2.58)

\boldsymbol{B}:\Lambda\to(C^{n}\cap W^{n,\infty}\cap H^{n})(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R}^{d}).

Moreover, the Lipschitz constant of (
2.2.58
) depends only on
R
,
\text{\char 23\relax}_{n+1}
,
\overline{\text{\char 107\relax}}_{n}
,
\overline{\text{\char 19\relax}}_{n-1}
,
K
,
\alpha
,
\Psi_{0}
, and
\mathsf{X}
.

Proof.

For
\lambda\in\Lambda
we calculate

(2.2.59)

[{\boldsymbol{B}(\lambda)}]^{\prime}(t)=\Psi_{0}((\mathsf{X}+\boldsymbol{B}(\lambda))(t))-\Psi_{0}(\mathsf{X}(t))+\Psi_{1}(\lambda,t,(\mathsf{X}(t)+\boldsymbol{B}(\lambda))(t))\text{ for }t\in\mathbb{R}.

This identity then allows for a standard inductive regularity promotion argument, which establishes the Lipschitz continuity of (
2.2.58
). We omit further details for the sake of brevity.
∎

3.
PDEs in thin domains

In Section
2
we have shown the existence of heteroclinic orbit solutions to the one-dimensional shallow water equations (
2.1.1
). We expect the shallow water solutions to describe our desired Navier-Stokes solutions to leading order. The task for this section is for us to develop tools that can be used for the quantitative connection between PDE theory in two-dimensional thin domains and the one-dimensional shallow water ODE theory. These will then be essential throughout Sections
4
and
5
when we set up and execute a fixed point argument.

3.1.
Thin domain toolbox

The purpose of this subsection is to pay special attention to the constants arising in certain Sobolev-theoretic inequalities and constructions in
\varepsilon
-thin domains for
0<\varepsilon\ll 1
.

We embark with the study of extension operators and right inverses to the divergence.

Lemma 3.1

(Right inverses to the trace and the divergence)
.

Given
\varepsilon,\delta\in(0,1)
the following hold for a constant
C\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
depending only on
\delta
.

(1)

There exists a bounded linear operator
\mathcal{E}:H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{0})\to H^{1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})
such that for all
\phi\in H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{0})
we have

(3.1.1)

\mathrm{Tr}_{\Sigma_{0}}\mathcal{E}\phi=\phi,\quad\sup_{0\leqslant y\leqslant\varepsilon}\char 12\relax{\mathscr{F}[{\mathcal{E}\phi}](\cdot,y)}\leqslant\char 12\relax{\mathscr{F}[\phi]},\text{ and }\lVert\mathcal{E}\phi\rVert_{H^{1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}\leqslant C\lVert\phi\rVert_{H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{0})}.

(2)

Suppose that
h\in W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{R})
satisfies
\lVert h\rVert_{W^{1,\infty}}\leqslant\delta^{-1}
and
\delta\leqslant h\leqslant\delta^{-1}
. There exists a bounded linear operator
\Uppi_{h}:L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})\to H^{1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon};\mathbb{R}^{2})
such that for all
f\in L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})

(3.1.2)

\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{h}}\cdot\Uppi_{h}f=f,\quad\mathrm{Tr}_{\Sigma_{0}}\Uppi_{h}f=0,\text{ and }\lVert\Uppi_{h}f\rVert_{H^{1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}\leqslant C\lVert f\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})},

where
\mathcal{A}_{h}
is the geometry matrix as defined in (
1.3.5
).

Proof.

The extension operator
\mathcal{E}
of the first item can be taken to be the standard Poisson extension: in terms of the horizontal Fourier transform, we define

(3.1.3)

\mathscr{F}[\mathcal{E}\phi](\xi,y)=e^{-y|\xi|}\mathscr{F}[\phi](\xi)\text{ for }(\xi,y)\in\mathbb{R}\times(0,\varepsilon)\text{ and }\phi\in H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{0}).

Then (
3.1.1
) follow from Plancherel’s theorem and elementary computations.

For the second item we first note that thanks to Proposition 2.1 in Leoni and Tice
[
47
]
(see also Lemma 2.2 in Stevenson and Tice
[
80
]
) there exists a bounded linear right inverse to the divergence operator
\widetilde{\Uppi}:L^{2}(\Omega_{1/\delta})\to H^{1}(\Omega_{1/\delta};\mathbb{R}^{2})
satisfying

(3.1.4)

\nabla\cdot\widetilde{\Uppi}f=f\text{ and }\mathrm{Tr}_{\Sigma_{0}}\widetilde{\Uppi}f=0\text{ for all }f\in L^{2}(\Omega_{1/\delta})

and whose operator norm depends only on
\delta
. On the other hand, for the
h
-flattening map

(3.1.5)

F_{h}^{\varepsilon}:\begin{cases}L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon h})\to L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon}),\\
H^{1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon h})\to H^{1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon}),\end{cases}\text{ defined by }F^{\varepsilon}_{h}f=f\circ\mathfrak{F}_{h},

where
\mathfrak{F}_{h}:\Omega_{\varepsilon}\to\Omega_{\varepsilon h}
is as in Section
1.3
, it is straightforward to check that
F^{\varepsilon}_{h}
is invertible and satisfies

(3.1.6)

\sum_{n=0}^{1}(\lVert F^{\varepsilon}_{h}\rVert_{\mathcal{L}(H^{n}(\Omega_{\varepsilon h});H^{n}(\Omega_{\varepsilon}))}+\lVert(F^{\varepsilon}_{h})^{-1}\rVert_{\mathcal{L}(H^{n}(\Omega_{\varepsilon});H^{n}(\Omega_{\varepsilon h}))})\leqslant C

for a constant
C
depending only on
\delta
. We now define
\Uppi_{h}:L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})\to H^{1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon};\mathbb{R}^{2})
via

(3.1.7)

\Uppi_{h}f=F^{\varepsilon}_{h}\circ\rho_{\varepsilon h}\circ\widetilde{\Uppi}\circ\iota_{\varepsilon h}\circ(F^{\varepsilon}_{h})^{-1}f,

where
\iota_{\varepsilon h}:L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon h})\to L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon/\delta})
is the (nonexpansive) trivial extension operator and
\rho_{\varepsilon h}:H^{1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon/\delta})\to H^{1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon h})
is the (nonexpansive) restriction operator. Our construction ensures that the operator norm of
\Uppi_{h}
depends only on
\delta
. In turn, elementary computations yield the first two identities of (
3.1.2
).
∎

We next study traces. This necessitates the introduction of boundary norms adapted to thin domains.

Lemma 3.2

(Adapted boundary norms)
.

There exists
C\in(1,\infty)
such that for all
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
and all
\phi\in H^{1/2}(\mathbb{R})
the norms

(3.1.8)

\lVert\phi\rVert^{(\varepsilon,-)}_{H^{1/2}(\mathbb{R})}=\inf_{\phi_{0}+\phi_{1}=\phi}(\lVert\phi_{0}\rVert_{H^{1/2}(\mathbb{R})}+\varepsilon^{1/2}\lVert\phi_{1}\rVert_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R})})\text{ and }\lVert\phi\rVert^{(\varepsilon,+)}_{H^{1/2}(\mathbb{R})}=\lVert\phi\rVert_{H^{1/2}(\mathbb{R})}+\varepsilon^{-1/2}\lVert\phi\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}

satisfy the following.

(1)

We have

(3.1.9)

C^{-1}\varepsilon^{1/2}\lVert\phi\rVert_{H^{1/2}(\mathbb{R})}\leqslant\lVert\phi\rVert^{(\varepsilon,-)}_{H^{1/2}(\mathbb{R})}\leqslant\lVert\phi\rVert_{H^{1/2}(\mathbb{R})}\leqslant\lVert\phi\rVert^{(\varepsilon,+)}_{H^{1/2}(\mathbb{R})}\leqslant\varepsilon^{-1/2}C\lVert\phi\rVert_{H^{1/2}(\mathbb{R})}.

(2)

We have

(3.1.10)

C^{-1}\lVert\phi\rVert^{(\varepsilon,-)}_{H^{1/2}(\mathbb{R})}\leqslant\Big(\varepsilon\int_{(-1/\varepsilon,1/\varepsilon)}\langle\xi\rangle^{2}\char 12\relax{\mathscr{F}[\phi](\xi)}^{2}\;\mathrm{d}\xi+\int_{\mathbb{R}\setminus(-1/\varepsilon,1/\varepsilon)}\langle\xi\rangle\char 12\relax{\mathscr{F}[\phi](\xi)}^{2}\;\mathrm{d}\xi\Big)^{1/2}\leqslant C\lVert\phi\rVert^{(\varepsilon,-)}_{H^{1/2}(\mathbb{R})}.

(3)

We have

(3.1.11)

C^{-1}\lVert\phi\rVert^{(\varepsilon,+)}_{H^{1/2}(\mathbb{R})}\leqslant\Big(\varepsilon^{-1}\int_{(-1/\varepsilon,1/\varepsilon)}\char 12\relax{\mathscr{F}[\phi](\xi)}^{2}\;\mathrm{d}\xi+\int_{\mathbb{R}\setminus(-1/\varepsilon,1/\varepsilon)}\langle\xi\rangle\char 12\relax{\mathscr{F}[\phi](\xi)}^{2}\;\mathrm{d}\xi\Big)^{1/2}\leqslant C\lVert\phi\rVert^{(\varepsilon,+)}_{H^{1/2}(\mathbb{R})}.

Proof.

The second and third items readily imply the first, and the third item is an elementary consequence of the Plancherel theorem. To prove the second item we split a general
\phi\in H^{1/2}(\mathbb{R})
according to
\phi=\phi_{0}+\phi_{1}
with
\phi_{0}=\mathds{1}_{(-1/\varepsilon,1/\varepsilon)}(D)\phi
and
\phi_{1}=\mathds{1}_{\mathbb{R}\setminus(-1/\varepsilon,1/\varepsilon)}(D)
in order to see that the left hand inequality in (
3.1.10
) holds. To prove the right hand inequality we first note that

(3.1.12)

\varepsilon^{1/2}\lVert\mathds{1}_{(-1/\varepsilon,1/\varepsilon)}(D)\psi\rVert_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R})}\leqslant C\lVert\mathds{1}_{(-1/\varepsilon,1/\varepsilon)}(D)\psi\rVert_{H^{1/2}(\mathbb{R})},\\
\lVert\mathds{1}_{\mathbb{R}\setminus(-1/\varepsilon,1/\varepsilon)}(D)\psi\rVert_{H^{1/2}}\leqslant C\varepsilon^{1/2}\lVert\mathds{1}_{\mathbb{R}\setminus(-1/\varepsilon,1/\varepsilon)}(D)\psi\rVert_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R})}

and then apply (
3.1.12
) to the pieces of a general decomposition of
\phi=\phi_{0}+\phi_{1}
.
∎

The boundary norms of Lemma
3.2
play an important role our next result. We refer to the work of Fernández Bonder, Martínez, and Rossi
[
30
]
for more general results on thin domain trace inequalities.

Lemma 3.3

(Traces)
.

There exists
C\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
such that the following hold for all
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
,
f\in H^{1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})
, and
\phi\in H^{1/2}(\mathbb{R})
.

(1)

We have the bound
\lVert\mathrm{Tr}_{\Sigma_{\varepsilon}}f-\mathrm{Tr}_{\Sigma_{0}}f\rVert_{H^{1/2}(\mathbb{R})}^{(\varepsilon,+)}\leqslant C\lVert f\rVert_{H^{1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}
.

(2)

We have the bound
\lVert\mathrm{Tr}_{\Sigma_{\varepsilon}}f\rVert^{(\varepsilon,-)}_{H^{1/2}(\mathbb{R})}+\lVert\mathrm{Tr}_{\Sigma_{0}}f\rVert^{(\varepsilon,-)}_{H^{1/2}(\mathbb{R})}\leqslant C\lVert f\rVert_{H^{1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}
.

(3)

For
\Sigma\in\{{\Sigma_{0},\Sigma_{\varepsilon}}\}
we have the bound

(3.1.13)

\Big|\int_{\Sigma}\phi f\Big|+\Big|\int_{\Sigma}\langle D\rangle^{1/2}\phi\cdot\langle D\rangle^{-1/2}\partial_{1}f\Big|\leqslant C\lVert\phi\rVert^{(\varepsilon,+)}_{H^{1/2}(\mathbb{R})}\lVert f\rVert_{H^{1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}.

(4)

If we assume additionally that
\mathrm{Tr}_{\Sigma_{0}}f=0
, then

(3.1.14)

\Big|\int_{\Sigma_{\varepsilon}}\phi f\Big|+\Big|\int_{\Sigma_{\varepsilon}}\langle D\rangle^{1/2}\phi\cdot\langle D\rangle^{-1/2}\partial_{1}f\Big|\leqslant C\lVert\phi\rVert^{(\varepsilon,-)}_{H^{1/2}(\mathbb{R})}\lVert f\rVert_{H^{1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}.

Proof.

For the first item it suffices to prove

(3.1.15)

\varepsilon^{-1/2}\lVert\mathrm{Tr}_{\Sigma_{\varepsilon}}f-\mathrm{Tr}_{\Sigma_{0}}f\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}+\lVert\mathrm{Tr}_{\Sigma_{\varepsilon}}f-\mathrm{Tr}_{\Sigma_{0}}f\rVert_{\dot{H}^{1/2}(\mathbb{R})}\leqslant C\lVert\nabla f\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}.

To prove this we will reduce to the case for the domain
\Omega_{1}
via a scaling argument. The validity of (
3.1.15
) for the domain
\Omega_{1}
is a consequence of standard trace theory. Then for
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
and
f\in H^{1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})
the function defined by
f_{\varepsilon}(x,y)=f(\varepsilon x,\varepsilon y)
belongs to
H^{1}(\Omega_{1})
, so we may invoke the special case of (
3.1.15
) in
\Omega_{1}
. We get the claimed estimate in
\Omega_{\varepsilon}
by then noting that

(3.1.16)

\lVert\nabla f_{\varepsilon}\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega_{1})}=\lVert\nabla f\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})},\;\lVert g_{\varepsilon}\rVert_{\dot{H}^{1/2}(\mathbb{R})}=\lVert g\rVert_{\dot{H}^{1/2}(\mathbb{R})},\text{ and }\lVert g_{\varepsilon}\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}=\varepsilon^{-1/2}\lVert g\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})},

where
g_{\varepsilon}=(\mathrm{Tr}_{\Sigma_{1}}-\mathrm{Tr}_{\Sigma_{0}})f_{\varepsilon}
and
g=(\mathrm{Tr}_{\Sigma_{1}}-\mathrm{Tr}_{\Sigma_{0}})f
.

For the second item we only prove the trace estimate onto
\Sigma_{0}
, as the case
\Sigma_{\varepsilon}
is similar. The restrictions to the strip
\Omega_{\varepsilon}
of functions in
C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
is a dense subspace of
H^{1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})
, so we only need to establish the bound for
f\in H^{1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})
belonging to this subspace. For any
y\in(0,\varepsilon)
and
\xi\in\mathbb{R}
we may then apply the fundamental theorem of calculus to the difference
\char 12\relax{\mathscr{F}[f](\xi,y)}^{2}-\char 12\relax{\mathscr{F}(\xi,0)}^{2}
and then use Cauchy-Schwarz to bound

(3.1.17)

\langle\xi\rangle\char 12\relax{\mathscr{F}[f](\xi,0)}^{2}\leqslant\langle\xi\rangle\char 12\relax{\mathscr{F}[f](\xi,y)}^{2}+2\Big(\int_{0}^{\varepsilon}\langle\xi\rangle^{2}\char 12\relax{\mathscr{F}[f](\xi,z)}^{2}\;\mathrm{d}z\Big)^{1/2}\Big(\int_{0}^{\varepsilon}\char 12\relax{\partial_{2}\mathscr{F}[f](\xi,z)}^{2}\;\mathrm{d}z\Big)^{1/2}.

Next we employ the second item of Lemma
3.2
, which permits us to estimate separately the high and low mode norms. For the low mode piece we multiply (
3.1.17
) by
\langle\xi\rangle
, integrate over
(\xi,y)\in(-1/\varepsilon,1/\varepsilon)\times(0,\varepsilon)
, apply the inequalities of Young and Cauchy-Schwarz, use that
\langle\xi\rangle\leqslant C\varepsilon^{-1}
, and invoke Plancherel’s theorem to arrive at the bound

(3.1.18)

\varepsilon^{1/2}\lVert\mathds{1}_{(-1/\varepsilon,1/\varepsilon)}(D)\mathrm{Tr}_{\Sigma_{0}}f\rVert_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R})}\leqslant\lVert f\rVert_{H^{1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}.

On the other hand, to get the high mode estimate, we integrate (
3.1.17
) over
(\xi,y)\in(\mathbb{R}\setminus(-1/\varepsilon,1/\varepsilon))\times(0,\varepsilon)
and use
\langle\xi\rangle^{-1}\lesssim\varepsilon
along with the aforementioned tricks to obtain the bound

(3.1.19)

\lVert\mathds{1}_{\mathbb{R}\setminus(-1/\varepsilon,1/\varepsilon)}(D)\mathrm{Tr}_{\Sigma_{0}}f\rVert_{H^{1/2}(\mathbb{R})}\leqslant\lVert f\rVert_{H^{1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}.

Summing (
3.1.18
) and (
3.1.19
) gives the claimed estimate of the second item.

We now turn our attention to the third item. We employ the equivalence second item of Lemma
3.2
together with Cauchy-Schwarz and Plancherel to see that

(3.1.20)

\Big|\int_{\Sigma}\phi f\Big|+\Big|\int_{\Sigma}\langle D\rangle^{1/2}\phi\cdot\langle D\rangle^{-1/2}\partial_{1}f\Big|\lesssim\int_{(-1/\varepsilon,1/\varepsilon)}[{\varepsilon^{-1/2}\char 12\relax{\mathscr{F}[{\phi}](\xi)}}]\cdot[{\varepsilon^{1/2}\langle\xi\rangle\char 12\relax{\mathscr{F}[{\mathrm{Tr}f}](\mathrm{\xi})}}]\;\mathrm{d}\xi\\
+\int_{\mathbb{R}\setminus(-1/\varepsilon,1/\varepsilon)}[{\langle\xi\rangle^{1/2}\char 12\relax{\mathscr{F}[{\phi}](\xi)}}]\cdot[{\langle\xi\rangle^{1/2}\char 12\relax{\mathscr{F}[{\mathrm{Tr}f}](\mathrm{\xi})}}]\;\mathrm{d}\xi\lesssim\lVert\phi\rVert_{H^{1/2}(\mathbb{R})}^{(\varepsilon,+)}\lVert\mathrm{Tr}f\rVert_{H^{1/2}(\mathbb{R})}^{(\varepsilon,-)}.

The third item now follows by combining this with the trace estimate from the second item. The proof of the fourth item is nearly identical, except that now since
\mathrm{Tr}_{\Sigma_{0}}f=0
we can use the first item to bound
\lVert\mathrm{Tr}_{\Sigma_{\varepsilon}}f\rVert_{H^{1/2}(\mathbb{R})}^{(\varepsilon,+)}
, which permits
\phi
to be estimated in the weaker norm
\lVert\phi\rVert^{(\varepsilon,-)}_{H^{1/2}(\mathbb{R})}
.
∎

We continue by considering Poincaré and Sobolev type inequalities. Similar results in three spatial dimensions have been obtained in Section 2 of Temam and Ziane
[
84
]
. We also refer to the work of Marušić
[
55
]
.

Lemma 3.4

(Poincaré and Sobolev)
.

For each
q\in[2,\infty]
there exists
C_{q}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
such that the following hold for all
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
.

(1)

If
q<\infty
and
f\in H^{1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})
, then

(3.1.21)

\lVert f-\mathrm{Tr}_{\Sigma_{\varepsilon}}f\rVert_{L^{q}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}+\lVert f-\mathrm{Tr}_{\Sigma_{0}}f\rVert_{L^{q}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}+\Big\lVert f-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{\varepsilon}f(\cdot,y)\;\mathrm{d}y\Big\rVert_{L^{q}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}\leqslant\varepsilon^{2/q}C_{q}\lVert\nabla f\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})},

where for
\chi\in\{{0,\varepsilon}\}
the symbol
f-\mathrm{Tr}_{\Sigma_{\chi}}f
denotes the function
\Omega_{\varepsilon}\ni(x,y)\mapsto f(x,y)-(\mathrm{Tr}_{\Sigma_{\chi}}f)(x)\in\mathbb{R}
.

(2)

If
q<\infty
and
f\in H^{1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})
, then

(3.1.22)

\varepsilon^{1/2-1/q}\lVert f\rVert_{L^{q}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}\leqslant C_{q}\lVert f\rVert_{H^{1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}.

(3)

For all
f\in H^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})
we have the estimate

(3.1.23)

\varepsilon^{1/2}\lVert f\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}\leqslant C_{\infty}\lVert f\rVert_{H^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}.

Proof.

To prove the first item, we begin by reducing to the case
\varepsilon=1
. Assume that (
3.1.21
) holds for
\Omega_{1}
and let
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
and
f\in H^{1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})
. Then the function
f_{\varepsilon}(x,y)=f(\varepsilon x,\varepsilon y)
belongs to
H^{1}(\Omega_{1})
and so we may invoke the special case of (
3.1.21
) in
\Omega_{1}
. We get the claimed estimate in
\Omega_{\varepsilon}
by noting that

(3.1.24)

\lVert\nabla f_{\varepsilon}\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega_{1})}=\lVert\nabla f\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}\text{ and }\lVert f_{\varepsilon}-Af_{\varepsilon}\rVert_{L^{q}(\Omega_{1})}=\varepsilon^{-2/q}\lVert f-Af\rVert_{L^{q}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}

where
Af
and
Af_{\varepsilon}
refer to one of the trace or averaging operations in (
3.1.21
). This completes the proof of the reduction, so we now consider the case
\varepsilon=1
.
For any
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}
we let
Q_{\ell}=(\ell-1,\ell+1)\times(0,1)
. Standard Sobolev space arguments yield the bound

(3.1.25)

\lVert f-\mathrm{Tr}_{\Sigma_{1}}f\rVert_{L^{q}(Q_{\ell})}+\lVert f-\mathrm{Tr}_{\Sigma_{0}}f\rVert_{L^{q}(Q_{\ell})}+\Big\lVert f-\int_{0}^{1}f(\cdot,y)\;\mathrm{d}y\Big\rVert_{L^{q}(Q_{\ell})}\leqslant C_{q}\lVert\nabla f\rVert_{L^{2}(Q_{\ell})}

for a constant
C_{q}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
depending only
q
. Raising expression (
3.1.25
) to the
q^{\mathrm{th}}
power, summing over
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}
, taking the
q^{\mathrm{th}}
-root, and using
q/2\geqslant 1
to invoke Minkowski’s inequality then proves (
3.1.21
) in the
\Omega_{1}
case. This completes the proof of the first item.

We turn our attention to the second item. Given
f\in H^{1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})
we decompose
f=f^{0}+f^{1}
, where
f^{0}=f-\mathcal{E}\mathrm{Tr}_{\Sigma_{0}}f
and
f^{1}=\mathcal{E}\mathrm{Tr}_{\Sigma_{0}}f
, with
\mathcal{E}
the extension operator from Lemma
3.1
. Then, thanks to (
3.1.21
), the fact that
\mathrm{Tr}_{\Sigma_{0}}f^{0}=0
, and (
3.1.1
), we initially have

(3.1.26)

\lVert f\rVert_{L^{q}}\leqslant C_{q}\varepsilon^{2/q}(\lVert f\rVert_{H^{1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}+\lVert\mathrm{Tr}_{\Sigma_{0}}f\rVert_{H^{1/2}})+\lVert f_{1}\rVert_{L^{q}}.

On the other hand, the Hausdorff-Young inequality along with (
3.1.1
) gives

(3.1.27)

\lVert f_{1}\rVert_{L^{q}}\leqslant\Big(\int_{0}^{\varepsilon}\Big(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\char 12\relax{\mathscr{F}[f_{1}](\xi,y)}^{q/(q-1)}\;\mathrm{d}\xi\Big)^{q-1}\;\mathrm{d}y\Big)^{1/q}\leqslant\varepsilon^{1/q}\lVert\mathscr{F}[{\mathrm{Tr}_{\Sigma_{0}}f}]\rVert_{L^{q/(q-1)}}.

Hölder’s inequality with the product of
\langle\cdot\rangle^{-1/2}
and
\langle\cdot\rangle^{1/2}\mathrm{Tr}_{\Sigma_{0}}f
allows us to estimate further

(3.1.28)

\lVert\mathscr{F}[{\mathrm{Tr}_{\Sigma_{0}}f}]\rVert_{L^{q/(q-1)}}\leqslant C_{q}\lVert\mathrm{Tr}_{\Sigma_{0}}f\rVert_{H^{1/2}}.

Synthesizing (
3.1.26
), (
3.1.27
), and (
3.1.28
) with the second item of Lemma
3.3
and the first item of Lemma
3.2
gives (
3.1.22
).

We now prove the third item. The strategy is similar to the proof of the second item of Lemma
3.3
. Let
f\in H^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})
be the restriction to
\Omega_{\varepsilon}
of a function in
C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
. By arguing as in (
3.1.17
), we find that for any
y,z\in(0,\varepsilon)
and
\xi\in\mathbb{R}
it holds

(3.1.29)

\langle\xi\rangle^{2}\char 12\relax{\mathscr{F}[f](\xi,y)}^{2}\leqslant\langle\xi\rangle^{2}\char 12\relax{\mathscr{F}[f](\xi,z)}^{2}+2\Big(\int_{0}^{\varepsilon}\langle\xi\rangle^{2}\char 12\relax{\mathscr{F}[f](\xi,s)}^{2}\;\mathrm{d}s\Big)^{1/2}\Big(\int_{0}^{\varepsilon}\langle\xi\rangle^{2}\char 12\relax{\partial_{2}\mathscr{F}[f](\xi,s)}^{2}\;\mathrm{d}s\Big)^{1/2}.

Integrating over
(\xi,z)\in\Omega_{\varepsilon}
, taking the square root, and invoking the Cauchy-Schwarz and Young’s inequalities, we then find that

(3.1.30)

\varepsilon^{1/2}\lVert\mathrm{Tr}_{\Sigma_{y}}f\rVert_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R})}\leqslant C\lVert f\rVert_{H^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}.

Inequality (
3.1.23
) now follows from
H^{1}(\mathbb{R})\hookrightarrow L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
and taking the supremum over
y\in(0,\varepsilon)
.
∎

We now consider the Korn inequality in thin domains. We have the following preliminary result.

Lemma 3.5

(Korn, I)
.

Let
\varepsilon,\delta\in(0,1)
and
h\in W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{R})
satisfy
\lVert h\rVert_{W^{1,\infty}}\leqslant\delta^{-1}
and
\delta\leqslant h\leqslant\delta^{-1}
. There exists a constant
C\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
, depending only on
\delta
, such that for all
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
and
\varphi\in H^{1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon h}\cap Q^{\varepsilon}(x_{0});\mathbb{R}^{2})
we have the estimate

(3.1.31)

\Big(\int_{\Omega_{\varepsilon h}\cap Q^{\varepsilon}(x_{0})}\Big|\nabla\varphi-\frac{1}{|\Omega_{\varepsilon h}\cap Q^{\varepsilon}(x_{0})|}\int_{\Omega_{\varepsilon h}\cap Q^{\varepsilon}(x_{0})}\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{A}\psi\Big|^{2}\Big)^{1/2}\leqslant C\lVert\mathbb{D}\varphi\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon h}\cap Q^{\varepsilon}(x_{0}))}

where
Q^{\varepsilon}(x_{0})=(x_{0}-\varepsilon\delta^{2}/4,x_{0}+\varepsilon\delta^{2}/4)\times(0,\varepsilon/\delta)
,
\mathbb{A}\varphi=\nabla\varphi-\nabla\varphi^{\mathrm{t}}
, and
|\Omega^{\varepsilon}\cap Q^{\varepsilon}(x_{0})|
is the planar Lebesgue measure of the set
\Omega_{\varepsilon h}\cap Q^{\varepsilon}(x_{0})

Proof.

We aim to directly invoke Corollary 3.20 from Lewicka
[
50
]
. To do so we only need to check that the domain
\Omega_{\varepsilon h}\cap Q^{\varepsilon}(x_{0})
is star-shaped with respect to an interior ball and the inverse ratio of this interior ball’s diameter to the diameter of
\Omega_{\varepsilon h}\cap Q^{\varepsilon}(x_{0})
is bounded above by a function of
\delta
alone.

To prove that
\Omega_{\varepsilon h}\cap Q^{\varepsilon}(x_{0})
is star-shaped with respect to a ball, we consider the open sets indexed by
z\in(x_{0}-\varepsilon\delta^{2}/4,x_{0}+\varepsilon\delta^{2}/4)
and defined via

(3.1.32)

C^{\varepsilon}(z)=\{{(x,y)\in Q^{\varepsilon}(x_{0})\;:\;y/\varepsilon<h(z)-\delta^{-1}|x-z|}\}.

The set
C^{\varepsilon}(z)
is convex, as it is the intersection of a cone with a rectangle. The bound
\lVert h\rVert_{W^{1,\infty}}\leqslant\delta^{-1}
then implies that
C^{\varepsilon}(z)\subseteq\Omega_{\varepsilon h}\cap Q^{\varepsilon}(x_{0})
; indeed, if
(x,y)\in C^{\varepsilon}(z)
, then
|h(x)-h(z)|\leqslant\delta^{-1}|x-z|
and so the defining inequality in (
3.1.32
) forces
0<y/\varepsilon<h(x)
and hence
(x,y)\in\Omega_{\varepsilon h}
. In fact, since
(x,y)\in C^{\varepsilon}(x)
for all pairs
(x,y)\in\Omega_{\varepsilon h}\cap Q^{\varepsilon}(x_{0})
, we have the identity

(3.1.33)

\bigcup_{|z-x_{0}|<\varepsilon\delta^{2}/4}C^{\varepsilon}(z)=\Omega_{\varepsilon h}\cap Q^{\varepsilon}(x_{0}).

Thus, if we could find an open ball

(3.1.34)

\varnothing\neq B\subseteq\bigcap_{|z-x_{0}|<\varepsilon\delta^{2}/4}C^{\varepsilon}(z)

then the convexity of each
C^{\varepsilon}(z)
and (
3.1.33
) would imply that
\Omega_{\varepsilon h}\cap Q^{\varepsilon}(x_{0})
is star-shaped with respect to
B
. We will prove this.

We claim that the choice
B=B((x_{0},\varepsilon\delta/4),\varepsilon\delta^{2}/8)
satisfies (
3.1.34
). We have
B\subset(x_{0}-\varepsilon\delta^{2}/4,x_{0}+\varepsilon\delta^{2}/4)\times(0,\varepsilon\delta/2)
, so it suffices to prove that the set on the right is a subset of
C^{\varepsilon}(z)
. Supposing that
|x-x_{0}|<\varepsilon\delta^{2}/4
and
0<y<\varepsilon\delta/2
, we use
h(z)\geqslant\delta
to check that

(3.1.35)

h(z)-\delta^{-1}|x-z|\geqslant\delta-\varepsilon\delta/2\geqslant\delta/2>y/\varepsilon,

and hence
(x,y)\in C^{\varepsilon}(z)
. This completes the proof that
\Omega_{\varepsilon h}\cap Q^{\varepsilon}(x_{0})
is star-shaped with respect to
B
. We can thus conclude that (
3.1.31
) holds with the constant
C
depending on
\mathrm{diam}(\Omega_{\varepsilon h}\cap Q^{\varepsilon}(x_{0}))/\mathrm{diam}B\leqslant 2\varepsilon\delta^{-1}/(\varepsilon\delta^{2}/8)=16\delta^{-3}
.
∎

Next, we present a version of the Korn inequality, closely related to and inspired by the theory developed by Lewicka and Müller
[
51
]
. The key differences between what they prove and what we do are: 1) we are concerned with the unbounded thin domains
\Omega_{\varepsilon h}
; and 2) we are permitting the
H^{1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon h};\mathbb{R}^{2})
norm to not only be controlled by the symmetric gradient, but also a small trace norm. We remark that a version of a thin domain Korn inequality in three spatial dimensions for a flat slab appears in Section 3.1.2 of Bresch and Noble
[
14
]
.

Proposition 3.6

(Korn, II)
.

Let
\varepsilon,\delta\in(0,1)
and
h\in W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{R})
satisfy
\lVert h\rVert_{W^{1,\infty}}\leqslant\delta^{-1}
and
\delta\leqslant h\leqslant\delta^{-1}
. There exists a constant
C\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
, depending only on
\delta
, such that for all
\varphi\in H^{1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon};\mathbb{R}^{2})
satisfying
\mathrm{Tr}_{\Sigma_{0}}\varphi_{2}=0
we have the estimate

(3.1.36)

\lVert\varphi\rVert_{H^{1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}\leqslant C(\varepsilon^{1/2}\lVert\varphi_{1}\rVert_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{0})}+\lVert\mathbb{D}^{\mathcal{A}_{h}}\varphi\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}).

Proof.

We divide the proof into steps.

Step 1 – Reductions:
To begin, we make a pair of reductions. First, we claim that for
\delta
fixed it suffices to prove (
3.1.36
) is valid for all
0<\varepsilon\leqslant\varepsilon_{0}
for some
\varepsilon_{0}\in(0,1)
depending on
\delta
. Indeed, once this is established, the remaining range
\varepsilon\in(\varepsilon_{0},1)
may be recovered via an elementary scaling argument.

Second, we claim that it suffices to prove the existence of a constant
C
, depending only on
\delta
, such that for all
\psi\in H^{1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon h};\mathbb{R}^{2})
satisfying
\mathrm{Tr}_{\Sigma_{0}}\psi_{2}=0
we have the estimate

(3.1.37)

\lVert\nabla\psi\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon h})}\leqslant C(\lVert\psi\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon h})}+\lVert\mathbb{D}\psi\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon h})}).

To prove the claim, let us suppose that (
3.1.37
) holds and let
\varphi\in H^{1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon};\mathbb{R}^{2})
satisfy
\varphi_{2}=0
on
\Sigma_{0}
. We use the flattening map
F_{h}^{\varepsilon}
constructed in (
3.1.5
) from the proof of Lemma
3.1
and let
\psi=(F_{h}^{\varepsilon})^{-1}\varphi\in H^{1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon h};\mathbb{R}^{2})
, which satisfies
\mathrm{Tr}_{\Sigma_{0}}\psi=0
along with the estimates

(3.1.38)

\lVert\varphi\rVert_{H^{1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}\leqslant C\lVert\psi\rVert_{H^{1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon h})},\;\lVert\psi\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon h})}\leqslant C\lVert\varphi\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})},\text{ and }\lVert\mathbb{D}\psi\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon h})}\leqslant C\lVert\mathbb{D}^{\mathcal{A}_{h}}\varphi\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}

for
C
depending only on
\delta
. We can then invoke the assumed reduced estimate (
3.1.37
) for
\psi
to find that

(3.1.39)

\lVert\varphi\rVert_{H^{1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}\lesssim\lVert\psi\rVert_{H^{1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon h})}\lesssim\lVert\psi\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon h})}+\lVert\mathbb{D}\psi\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon h})}\lesssim\lVert\varphi\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}+\lVert\mathbb{D}^{\mathcal{A}_{h}}\varphi\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})},

again for implicit constants depending only on
\delta
. To move from (
3.1.39
) to (
3.1.36
) we take
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
