sufficiently small and employ the estimates from the first item of Lemma
3.4
to bound

(3.1.40)

\lVert\varphi\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}\leqslant\varepsilon^{1/2}\lVert\varphi_{1}\rVert_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{0})}+\lVert\varphi_{1}-\mathrm{Tr}_{\Sigma_{0}}\varphi_{1}\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}+\lVert\varphi_{2}\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}\leqslant\varepsilon^{1/2}\lVert\varphi_{1}\rVert_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{0})}+\varepsilon\lVert\nabla\varphi\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}

and then adsorb. This proves the second claim.

Step 2 – The gradient replacement:
We have now reduced to proving the estimate (
3.1.37
) for sufficiently small
\varepsilon
. The next step is to argue as in the proof of Theorem 5.1 in
[
51
]
: we claim that for each
\psi\in H^{1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon h};\mathbb{R}^{2})
there exists
R\psi\in(W^{1,\infty}\cap H^{1})(\Sigma_{0};\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}_{\mathrm{asym}})
for which

(3.1.41)

\lVert\nabla\psi-R\psi\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon h})}\leqslant C\lVert\mathbb{D}\psi\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon h})}\text{ and }\lVert(R\psi)^{\prime}\rVert_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{0})}\leqslant C\varepsilon^{-3/2}\lVert\mathbb{D}\psi\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon h})},

with the constant
C
depending only on
\delta
.

In broad strokes, the map
R\psi
will be taken to be a mollified version of
\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{A}\psi
(where this operator is introduced in Lemma
3.5
). More precisely, let us fix
\vartheta\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R})
with
\mathrm{supp}\vartheta\subset(-\delta^{2}/4,\delta^{2}/4)
,
\vartheta\geqslant 0
, and
\int_{\mathbb{R}}\vartheta=1
. Then define
\Theta:\mathbb{R}\times\Omega_{\varepsilon h}\to\mathbb{R}
via
\Theta(x,z)=\vartheta((z_{1}-x)/\varepsilon)/(\varepsilon^{2}h(x))
and
R\psi:\Sigma_{0}\to\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}_{\mathrm{asym}}
via

(3.1.42)

R\psi(x)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega_{\varepsilon h}}\Theta(x,z)\mathbb{A}\psi(z)\;\mathrm{d}z.

The kernels
\Theta
satisfy
\Theta(x,z)=0
when
|x-z_{1}|\geqslant\varepsilon\delta^{2}/4
and satisfy the additional conditions

(3.1.43)

\int_{\Omega_{\varepsilon h}}\Theta(x,z)\;\mathrm{d}z=1,\quad\int_{\Omega_{\varepsilon h}}D_{1}\Theta(x,z)\;\mathrm{d}z=0,\quad\char 12\relax{\Theta(x,z)}\leqslant C\varepsilon^{-2},\text{ and }\char 12\relax{D_{1}\Theta(x,z)}\leqslant C\varepsilon^{-3}

with
C
depending only on
\delta
. Now, given any
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
, we employ the notation Lemma
3.5
to write

(3.1.44)

A_{x_{0}}\psi=\frac{1}{|\Omega_{\varepsilon h}\cap Q^{\varepsilon}(x_{0})|}\int_{\Omega_{\varepsilon h}\cap Q^{\varepsilon}(x_{0})}\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{A}\psi.

By using (
3.1.43
), Cauchy-Schwarz, and Lemma
3.5
, we then estimate the difference

(3.1.45)

|R\psi(x_{0})-A_{x_{0}}\psi|\leqslant\int_{\Omega_{\varepsilon h}}\Theta(x_{0},z)\Big|\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{A}\psi(z)-A_{x_{0}}\psi\Big|\;\mathrm{d}z\leqslant C\varepsilon^{-1}\lVert\mathbb{D}\psi\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon h}\cap Q^{\varepsilon}(x_{0}))}.

In a similar fashion, we also have

(3.1.46)

|(R\psi)^{\prime}(x_{0})|\leqslant\int_{\Omega_{\varepsilon h}}|D_{1}\Theta(x_{0},z)|\Big|\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{A}\psi(z)-A_{x_{0}}\psi\Big|\;\mathrm{d}z\leqslant C\varepsilon^{-2}\lVert\mathbb{D}\psi\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon h}\cap Q^{\varepsilon}(x_{0}))}.

From (
3.1.46
) we may also derive the estimate

(3.1.47)

\sup_{|x-x_{0}|<\varepsilon\delta^{2}/4}|(R\psi)^{\prime}(x)|\leqslant C\varepsilon^{-2}\lVert\mathbb{D}\psi\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon h}\cap Q^{2\varepsilon}(x_{0}))},

which is a local Lipschitz estimate for
R\psi
near
x_{0}
; hence, in particular we have that

(3.1.48)

\sup_{|x-x_{0}|<\varepsilon\delta^{2}/4}\char 12\relax{R\psi(x)-R\psi(x_{0})}\leqslant C\varepsilon^{-1}\lVert\mathbb{D}\psi\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon h}\cap Q^{2\varepsilon}(x_{0}))}.

We now let
(x,y)\in\Omega_{\varepsilon h}\cap Q^{\varepsilon}(x_{0})
and use (
3.1.45
) and (
3.1.48
) to write

(3.1.49)

\char 12\relax{\nabla\psi(x,y)-R\psi(x)}\leqslant\char 12\relax{\nabla\psi(x,y)-A_{x_{0}}\psi}+\char 12\relax{A_{x_{0}}\psi-R\psi(x_{0})}+\char 12\relax{R\psi(x_{0})-R\psi(x)}\\
\leqslant C\char 12\relax{\nabla\psi(x,y)-A_{x_{0}}\psi}+C\varepsilon^{-1}\lVert\mathbb{D}\psi\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon h}\cap Q^{2\varepsilon}(x_{0}))}.

Now we take the norm in
L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon h}\cap Q^{\varepsilon}(x_{0}))
of the above expression and once again invoke Lemma
3.5
to obtain the estimate

(3.1.50)

\lVert\nabla\psi-R\psi\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon h}\cap Q^{\varepsilon}(x_{0}))}\leqslant C\lVert\mathbb{D}\psi\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon h}\cap Q^{2\varepsilon}(x_{0}))}.

By squaring (
3.1.50
) and summing over
x_{0}\in\varepsilon\delta^{2}\mathbb{Z}/4
we obtain the left estimate of (
3.1.41
). The right hand estimate in (
3.1.41
) can be obtained from (
3.1.47
) by noting that the latter implies

(3.1.51)

\lVert(R\psi)^{\prime}\rVert_{L^{2}(x_{0}-\varepsilon\delta^{2}/4,x_{0}+\varepsilon\delta^{2}/4)}\leqslant C\varepsilon^{-3/2}\lVert\mathbb{D}\psi\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon h}\cap Q^{2\varepsilon}(x_{0}))};

therefore, upon squaring and summing over the appropriate lattice again, we obtain the desired bound. The proof of the claim is now complete.

Step 3 – Integration by parts tricks:
At this point we have successfully established (
3.1.41
). This reduces proving (
3.1.37
) to obtaining further estimates on the map
R\psi
. Assume now that
\psi\in H^{1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon h};\mathbb{R}^{2})
satisfies
\mathrm{Tr}_{\Sigma_{0}}\psi_{2}=0
. As
R\psi\in\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}_{\mathrm{asym}}
, we know that
R\psi_{11}=R\psi_{22}=0
and
R\psi_{12}=-R\psi_{21}
, so we only need to obtain estimates on
R\psi_{12}
.

By using the expression (
3.1.42
) along with the identity
\mathbb{A}\psi_{12}=\mathbb{D}\psi_{12}-2\partial_{1}\psi_{2}
we split
R\psi_{12}=R^{1}\psi-2R^{2}\psi
for

(3.1.52)

R^{1}\psi(x)=\int_{\Omega_{\varepsilon h}}\Theta(x,z)\mathbb{D}\psi(z)_{12}\;\mathrm{d}z\text{ and }R^{2}\psi(x)=\int_{\Omega_{\varepsilon h}}\Theta(x,z)\partial_{1}\psi_{2}(z)\;\mathrm{d}z.

Thanks to (
3.1.43
) and Cauchy-Schwarz, we have the pointwise estimate

(3.1.53)

|R^{1}\psi(x)|\leqslant C\varepsilon^{-1}\Big(\int_{\Omega_{\varepsilon h}\cap Q^{\varepsilon}(x)}\char 12\relax{\mathbb{D}\psi(z)}^{2}\;\mathrm{d}z\Big)^{1/2},

which, by Tonelli’s theorem, yields the bound

(3.1.54)

\lVert R^{1}\psi\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\leqslant C\varepsilon^{-1/2}\lVert\mathbb{D}\psi\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon h})}.

Next, we turn to the
R^{2}\psi
term. In fact, the elementary inner-product inequality

(3.1.55)

\lVert R\psi_{12}\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}\leqslant\lVert R^{1}\psi\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}+4\char 12\relax{\langle R\psi_{12},R^{2}\psi\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}}

allows us to focus only on the inner-product between
R\psi_{12}
and
R^{2}\psi
. The definition of
\Theta
allows us to compute

(3.1.56)

D_{2,1}\Theta(x,z)=-D_{1}\Theta(x,z)-(h^{\prime}(x)/h(x))\Theta(x,z),

where
D_{2,1}
denotes the derivative with respect to
z_{1}
. From this identity, Fubini’s theorem, and two integrations by parts, we then learn that

(3.1.57)

\langle R\psi_{12},R^{2}\psi\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\Omega_{\varepsilon h}}(R\psi_{12})^{\prime}(x)\Theta(x,z)\psi_{2}(z)\;\mathrm{d}z\;\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{h^{\prime}(x)}{h(x)}\int_{\Omega_{\varepsilon h}}R\psi_{12}(x)\Theta(x,z)\psi_{2}(z)\;\mathrm{d}z\;\mathrm{d}x\\
-\varepsilon\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}h^{\prime}(w)R\psi_{12}(x)\Theta(x,w,\varepsilon h(w))\psi_{2}(w,\varepsilon h(w))\;\mathrm{d}w\;\mathrm{d}x=\mathbf{I}_{1}+\mathbf{I}_{2}+\mathbf{I}_{3}.

We will next estimate
\mathbf{I}_{i}
for
i\in\{{1,2,3}\}
individually.

For
\mathbf{I}_{1}
we use repeated applications of Cauchy-Schwarz, the estimates in (
3.1.43
), the
(R\psi)^{\prime}
estimate of (
3.1.41
), and Fubini’s theorem:

(3.1.58)

\mathbf{I}_{1}\leqslant\frac{C}{\varepsilon}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\char 12\relax{(R\psi_{12})^{\prime}(x)}\Big(\int_{\Omega_{\varepsilon h}\cap Q^{\varepsilon}(x)}\char 12\relax{\psi_{2}(z)}^{2}\;\mathrm{d}z\Big)^{1/2}\;\mathrm{d}x\leqslant C\varepsilon^{-2}\lVert\mathbb{D}\psi\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon h})}\lVert\psi_{2}\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon h})}.

To estimate the second term on the right here, we employ the flattening map from (
3.1.5
) to change into
\Omega_{\varepsilon}
; in light of the estimates (
3.1.6
), the vanishing trace
\mathrm{Tr}_{\Sigma_{0}}F^{\varepsilon}_{h}\psi_{2}=0
, and the first item of Lemma
3.4
, we may bound

(3.1.59)

\lVert\psi_{2}\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon h})}\lesssim\lVert F^{\varepsilon}_{h}\psi_{2}\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}\lesssim\varepsilon\lVert F^{\varepsilon}_{h}\psi\rVert_{H^{1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}\lesssim\varepsilon\lVert\psi\rVert_{H^{1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon h})}.

Combining (
3.1.58
) and (
3.1.59
) then grants the bound

(3.1.60)

\mathbf{I}_{1}\leqslant C\varepsilon^{-1}\lVert\mathbb{D}\psi\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon h})}\lVert\psi\rVert_{H^{1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon h})}.

For
\mathbf{I}_{2}
we use similar strategy in order to get

(3.1.61)

\mathbf{I}_{2}\leqslant\frac{C}{\varepsilon}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\char 12\relax{R\psi_{12}(x)}\Big(\int_{\Omega_{\varepsilon h}\cap Q^{\varepsilon}(x)}\char 12\relax{\psi_{2}(z)}^{2}\;\mathrm{d}z\Big)^{1/2}\;\mathrm{d}x\leqslant C\varepsilon^{-1/2}\lVert R\psi_{12}\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\lVert\psi_{2}\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon h})}.

Applying (
3.1.59
) again then yields

(3.1.62)

\mathbf{I}_{2}\leqslant C\varepsilon^{1/2}\lVert R\psi_{12}\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\lVert\psi\rVert_{H^{1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon h})}.

For
\mathbf{I}_{3}
, we use (
3.1.43
) and Fubini’s theorem followed by the Cauchy-Schwarz and Young’s inequalities to bound

(3.1.63)

\mathbf{I}_{3}\leqslant C\int_{\mathbb{R}}\Big(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{w-\varepsilon\delta^{2}/4}^{w+\varepsilon\delta^{2}/4}\char 12\relax{R\psi_{12}(x)}\;\mathrm{d}x\Big)\char 12\relax{\psi_{2}(w,\varepsilon h(w))}\;\mathrm{d}w\leqslant C\lVert R\psi_{12}\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\lVert\mathrm{Tr}_{\Sigma_{\varepsilon}}F^{\varepsilon}_{h}\psi_{2}\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}.

Again appealing to (
3.1.6
) and using the identity
\mathrm{Tr}_{\Sigma_{0}}F^{\varepsilon}_{h}\psi_{2}=0
with the first item of Lemma
3.3
, we further estimate

(3.1.64)

\mathbf{I}_{3}\leqslant C\varepsilon^{1/2}\lVert R\psi_{12}\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\lVert\psi\rVert_{H^{1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon h})}.

Synthesizing (
3.1.60
), (
3.1.62
), and (
3.1.64
) with (
3.1.54
), (
3.1.55
), and (
3.1.57
) shows that

(3.1.65)

\lVert R\psi_{12}\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\lesssim\varepsilon^{-1}\lVert\mathbb{D}\psi\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon h})}+\varepsilon^{-1}\lVert\mathbb{D}\psi\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon h})}\lVert\psi\rVert_{H^{1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon h})}+\varepsilon^{1/2}\lVert R\psi_{12}\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\lVert\psi\rVert_{H^{1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon h})}.

Absorbing the
R\psi_{12}
term on the left and using the trivial bound
\char 12\relax{\mathbb{D}\psi}\leqslant 2\char 12\relax{\nabla\psi}
, we then deduce from this that

(3.1.66)

\lVert R\psi_{12}\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\lesssim\varepsilon^{-1/2}\lVert\mathbb{D}\psi\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon h})}^{1/2}\lVert\psi\rVert_{H^{1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon h})}^{1/2}+\varepsilon^{1/2}\lVert\psi\rVert_{H^{1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon h})}.

Step 4 – Conclusion:
Finally, we are ready to conclude. For any
\psi\in H^{1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon h};\mathbb{R}^{2})
that satisfies
\mathrm{Tr}_{\Sigma_{0}}\psi_{2}=0
we use (
3.1.41
) and (
3.1.66
) to estimate

(3.1.67)

\lVert\nabla\psi\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon h})}\leqslant\lVert\nabla\psi-R\psi\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon h})}+\lVert R\psi\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon h})}\lesssim\lVert\mathbb{D}\psi\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon h})}+\varepsilon^{1/2}\lVert R\psi_{12}\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\lesssim\lVert\mathbb{D}\psi\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon h})}^{1/2}\lVert\psi\rVert_{H^{1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon h})}^{1/2}\\
+\varepsilon\lVert\psi\rVert_{H^{1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon h})}\lesssim\lVert\mathbb{D}\psi\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon h})}^{1/2}(\lVert\psi\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon h})}+\lVert\nabla\psi\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon h})})^{1/2}+\varepsilon(\lVert\psi\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon h})}+\lVert\nabla\psi\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon h})}).

By taking
\varepsilon>0
sufficiently small (depending on
\delta
) and employing Cauchy’s inequality, we may adsorb the
\nabla\psi
terms onto the left and arrive at the desired bound (
3.1.37
).
∎

3.2.
The Stokes equations with stress boundary conditions

We now turn our attention to a system of linear Stokes equations in an
\varepsilon
-thin strip domain with the Navier slip boundary condition on the bottom and stress boundary conditions on the top. The system for the unknown velocity
u:\Omega_{\varepsilon}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
and pressure
p:\Omega_{\varepsilon}\to\mathbb{R}
is given by

(3.2.1)

\begin{cases}\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{h}}p-\upmu\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{h}}\cdot\mathbb{D}^{\mathcal{A}_{h}}u=\varphi^{1},\quad\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{h}}\cdot u=\varphi^{2}&\text{in }\Omega_{\varepsilon},\\
-(p-\upmu\mathbb{D}^{\mathcal{A}_{h}}u)\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon h}=\psi^{1}&\text{on }\Sigma_{\varepsilon},\\
u_{2}=0,\quad\upmu\partial_{2}^{\mathcal{A}_{h}}u_{1}-\varepsilon\mathrm{a}u_{1}=\psi^{2}&\text{on }\Sigma_{0}.\end{cases}

The given data are the functions
\varphi^{1}:\Omega_{\varepsilon}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
\varphi^{2}:\Omega_{\varepsilon}\to\mathbb{R}
,
\psi^{1}:\Sigma_{\varepsilon}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
, and
\psi^{2}:\Sigma_{0}\to\mathbb{R}
. The equations (
3.2.1
) come with a functional parameter
h:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}^{+}
which determines the matrices
\mathcal{A}_{h}
as in (
1.3.5
) and the vectors
\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon h}
, defined after (
1.1.3
). We think of the parameters
\upmu,\mathrm{a}>0
as fixed and track the dependence of the solution operator on the function
h
, with estimates uniform for
0<\varepsilon\ll 1
.

We begin with a series of definitions of function spaces and norms that we will employ in our analysis of (
3.2.1
). First, we discuss the role of the functional parameter
h
appearing in system (
3.2.1
); its container function space is

(3.2.2)

\mathbf{Z}=\{{h\in W^{2,3}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R})\;:\;h=h^{0}+h^{1},\;h^{0}\in W^{2,3}(\mathbb{R}),\;h^{1}\in W^{2,\infty}(\mathbb{R})}\}.

The norm on this space, which makes it Banach, is given by

(3.2.3)

\lVert h\rVert_{\mathbf{Z}}=\inf_{h=h^{0}+h^{1}}(\lVert h^{0}\rVert_{W^{2,3}}+\lVert h^{1}\rVert_{W^{2,\infty}}).

Given
\delta\in(0,1)
, we define the following closed, convex subsets of
\mathbf{Z}
:

(3.2.4)

Z_{\delta}=\{{h\in\mathbf{Z}\;:\;\lVert h\rVert_{\mathbf{Z}}\leqslant\delta^{-1},\;\delta\leqslant h\leqslant\delta^{-1}}\}.

We will assume that
h\in Z_{\delta}
and allow estimates for solutions to (
3.2.1
) to depend on
\delta
. Two remarks about our choices here are in order. First, for every
h\in\mathbf{Z}
with
\inf h>0
there exists
\delta>0
such that
h\in Z_{\delta}
, so assuming
h\in Z_{\delta}
in not a particularly strenuous constraint. Second, our specific choice of
\mathbf{Z}
is primarily for convenience and simplicity in our analysis of (
3.2.1
) for its intended application, and in principal many other choices for the
h
-parameter function space could work.

Next, we define the spaces for the velocity and pressure. For
n\in\{{0,1}\}
the closed subspace of vector fields that are tangent to
\Sigma_{0}
is denoted by

(3.2.5)

H^{n+1}_{\mathrm{tan}}(\Omega_{\varepsilon};\mathbb{R}^{2})=\{{u\in H^{n+1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon};\mathbb{R}^{2})\;:\;\mathrm{Tr}_{\Sigma_{0}}u_{2}=0}\}.

Using (
3.2.5
), we define the domain space

(3.2.6)

\mathbf{X}^{n}_{\varepsilon}=H^{n+1}_{\mathrm{tan}}(\Omega_{\varepsilon};\mathbb{R}^{2})\times H^{n}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})\text{ with norm }\lVert u,p\rVert_{\mathbf{X}^{n}_{\varepsilon}}=\lVert u\rVert_{H^{n+1}}+\lVert p\rVert_{H^{n}}.

We also need to equip
\mathbf{X}^{1}_{\varepsilon}
with a family of larger norms:

(3.2.7)

\lVert u,p\rVert_{\mathbf{X}^{1}_{\varepsilon}}^{(+)}=\lVert u,p\rVert_{\mathbf{X}^{1}_{\varepsilon}}+\lVert\partial_{2}u_{1}\rVert_{H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{0})}^{(\varepsilon,+)}+\lVert\partial_{2}u_{1}\rVert_{H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})}^{(\varepsilon,+)}.

While each of the norms (
3.2.7
) is equivalent to (
3.2.6
), we see that from Lemma
3.2
that the constants witnessing this equivalence are degenerating as
\varepsilon>0
tends to zero.

The weak codomain space is

(3.2.8)

\mathbf{Y}^{0}_{\varepsilon}=(H^{1}_{\mathrm{tan}}(\Omega_{\varepsilon};\mathbb{R}^{2}))^{\ast}\times L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})\text{ with norm }\lVert\varphi^{1},\varphi^{2}\rVert_{\mathbf{Y}^{0}_{\varepsilon}}=\lVert\varphi^{1}\rVert_{(H^{1}_{\mathrm{tan}})^{\ast}}+\lVert\varphi^{2}\rVert_{L^{2}}.

The strong codomain space is

(3.2.9)

\mathbf{Y}^{1}_{\varepsilon}=L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon};\mathbb{R}^{2})\times H^{1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})\times H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon};\mathbb{R}^{2})\times H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{0})

with norm given by

(3.2.10)

\lVert\varphi^{1},\varphi^{2},\psi^{1},\psi^{2}\rVert_{\mathbf{Y}^{1}_{\varepsilon}}=\lVert\varphi^{1}\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}+\lVert\varphi^{2}\rVert_{H^{1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}+\lVert\psi^{1}_{1}\rVert_{H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})}^{(\varepsilon,+)}+\lVert\psi^{1}_{2}\rVert_{H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})}^{(\varepsilon,-)}+\lVert\psi^{2}\rVert_{H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{0})}^{(\varepsilon,+)},

where we recall that the special
H^{1/2}(\mathbb{R})
norms used here are defined in Lemma
3.2
.

Our first result studies the weak formulation of system (
3.2.1
).

Proposition 3.7

(Theory of weak solutions)
.

Let
\varepsilon,\delta\in(0,1)
and
h\in Z_{\delta}
. There exists a constant
C\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
, depending only on
\delta
,
\upmu
, and
\mathrm{a}
such the following hold.

(1)

The bilinear form
B^{\varepsilon}_{h}:H^{1}_{\mathrm{tan}}(\Omega_{\varepsilon};\mathbb{R}^{2})\times H^{1}_{\mathrm{tan}}(\Omega_{\varepsilon};\mathbb{R}^{2})\to\mathbb{R}
given by

(3.2.11)

B^{\varepsilon}_{h}(u,v)=\frac{\upmu}{2}\int_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}h\mathbb{D}^{\mathcal{A}_{h}}u:\mathbb{D}^{\mathcal{A}_{h}}v+\varepsilon\mathrm{a}\int_{\Sigma_{0}}u_{1}v_{1}

is well-defined and continuous. Moreover for all
u,v\in H^{1}_{\mathrm{tan}}(\Omega_{\varepsilon};\mathbb{R}^{2})
we have the estimates

(3.2.12)

\char 12\relax{B^{\varepsilon}_{h}(u,v)}\leqslant C\lVert u\rVert_{H^{1}}\lVert v\rVert_{H^{1}}\text{ and }\lVert u\rVert_{H^{1}}^{2}\leqslant CB^{\varepsilon}_{h}(u,u).

(2)

The linear map
I^{\varepsilon}_{h}:\mathbf{X}^{0}_{\varepsilon}\to(H^{1}_{\mathrm{tan}}(\Omega_{\varepsilon};\mathbb{R}^{2}))^{\ast}
given by

(3.2.13)

\langle I^{\varepsilon}_{h}(u,p),v\rangle_{H^{1}_{\mathrm{tan}},(H^{1}_{\mathrm{tan}})^{\ast}}=B^{\varepsilon}_{h}(u,v)-\int_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}hp\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{h}}\cdot v

is well-defined and continuous. Moreover
\lVert I^{\varepsilon}_{h}(u,p)\rVert_{(H^{1}_{\mathrm{tan}})^{\ast}}\leqslant C\lVert u,p\rVert_{\mathbf{X}^{0}_{\varepsilon}}
for all
(u,p)\in\mathbf{X}^{0}_{\varepsilon}
.

(3)

The linear map
J^{\varepsilon}_{h}:\mathbf{X}^{0}_{\varepsilon}\to\mathbf{Y}^{0}_{\varepsilon}
given by
J^{\varepsilon}_{h}(u,p)=(I^{\varepsilon}_{h}(u,p),\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{h}}\cdot u)
is well-defined, continuous, and invertible. Moreover, for all
(u,p)\in\mathbf{X}^{0}_{\varepsilon}
we have the estimates

(3.2.14)

\lVert u,p\rVert_{\mathbf{X}^{0}_{\varepsilon}}\leqslant C\lVert J^{\varepsilon}_{h}(u,p)\rVert_{\mathbf{Y}^{0}_{\varepsilon}}\text{ and }\lVert J^{\varepsilon}_{h}(u,p)\rVert_{\mathbf{Y}^{0}_{\varepsilon}}\leqslant C\lVert u,p\rVert_{\mathbf{X}^{0}_{\varepsilon}}.

Proof.

Throughout the proof we permit implicit constants to depend only on
\delta
,
\upmu
, and
\mathrm{a}
.

For the first item, the boundedness estimate for
B^{\varepsilon}_{h}
in (
3.2.12
) follows from the fact that
\lVert h,\mathcal{A}_{h}\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim 1
, Cauchy-Schwarz, and the trace estimate of the second item of Lemma
3.3
. On the other hand, the coercive estimate in (
3.2.12
) is a consequence of the thin domain Korn inequality, Proposition
3.6
.

The second item follows directly from the first item and Cauchy-Schwarz.

We now turn our attention to the third item. The map
J^{\varepsilon}_{h}
is well-defined, continuous, and satisfies the right hand bound in (
3.2.14
) due to simple computations and the first two items. We now prove the left hand estimate in (
3.2.14
) and the invertibility of this operator.

Let
(\varphi^{1},\varphi^{2})\in\mathbf{Y}^{0}_{\varepsilon}
. For
(u,p)\in\mathbf{X}^{0}_{\varepsilon}
we use the right inverse to the divergence
\Uppi_{h}:L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})\to H^{1}_{\mathrm{tan}}(\Omega_{\varepsilon};\mathbb{R}^{2})
from the second item of Lemma
3.1
to derive the following equivalence:

(3.2.15)

J^{\varepsilon}_{h}(u,p)=(\varphi^{1},\varphi^{2})\;\Leftrightarrow\;\begin{cases}w=u-\Uppi_{h}\varphi^{2}\in H^{1}_{\mathrm{tan}}(\Omega_{\varepsilon};\mathbb{R}^{2}),\\
\widetilde{\varphi}^{1}=\varphi^{1}-I^{\varepsilon}_{h}(\Uppi_{h}\varphi^{2},0)\in(H^{1}_{\mathrm{tan}}(\Omega_{\varepsilon};\mathbb{R}^{2}))^{\ast},\\
J^{\varepsilon}_{h}(w,p)=(\widetilde{\varphi}^{1},0).\end{cases}

As
\lVert u\rVert_{H^{1}}\lesssim\lVert w\rVert_{H^{1}}+\lVert\varphi^{2}\rVert_{L^{2}}
and
\lVert\widetilde{\varphi}^{1}\rVert_{(H^{1}_{\mathrm{tan}})^{\ast}}\lesssim\lVert\varphi^{1},\varphi^{2}\rVert_{\mathbf{Y}^{0}_{\varepsilon}}
, we have reduced to proving that there exists a unique
(w,p)\in\mathbf{X}^{0}_{\varepsilon}
satisfying
J^{\varepsilon}_{h}(w,p)=(\widetilde{\varphi}^{1},0)
and
\lVert w,p\rVert_{\mathbf{X}^{0}_{\varepsilon}}\lesssim\lVert\widetilde{\varphi}^{1}\rVert_{(H^{1}_{\mathrm{tan}})^{\ast}}
.

We now prove the latter estimate, which will also grant us uniqueness. We test the equation
I^{\varepsilon}_{h}(w,p)=\widetilde{\varphi}^{1}
with
w
and note that
\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{h}}\cdot w=0
implies that the
p
contributions vanish; this yields

(3.2.16)

B^{\varepsilon}_{h}(w,w)=\langle\widetilde{\varphi}^{1},w\rangle_{(H^{1}_{\mathrm{tan}})^{\ast},H^{1}_{\mathrm{tan}}}.

By the coercive estimate in (
3.2.12
) it follows that
\lVert w\rVert_{H^{1}}\lesssim\lVert\widetilde{\varphi}^{1}\rVert_{(H^{1}_{\mathrm{tan}})^{\ast}}
. Now, to obtain a bound on
p
, we test the equation
I^{\varepsilon}_{h}(w,p)=\widetilde{\varphi}^{1}
with
\Uppi_{h}p\in H^{1}_{\mathrm{tan}}(\Omega_{\varepsilon};\mathbb{R}^{2})
and use the identity
\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{h}}\cdot\Uppi_{h}p=p
to equate

(3.2.17)

\int_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}h\char 12\relax{p}^{2}=B^{\varepsilon}_{h}(w,\Uppi_{h}p)-\langle\widetilde{\varphi}^{1},\Uppi_{h}p\rangle_{(H^{1}_{\mathrm{tan}})^{\ast},H^{1}_{\mathrm{tan}}}.

Since
\lVert\Uppi_{h}p\rVert_{H^{1}}\lesssim\lVert p\rVert_{L^{2}}
and
\delta\leqslant h
, we deduce that
\lVert p\rVert_{L^{2}}\lesssim\lVert\widetilde{\varphi}^{1}\rVert_{(H^{1}_{\mathrm{tan}})^{\ast}}
.

It remains to establish the existence of solutions to the reduced equation. Consider the following Hilbert space that is a closed subspace of
H^{1}_{\mathrm{tan}}(\Omega_{\varepsilon};\mathbb{R}^{2})
:

(3.2.18)

{{}_{\mathrm{sol}_{h}}}H^{1}_{\mathrm{tan}}(\Omega_{\varepsilon};\mathbb{R}^{2})=\{{v\in H^{1}_{\mathrm{tan}}(\Omega_{\varepsilon};\mathbb{R}^{2})\;:\;\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{h}}\cdot v=0}\}.

The estimates (
3.2.12
) from the first item show that the restriction of the bilinear form
B^{\varepsilon}_{h}
to the product of the space (
3.2.18
) with itself satisfies the hypotheses of the Lax-Milgram theorem (see, for instance, Theorem 6 in Chapter 6 of Lax
[
46
]
). The restriction of
\widetilde{\varphi}^{1}
to
{{}_{\mathrm{sol}_{h}}}H^{1}_{\mathrm{tan}}(\Omega_{\varepsilon};\mathbb{R}^{2})
defines an element in the dual space to (
3.2.18
) and so, by Lax-Milgram, there exists
w\in{{}_{\mathrm{sol}_{h}}}H^{1}_{\mathrm{tan}}(\Omega_{\varepsilon};\mathbb{R}^{2})
with the property that

(3.2.19)

B^{\varepsilon}_{h}(w,v)=\langle\widetilde{\varphi}^{1},v\rangle_{(H^{1}_{\mathrm{tan}})^{\ast},H^{1}_{\mathrm{tan}}}\text{ for all }v\in{{}_{\mathrm{sol}_{h}}}H^{1}_{\mathrm{tan}}(\Omega_{\varepsilon};\mathbb{R}^{2}).

Next, we define the bounded linear functional
Q\in(L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon}))^{\ast}
via

(3.2.20)

\langle Q,q\rangle_{(L^{2})^{\ast},L^{2}}=B^{\varepsilon}_{h}(w,\Uppi_{h}q)-\langle\widetilde{\varphi}^{1},\Uppi_{h}q\rangle_{(H^{1}_{\mathrm{tan}})^{\ast},H^{1}_{\mathrm{tan}}}

and employ the Riesz representation theorem (see, for instance, Theorem II.4 in Reed and Simon
[
74
]
) to produce a
p\in L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})
such that

(3.2.21)

\langle Q,q\rangle_{(L^{2})^{\ast},L^{2}}=\int_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}hpq\text{ for }q\in L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon}).

To conclude, we use (
3.2.19
) and (
3.2.21
) to prove that for any
v\in H^{1}_{\mathrm{tan}}(\Omega_{\varepsilon};\mathbb{R}^{2})
it holds that

(3.2.22)

\langle I^{\varepsilon}_{h}(w,p),v\rangle_{(H^{1}_{\mathrm{tan}})^{\ast},H^{1}_{\mathrm{tan}}}=\langle I^{\varepsilon}_{h}(w,p),v-\Uppi_{h}\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{h}}\cdot v\rangle_{(H^{1}_{\mathrm{tan}})^{\ast},H^{1}_{\mathrm{tan}}}+\langle I^{\varepsilon}_{h}(w,p),\Uppi_{h}\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{h}}\cdot v\rangle_{{}_{(H^{1}_{\mathrm{tan}})^{\ast},H^{1}_{\mathrm{tan}}}}\\
=\langle\widetilde{\varphi}^{1},v-\Uppi_{h}\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{h}}\cdot v\rangle_{{}_{(H^{1}_{\mathrm{tan}})^{\ast},H^{1}_{\mathrm{tan}}}}+B^{\varepsilon}_{h}(w,\Uppi_{h}\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{h}}\cdot v)_{(H^{1}_{\mathrm{tan}})^{\ast},H^{1}_{\mathrm{tan}}}-\langle Q,\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{h}}\cdot v\rangle_{(L^{2})^{\ast},L^{2}}=\langle\widetilde{\varphi}^{1},v\rangle_{(H^{1}_{\mathrm{tan}})^{\ast},H^{1}_{\mathrm{tan}}},

and hence
J^{\varepsilon}_{h}(w,p)=(\widetilde{\varphi}^{1},0)
.
∎

Our next result studies the strong formulation of (
3.2.1
). We associate to the system a family of partial differential operators depending on
h\in Z_{\delta}
and establish a priori estimates and Lipschitz continuity. The strategy for regularity in the following mirrors that which is typical for the Stokes equations, e.g. see Sections 6 and 7 of Chapter IV in Boyer and Fabrie
[
12
]
and references therein. However, we must enumerate the details precisely as to keep track of the thin domain parameter
\varepsilon
and the adapted norms.

Proposition 3.8

(Theory of strong solutions)
.

Let
\varepsilon,\delta\in(0,1)
,
h\in Z_{\delta}
. The following hold for a constant
C\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
depending only
\delta
,
\upmu
, and
\mathrm{a}
.

(1)

For
h\in Z_{\delta}
, the linear map
L^{\varepsilon}_{h}:\mathbf{X}^{1}_{\varepsilon}\to\mathbf{Y}^{1}_{\varepsilon}
given via

(3.2.23)

L^{\varepsilon}_{h}(u,p)=(\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{h}}p-\upmu\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{h}}\cdot\mathbb{D}^{\mathcal{A}_{h}}u,\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{h}}\cdot u,-(p-\upmu\mathbb{D}^{\mathcal{A}_{h}}u)\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon h},\upmu\partial_{2}^{\mathcal{A}_{h}}u_{1}-\varepsilon\mathrm{a}u_{1})

is well-defined and continuous. Moreover, for all
(u,p)\in\mathbf{X}^{1}_{\varepsilon}
we have the estimates

(3.2.24)

\lVert u,p\rVert_{\mathbf{X}^{1}_{\varepsilon}}^{(+)}\leqslant C\lVert L^{\varepsilon}_{h}(u,p)\rVert_{\mathbf{Y}^{1}_{\varepsilon}}\text{ and }\lVert L^{\varepsilon}_{h}(u,p)\rVert_{\mathbf{Y}^{1}_{\varepsilon}}\leqslant C\lVert u,p\rVert_{\mathbf{X}^{1}_{\varepsilon}}^{(+)}.

(2)

For
h,\widetilde{h}\in Z_{\delta}
and
(u,p)\in\mathbf{X}^{1}_{\varepsilon}
, we have the estimate

(3.2.25)

\lVert(L^{\varepsilon}_{h}-L^{\varepsilon}_{\widetilde{h}})(u,p)\rVert_{\mathbf{Y}^{1}_{\varepsilon}}\leqslant C\lVert h-\widetilde{h}\rVert_{\mathbf{Z}}\lVert u,p\rVert_{\mathbf{X}^{1}_{\varepsilon}}^{(+)}.

Proof.

Throughout the proof we permit implicit constants or the symbol
C
to depend only on
\delta
,
\upmu
, and
\mathrm{a}
. We divide the proof into steps.

Step 1 – The
\mathcal{A}
-gradient:
We begin with the following auxiliary claim: for
n\in\{{0,1}\}
and
h\in Z_{\delta}
the map

(3.2.26)

G^{\varepsilon}_{h}:H^{n+1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})\to H^{n}(\Omega_{\varepsilon};\mathbb{R}^{2})\text{ defined by }G^{\varepsilon}_{h}\varphi=\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{h}}\varphi

is well-defined, continuous, and obeys the bounds

(3.2.27)

\lVert G^{\varepsilon}_{h}\varphi\rVert_{H^{n}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}\leqslant C\lVert\varphi\rVert_{H^{n+1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}\text{ and }\lVert(G^{\varepsilon}_{h}-G^{\varepsilon}_{\widetilde{h}})\varphi\rVert_{H^{n}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}\leqslant C\lVert h-\widetilde{h}\rVert_{\mathbf{Z}}\lVert\varphi\rVert_{H^{n+1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}

for all
\varphi\in H^{n+1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})
and
h,\widetilde{h}\in Z_{\delta}
. The case
n=0
is straightforward: the embedding
\mathbf{Z}\hookrightarrow W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{R})
leads to the bounds

(3.2.28)

\lVert\mathcal{A}_{h}\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\leqslant C\text{ and }\lVert\mathcal{A}_{h}-\mathcal{A}_{\widetilde{h}}\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\leqslant C\lVert h-\widetilde{h}\rVert_{\mathbf{Z}}

so the case
n=0
is handled with a direct estimate.

For the case
n=1
, it suffices to study
\partial_{j}G^{\varepsilon}_{h}\varphi
mapping into
L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon};\mathbb{R}^{2})
for
j\in\{{1,2}\}
. We use the product rule to expand

(3.2.29)

\partial_{j}G^{\varepsilon}_{h}\varphi=G^{\varepsilon}_{h}\partial_{j}\varphi+(\partial_{j}\mathcal{A}_{h})\nabla\varphi.

The already established
n=0
mapping properties in (
3.2.27
) permit us to ignore the first term on the right hand side above and focus on the second one.

When
j=2
we calculate that

(3.2.30)

\partial_{2}\mathcal{A}_{h}=-\begin{pmatrix}0&h^{\prime}/h\\
0&0\end{pmatrix},\text{ and hence }\lVert\partial_{2}\mathcal{A}_{h}\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\leqslant C\text{ and }\lVert\partial_{2}\mathcal{A}_{h}-\partial_{2}\mathcal{A}_{\widetilde{h}}\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\leqslant C\lVert h-\widetilde{h}\rVert_{\mathbf{Z}}.

The desired bounds on
(\partial_{2}\mathcal{A}_{h})\nabla\varphi
now follow.

On the other hand, when
j=1
, we need to be more careful. We now calculate that

(3.2.31)

\partial_{1}\mathcal{A}_{h}=A^{1}_{h}+A^{2}_{h},\text{ for }A_{h}^{1}=\begin{pmatrix}0&y(h^{\prime})^{2}/h^{2}\\
0&-h^{\prime}/h\end{pmatrix}\text{ and }A^{2}_{h}=\begin{pmatrix}0&-yh^{\prime\prime}/h\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}.

By the same strategy as before, we see that the
A^{1}_{h}
contribution satisfies

(3.2.32)

\lVert A^{1}_{h}\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\leqslant C\text{ and }\lVert A^{1}_{h}-A^{1}_{\widetilde{h}}\rVert_{L^{\infty}}\leqslant C\lVert h-\widetilde{h}\rVert_{\mathbf{Z}},

which means we only need to consider the remaining
A^{2}_{h}
contribution. According to the definition of the space
\mathbf{Z}
given in (
3.2.2
) and (
3.2.3
), we may decompose
h=h^{0}+h^{1}
with
\lVert h^{0}\rVert_{W^{2,3}}\leqslant 2\delta^{-1}
and
\lVert h^{1}\rVert_{W^{2,\infty}}\leqslant 2\delta^{-1}
. In turn, after using the bounds
y<\varepsilon
and
\delta\leqslant h
we have that

(3.2.33)

\lVert A^{2}_{h}\nabla\varphi\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}\lesssim\varepsilon\lVert(h^{0})^{\prime\prime}\partial_{2}\varphi\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}+\varepsilon\lVert(h^{1})^{\prime\prime}\partial_{2}\varphi\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}\lesssim\varepsilon\lVert\varphi\rVert_{H^{1}}+\varepsilon\lVert h^{0}\rVert_{W^{2,3}(\mathbb{R})}\lVert\partial_{2}\varphi\rVert_{L^{6}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}.

The second item of Lemma
3.4
provides the estimate
\varepsilon^{1/3}\lVert\partial_{2}\varphi\rVert_{L^{6}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}\lesssim\lVert\varphi\rVert_{H^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}
. Combining these observations with (
3.2.33
) yields the estimate
\lVert A^{2}_{h}\nabla\varphi\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}\lesssim\varepsilon^{2/3}\lVert\varphi\rVert_{H^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}
, but then similar arguments show that

(3.2.34)

\lVert(A^{2}_{h}-A^{2}_{\widetilde{h}})\nabla\varphi\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}\lesssim\varepsilon^{2/3}\lVert h-\widetilde{h}\rVert_{\mathbf{Z}}\lVert\varphi\rVert_{H^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}.

We combine the above bounds on
A^{1}_{h}
,
A^{2}_{h}
to acquire the desired estimates on
(\partial_{1}\mathcal{A}_{h})\nabla\varphi
, which completes the proof of the claims (
3.2.26
) and (
3.2.27
) in the case
n=1
.

Step 2 – Lipschitz bounds on the operator norm:
We are now in a position to prove the second estimate of (
3.2.24
) and the Lipschitz estimate of (
3.2.25
). This will be achieved by splitting the operator
L^{\varepsilon}_{h}
into various pieces.

Consider first the stress tensor maps
{{}_{1}}S^{\varepsilon}_{h},{{}_{2}}S^{\varepsilon}_{h}:\mathbf{X}^{1}_{\varepsilon}\to H^{1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon};\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2})
defined via

(3.2.35)

{{}_{1}}S^{\varepsilon}_{h}(u,p)=pI-2\upmu\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{1}^{\mathcal{A}_{h}}u_{1}&0\\
0&\partial_{2}^{\mathcal{A}_{h}}u_{2}\end{pmatrix}\text{ and }{{}_{2}}S^{\varepsilon}_{h}(u,p)=-\upmu\begin{pmatrix}0&\partial^{\mathcal{A}_{h}}_{2}u_{1}+\partial^{\mathcal{A}_{h}}_{1}u_{2}\\
\partial^{\mathcal{A}_{h}}_{2}u_{1}+\partial^{\mathcal{A}_{h}}_{1}u_{2}&0\end{pmatrix}.

The estimates we have established for the maps
G^{\varepsilon}_{h}
from (
3.2.26
) allow us to read off the following bounds for
j\in\{{1,2}\}
:

(3.2.36)

\lVert{{}_{j}}S^{\varepsilon}_{h}(u,p)\rVert_{H^{1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}\leqslant C\lVert u,p\rVert_{\mathbf{X}^{1}_{\varepsilon}}\text{ and }\lVert({{}_{j}}S^{\varepsilon}_{h}-{{}_{j}}S^{\varepsilon}_{\widetilde{h}})(u,p)\rVert_{H^{1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}\leqslant C\lVert h-\widetilde{h}\rVert_{\mathbf{Z}}\lVert u,p\rVert_{\mathbf{X}^{1}_{\varepsilon}}.

Similarly, we have the following estimates for the
\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{h}}
-divergence of
{{}_{j}}S^{\varepsilon}_{h}
:

(3.2.37)

\lVert\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{h}}\cdot{{}_{j}}S^{\varepsilon}_{h}(u,p)\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}\leqslant C\lVert u,p\rVert_{\mathbf{X}^{1}_{\varepsilon}}\text{ and }\lVert(\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{h}}\cdot{{}_{j}}S^{\varepsilon}_{h}-\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{\widetilde{h}}}\cdot{{}_{j}}S^{\varepsilon}_{\widetilde{h}})(u,p)\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}\leqslant C\lVert h-\widetilde{h}\rVert_{\mathbf{Z}}\lVert u,p\rVert_{\mathbf{X}^{1}_{\varepsilon}}.

Summing
{{}_{1}}S^{\varepsilon}_{h}+{{}_{2}}S^{\varepsilon}_{h}
and employing (
3.2.37
) then yields bounds for the momentum equation components of
L^{\varepsilon}_{h}
:

(3.2.38)

\lVert\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{h}}p-\upmu\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{h}}\cdot\mathbb{D}^{\mathcal{A}_{h}}u\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}\leqslant C\lVert u,p\rVert_{\mathbf{X}^{1}_{\varepsilon}},\text{ and }

(3.2.39)

\lVert(\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{h}}-\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{\widetilde{h}}})p-\upmu(\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{h}}\cdot\mathbb{D}^{\mathcal{A}_{h}}-\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{\widetilde{h}}}\cdot\mathbb{D}^{\mathcal{A}_{\widetilde{h}}})u\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}\leqslant C\lVert h-\widetilde{h}\rVert_{\mathbf{Z}}\lVert u,p\rVert_{\mathbf{X}^{1}_{\varepsilon}}.

Using (
3.2.36
) with
p=0
and taking the trace of
{{}_{1}}S^{\varepsilon}_{h}
, we also may read off the
L^{\varepsilon}_{h}
divergence equation bounds:

(3.2.40)

\lVert\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{h}}\cdot u\rVert_{H^{1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}\leqslant C\lVert u,p\rVert_{\mathbf{X}^{1}_{\varepsilon}}\text{ and }\lVert(\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{h}}-\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{\widetilde{h}}})\cdot u\rVert_{H^{1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}\leqslant C\lVert h-\widetilde{h}\rVert_{\mathbf{Z}}\lVert u,p\rVert_{\mathbf{X}^{1}_{\varepsilon}}.

Note that (
3.2.38
), (
3.2.39
), and (
3.2.40
) give stronger Lipschitz estimates than what is asserted in (
3.2.25
), as we have not yet encountered the adapted norms (
3.2.7
).

The bulk components of
L^{\varepsilon}_{h}
are now handled, so we next turn our attention to the boundary components, for which case the adapted norms (
3.2.7
) will now play a role. We first consider the Navier slip component of
L^{\varepsilon}_{h}
. Due to the embedding
\mathbf{Z}\hookrightarrow W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{R})
, the boundedness of the product map
H^{1/2}(\mathbb{R})\times W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{R})\to H^{1/2}(\mathbb{R})
, and the estimates from the second item of Lemma
3.3
and the first item of Lemma
3.2
, we find the estimates

(3.2.41)

\lVert\partial_{2}^{\mathcal{A}_{h}}u_{1}-\varepsilon\mathrm{a}u_{1}\rVert_{H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{0})}^{(\varepsilon,+)}\lesssim\lVert\partial_{2}u_{1}\rVert_{H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{0})}^{(\varepsilon,+)}+\lVert u_{1}\rVert_{H^{1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}\lesssim\lVert u,p\rVert^{(+)}_{\mathbf{X}^{1}_{\varepsilon}},

and similarly

(3.2.42)

\lVert(\partial_{2}^{\mathcal{A}_{h}}-\partial_{2}^{\mathcal{A}_{\widetilde{h}}})u_{1}\rVert^{(\varepsilon,+)}_{H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{0})}\lesssim\lVert h-\widetilde{h}\rVert_{\mathbf{Z}}\lVert u,p\rVert^{(+)}_{\mathbf{X}^{1}_{\varepsilon}}.

Now we consider the stress boundary condition components of
L^{\varepsilon}_{h}
. According to (
3.2.35
), on
\Sigma_{\varepsilon}
we have the equality

(3.2.43)

(p-\upmu\mathbb{D}^{\mathcal{A}_{h}}u)\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon h}={{}_{1}}S^{\varepsilon}_{h}(u,p)\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon h}+{{}_{2}}S^{\varepsilon}_{h}(u,p)\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon h}.

We will resolve the terms on the right into components, recalling that
\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon h}=(-\varepsilon h^{\prime},1)
. By using the boundedness of the product map
(W^{1,3}+W^{1,\infty})(\mathbb{R})\times H^{1/2}(\mathbb{R})\to H^{1/2}(\mathbb{R})
, the second item of Lemma
3.3
, the first item of Lemma
3.2
, and the stress tensor bounds (
3.2.36
) we get

(3.2.44)

\lVert{{}_{1}}S^{\varepsilon}_{h}(u,p)\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon h}\cdot e_{1}\rVert^{(\varepsilon,+)}_{H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})}+\lVert{{}_{2}}S^{\varepsilon}_{h}(u,p)\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon h}\cdot e_{2}\rVert_{H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})}^{(\varepsilon,-)}\lesssim\lVert{{}_{1}}S^{\varepsilon}_{h}(u,p)\rVert_{H^{1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}+\lVert{{}_{2}}S^{\varepsilon}_{h}(u,p)\rVert_{H^{1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}\lesssim\lVert u,p\rVert_{\mathbf{X}^{1}_{\varepsilon}}

and, by similar arguments,

(3.2.45)

\lVert({{}_{1}}S^{\varepsilon}_{h}(u,p)\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon h}-{{}_{1}}S^{\varepsilon}_{\widetilde{h}}(u,p)\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon\widetilde{h}})\cdot e_{1}\rVert^{(\varepsilon,+)}_{H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})}+\lVert({{}_{2}}S^{\varepsilon}_{h}(u,p)\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon h}-{{}_{2}}S^{\varepsilon}_{\widetilde{h}}(u,p)\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon\widetilde{h}})\cdot e_{2}\rVert^{(\varepsilon,-)}_{H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})}\lesssim\lVert h-\widetilde{h}\rVert_{\mathbf{Z}}\lVert u,p\rVert_{\mathbf{X}^{1}_{\varepsilon}}.

For the term
{{}_{1}}S^{\varepsilon}_{h}(u,p)\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon h}\cdot e_{2}=p-2\upmu\partial_{2}^{\mathcal{A}_{h}}u_{2}
we again use the second item of Lemma
3.3
to get

(3.2.46)

\lVert{{}_{1}}S^{\varepsilon}_{h}(u,p)\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon h}\cdot e_{2}\rVert_{H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})}^{(\varepsilon,-)}\lesssim\lVert p\rVert^{(\varepsilon,-)}_{H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})}+\lVert 2\upmu\partial_{2}^{\mathcal{A}_{h}}u_{2}\rVert^{(\varepsilon,-)}_{H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})}\lesssim\lVert u,p\rVert_{\mathbf{X}^{1}_{\varepsilon}},

but estimating the Lipschitz norm is no different:

(3.2.47)

\lVert({{}_{1}}S^{\varepsilon}_{h}(u,p)\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon h}-{{}_{1}}S^{\varepsilon}_{\widetilde{h}}(u,p)\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon\widetilde{h}})\cdot e_{2}\rVert^{(\varepsilon,-)}_{H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})}\lesssim\lVert(1/h-1/\widetilde{h})\partial_{2}u_{2}\rVert^{(\varepsilon,-)}_{H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})}\lesssim\lVert h-\widetilde{h}\rVert_{W^{1,\infty}}\lVert u,p\rVert_{\mathbf{X}^{1}_{\varepsilon}}.

The story is similar for
{{}_{2}}S^{\varepsilon}_{h}(u,p)\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon h}\cdot e_{1}=\partial_{2}^{\mathcal{A}_{h}}u_{1}+\partial_{1}^{\mathcal{A}_{h}}u_{2}=(\partial_{2}u_{1}+h\partial_{1}u_{2}-\varepsilon h^{\prime}\partial_{2}u_{2})/h
. Via the aforementioned product estimates, the definition of the adapted norms (
3.2.7
), the first and second items of Lemma
3.3
, the first item of Lemma
3.2
, and the fact that
\mathrm{Tr}_{\Sigma_{0}}\partial_{1}u_{2}=0
, we find that

(3.2.48)

\lVert{{}_{2}}S^{\varepsilon}_{h}(u,p)\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon h}\cdot e_{1}\rVert^{(\varepsilon,+)}_{H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})}\lesssim\lVert\partial_{2}u_{1}\rVert_{H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})}^{(\varepsilon,+)}+\lVert\partial_{1}u_{2}\rVert^{(\varepsilon,+)}_{H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})}+\varepsilon\lVert\partial_{2}u_{2}\rVert^{(\varepsilon,+)}_{H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})}\lesssim\lVert u,p\rVert^{(+)}_{\mathbf{X}^{1}_{\varepsilon}}.

For the difference, the same tricks give the bound

(3.2.49)

\lVert({{}_{2}}S^{\varepsilon}_{h}(u,p)\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon h}-{{}_{2}}S^{\varepsilon}_{\widetilde{h}}(u,p)\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon\widetilde{h}})\cdot e_{1}\rVert^{(\varepsilon,+)}_{H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})}\lesssim\lVert h-\widetilde{h}\rVert_{\mathbf{Z}}\lVert u,p\rVert^{(+)}_{\mathbf{X}^{1}_{\varepsilon}}.

Combining (
3.2.38
), (
3.2.40
), (
3.2.41
), (
3.2.43
), (
3.2.44
), (
3.2.46
), and (
3.2.48
) grants us the operator bound
\lVert L^{\varepsilon}_{h}(u,p)\rVert_{\mathbf{Y}^{1}_{\varepsilon}}\lesssim\lVert u,p\rVert_{\mathbf{X}^{1}_{\varepsilon}}^{(+)}
of the first item. On the other hand, combining (
3.2.39
), (
3.2.40
), (
3.2.42
), (
3.2.43
), (
3.2.45
), (
3.2.47
), and (
3.2.49
) grants us the claimed Lipschitz bound (
3.2.25
) of the second item.

Step 3 – The a priori estimate:
It remains only to prove the a priori estimate
\lVert u,p\rVert^{(+)}_{\mathbf{X}^{1}_{\varepsilon}}\lesssim\lVert L^{\varepsilon}_{h}(u,p)\rVert_{\mathbf{Y}^{1}_{\varepsilon}}
from the first item. Let
(u,p)\in\mathbf{X}^{1}_{\varepsilon}
and define
\upsilon=(\varphi^{1},\varphi^{2},\psi^{1},\psi^{2})\in\mathbf{Y}^{1}_{\varepsilon}
via
L^{\varepsilon}_{h}(u,p)=\upsilon
so that system (
3.2.1
) is satisfied.

For any
w\in H^{1}_{\mathrm{tan}}(\Omega_{\varepsilon};\mathbb{R}^{2})
we test the fist equation in system (
3.2.1
) with
hw
and integrate by parts to derive the identity

(3.2.50)

\langle I^{\varepsilon}_{h}(u,p),w\rangle_{(H^{1}_{\mathrm{tan}})^{\ast},H^{1}_{\mathrm{tan}}}=\langle T_{\upsilon},w\rangle_{(H^{1}_{\mathrm{tan}})^{\ast},H^{1}_{\mathrm{tan}}},

where
I^{\varepsilon}_{h}
is the linear map considered in the second item of Proposition
3.7
and
T_{\upsilon}\in(H^{1}_{\mathrm{tan}}(\Omega_{\varepsilon};\mathbb{R}^{2}))^{\ast}
is defined by

(3.2.51)

\langle T_{\upsilon},w\rangle_{(H^{1}_{\mathrm{tan}})^{\ast},H^{1}_{\mathrm{tan}}}=\int_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}h\varphi^{1}\cdot w+\int_{\Sigma_{\varepsilon}}\psi^{1}\cdot w-\int_{\Sigma_{0}}\psi^{2}w_{1}.

In the language of the third item of Proposition
3.7
, we have that
J^{\varepsilon}_{h}(u,p)=(T_{\upsilon},\varphi^{2})
and so, from the same result, we have the (low regularity) estimate

(3.2.52)

\lVert u,p\rVert_{\mathbf{X}^{0}_{\varepsilon}}\lesssim\lVert T_{\upsilon},\varphi^{2}\rVert_{\mathbf{Y}^{0}_{\varepsilon}}=\lVert T_{\upsilon}\rVert_{(H^{1}_{\mathrm{tan}})^{\ast}}+\lVert\varphi^{2}\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}.

The definition of the norm on
\mathbf{Y}^{1}_{\varepsilon}
(
3.2.10
) shows that
\lVert\varphi^{2}\rVert_{L^{2}}\leqslant\lVert\upsilon\rVert_{\mathbf{Y}^{1}_{\varepsilon}}
. On the other hand, Cauchy-Schwarz and the third and fourth items of Lemma
3.3
show that

(3.2.53)

\char 12\relax{\langle T_{\upsilon},w\rangle_{(H^{1}_{\mathrm{tan}})^{\ast},H^{1}_{\mathrm{tan}}}}\lesssim(\lVert\varphi^{1}\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}+\lVert\psi^{1}_{1}\rVert^{(\varepsilon,+)}_{H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})}+\lVert\psi^{1}_{2}\rVert^{(\varepsilon,-)}_{H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})}+\lVert\psi^{2}\rVert^{(\varepsilon,+)}_{H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{0})})\lVert w\rVert_{H^{1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})},

which implies that
\lVert T_{\upsilon}\rVert_{(H^{1}_{\mathrm{tan}})^{\ast}}\lesssim\lVert\upsilon\rVert_{\mathbf{Y}^{1}_{\varepsilon}}
. Hence,
\lVert u,p\rVert_{\mathbf{X}^{0}_{\varepsilon}}\lesssim\lVert\upsilon\rVert_{\mathbf{Y}^{1}_{\varepsilon}}
.

We need to estimate one more derivative of
u
and
p
in terms of the data
\upsilon
; in doing so we will break to cases, first considering the tangential (
e_{1}
direction) derivatives and then the normal (
e_{2}
direction) derivatives. For the tangential derivative we again employ the weak formulation, but this time the one satisfied by
\partial_{1}u
and
\partial_{1}p
. To compute this we let
w\in H^{2}_{\mathrm{tan}}(\Omega_{\varepsilon};\mathbb{R}^{2})
, insert
-\partial_{1}w\in H^{1}_{\mathrm{tan}}(\Omega_{\varepsilon};\mathbb{R}^{2})
into the weak formulation (
3.2.50
), and integrate by parts to arrive at the identity

(3.2.54)

\langle I^{\varepsilon}_{h}(\partial_{1}u,\partial_{1}p),w\rangle_{(H^{1}_{\mathrm{tan}})^{\ast},H^{1}_{\mathrm{tan}}}+\langle C^{\varepsilon}_{h}(u,p),w\rangle_{(H^{1}_{\mathrm{tan}})^{\ast},H^{1}_{\mathrm{tan}}}=\langle T^{1}_{\upsilon},w\rangle_{(H^{1}_{\mathrm{tan}}),^{\ast},H^{1}_{\mathrm{tan}}}

where

(3.2.55)

\langle C^{\varepsilon}_{h}(u,p),w\rangle_{(H^{1}_{\mathrm{tan}})^{\ast},H^{1}_{\mathrm{tan}}}=\int_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}\partial_{1}h\Big(\frac{\upmu}{2}\mathbb{D}^{\mathcal{A}_{h}}u:\mathbb{D}^{\mathcal{A}_{h}}w-p\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{h}}\cdot w\Big)\\
+\int_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}\frac{\upmu h}{2}(\mathbb{D}^{\partial_{1}\mathcal{A}_{h}}u:\mathbb{D}^{\mathcal{A}_{h}}w+\mathbb{D}^{\mathcal{A}_{h}}u:\mathbb{D}^{\partial_{1}\mathcal{A}_{h}}w)-hp\nabla^{\partial_{1}\mathcal{A}_{h}}\cdot w,

and

(3.2.56)

\langle T^{1}_{\upsilon},w\rangle_{(H^{1}_{\mathrm{tan}})^{\ast},H^{1}_{\mathrm{tan}}}=-\int_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}h\varphi^{1}\cdot\partial_{1}w-\int_{\Sigma_{\varepsilon}}\langle D\rangle^{1/2}\psi^{1}\cdot\langle D\rangle^{-1/2}\partial_{1}w+\int_{\Sigma_{0}}\langle D\rangle^{1/2}\psi^{2}\cdot\langle D\rangle^{-1/2}\partial_{1}w_{1}.

By a simple density argument, we find that identities (
3.2.54
), (
3.2.55
), and (
3.2.56
) continue to hold for all
w\in H^{1}_{\mathrm{tan}}(\Omega_{\varepsilon};\mathbb{R}^{2})
. We also find that

(3.2.57)

\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{h}}\cdot\partial_{1}u+\nabla^{\partial_{j}\mathcal{A}_{h}}\cdot u=\partial_{1}\varphi^{2},

and so the third item of Proposition
3.7
provides the estimate

(3.2.58)

\lVert\partial_{1}u,\partial_{1}p\rVert_{\mathbf{X}^{0}_{\varepsilon}}\lesssim\lVert T^{1}_{\upsilon}\rVert_{(H^{1}_{\mathrm{tan}})^{\ast}}+\lVert\partial_{1}\varphi^{2}\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}+\lVert C^{\varepsilon}_{h}(u,p)\rVert_{(H^{1}_{\mathrm{tan}})^{\ast}}+\lVert\nabla^{\partial_{1}\mathcal{A}_{h}}\cdot u\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}.

We now estimate each of the four contributions to the right hand side of (
3.2.58
). The first of these is handled via the second and third items of Lemma
3.3
and the definition of the norm (
3.2.10
):

(3.2.59)

\char 12\relax{\langle T^{1}_{\upsilon},w\rangle_{(H^{1}_{\mathrm{tan}})^{\ast},H^{1}_{\mathrm{tan}}}}\lesssim(\lVert\varphi^{1}\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}+\lVert\psi^{1}_{1}\rVert^{(\varepsilon,+)}_{H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})}+\lVert\psi^{1}_{2}\rVert^{(\varepsilon,-)}_{H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})}+\lVert\psi^{2}\rVert^{(\varepsilon,+)}_{H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{0})})\lVert w\rVert_{H^{1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})},

which implies that
\lVert T^{1}_{\upsilon}\rVert_{(H^{1}_{\mathrm{tan}})^{\ast}}\lesssim\lVert\upsilon\rVert_{\mathbf{Y}^{1}_{\varepsilon}}
.
The second term in (
3.2.58
) is directly estimated with
\lVert\partial_{1}\varphi^{2}\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}\leqslant\lVert\upsilon\rVert_{\mathbf{Y}^{1}_{\varepsilon}}
.

The third term in (
3.2.58
) is more subtle. We initially use the embedding
\mathbf{Z}\hookrightarrow W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{R})
, the left hand estimate of (
3.2.28
), and Cauchy-Schwarz to bound

(3.2.60)

\char 12\relax{\langle C^{\varepsilon}_{h}(u,p),w\rangle_{(H^{1}_{\mathrm{tan}})^{\ast},H^{1}_{\mathrm{tan}}}}\lesssim\Big(\int_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}(1+\char 12\relax{\partial_{1}\mathcal{A}_{h}})^{2}(\char 12\relax{p}^{2}+\char 12\relax{\nabla u}^{2})\Big)^{1/2}\lVert w\rVert_{H^{1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}.

We combine this with the already established bound
\lVert u,p\rVert_{\mathbf{X}^{0}_{\varepsilon}}\lesssim\lVert\upsilon\rVert_{\mathbf{Y}^{1}_{\varepsilon}}
, the decomposition
\partial_{1}\mathcal{A}_{h}=A_{h}^{1}+A_{h}^{2}
from (
3.2.31
), and the left hand estimate of (
3.2.32
), to the see that

(3.2.61)

\lVert C^{\varepsilon}_{h}(u,p)\rVert_{(H^{1}_{\mathrm{tan}})^{\ast}}\lesssim\lVert\upsilon\rVert_{\mathbf{Y}^{1}_{\varepsilon}}+\varepsilon\Big(\int_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}\char 12\relax{h^{\prime\prime}}^{2}(\char 12\relax{p}^{2}+\char 12\relax{\nabla u}^{2})\Big)^{1/2}.

Now, by the definition of the norm (
3.2.3
) on the space
\mathbf{Z}
we have
h=h^{0}+h^{1}
with
\lVert h^{0}\rVert_{W^{2,3}}\leqslant 2\delta^{-1}
and
\lVert h^{1}\rVert_{W^{2,\infty}}\leqslant 2\delta^{-1}
. We split the
h
appearing in (
3.2.61
) according to this decomposition. The
h^{0}
contribution is then bounded in
L^{3}(\mathbb{R})
, whereas the
h^{1}
contribution can be estimated in
L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
. For the
h^{0}
contribution we then use Hölder and estimate the resulting
L^{6}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})
-norm on
\nabla u
and
p
by interpolating between the
L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})
and
L^{12}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})
norms. Executing this gives us the bound

(3.2.62)

\lVert C^{\varepsilon}_{h}(u,p)\rVert_{(H^{1}_{\mathrm{tan}})^{\ast}}\lesssim\lVert\upsilon\rVert_{\mathbf{Y}^{1}_{\varepsilon}}+\varepsilon\lVert\upsilon\rVert_{\mathbf{Y}^{1}_{\varepsilon}}^{1/5}\lVert p,\nabla u\rVert^{4/5}_{L^{12}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}.

Finally, we use the second item of Lemma
3.4
to bound
\lVert p,\nabla u\rVert_{L^{12}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}\lesssim\varepsilon^{-5/12}\lVert u,p\rVert_{\mathbf{X}^{1}_{\varepsilon}}
. The same strategy also allows us to estimate the final contribution in (
3.2.58
). Synthesizing these bounds, we derive the tangential regularity estimate

(3.2.63)

\lVert\partial_{1}u,\partial_{1}p\rVert_{\mathbf{X}^{0}_{\varepsilon}}\lesssim\lVert\upsilon\rVert_{\mathbf{Y}^{1}_{\varepsilon}}+\varepsilon^{2/3}\lVert\upsilon\rVert_{\mathbf{Y}^{1}_{\varepsilon}}^{1/5}\lVert u,p\rVert^{4/5}_{\mathbf{X}^{1}_{\varepsilon}}.

With the tangential estimates (
3.2.63
) in hand, we now aim to promote to normal derivative estimates. In doing so, our first task is to solve for
\partial_{2}p
in terms of quantities that can be estimated by
\lVert\upsilon\rVert_{\mathbf{Y}^{1}_{\varepsilon}}
or
\lVert\partial_{1}u,\partial_{1}p\rVert_{\mathbf{X}^{0}_{\varepsilon}}
, and we accomplish this by passing through an intermediate quantity derived from the divergence equation in (
3.2.1
). Indeed, we apply
\partial_{2}
to this equation and solve for
\partial_{2}^{2}u\cdot\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}_{h}
, where
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}_{h}=(-yh^{\prime},1)=\mathcal{M}_{h}^{\mathrm{t}}e_{2}
:

(3.2.64)

\partial_{2}^{2}u\cdot\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}_{h}=h\partial_{2}\varphi^{2}+h^{\prime}\partial_{2}u_{1}-h\partial_{1}\partial_{2}u_{1}.

With (
3.2.64
) in mind, we rewrite the momentum equation in (
3.2.1
) as

(3.2.65)

\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{h}}p-\upmu\Delta^{\mathcal{A}_{h}}u=\varphi^{1}+\upmu\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{h}}\varphi^{2},

take the inner product of (
3.2.65
) with the vector field
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}_{h}
, and then substitute (
3.2.64
) where appropriate to derive the identity

(3.2.66)

(1+(yh^{\prime})^{2})\partial_{2}p/h=yh^{\prime}\partial_{1}p+(\varphi^{1}+\upmu\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{h}}\varphi^{2}+\partial_{1}^{2}u-2yh^{\prime}\partial_{1}\partial_{2}u+y(2(h^{\prime}/h)^{2}-h^{\prime\prime}/h)\partial_{2}u)\cdot\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}_{h}\\
+(1+(yh^{\prime})^{2})(h\partial_{2}\varphi^{2}+h^{\prime}\partial_{2}u_{1}-h\partial_{1}\partial_{2}u_{1})/h^{2}.

We next divide both sides of (
3.2.66
) by
(1+(yh^{\prime})^{2})/h
and take the norm in
L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})
. The bounds
\lVert h,1/h\rVert_{W^{1,\infty}}\lesssim 1
, along with (
3.2.63
) can be applied to each term with a single exception, the product
h^{\prime\prime}\partial_{2}u
. This term instead requires the exact same strategy we have seen in (
3.2.62
). We therefore arrive at the estimate

(3.2.67)

\lVert\partial_{2}p\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}\lesssim\lVert\upsilon\rVert_{\mathbf{Y}^{1}_{\varepsilon}}+\varepsilon^{2/3}\lVert\upsilon\rVert_{\mathbf{Y}^{1}_{\varepsilon}}^{1/5}\lVert u,p\rVert^{4/5}_{\mathbf{X}^{1}_{\varepsilon}}+\varepsilon\lVert h^{\prime\prime}\partial_{2}u\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}\lesssim\lVert\upsilon\rVert_{\mathbf{Y}^{1}_{\varepsilon}}+\varepsilon^{2/3}\lVert\upsilon\rVert_{\mathbf{Y}^{1}_{\varepsilon}}^{1/5}\lVert u,p\rVert^{4/5}_{\mathbf{X}^{1}_{\varepsilon}}.

To get the normal regularity of the velocity, we solve for
\partial_{2}^{2}u
in identity (
3.2.65
):

(3.2.68)

\upmu(1+(yh^{\prime})^{2})\partial_{2}^{2}u/h^{2}=\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{h}}p-\varphi^{1}-\upmu\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{h}}\varphi^{2}-\upmu(\partial_{1}^{2}u-2yh^{\prime}\partial_{1}\partial_{2}u/h+y(2(h^{\prime}/h)^{2}-h^{\prime\prime}/h)\partial_{2}u).

Taking the
L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon};\mathbb{R}^{2})
-norm, employing (
3.2.63
) and (
3.2.67
), and using the same care previously mentioned with the product
h^{\prime\prime}\partial_{2}u
, we find

(3.2.69)

\lVert\partial_{2}^{2}u\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}\lesssim\lVert\upsilon\rVert_{\mathbf{Y}^{1}_{\varepsilon}}+\varepsilon^{2/3}\lVert\upsilon\rVert_{\mathbf{Y}^{1}_{\varepsilon}}^{1/5}\lVert u,p\rVert^{4/5}_{\mathbf{X}^{1}_{\varepsilon}}.

We now combine (
3.2.63
), (
3.2.67
), and (
3.2.69
) with the estimates on weak solutions and a simple adsorption argument to deduce that

(3.2.70)

\lVert u,p\rVert_{\mathbf{X}^{1}_{\varepsilon}}\lesssim\lVert u,p\rVert_{\mathbf{X}^{0}_{\varepsilon}}+\lVert\partial_{1}u,\partial_{1}p\rVert_{\mathbf{X}^{0}_{\varepsilon}}+\lVert\partial_{2}^{2}u,\partial_{2}p\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}\lesssim\lVert\upsilon\rVert_{\mathbf{Y}^{1}_{\varepsilon}}.

The estimate (
3.2.70
) is nearly the left bound in (
3.2.24
), but we still need to promote to the adapted norms (
3.2.7
). Fortunately, this is now simple and we need only solve for the special boundary quantities identified by the adapted norms in the equations (
3.2.1
) and then measure their size. If we look to the Navier slip boundary condition, we find that

(3.2.71)

\lVert\partial_{2}u_{1}\rVert_{H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{0})}^{(\varepsilon,+)}\lesssim\varepsilon\lVert u_{1}\rVert^{(\varepsilon,+)}_{H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{0})}+\lVert\psi^{2}\rVert_{H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{0})}^{(\varepsilon,+)}\lesssim\lVert\upsilon\rVert_{\mathbf{Y}^{1}_{\varepsilon}}.

On the other hand, in the
e_{1}
-stress boundary condition, we have

(3.2.72)

\lVert\partial_{2}u_{1}\rVert_{H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})}^{(\varepsilon,+)}\lesssim\varepsilon\lVert p-2\upmu\partial_{1}^{\mathcal{A}_{h}}u_{1}\rVert^{(\varepsilon,+)}_{H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})}+\lVert\partial_{1}^{\mathcal{A}_{h}}u_{2}\rVert^{(\varepsilon,+)}_{H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})}+\lVert\psi^{1}_{1}\rVert_{H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})}^{(\varepsilon,+)}\lesssim\lVert\upsilon\rVert_{\mathbf{Y}^{1}_{\varepsilon}}.

The left estimate of (
3.2.24
) then follows by combining (
3.2.70
) with (
3.2.71
) and (
3.2.72
).
∎

Our next result synthesizes a method of continuity argument with the previous result.

Theorem 3.9

(Synthesis of linear analysis)
.

Let
\varepsilon,\delta\in(0,1)
. For all
h\in Z_{\delta}
, the linear map
L^{\varepsilon}_{h}:\mathbf{X}^{1}_{\varepsilon}\to\mathbf{Y}^{1}_{\varepsilon}
defined in (
3.2.23
) is invertible. Moreover, for a constant
C\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
depending only on
\delta
,
\upmu
, and
\mathrm{a}
, we have the following estimates for all
\upsilon=(\varphi^{1},\varphi^{2},\psi^{1},\psi^{2})\in\mathbf{Y}^{1}_{\varepsilon}
and
h,\widetilde{h}\in Z_{\delta}
:

(3.2.73)

\lVert(L^{\varepsilon}_{h})^{-1}\upsilon\rVert_{\mathbf{X}^{1}_{\varepsilon}}^{(+)}\leqslant C\lVert\upsilon\rVert_{\mathbf{Y}^{1}_{\varepsilon}}\text{ and }\lVert[{(L^{\varepsilon}_{h})^{-1}-(L^{\varepsilon}_{\widetilde{h}})^{-1}}]\upsilon\rVert_{\mathbf{X}^{1}_{\varepsilon}}^{(+)}\leqslant C\lVert h-\widetilde{h}\rVert_{\mathbf{Z}}\lVert\upsilon\rVert_{\mathbf{Y}^{1}_{\varepsilon}}.

Proof.

We begin by proving that the map (
3.2.23
) is invertible. Once this is known, the left hand operator norm estimate in (
3.2.73
) follows immediately from estimate (
3.2.24
) from Proposition
3.8
. By the method of continuity (see, for instance, Theorem 5.2 in Gilbarg and Trudinger
[
33
]
or, for a more general version, Theorem 4.51 in Abramovich and Aliprantis
[
1
]
), the Lipschitz estimate from the second item of Proposition
3.8
, and the a priori estimate from the first item of the aforementioned result, we see that it suffices to prove that when
h=1\in Z_{\delta}
the operator
L^{\varepsilon}_{1}:\mathbf{X}^{1}_{\varepsilon}\to\mathbf{Y}^{1}_{\varepsilon}
is an isomorphism.

Proposition
3.8
shows that
L^{\varepsilon}_{1}:\mathbf{X}^{1}_{\varepsilon}\to\mathbf{Y}^{1}_{\varepsilon}
is injective, so we need only establish surjectivity here. Let
\upsilon=(\varphi^{1},\varphi^{2},\psi^{1},\psi^{2})\in\mathbf{Y}^{1}_{\varepsilon}
and define
T_{\upsilon}\in(H^{1}_{\mathrm{tan}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{2}))^{\ast}
as in (
3.2.51
). By the theory of weak solutions (specifically, the third item of Proposition
3.7
) we are guaranteed the existence of
(u,p)\in\mathbf{X}^{0}_{\varepsilon}
such that
J^{\varepsilon}_{1}(u,p)=(T_{\upsilon},\varphi^{2})
. It suffices to check that
(u,p)
belongs to the higher regularity space
\mathbf{X}^{1}_{\varepsilon}
, as this permits an integration by parts to learn that
L^{\varepsilon}_{1}(u,p)=\upsilon
. There are many ways to achieve this regularity promotion for
(u,p)
. One possibility is to argue by standard elliptic regularity theory for systems (see, for instance, Agmon, Douglis, and Nirenberg
[
2
]
). An alternate approach is to observe that when
h=1
we have that
\mathcal{A}_{h}=I
, and so the system (
3.2.1
) commutes with horizontal translations. This then allows for a tangential to normal regularity promotion argument of the same form used in the proof of Theorem 2.5 in Stevenson and Tice
[
80
]
. Taking one of these two routes, we obtain the regularity promotion and thereby complete the proof that
L^{\varepsilon}_{1}
is an isomorphism.

It remains to establish the Lipschitz estimate of (
3.2.73
). Let
\upsilon\in\mathbf{Y}^{1}_{\varepsilon}
and
h,\widetilde{h}\in Z_{\delta}
. Set
\chi=(L^{\varepsilon}_{h})^{-1}\upsilon-(L^{\varepsilon}_{\widetilde{h}})^{-1}\upsilon\in\mathbf{X}^{1}_{\varepsilon}
. Starting with the identity
L^{\varepsilon}_{h}\chi=(L^{\varepsilon}_{\widetilde{h}}-L^{\varepsilon}_{h})(L^{\varepsilon}_{\widetilde{h}})^{-1}\upsilon
we use the estimates (
3.2.24
) and (
3.2.25
) to get

(3.2.74)

\lVert\chi\rVert_{\mathbf{X}^{1}_{\varepsilon}}^{(+)}\lesssim\lVert h-\widetilde{h}\rVert_{\mathbf{Z}}\lVert(L^{\varepsilon}_{\widetilde{h}})^{-1}\upsilon\rVert_{\mathbf{X}^{1}_{\varepsilon}}^{(+)}\lesssim\lVert h-\widetilde{h}\rVert_{\mathbf{Z}}\lVert\upsilon\rVert_{\mathbf{Y}^{1}_{\varepsilon}},

where the implicit constants depend only on
\delta
,
\upmu
, and
\mathrm{a}
.
The right hand estimate (
3.2.73
) follows.
∎

4.
The shallow water and residual Stokes equations

In comparison to what we have explored so far, this section is heavily computational in nature. We aim to quantify a relationship between the free boundary Navier-Stokes system (
1.4.8
), the shallow water equations (
2.1.1
), and the linear Stokes equations (
3.2.1
), specifically when we are working around the distinguished shallow water heteroclinic orbit solutions produced by Theorem
2.5
. This is a lengthy, but essential task.

4.1.
Ansatz and derivation

Recall that in Sections
1.5
and
2.1
we made the claim that the shallow water ODEs (
2.1.1
) are, in a sense, the limit of the free boundary Navier-Stokes system (
1.4.8
) as the parameter
\varepsilon>0
tends to zero. The goal of this subsection is to begin making this claim more precise by carrying out a plethora of important calculations. We produce an ansatz for solutions to the Navier-Stokes system (
1.4.8
) which is the sum of a main part that is intimately related to the shallow water ODEs (
2.1.1
) and a small residual. We then calculate the Stokes-type equations satisfied by the residual, given that the main part actually solves a perturbation of the shallow water equations.

Theorem 4.1

(The shallow water and residual Stokes equations)
.

Let
\varepsilon,\mathrm{A}\in(0,1)
,
\upmu,\mathrm{a}>0
,
\mathrm{g},\upsigma\geqslant 0
, and let
\overline{\upgamma},\overline{\mathrm{A}}\in\mathbb{R}
be defined via (
1.4.5
). Consider the following six assertions.

(1)

The functions

(4.1.1)

H,U,U_{1},U_{2},P,P_{1},P_{2}\in W^{\infty,\infty}(\mathbb{R}),\quad(\eta,u,p)\in H^{5/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})\times H^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon};\mathbb{R}^{2})\times H^{1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon}),\text{ and }\mathfrak{r}_{1},\mathfrak{r}_{3},\mathfrak{r}_{4}\in H^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}),

are given and satisfy the bound
\inf(H+\varepsilon^{2}\eta)>0
.

(2)

The triple
(\boldsymbol{\zeta},\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{p})\in H^{5/2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R})\times H^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega_{\varepsilon};\mathbb{R}^{2})\times H^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})
is defined via

(4.1.2)

\boldsymbol{\zeta}=H+\varepsilon^{2}\eta,\quad\boldsymbol{u}=X+\varepsilon^{2}u,\text{ and }\boldsymbol{p}=P+\varepsilon^{2}Q+\varepsilon^{2}p,

where
X,V,W\in H^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega_{\varepsilon};\mathbb{R}^{2})
and
Q\in H^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})
are determined via (writing
y
for the vertical variable in
\Omega_{\varepsilon}
)

(4.1.3)

X=V+\varepsilon^{2}W,\quad V=Ue_{1}-y\boldsymbol{\zeta}U^{\prime}e_{2},\quad W=\Big(\frac{y\boldsymbol{\zeta}}{\varepsilon}U_{1}+\frac{1}{2}\Big(\frac{y\boldsymbol{\zeta}}{\varepsilon}\Big)^{2}U_{2}\Big)e_{1}-\varepsilon\Big(\frac{1}{2}\Big(\frac{y\boldsymbol{\zeta}}{\varepsilon}\Big)^{2}U_{1}^{\prime}+\frac{1}{6}\Big(\frac{y\boldsymbol{\zeta}}{\varepsilon}\Big)^{3}U_{2}^{\prime}\Big)e_{2},

and

(4.1.4)

Q=\frac{y\boldsymbol{\zeta}}{\varepsilon}P_{1}+\frac{1}{2}\Big(\frac{y\boldsymbol{\zeta}}{\varepsilon}\Big)^{2}P_{2}.

(3)

The adversarial contribution
\mathfrak{L}\eta\in H^{3/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})
is defined via

(4.1.5)

\mathfrak{L}\eta=(P-\mathrm{g}H-2\upmu U^{\prime})\eta^{\prime}+\upmu\eta(U_{2}-U^{\prime\prime})-\mathrm{g}\eta H^{\prime}.

(4)

The residual forcing terms
\mathfrak{f}_{1},\mathfrak{f}_{2}\in L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})
and
\mathfrak{f}_{0},\mathfrak{f}_{3},\mathfrak{f}_{4}\in H^{1/2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})
are defined via

(4.1.6)

\mathfrak{f}_{0}=(1-\partial_{1}^{2})(\overline{\upgamma}H-\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{2}U_{1}/2-\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{3}U_{2}/6-\overline{\mathrm{A}}),

(4.1.7)

\mathfrak{f}_{1}=-\overline{\upgamma}U^{\prime}+(V-(4+\varepsilon^{2}\overline{\upgamma})e_{1})\cdot\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}W_{1}+W\cdot\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}V_{1}+\varepsilon^{2}W\cdot\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}W_{1}+\varepsilon^{2}u\cdot\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}u_{1}+\partial_{1}^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}Q-\upmu(\partial_{1}^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}})^{2}W_{1},

(4.1.8)

\mathfrak{f}_{2}=\overline{\upgamma}y\boldsymbol{\zeta}U^{\prime\prime}+(V-(4+\varepsilon^{2}\overline{\upgamma})e_{1})\cdot\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}W_{2}+W\cdot\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}V_{2}+\varepsilon^{2}W\cdot\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}W_{2}+\varepsilon^{2}u\cdot\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}u_{2}-\upmu(\partial_{1}^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}})^{2}W_{2},

(4.1.9)

\mathfrak{f}_{3}=\varepsilon Q\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{\prime}-2\varepsilon\upmu\partial_{1}^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}W_{1}\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{\prime}+\upmu\partial_{1}^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}W_{2}+\upsigma\mathcal{H}(\varepsilon\boldsymbol{\zeta})\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{\prime}-\varepsilon^{3}\mathrm{g}\eta\eta^{\prime},

and

(4.1.10)

\mathfrak{f}_{4}=-Q+\upmu\boldsymbol{\zeta}\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{\prime}U^{\prime\prime}+\upmu\mathbb{D}^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}W\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon\boldsymbol{\zeta}}\cdot e_{2}-\varepsilon^{-1}\upsigma\mathcal{H}(\varepsilon\boldsymbol{\zeta}).

(5)

The following system of ODEs is satisfied in
\mathbb{R}
:

(4.1.11)

(4-U)H-\mathrm{A}=0,\quad(U-4)U^{\prime}+P^{\prime}-\upmu U^{\prime\prime}-\upmu U_{2}-4\mathrm{a}+\varepsilon^{2}\mathfrak{r}_{1}=0,

(4.1.12)

P_{1}+\upmu U_{1}^{\prime}=0,\quad P_{2}+(4-U)U^{\prime\prime}+(U^{\prime})^{2}+\upmu U^{\prime\prime\prime}+\upmu U_{2}^{\prime}=0,\quad\upmu U_{1}-\mathrm{a}U=0,

(4.1.13)

(P-\mathrm{g}H-2\upmu U^{\prime})H^{\prime}+\upmu U_{1}+\upmu H(U_{2}-U^{\prime\prime})+\varepsilon\mathfrak{r}_{3}=0,\quad-P-2\upmu U^{\prime}+\mathrm{g}H+\varepsilon^{2}\mathfrak{r}_{4}=0.

(6)

The following system of PDEs is satisfied:

(4.1.14)

\begin{cases}(X-(4+\varepsilon^{2}\overline{\upgamma})e_{1})\cdot\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}u+u\cdot\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}X+\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}p-\upmu\Delta^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}u+(\mathfrak{f}_{1}-\mathfrak{r}_{1},\mathfrak{f}_{2})=0,\quad\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}\cdot u=0&\text{in }\Omega_{\varepsilon},\\
-(p-\upmu\mathbb{D}^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}u)\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon\boldsymbol{\zeta}}+(\varepsilon\mathfrak{L}\eta,\mathrm{g}\eta)+(\mathfrak{f}_{3}-\mathfrak{r}_{3},\mathfrak{f}_{4}-\mathfrak{r}_{4})=0&\text{on }\Sigma_{\varepsilon},\\
u_{2}=0,\quad\upmu\partial_{2}^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}u_{1}-\varepsilon\mathrm{a}u_{1}=0&\text{on }\Sigma_{0},\\
(1-\partial_{1}^{2})(((4+\varepsilon^{2}\overline{\upgamma})-U)\eta)-\frac{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{\varepsilon}u_{1}(\cdot,y)\;\mathrm{d}y-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\partial_{1}(u\cdot\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon\boldsymbol{\zeta}})+\mathfrak{f}_{0}=0&\text{on }\Sigma_{\varepsilon}.\end{cases}

If all six assertions are satisfied, then the triple
(\boldsymbol{\zeta},\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{p})
of (
4.1.2
) is a solution to the free boundary Navier-Stokes system (
1.4.8
). Conversely, if the first through fifth assertions are satisfied and the triple
(\boldsymbol{\zeta},\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{p})
of (
4.1.2
) is a solution to (
1.4.8
), then the sixth item is satisfied by the residuals
(\eta,u,p)
.

The proof of this theorem is given at the end of this subsection. In essence, the proof consists of a large number of computations, which we have recorded below in more self-contained pieces. We emphasize that the role of the functional parameters
\mathfrak{r}_{1}
,
\mathfrak{r}_{3}
, and
\mathfrak{r}_{4}
, which do not explicitly appear in (
4.1.2
), (
4.1.3
), or (
4.1.4
), is to specify a coupling between the shallow water ODE part and the Stokes PDE residual part. Moreover, we do not need a functional parameter in the second momentum equation, which explains the lack of
\mathfrak{r}_{2}
above. The precise choice of these functions is not made until Section
4.2
.

We now embark on the computation of the residuals, handling each component of the system of equations (
1.4.8
) separately. We begin with the conservation of relative velocity flux equation.

Proposition 4.2

(Conservation of relative velocity flux’s residuals)
.

Assuming the first and second hypotheses of Theorem
4.1
, the following are equivalent.

(1)

We have the following equality on
\Sigma_{\varepsilon}
:

(4.1.15)

\Big(4+\varepsilon^{2}\overline{\upgamma}-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{1}(\cdot,y)\;\mathrm{d}y\Big)\boldsymbol{\zeta}-(4+\varepsilon^{2}\overline{\upgamma})\partial_{1}^{2}\boldsymbol{\zeta}-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\partial_{1}(\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon\boldsymbol{\zeta}})=\mathrm{A}+\varepsilon^{2}\overline{\mathrm{A}}.

(2)

We have the following equality on
\Sigma_{\varepsilon}
:

(4.1.16)

(1-\partial_{1}^{2})(((4+\varepsilon^{2}\overline{\upgamma})-U)\eta)-\frac{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{\varepsilon}u_{1}(\cdot,y)\;\mathrm{d}y-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\partial_{1}(u\cdot\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon\boldsymbol{\zeta}})+\mathfrak{f}_{0}+\varepsilon^{-2}\mathfrak{S}_{0}=0,

where

(4.1.17)

\mathfrak{S}_{0}=(1-\partial_{1}^{2})((4-U)H-\mathrm{A})

and
\mathfrak{f}_{0}
is given by (
4.1.6
).

Proof.

The strategy is simply to plug the ansatz (
4.1.2
) into the final equation of (
1.4.8
), divide by
\varepsilon^{2}
, and sort the resulting terms. We first compute the residual vertical average of
\boldsymbol{u}_{1}
as

(4.1.18)

\varepsilon^{-3}\int_{0}^{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{1}(\cdot,y)\;\mathrm{d}y=\varepsilon^{-2}U+\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{\zeta}U_{1}+\frac{1}{6}\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{2}U_{2}+\varepsilon^{-1}\int_{0}^{\varepsilon}u_{1}(\cdot,y)\;\mathrm{d}y.

In turn, we find that

(4.1.19)

\varepsilon^{-2}\Big(\Big(4+\varepsilon^{2}\overline{\upgamma}-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{u}_{1}(\cdot,y)\;\mathrm{d}y\Big)\boldsymbol{\zeta}-(\mathrm{A}+\varepsilon^{2}\overline{\mathrm{A}})\Big)=\varepsilon^{-2}((4-U)H-\mathrm{A})\\
+\Big(\overline{\upgamma}H-\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{2}U_{1}-\frac{1}{6}\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{3}U_{2}-\overline{\mathrm{A}}\Big)+(4+\varepsilon^{2}\overline{\upgamma}-U)\eta-\frac{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{\varepsilon}u_{1}(\cdot,y)\;\mathrm{d}y.

On the other hand, we compute

(4.1.20)

-\varepsilon^{-3}\partial_{1}(\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon\boldsymbol{\zeta}})=\varepsilon^{-2}(UH)^{\prime\prime}+\Big(\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{2}U_{1}+\frac{1}{6}\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{3}U_{2}\Big)^{\prime\prime}+(U\eta)^{\prime\prime}-\varepsilon^{-1}\partial_{1}(u\cdot\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon\boldsymbol{\zeta}})

and

(4.1.21)

-\varepsilon^{-2}(4+\varepsilon^{2}\overline{\upgamma})\partial_{1}^{2}\boldsymbol{\zeta}=-\varepsilon^{-2}4H^{\prime\prime}-\overline{\upgamma}H^{\prime\prime}-(4+\varepsilon^{2}\overline{\upgamma})\eta^{\prime\prime}.

The shallow water contribution (
4.1.17
) is the sum of the first terms of the right hand sides of (
4.1.19
), (
4.1.20
), and (
4.1.21
). The residual contribution (
4.1.6
) is the sum of the second grouping of terms. The remaining terms then belong to the residual conservation of relative momentum flux (
4.1.16
).
∎

Our next two results handle the momentum equation’s components.

Proposition 4.3

(First momentum equation’s residuals)
.

Assuming the first and second hypotheses of Theorem
4.1
, the following are equivalent.

(1)

We have the following equality in
\Omega_{\varepsilon}
:

(4.1.22)

(\boldsymbol{u}-(4+\varepsilon^{2}\overline{\upgamma})e_{1})\cdot\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}\boldsymbol{u}_{1}+\partial_{1}^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}\boldsymbol{p}-\upmu\Delta^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}\boldsymbol{u}_{1}=4\mathrm{a}.

(2)

We have the following equality in
\Omega_{\varepsilon}
:

(4.1.23)

(X-(4+\varepsilon^{2}\overline{\upgamma})e_{1})\cdot\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}u_{1}+u\cdot\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}X_{1}+\partial_{1}^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}p-\upmu\Delta^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}u_{1}+\mathfrak{f}_{1}-\mathfrak{r}_{1}+\varepsilon^{-2}\mathfrak{S}_{1}=0

where

(4.1.24)

\mathfrak{S}_{1}=(U-4)U^{\prime}+P^{\prime}-\upmu U^{\prime\prime}-\upmu U_{2}-4\mathrm{a}+\varepsilon^{2}\mathfrak{r}_{1}

and
\mathfrak{f}_{1}
is given by (
4.1.7
).

Proof.

Again, the strategy is to plug the ansatz (
4.1.2
) into the first equation of system (
1.4.8
), divide by
\varepsilon^{2}
, and sort the resulting terms. We begin with the advective derivative, calculating that

(4.1.25)

\varepsilon^{-2}(\boldsymbol{u}-(4+\varepsilon^{2}\overline{\upgamma})e_{1})\cdot\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}\boldsymbol{u}_{1}=\varepsilon^{-2}(X-(4+\varepsilon^{2}\overline{\upgamma})e_{1})\cdot\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}X_{1}\\
+(X-(4+\varepsilon^{2}\overline{\upgamma})e_{1})\cdot\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}u_{1}+u\cdot\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}X_{1}+\varepsilon^{2}u\cdot\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}u_{1}.

The second and third terms are the residual advective derivative in (
4.1.23
) while the final term is sorted to (
4.1.7
). The first term requires further dissection:

(4.1.26)

\varepsilon^{-2}(X-(4+\varepsilon^{2}\overline{\upgamma})e_{1})\cdot\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}X_{1}=\varepsilon^{-2}(U-4)U^{\prime}-\overline{\upgamma}U^{\prime}\\
+(V-(4+\varepsilon^{2}\overline{\upgamma})e_{1})\cdot\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}W_{1}+W\cdot\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}V_{1}+\varepsilon^{2}W\cdot\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}W_{1}.

The first term is then a shallow water (
4.1.24
) contribution, while what remains are residual forcing (
4.1.7
).

For the pressure’s contribution we calculate

(4.1.27)

\varepsilon^{-2}\partial_{1}^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}\boldsymbol{p}=\varepsilon^{-2}P^{\prime}+\partial_{1}^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}p+\partial_{1}^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}Q.

The first grouping of terms belongs to the category (
4.1.24
). The second term is the residual pressure in (
4.1.23
). The final term is sorted to (
4.1.7
).

For the dissipation’s contribution, we compute

(4.1.28)

\varepsilon^{-2}\Delta^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}\boldsymbol{u}_{1}=\varepsilon^{-2}(U^{\prime\prime}+U_{2})+\Delta^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}u_{1}+(\partial_{1}^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}})^{2}W_{1}.

The first grouping of terms belongs to the category (
4.1.24
). The second term is the residual dissipation in (
4.1.23
). The final term is sorted to (
4.1.7
).
∎

Next, we handle the second component.

Proposition 4.4

(Second momentum equation’s residuals)
.

Assuming the first and second hypotheses of Theorem
4.1
, the following are equivalent.

(1)

We have the following equality in
\Omega_{\varepsilon}
:

(4.1.29)

(\boldsymbol{u}-(4+\varepsilon^{2}\overline{\upgamma})e_{1})\cdot\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}\boldsymbol{u}_{2}+\partial_{2}^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}\boldsymbol{p}-\upmu\Delta^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}\boldsymbol{u}_{2}=0.

(2)

We have the following equality in
\Omega_{\varepsilon}
:

(4.1.30)

(X-(4+\varepsilon^{2}\overline{\upgamma})e_{1})\cdot\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}u_{2}+u\cdot\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}X_{2}+\partial_{2}^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}p-\upmu\Delta^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}u_{2}+\mathfrak{f}_{2}+\varepsilon^{-2}\mathfrak{S}_{2}=0,

where

(4.1.31)

\mathfrak{S}_{2}=\varepsilon(P_{1}+\upmu U_{1}^{\prime})+y\boldsymbol{\zeta}(P_{2}+(4-U)U^{\prime\prime}+(U^{\prime})^{2}+\upmu U^{\prime\prime\prime}+\upmu U_{2}^{\prime})

and
\mathfrak{f}_{2}
is given by (
4.1.8
).

Proof.

The strategy is the same as that of Proposition
4.3
. We begin by considering the advective derivative.

(4.1.32)

\varepsilon^{-2}(\boldsymbol{u}-(4+\varepsilon^{2}\overline{\upgamma})e_{1})\cdot\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}\boldsymbol{u}_{2}=\varepsilon^{-2}(X-(4+\varepsilon^{2}\overline{\upgamma})e_{1})\cdot\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}X_{2}\\
+(X-(4+\varepsilon^{2}\overline{\upgamma})e_{1})\cdot\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}u_{2}+u\cdot\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}X_{2}+\varepsilon^{2}u\cdot\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}u_{2}.

The right hand side’s second and third terms are sorted to (
4.1.30
) and the final term belongs to (
4.1.8
). The first term we now expand further

(4.1.33)

\varepsilon^{-2}(X-(4+\varepsilon^{2}\overline{\upgamma})e_{1})\cdot\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}X_{2}=\varepsilon^{-2}y\boldsymbol{\zeta}((4+\varepsilon^{2}\overline{\upgamma}-U)U^{\prime\prime}+(U^{\prime})^{2})\\
+(V-(4+\varepsilon^{2}\overline{\upgamma})e_{1})\cdot\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}W_{2}+W\cdot\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}V_{2}+\varepsilon^{2}W\cdot\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}W_{2}.

The term
\overline{\upgamma}y\boldsymbol{\zeta}U^{\prime\prime}
along with the final three terms are residual forcing (
4.1.8
) while the rest of the first term belongs to (
4.1.31
).

For the pressure’s contribution, we calculate

(4.1.34)

\varepsilon^{-2}\partial_{2}^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}\boldsymbol{p}=\varepsilon^{-2}(\varepsilon P_{1}+y\boldsymbol{\zeta}P_{2})+\partial_{2}^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}p

and sort the first term to (
4.1.31
) and the second term to (
4.1.30
).

Finally, we consider the dissipation’s contribution by computing that

(4.1.35)

\varepsilon^{-2}\Delta^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}\boldsymbol{u}_{2}=-\varepsilon^{-2}(\varepsilon U_{1}^{\prime}+y\boldsymbol{\zeta}(U^{\prime\prime\prime}+U_{2}^{\prime}))+(\partial_{1}^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}})^{2}W_{2}+\Delta^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}u_{2}.

We sort the first term to (
4.1.31
), the second to (
4.1.8
), and the third to (
4.1.30
).
∎

The structure of the ansatz leads to a pleasantly simple divergence equation on remainders.

Proposition 4.5

(Incompressibility constraint’s residuals)
.

Assuming the first and second hypotheses of Theorem
4.1
, the following are equivalent

(1)

We have the following equality in
\Omega_{\varepsilon}
:

(4.1.36)

\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}\cdot\boldsymbol{u}=0.

(2)

We have the following equality in
\Omega_{\varepsilon}
:

(4.1.37)

\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}\cdot u=0.

Proof.

By construction
\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}\cdot X=0
and
\boldsymbol{u}=X+\varepsilon^{2}u
.
∎

Next, we take a look at the
e_{1}
-dynamic boundary condition in (
1.4.8
). In contrast with the previous computations, we now encounter a new object category for the residual equation. This is the adversarial linear operator which appears in equation (
4.1.5
).

Proposition 4.6

(First dynamic boundary condition’s residuals)
.

Assuming the first and second hypotheses of Theorem
4.1
, the following are equivalent.

(1)

We have the following equality on
\Sigma_{\varepsilon}
:

(4.1.38)

-(\boldsymbol{p}-\upmu\mathbb{D}^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}\boldsymbol{u})\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon\boldsymbol{\zeta}}\cdot e_{1}+(\mathrm{g}\boldsymbol{\zeta}-\varepsilon\upsigma\mathcal{H}(\varepsilon\boldsymbol{\zeta}))\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon\boldsymbol{\zeta}}\cdot e_{1}=0.

(2)

We have the following equality on
\Sigma_{\varepsilon}
:

(4.1.39)

-(p-\upmu\mathbb{D}^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}u)\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon\boldsymbol{\zeta}}\cdot e_{1}+\varepsilon\mathfrak{L}\eta+\mathfrak{f}_{3}-\mathfrak{r}_{3}+\varepsilon^{-1}\mathfrak{S}_{3}=0,

where

(4.1.40)

\mathfrak{S}_{3}=(P-\mathrm{g}H-2\upmu U^{\prime})H^{\prime}+\upmu U_{1}+\upmu H(U_{2}-U^{\prime\prime})+\varepsilon\mathfrak{r}_{3},

\mathfrak{L}\eta
is given by (
4.1.5
), and
\mathfrak{f}_{3}
is given by (
4.1.9
).

Proof.

Again the strategy is similar to that of Proposition
4.3
. First we compute that the pressure satisfies

(4.1.41)

-\varepsilon^{-2}\boldsymbol{p}\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon\boldsymbol{\zeta}}\cdot e_{1}=\varepsilon^{-1}P\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{\prime}-(Q+p)\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon\boldsymbol{\zeta}}\cdot e_{1}.

The
Q
term above belongs to (
4.1.9
), while the
p
term is seen in (
4.1.39
).

We also compute that the viscous stress satisfies

(4.1.42)
