\varepsilon^{-2}\mathbb{D}^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}\boldsymbol{u}\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon\boldsymbol{\zeta}}\cdot e_{1}=-\varepsilon^{-1}2\partial_{1}^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}X_{1}\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{\prime}+\varepsilon^{-2}(\partial_{2}^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}X_{1}+\partial_{1}^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}X_{2})+\mathbb{D}^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}u\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon\boldsymbol{\zeta}}\cdot e_{1}\\
=\varepsilon^{-1}(-2U^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{\prime}+U_{1}+\boldsymbol{\zeta}U_{2}-\boldsymbol{\zeta}U^{\prime\prime})-2\varepsilon\partial_{1}^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}W_{1}\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{\prime}+\partial_{1}^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}W_{2}+\mathbb{D}^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}u\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon\boldsymbol{\zeta}}\cdot e_{1}.

The right hand side’s second and third terms are sorted to (
4.1.9
), and the final term is the residual viscous stress in (
4.1.39
).

Now we consider the gravity-capillary operator:

(4.1.43)

\varepsilon^{-2}(\mathrm{g}\boldsymbol{\zeta}-\varepsilon\upsigma\mathcal{H}(\varepsilon\boldsymbol{\zeta}))\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon\boldsymbol{\zeta}}\cdot e_{1}=-\varepsilon^{-1}\mathrm{g}\boldsymbol{\zeta}\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{\prime}+\upsigma\mathcal{H}(\varepsilon\boldsymbol{\zeta})\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{\prime}.

The final term above is also a residual forcing, and so is given to (
4.1.9
).

It remains to examine the right hand side contributions in (
4.1.41
), (
4.1.42
), and (
4.1.43
) which have a leading coefficient of
\varepsilon^{-1}
. We sum these and expand
\boldsymbol{\zeta}=H+\varepsilon^{2}\eta
to get

(4.1.44)

\varepsilon^{-1}((P-\mathrm{g}\boldsymbol{\zeta}-2\upmu U^{\prime})\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{\prime}+\upmu U_{1}+\upmu\boldsymbol{\zeta}(U_{2}-U^{\prime\prime}))=\varepsilon^{-1}((P-\mathrm{g}H-2\upmu U^{\prime})H^{\prime}+\upmu U_{1}+\upmu H(U_{2}-U^{\prime\prime}))\\
+\varepsilon((P-2\upmu U^{\prime})\eta^{\prime}+\upmu\eta(U_{2}-U^{\prime\prime})-\mathrm{g}H\eta^{\prime}-\mathrm{g}\eta H^{\prime}-\varepsilon^{2}\mathrm{g}\eta\eta^{\prime}).

The first grouping of terms is the shallow water contribution in (
4.1.40
). The next group is the sum of the adversarial linear operator (
4.1.5
) with a forcing term belonging to (
4.1.9
).
∎

Now we consider the
e_{2}
-dynamic boundary condition.

Proposition 4.7

(Second dynamic boundary condition’s residuals)
.

Assuming the first and second hypotheses of Theorem
4.1
, the following are equivalent.

(1)

We have the following equality on
\Sigma_{\varepsilon}
:

(4.1.45)

-(\boldsymbol{p}-\upmu\mathbb{D}^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}\boldsymbol{u})\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon\boldsymbol{\zeta}}\cdot e_{2}+(\mathrm{g}\boldsymbol{\zeta}-\varepsilon\upsigma\mathcal{H}(\varepsilon\boldsymbol{\zeta}))\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon\boldsymbol{\zeta}}\cdot e_{2}=0.

(2)

We have the following equality on
\Sigma_{\varepsilon}
:

(4.1.46)

-(p-\upmu\mathbb{D}^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}u)\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon\boldsymbol{\zeta}}\cdot e_{2}+\mathrm{g}\eta+\mathfrak{f}_{4}-\mathfrak{r}_{4}+\varepsilon^{-2}\mathfrak{S}_{4}=0,

where

(4.1.47)

\mathfrak{S}_{4}=-P-2\upmu U^{\prime}+\mathrm{g}H+\varepsilon^{2}\mathfrak{r}_{4}

and
\mathfrak{f}_{4}
is given by (
4.1.10
).

Proof.

For the pressure contribution, we compute that

(4.1.48)

-\varepsilon^{-2}\boldsymbol{p}\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon\boldsymbol{\zeta}}\cdot e_{2}=-\varepsilon^{-2}P-Q-p

and sort the first, second, and third terms into (
4.1.47
), (
4.1.10
), and (
4.1.46
), respectively.

For the viscous stress contribution, we compute that

(4.1.49)

\varepsilon^{-2}\mathbb{D}^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}\boldsymbol{u}\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon\boldsymbol{\zeta}}\cdot e_{2}=-\varepsilon^{-2}2U^{\prime}+\boldsymbol{\zeta}\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{\prime}U^{\prime\prime}+\mathbb{D}^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}W\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon\boldsymbol{\zeta}}\cdot e_{2}+\mathbb{D}^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}u\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon\boldsymbol{\zeta}}\cdot e_{2}.

The first term is of the shallow water category (
4.1.47
). The second and third terms are residual forcing (
4.1.10
). The final term is the residual viscous stress appearing in (
4.1.46
).

Finally, for the gravity-capillary operator we find that

(4.1.50)

\varepsilon^{-2}(\mathrm{g}\boldsymbol{\zeta}-\varepsilon\upsigma\mathcal{H}(\varepsilon\boldsymbol{\zeta}))\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon\boldsymbol{\zeta}}\cdot e_{2}=\varepsilon^{-2}\mathrm{g}H+\mathrm{g}\eta-\varepsilon^{-1}\upsigma\mathcal{H}(\varepsilon\boldsymbol{\zeta})

and sort the right hand side’s first term to (
4.1.47
), second term to (
4.1.46
), and final term to (
4.1.10
).
∎

Finally we handle the residuals for the Navier slip boundary condition.

Proposition 4.8

(Navier slip residuals)
.

Assuming the first and second hypotheses of Theorem
4.1
, the following are equivalent.

(1)

We have the following equalities on
\Sigma_{0}
:

(4.1.51)

\boldsymbol{u}_{2}=0\text{ and }\upmu\partial_{2}^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}\boldsymbol{u}_{1}-\varepsilon\mathrm{a}\boldsymbol{u}_{1}=0.

(2)

We have the following equalities on
\Sigma_{0}
:

(4.1.52)

u_{2}=0\text{ and }\upmu\partial_{2}^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}u_{1}-\varepsilon\mathrm{a}u_{1}+\varepsilon^{-1}\mathfrak{S}_{5}=0

where

(4.1.53)

\mathfrak{S}_{5}=\upmu U_{1}-\mathrm{a}U.

Proof.

Since
\boldsymbol{u}=X+\varepsilon^{2}u
and by construction
X\cdot e_{2}=0
on
\Sigma_{0}
, we find that
\boldsymbol{u}_{2}=0
if and only if
u_{2}=0
on
\Sigma_{0}
. Next, we simply compute that

(4.1.54)

\varepsilon^{-2}(\upmu\partial_{2}^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}\boldsymbol{u}_{1}-\varepsilon\mathrm{a}\boldsymbol{u}_{1})=\varepsilon^{-1}(\upmu U_{1}-\mathrm{a}U)+\upmu\partial_{2}^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}u_{1}-\varepsilon\mathrm{a}u_{1}

and sort the first grouping of terms to (
4.1.53
) and the remaining terms to (
4.1.52
).
∎

We now synthesize the calculations of Propositions
4.2
–
4.8
in order to prove Theorem
4.1
.

Proof of Theorem
4.1
.

If the ODEs identities (
4.1.11
), (
4.1.12
), and (
4.1.13
) hold, then we have
\mathfrak{S}_{0}=\mathfrak{S}_{1}=\dots=\mathfrak{S}_{5}=0
, where these are the shallow water contributions defined in (
4.1.17
), (
4.1.24
), (
4.1.31
), (
4.1.40
), (
4.1.47
), and (
4.1.53
). Thus, the satisfaction of
\boldsymbol{\zeta}>0
and (
4.1.14
) along with the sufficient directions of the previous family of propositions yield the first result, which establishes that
(\boldsymbol{\zeta},\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{\eta})
solve (
1.4.8
). On the other hand, the necessary directions of these proposition yields the converse statement.
∎

4.2.
The shallow water bore map

The free functional parameters
\mathfrak{r}_{1}
,
\mathfrak{r}_{3}
, and
\mathfrak{r}_{4}
of Theorem
4.1
determine how the ODEs (
4.1.11
), (
4.1.12
), and (
4.1.13
) are coupled to the PDEs (
4.1.14
). The simple choice
\mathfrak{r}_{1}=\mathfrak{r}_{3}=\mathfrak{r}_{4}=0
, which corresponds to a complete decoupling, would allow us to first solve a closed system for
H
,
U
,
U_{1}
,
U_{2}
,
P
,
P_{1}
, and
P_{2}
and then use these as coefficients and data terms in the residual PDEs for
\eta
,
u
, and
p
. Unfortunately, with the complete decoupling strategy the solvability of the resulting PDE for the residuals is far from clear. The issue is the presence of adversarial linear terms in (
4.1.14
), namely the terms
(\varepsilon\mathfrak{L}\eta,\mathrm{g}\eta)
appearing in the residual dynamic boundary condition and
u\cdot\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}X_{1}
appearing in the
e_{1}
-residual momentum equation, which perturb us away from the Stokes system studied in Section
3.2
. Our strategy, therefore, is to judiciously select a mechanism of coupling
\mathfrak{r}_{1}
,
\mathfrak{r}_{3}
, and
\mathfrak{r}_{4}
which pacifies these linear enemies.

The choice of coupling made here adds small perturbations (having coefficient
\varepsilon
) to the ODEs (
4.1.11
), (
4.1.12
), and (
4.1.13
) that are functions of the residual variables
\eta
,
u
, and
p
as well as the shallow water variables
H
,
U
,
U_{1}
,
U_{2}
,
P
,
P_{1}
, and
P_{2}
. When
\varepsilon=0
things are again decoupled and the ODEs reduce to the one-dimensional viscous shallow water equations with laminar drag, for which bore wave solutions are found in Section
2.1
. Then, when
\varepsilon>0
, we can solve the perturbed shallow water ODEs with the theory of Section
2.2
and produce a
bore map
, which solves the family of perturbed equations, producing bore profiles as a function of the residual variables.

Our first result specifies the form of the perturbations and derives an equivalent form of the ODEs. In what follows we encounter, for
\Xi\in(1,\infty)
, the Fourier multiplication operator
\uppi_{\Xi}=\upchi(D/\Xi)
with
\upchi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(B(0,2))
a fixed, even cut-off satisfying
0\leqslant\upchi\leqslant 1
and
\upchi=1
on
B(0,1)
. We also encounter a new variable
w\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R})
that is meant to be a proxy for the vertical average of the residual
u_{1}
.

Proposition 4.9

(Coupling specification)
.

Let
\upmu,\mathrm{a}>0
,
\mathrm{g}\geqslant 0
, and
\mathrm{A}\in(0,1)
, and define the set
D=\{{z\in\mathbb{R}^{6}\;:\;z_{1}+e^{z_{5}}>0}\}
. Then there exists a smooth function

(4.2.1)

\aleph:D\to\mathbb{R}^{2}\text{ for which }\aleph(0,0,0,0,\cdot,\cdot)=0

such that the following holds. If
\varepsilon,\delta\in(0,1)
,
\Xi\in(1,\infty)
,
H,U,U_{1},U_{2},P,P_{1},P_{2}\in W^{\infty,\infty}(\mathbb{R})
, and
\eta,w\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R})
satisfy
\delta\leqslant H
,
|\varepsilon^{2}\uppi_{\Xi}\eta|\leqslant\delta/2
, and
U\leqslant 4-\delta
, and the functions
\mathfrak{r}_{1},\mathfrak{r}_{3},\mathfrak{r}_{4}\in H^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
are defined via

(4.2.2)

\mathfrak{r}_{1}=((U-4)\uppi_{\Xi}w)^{\prime},\quad\mathfrak{r}_{3}=\varepsilon\mathfrak{L}\uppi_{\Xi}\eta,\text{ and }\mathfrak{r}_{4}=\mathrm{g}\uppi_{\Xi}\eta,

where
\mathfrak{L}
is the linear map defined in (
4.1.5
), then the following are equivalent.

(1)

The system of ODEs (
4.1.11
), (
4.1.12
), and (
4.1.13
) from Theorem
4.1
is satisfied.

(2)

There exists
\rho\in W^{\infty,\infty}(\mathbb{R})
such that
H=e^{\rho}
, the smooth vector field
Y=(\rho,\rho^{\prime}):\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
is a solution to the differential equation

(4.2.3)

Y^{\prime}(t)=\Phi(Y(t))+\Phi_{1}(t,Y(t))

where
\Phi:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
is the smooth vector field defined in (
2.1.2
) and
\Phi_{1}:\mathbb{R}\times[\log\delta,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
is determined by (
4.2.1
) by

(4.2.4)

\Phi_{1}(t,x,y)=\aleph(\varepsilon^{2}\uppi_{\Xi}\eta(t),\varepsilon^{2}\uppi_{\Xi}\eta^{\prime}(t),\varepsilon^{2}\uppi_{\Xi}w(t),\varepsilon^{2}\uppi_{\Xi}w^{\prime}(t),x,y),

and the functions
U
,
P
,
U_{1}
,
U_{2}
,
P_{1}
, and
P_{2}
are determined by
H
and
\eta
via

(4.2.5)

U=4-\mathrm{A}/H,\quad P=\mathrm{g}H-2\upmu U^{\prime}+\varepsilon^{2}\mathrm{g}\uppi_{\Xi}\eta,

(4.2.6)

U_{1}=\frac{\mathrm{a}}{\upmu}U,\quad U_{2}=U^{\prime\prime}-\frac{\upmu U_{1}+(P-\mathrm{g}H-2\upmu U^{\prime})(H^{\prime}+\varepsilon^{2}\uppi_{\Xi}\eta^{\prime})-\mathrm{g}H^{\prime}\varepsilon^{2}\uppi_{\Xi}\eta}{\upmu(H+\varepsilon^{2}\uppi_{\Xi}\eta)},

(4.2.7)

P_{1}=-\upmu U_{1}^{\prime},\quad P_{2}=(U-4)U^{\prime\prime}-(U^{\prime})^{2}-\upmu(U^{\prime\prime\prime}+U_{2}^{\prime}).

Proof.

Throughout the proof we will use the shorthand
\lambda_{1}=\varepsilon^{2}\uppi_{\Xi}\eta
and
\lambda_{2}=\varepsilon^{2}\uppi_{\Xi}w
. Since
\delta\leqslant H
and
|\lambda_{1}|\leqslant\delta/2
, the ODEs (
4.1.11
) are equivalent to the first identity in (
4.2.5
) along with the equation

(4.2.8)

(H+\lambda_{1})((U-4)U^{\prime}+P^{\prime}-\upmu U^{\prime\prime}-\upmu U_{2}-4\mathrm{a}+((U-4)\lambda_{2})^{\prime})=0.

Furthermore (
4.1.12
) is clearly equivalent to
U_{1}=\mathrm{a}U/\upmu
and identities (
4.2.7
). The second identity in (
4.1.13
) is equivalent to the second identity in (
4.2.5
). Finally, after some algebraic manipulation, we find that the first identity of (
4.1.13
) is equivalent to the second identity in (
4.2.6
).

Now we can simplify the second identity in (
4.2.6
) further to get that

(4.2.9)

-\upmu(H+\lambda_{1})(U^{\prime\prime}+U_{2})=\mathrm{a}U-2\upmu(H+\lambda_{1})U^{\prime\prime}-4\upmu(H^{\prime}+\lambda_{1}^{\prime})U^{\prime}+\mathrm{g}\lambda_{1}\lambda_{1}^{\prime}.

Then we substitute (
4.2.9
) along with the expression for
P
from (
4.2.5
) into (
4.2.8
) to arrive at the identity

(4.2.10)

(H+\lambda_{1})(U-4)U^{\prime}+\mathrm{a}U-4\upmu((H+\lambda_{1})U^{\prime})^{\prime}+(H+\lambda_{1})(\mathrm{g}(H^{\prime}+\lambda_{1}^{\prime})-4\mathrm{a})+(H+\lambda_{1})((U-4)\lambda_{2})^{\prime}+\mathrm{g}\lambda_{1}\lambda_{1}^{\prime}=0.

After some computation, the above can be arranged to have the form

(4.2.11)

4\upmu(HU^{\prime})^{\prime}=H(U-4)U^{\prime}+\mathrm{a}U+\mathrm{g}HH^{\prime}-4\mathrm{a}H+\widehat{\aleph}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{1}^{\prime},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{2}^{\prime},H,H^{\prime},U,U^{\prime})

where
\widehat{D}=\{{z\in\mathbb{R}^{8}\;:\;z_{1}+z_{5}>0}\}
and
\widehat{\aleph}:\widehat{D}\to\mathbb{R}
is a smooth function, depending only on the fixed values of
\upmu
,
\mathrm{a}
,
\mathrm{g}
, and
\mathrm{A}
, that satisfies
\widehat{\aleph}(0,0,0,0,\cdot,\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)=0
.

Notice that (
4.2.11
) is a small, nonautonomous perturbation of the shallow water equations (
2.1.1
). We now proceed as in Section
2.1
by writing
U=4-\mathrm{A}/H
and
H=e^{\rho}
to transform (
4.2.11
) into a single second order ODE for
\rho
:

(4.2.12)

\rho^{\prime\prime}=F(\rho)-G(\rho)\rho^{\prime}+(4\upmu\mathrm{A})^{-1}\widehat{\aleph}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{1}^{\prime},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{2}^{\prime},e^{\rho},e^{\rho}\rho^{\prime},4-\mathrm{A}e^{-\rho},\mathrm{A}e^{-\rho}\rho^{\prime}),

with
F
and
G
defined in (
1.5.16
). To complete the proof we set
Y=(\rho,\rho^{\prime}):\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
and define the
\aleph
of (
4.2.1
) via

(4.2.13)

\aleph(z)=(0,(4\upmu\mathrm{A})^{-1}\widehat{\aleph}(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3},z_{4},e^{z_{5}},e^{z_{5}}z_{6},4-\mathrm{A}e^{-z_{5}},\mathrm{A}e^{-z_{5}}z_{6})),

in order deduce that (
4.2.3
) and (
4.2.4
) hold.
∎

We now come to the main result of this subsection, which produces a solution operator to the system of ODEs (
4.1.11
), (
4.1.12
), and (
4.1.13
) when the coupling
\mathfrak{r}_{1}
,
\mathfrak{r}_{3}
, and
\mathfrak{r}_{4}
is determined by (
4.2.2
) of Proposition
4.9
. Recall that the regions
\mathfrak{C}_{\iota}
are defined in Lemma
2.2
and
\mathrm{H}_{\pm}=\mathrm{H}_{\pm}(\mathrm{A})
are defined in (
1.4.4
)

Proposition 4.10

(The bore map, I)
.

Let
\upmu,\mathrm{a}>0
,
\iota\in\{{-1,1}\}
, and
(\mathrm{g},\mathrm{A})\in\mathfrak{C}_{\iota}
. There exist

numbers
\alpha,\beta\in(0,1)
,
M\in(3/\mathrm{H}_{-}(\mathrm{A}),\infty)
, and a closed ball
\overline{B(0,\beta)}\subset[{(C^{10}\cap W^{10,\infty}\cap H^{10})(\mathbb{R})}]^{2}
,

smooth functions
\mathsf{H},\mathsf{U},\mathsf{U}_{1},\mathsf{U}_{2},\mathsf{P}\in W^{\infty,\infty}(\mathbb{R})
and
\mathsf{P}_{1},\mathsf{P}_{2}\in H^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
,

Lipschitz continuous maps

(4.2.14)

\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{u},\mathfrak{u}_{1}:\overline{B(0,\beta)}\to(C^{10}\cap W^{10,\infty}\cap H^{10})(\mathbb{R}),&\mathfrak{u}_{2}:\overline{B(0,\beta)}\to(C^{8}\cap W^{8,\infty}\cap H^{8})(\mathbb{R}),\\
\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{p}_{1}:\overline{B(0,\beta)}\to(C^{9}\cap W^{9,\infty}\cap H^{9})(\mathbb{R}),&\mathfrak{p}_{2}:\overline{B(0,\beta)}\to(C^{7}\cap W^{7,\infty}\cap H^{7})(\mathbb{R}),\end{array}

such that the following hold for all
(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}),(\widetilde{\lambda}_{1},\widetilde{\lambda}_{2})\in\overline{B(0,\beta)}
.

(1)

The pair
(\mathsf{H},\mathsf{U})
is a solution to the shallow water ODEs (
2.1.1
); moreover we have the limits

(4.2.15)

\lim_{t\to\pm\infty}\mathsf{H}(\iota t)=\mathrm{H}_{\pm},\quad\lim_{t\to\pm\infty}\mathsf{U}(\iota t)=4-\frac{\mathrm{A}}{\mathrm{H}_{\pm}},\quad\lim_{t\to\pm\infty}\mathsf{U}_{1}(\iota t)=\frac{\mathrm{a}}{\upmu}\Big(4-\frac{\mathrm{A}}{\mathrm{H}_{\pm}}\Big),\\
\lim_{t\to\pm\infty}\mathsf{U}_{2}(\iota t)=-\frac{\mathrm{a}}{\upmu}\Big(\frac{4}{\mathrm{H}_{\pm}}-\frac{\mathrm{A}}{\mathrm{H}^{2}_{\pm}}\Big),\quad\lim_{t\to\pm\infty}\mathsf{P}(\iota t)=\mathrm{g}\mathrm{H}_{\pm},\quad\lim_{t\to\pm\infty}\mathsf{P}_{1}(\iota t)=0,\text{ and }\lim_{t\to\pm\infty}\mathsf{P}_{2}(\iota t)=0.

(2)

For all
\mathsf{Z}\in\{{\mathsf{H},\mathsf{U},\mathsf{U}_{1},\mathsf{U}_{2},\mathsf{P},\mathsf{P}_{1},\mathsf{P}_{2}}\}
we have the bounds

(4.2.16)

\lVert\mathsf{Z}\rVert_{W^{10,\infty}}\leqslant M,\quad\sup_{t\leqslant 0}e^{\alpha|t|}\char 12\relax{\mathsf{Z}(t)-\lim_{\tau\to-\infty}\mathsf{Z}(\tau)}+\sup_{t\geqslant 0}e^{\alpha|t|}\char 12\relax{\mathsf{Z}(t)-\lim_{\tau\to\infty}\mathsf{Z}(\tau)}\leqslant M,\\
\text{ and }\sup_{1\leqslant m\leqslant 10}\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}e^{\alpha|t|}\char 12\relax{\mathsf{Z}^{(m)}(t)}\leqslant M.

(3)

For all
\mathfrak{Z}\in\{{\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{u},\mathfrak{u}_{1},\mathfrak{u}_{2},\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{p}_{1},\mathfrak{p}_{2}}\}
we have that
\mathfrak{Z}(0,0)=0
,
\lVert\mathfrak{Z}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})\rVert_{W^{7,\infty}\cap H^{7}}\leqslant M
,

(4.2.17)

\lVert\mathfrak{Z}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})-\mathfrak{Z}(\widetilde{\lambda}_{1},\widetilde{\lambda}_{2})\rVert_{W^{7,\infty}\cap H^{7}}\leqslant M\lVert(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})-(\widetilde{\lambda}_{1},\widetilde{\lambda}_{2})\rVert_{W^{10,\infty}\cap H^{10}},

and if
(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})\in[{H^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})}]^{2}
then
\mathfrak{Z}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})\in H^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
.

(4)

We have the bounds

(4.2.18)

\mathrm{H}_{-}\leqslant\inf\mathsf{H},\;\lVert\mathfrak{h}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})}\leqslant\mathrm{H}_{-}/3,\;(2/3)\mathrm{H}_{-}\leqslant\inf(\mathsf{H}+\mathfrak{h}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})),\text{ and }\mathsf{U}+\mathfrak{u}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})\leqslant 4-1/M.

(5)

If for some
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
,
\Xi\in(1,\infty)
, and
(\eta,w)\in[{H^{1}(\mathbb{R})}]^{2}
we have
\lambda_{1}=\varepsilon^{2}\uppi_{\Xi}\eta
and
\lambda_{2}=\varepsilon^{2}\uppi_{\Xi}w
(where
\uppi_{\Xi}
is the mollification operator defined prior to Proposition
4.9
), then the functions

(4.2.19)

H=\mathsf{H}+\mathfrak{h}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}),\quad U=\mathsf{U}+\mathfrak{u}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}),\quad U_{1}=\mathsf{U}_{1}+\mathfrak{u}_{1}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}),\quad U_{2}=\mathsf{U}_{2}+\mathfrak{u}_{2}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}),\\
P=\mathsf{P}+\mathfrak{p}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}),\quad P_{1}=\mathsf{P}_{1}+\mathfrak{p}_{1}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}),\text{ and }P_{2}=\mathsf{P}_{2}+\mathfrak{p}_{2}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})

satisfy the system of ODEs (
4.1.11
), (
4.1.12
), and (
4.1.13
) from Theorem
4.1
, under the coupling specification (
4.2.2
).

Proof.

To begin we define the function
\mathsf{H}\in W^{\infty,\infty}(\mathbb{R})
via
\mathsf{H}=e^{\varrho}
, where
\varrho:\mathbb{R}\to[\uprho_{-},\uprho_{\star}]
is a smooth map provided by Theorem
2.5
, using the first item when
\iota=1
and
G(\uprho_{\star})>0
, and the second item when
\iota=-1
and
G(\uprho_{-})<0
. We emphasize that the theorem does not provide a unique
\varrho
(see Remark
2.6
), so we choose arbitrarily here. Regardless of the choice, when
\iota=1
the solution is a heteroclinic orbit from the hyperbolic equilibrium
\uprho_{-}
to the sink
\uprho_{+}
that obeys the estimates (
2.1.25
), and when
\iota=-1
the solution is a heteroclinic orbit from the source
\uprho_{+}
to the hyperbolic equilibrium
\uprho_{-}
that obeys the estimates (
2.1.26
). These estimates translate to those stated in the second item for
\mathsf{Z}=\mathsf{H}
, provided that
M
is large enough.

For the sake of brevity, in the remainder of the proof we will only consider the case
\iota=1
, as the perturbation theory from Section
2.2
is adapted to the case in which the background solution connects the unstable manifold on the left to the sink on the right. The case
\iota=-1
may be handled by first reversing the background solution
\mathsf{H}
, arguing as in the case
\iota=1
, and then reversing the perturbed solutions again.

We now endeavor to construct the
\mathfrak{h}
-map in (
4.2.14
) when
\iota=1
. Proposition
4.9
suggests we should employ the theory of Section
2.2
to attack the equation (
4.2.3
).
To this end, define the distinguished heteroclinic orbit
\mathsf{Y}=(\varrho,\varrho^{\prime})
, which is a solution to
\mathsf{Y}^{\prime}=\Phi(\mathsf{Y})
satisfying
\lim_{t\to\pm\infty}\mathsf{Y}(t)=(\uprho_{\pm},0)
, where
\Phi:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
is the smooth vector field defined in (
2.1.2
).

Given any
0<\beta<\mathrm{H}_{-}/6
, which we will shrink throughout the proof as needed, we define the complete metric space

(4.2.20)

\Lambda=\overline{B(0,\beta)}\subset[{(C^{10}\cap W^{10,\infty}\cap H^{10})(\mathbb{R})}]^{2},\quad\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\Lambda}=\lVert\cdot\rVert_{W^{10,\infty}\cap H^{10}}.

We then take the functions
\Psi_{0}:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
\Psi_{1}:\Lambda\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
of the decomposition
\Psi=\Psi_{0}+\Psi_{1}
from (
2.2.1
) to be

(4.2.21)

\Psi_{0}(x)=\Phi(x)\text{ and }\Psi_{1}(\lambda,t,x)=\aleph(\lambda_{1}(t),\lambda_{1}^{\prime}(t),\lambda_{2}(t),\lambda_{2}^{\prime}(t),x)\theta(e^{x_{1}}/\mathrm{H}_{-})

where
\aleph
is the map granted by Proposition
4.9
, and
\theta:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
is a smooth function that satisfies
0\leqslant\theta\leqslant 1
along with
\theta(z)=0
for
z\leqslant 1/2
and
\theta(z)=1
for
z\geqslant 2/3
. The purpose of the cut-off
\theta
is to allow
\Psi_{1}
to be globally defined, since
\aleph
is only defined on
D
. Note that
\Psi_{0}^{-1}\{{0}\}=\{{(\uprho_{-},0),(\uprho_{+},0)}\}
and that
(\uprho_{-},0)
and
(\uprho_{+},0)
are hyperbolic and asymptotically stable equilibrium points for
\Psi_{0}
, respectively.

Next, we introduce the parameter
1\leqslant R\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
to determine the quantities (
2.2.4
), (
2.2.5
), and (
2.2.6
), and assume it is large enough that

(4.2.22)

|\uprho_{\star}|+|\uprho_{-}|+\lVert\mathsf{Y}-(\uprho_{+},0)\rVert_{L^{\infty}\cap L^{2}((0,\infty))}+\lVert\mathsf{Y}-(\uprho_{-},0)\rVert_{L^{\infty}\cap L^{2}((-\infty,0))}\leqslant R/10.

For
n\in\{{0,1,\dots,9}\}
the quantities
\overline{\text{\char 107\relax}}_{n}
and
\overline{\text{\char 19\relax}}_{n}
are finite. By using (
4.2.1
), it is not hard to see that

(4.2.23)

\overline{\text{\char 107\relax}}_{n}\lesssim\beta\text{ and }\overline{\text{\char 19\relax}}_{n}\lesssim\beta

for implicit constants depending only on
R
,
\upmu
,
\mathrm{a}
,
\mathrm{g}
, and
\mathrm{A}
. Taking
\beta
to be sufficiently small, depending only on
\mathsf{Y}
and the aforementioned parameters, we may guarantee that the hypotheses of Theorem
2.10
and Corollary
2.11
are satisfied. These then grant us a Lipschitz map

(4.2.24)

\mathfrak{Y}:\overline{B(0,\beta)}\to(C^{9}\cap W^{9,\infty}\cap H^{9})(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R}^{2})

such that
\mathfrak{Y}(0)=0
and for all
\lambda\in\overline{B(0,\beta)}
the function
Y=\mathsf{Y}+\mathfrak{Y}(\lambda)
is a solution to the ODE
Y^{\prime}=\Psi_{0}(Y)+\Psi_{1}(\lambda(\cdot),\cdot,Y)
. Further shrinking
\beta>0
if necessary, we may impose the extra condition that the cut-off function
\theta
in (
4.2.21
) only returns unity when evaluated at the
Y
produced via the map (
4.2.24
).

We are now ready to build the
\mathfrak{h}
-map in (
4.2.14
) by setting

(4.2.25)

\mathfrak{h}(\lambda)=\mathsf{H}(e^{\mathfrak{Y}_{1}(\lambda)}-1).

At first glance, (
4.2.24
) seems to suggest that
\mathfrak{h}
maps into the wrong codomain, with
9
derivatives instead of the stated
10
, but the promotion to
10
indeed holds thanks to the identity
\mathfrak{Y}_{1}^{\prime}=\mathfrak{Y}_{2}
.

The properties
\mathfrak{h}(0,0)=0
and
\lVert\mathfrak{h}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})}\leqslant\mathrm{H}_{-}/3
follow as long as we take
\beta
to be small enough, depending on
\upmu
,
\mathrm{a}
,
\mathrm{g}
,
\mathrm{A}
, and
\mathsf{H}
. That
\mathfrak{h}(\lambda)\in H^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
when
\lambda\in\overline{B(0,\beta)}\cap[{H^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})}]^{2}
, follows from an argument like that used in Corollary
2.11
. The Lipschitz constant of
\mathfrak{h}
may be taken as a function of only
\upmu
,
\mathrm{a}
,
\mathrm{g}
,
\mathrm{A}
, and
\mathsf{H}
.

Thanks to the properties of the map (
4.2.24
) and the identity
\log(\mathsf{H}+\mathfrak{h}(\lambda))=\mathsf{Y}_{1}+\mathfrak{Y}(\lambda)_{1}
we find that if for some
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
,
\Xi\in(1,\infty)
, and
(\eta,w)\in[{H^{1}(\mathbb{R})}]^{2}
we have
\lambda_{1}=\varepsilon^{2}\uppi_{\Xi}\eta
and
\lambda_{2}=\varepsilon^{2}\uppi_{\Xi}w
, the function
\rho=\log(\mathsf{H}+\mathfrak{h}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}))\in W^{\infty,\infty}(\mathbb{R})
is a solution to the ODEs (
4.2.3
) and (
4.2.4
).

With
\mathsf{H}
and
\mathfrak{h}
in hand, we now turn to the construction of the functions
\mathsf{U}
,
\mathsf{U}_{1}
,
\mathsf{U}_{2}
,
\mathsf{P}
,
\mathsf{P}_{1}
, and
\mathsf{P}_{2}
along with the remaining maps in (
4.2.14
). Once this is done, the claimed properties from the first, second, third, and fourth items follow from straightforward computations that we omit for brevity. We define

(4.2.26)

\mathsf{U}=4-\frac{\mathrm{A}}{\mathsf{H}},\quad\mathsf{P}=\mathrm{g}\mathsf{H}-2\upmu\mathsf{U}^{\prime},\quad\mathsf{U}_{1}=\frac{\mathrm{a}}{\upmu}\mathsf{U},\quad\mathsf{U}_{2}=\mathsf{U}^{\prime\prime}-\frac{\upmu\mathsf{U}_{1}+(\mathsf{P}-\mathrm{g}\mathsf{H}-2\upmu\mathsf{U}^{\prime})\mathsf{H}^{\prime}}{\upmu\mathsf{H}},\\
\mathsf{P}_{1}=-\upmu\mathsf{U}_{1}^{\prime},\text{ and }\mathsf{P}_{2}=(\mathsf{U}-4)\mathsf{U}^{\prime\prime}-(\mathsf{U}^{\prime})^{2}-\upmu(\mathsf{U}^{\prime\prime\prime}+\mathsf{U}_{2}^{\prime}).

Then, for
(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})\in\overline{B(0,\beta)}
we define

(4.2.27)

\mathfrak{u}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})=\frac{\mathrm{A}}{\mathsf{H}}-\frac{\mathrm{A}}{\mathsf{H}+\mathfrak{h}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})},\quad\mathfrak{p}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})=\mathrm{g}\mathfrak{h}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})-2\upmu[{\mathfrak{u}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})}]^{\prime}+\mathrm{g}\lambda_{1},\\
\mathfrak{p}_{1}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})=-\upmu[{\mathfrak{u}_{1}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})}]^{\prime},\quad\mathfrak{u}_{1}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})=\frac{\mathrm{a}}{\upmu}\mathfrak{u}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}),\quad\mathfrak{u}_{2}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})=-\mathsf{U}_{2}+(\mathsf{U}+\mathfrak{u}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}))^{\prime\prime}\\
-\frac{\upmu(\mathsf{U}_{1}+\mathfrak{u}_{1}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}))+(-4\upmu(\mathsf{U}+\mathfrak{u}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}))^{\prime}+\mathrm{g}\lambda_{1})(\mathsf{H}+\mathfrak{h}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})+\lambda_{1})^{\prime}-\mathrm{g}(\mathsf{H}+\mathfrak{h}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}))^{\prime}\lambda_{1}}{\upmu(\mathsf{H}+\mathfrak{h}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})+\lambda_{1})},\\
\text{ and }\mathfrak{p}_{2}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})=-\mathsf{P}_{2}+(\mathsf{U}+\mathfrak{u}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})-4)(\mathsf{U}+\mathfrak{u}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}))^{\prime\prime}-([{\mathsf{U}+\mathfrak{u}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})}]^{\prime})^{2}\\
-\upmu((\mathsf{U}+\mathfrak{u}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}))^{\prime\prime\prime}-(\mathsf{U}_{2}+\mathfrak{u}_{2}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}))^{\prime}).

To complete the proof, we now justify the fifth item by invoking the equivalence of Proposition
4.9
: our construction exactly ensures that
Y=(\rho,\rho^{\prime})
solves (
4.2.3
) and (
4.2.4
), while
U
,
U_{1}
,
U_{2}
,
P
,
P_{1}
, and
P_{2}
are exactly determined by
H=e^{\rho}
and
\eta
as in (
4.2.5
), (
4.2.6
), and (
4.2.7
).
∎

Remark 4.11

(On the domain space for the bore map)
.

The choice of the number of derivatives to keep track of in the domains of the maps (
4.2.14
) being equal to ten is somewhat arbitrary. The only mandate is that we feed the bore maps something with enough regularity so that the outputs have enough derivatives to comfortably handle the computations of the next section.

Proposition
4.10
provides precise information about the various pieces that contribute to the bore map. For our purposes later it will be convenient to trade some of this precision for uniformity in terms of spaces and derivative counts. The following result records this consolidated information about the bore map.

Corollary 4.12

(The bore map, II)
.

Assume the hypotheses and notation of Proposition
4.10
. For all
Z\in\{{H,U,U_{1},U_{2},P,P_{1},P_{2}}\}
the proposition allows us to view the sums

(4.2.28)

H=\mathsf{H}+\mathfrak{h},\;U=\mathsf{U}+\mathfrak{u},U_{1}=\mathsf{U}_{1}+\mathfrak{u}_{1},\;U_{2}=\mathsf{U}_{2}+\mathfrak{u}_{2},\;P=\mathsf{P}+\mathfrak{p},\;P_{1}=\mathsf{P}_{1}+\mathfrak{p}_{1},\;P_{2}=\mathsf{P}_{2}+\mathfrak{p}_{2}

as maps
Z:\overline{B(0,\beta)}\to(C^{7}\cap W^{7,\infty})(\mathbb{R})
for
\overline{B(0,\beta)}\subset[{(C^{10}\cap W^{10,\infty}\cap H^{10})(\mathbb{R})}]^{2}
. For all
(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}),(\widetilde{\lambda}_{1},\widetilde{\lambda}_{2})\in\overline{B(0,\beta)}
we have the estimates

(4.2.29)

\lVert Z(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})\rVert_{W^{7,\infty}}+\lVert[{Z(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})}]^{\prime}\rVert_{H^{6}}\leqslant 2M

and

(4.2.30)

\lVert Z(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})-Z(\widetilde{\lambda}_{1},\widetilde{\lambda}_{2})\rVert_{W^{7,\infty}\cap H^{7}}\leqslant M\lVert(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})-(\widetilde{\lambda}_{1},\widetilde{\lambda}_{2})\rVert_{W^{10,\infty}\cap H^{10}}.

Proof.

These are trivial consequences of Proposition
4.10
.
∎

4.3.
Properties of residual forcing and driving terms

With the specification of
\mathfrak{r}_{1}
,
\mathfrak{r}_{3}
, and
\mathfrak{r}_{4}
and the bore map theory of Section
4.2
in hand, we are now in a position to study the residual forcing terms
\mathfrak{f}_{0}
,
\mathfrak{f}_{1}
,
\mathfrak{f}_{2}
,
\mathfrak{f}_{3}
, and
\mathfrak{f}_{4}
appearing in system (
4.1.14
). In particular, we are interested in viewing these terms as maps built from the various subcomponents we constructed in the previous subsection and understanding their resulting mapping properties. To accomplish this we first need to introduce some notation.

To measure the free surface residual
\eta
, for
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
we employ the following
\varepsilon
-dependent norm on
H^{5/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})
:

(4.3.1)

\lVert\eta\rVert_{H^{5/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})}^{(\varepsilon)}=\varepsilon^{1/2}\lVert\eta\rVert_{H^{2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})}+\varepsilon\lVert\eta\rVert_{H^{5/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})}.

Next, we introduce the function
\varepsilon_{0}:(1,\infty)\to(0,1)
defined by
\varepsilon_{0}(R)=\sqrt{\mathrm{H}_{-}/(6CR)}
where
1\leqslant C<\infty
is a constant from the one-dimensional Morrey inequality:
\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})}\leqslant C\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R})}
. The main use of this function is in the following observation: if
f,g:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
satisfy
f\geqslant 2\mathrm{H}_{-}/3
and
\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R})}\leqslant R
, then for
0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}(R)
we have that
f+\varepsilon^{2}g\geqslant 2\mathrm{H}_{-}/3-\varepsilon^{2}\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})}\geqslant 2\mathrm{H}_{-}/3-\varepsilon^{2}CR\geqslant\mathrm{H}_{-}/2
.

The purpose of the following definition is to fix our choice of parameters and specify the functional dependence of the terms appearing in Theorem
4.1
on the bore map theory of Section
4.2
.

Definition 4.13

(Ansatz mapping terms and residual forcing terms)
.

Let
\upmu,\mathrm{a}>0
,
\iota\in\{{-1,1}\}
,
(\mathrm{g},\mathrm{A})\in\mathfrak{C}_{\iota}
, and
\upsigma\geqslant 0
. Thanks to Proposition
4.10
and Corollary
4.12
there exists
\beta\in(0,1)
and bore map constituents that are Lipschitz continuous mappings

(4.3.2)

H,U,U_{1},U_{2},P,P_{1},P_{2}:\overline{B(0,\beta)}\subset[{(C^{10}\cap W^{10,\infty}\cap H^{10})(\mathbb{R})}]^{2}\to(C^{7}\cap W^{7,\infty})(\mathbb{R})

defining a solution operator to the system of ODEs (
4.1.11
), (
4.1.12
), and (
4.1.13
) in the sense made precise by the fifth item of Proposition
4.10
. Note that
H
satisfies
H\geqslant(2/3)\mathrm{H}_{-}
. Given
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
,
\lambda\in\overline{B(0,\beta)}
, and
\eta\in H^{5/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})
, we define the following functions

(4.3.3)

\begin{split}\boldsymbol{\zeta}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)&=H(\lambda)+\varepsilon^{2}\eta:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R},\\
W_{1}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)&=\frac{y\boldsymbol{\zeta}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)}{\varepsilon}U_{1}(\lambda)+\frac{1}{2}\Big(\frac{y\boldsymbol{\zeta}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)}{\varepsilon}\Big)^{2}U_{2}(\lambda):\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R},\\
W_{2}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)&=-\varepsilon\Big(\frac{1}{2}\Big(\frac{y\boldsymbol{\zeta}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)}{\varepsilon}\Big)^{2}U_{1}(\lambda)^{\prime}+\frac{1}{6}\Big(\frac{y\boldsymbol{\zeta}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)}{\varepsilon}\Big)^{3}U_{2}(\lambda)^{\prime}\Big):\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R},\\
W(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)&=W_{1}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)e_{1}+W_{2}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)e_{2}:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{2},\\
V(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)&=U(\lambda)e_{1}-y\boldsymbol{\zeta}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)U(\lambda)^{\prime}e_{2}:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{2},\\
X(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)&=V(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)+\varepsilon^{2}W(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta):\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{2},\\
Q(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)&=\frac{y\boldsymbol{\zeta}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)}{\varepsilon}P_{1}(\lambda)+\frac{1}{2}\Big(\frac{y\boldsymbol{\zeta}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)}{\varepsilon}\Big)^{2}P_{2}(\lambda):\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}.\end{split}

Next, let
R\in(1,\infty)
and
\varepsilon_{0}:(1,\infty)\to(0,1)
be as above and suppose that
0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}(R)
. We then define
the sets

(4.3.4)

E^{R}_{\varepsilon}=\{{(\lambda,\eta)\in\overline{B(0,\beta)}\times H^{5/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})\;:\;\lVert\eta\rVert^{(\varepsilon)}_{H^{5/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})}\leqslant R}\}\text{ and }\\
F^{R}_{\varepsilon}=\{{(\lambda,\eta,u)\in\overline{B(0,\beta)}\times H^{5/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})\times H^{2}_{\mathrm{tan}}(\Omega_{\varepsilon};\mathbb{R}^{2}):\;\max\{{\lVert\eta\rVert^{(\varepsilon)}_{H^{5/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})},\lVert u\rVert_{H^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}}\}\leqslant R}\}.

Note that if
(\lambda,\eta)\in E^{R}_{\varepsilon}
then the choice of
\varepsilon_{0}
implies that
\boldsymbol{\zeta}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)\geqslant\mathrm{H}_{-}/2
, which in turn means that the geometry matrix
\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)}
from (
1.3.5
) is well-defined. In turn, we define the following maps, noting that each is well-defined on the given domain but that the appropriateness of the codomain will be verified in the subsequent result:

(4.3.5)

\mathfrak{f}_{0}(R,\varepsilon;\cdot),\;\mathfrak{f}_{3}(R,\varepsilon;\cdot),\;\mathfrak{f}_{4}(R,\varepsilon;\cdot):E^{R}_{\varepsilon}\to H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})\text{ and }\mathfrak{f}_{1}(R,\varepsilon;\cdot),\;\mathfrak{f}_{2}(R,\varepsilon;\cdot):F^{R}_{\varepsilon}\to L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})

are defined via

(4.3.6)

\mathfrak{f}_{0}(R,\varepsilon;\lambda,\eta)=(1-\partial_{1}^{2})(\overline{\upgamma}H(\lambda)-(H(\lambda)+\varepsilon^{2}\eta)^{2}U_{1}(\lambda)/2-(H(\lambda)+\varepsilon^{2}\eta)^{3}U_{2}(\lambda)/6-\overline{\mathrm{A}}),

(4.3.7)

\mathfrak{f}_{1}(R,\varepsilon;\lambda,\eta,u)=-\overline{\upgamma}U(\lambda)^{\prime}+(V(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)-(4+\varepsilon^{2}\overline{\upgamma})e_{1})\cdot\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)}}W_{1}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)\\
+W(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)\cdot\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)}}X_{1}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)+\partial_{1}^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)}}Q(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)-\upmu(\partial_{1}^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)}})^{2}W_{1}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)+\varepsilon^{2}u\cdot\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)}}u_{1},

(4.3.8)

\mathfrak{f}_{2}(R,\varepsilon;\lambda,\eta,u)=\overline{\upgamma}y\boldsymbol{\zeta}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)U(\lambda)^{\prime\prime}+(V(\lambda)-(4+\varepsilon^{2}\overline{\upgamma})e_{1})\cdot\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)}}W_{2}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)\\
+W(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)\cdot\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)}}X_{2}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)-\upmu(\partial_{1}^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)}})^{2}W_{2}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)+\varepsilon^{2}u\cdot\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)}}u_{2},

(4.3.9)

\mathfrak{f}_{3}(R,\varepsilon;\lambda,\eta)=\varepsilon Q(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)\boldsymbol{\zeta}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)^{\prime}-2\varepsilon\upmu\partial_{1}^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)}}W_{1}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)\boldsymbol{\zeta}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)^{\prime}+\upmu\partial_{1}^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)}}W_{2}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)\\
+\upsigma\mathcal{H}(\varepsilon\boldsymbol{\zeta}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta))\boldsymbol{\zeta}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)^{\prime}-\varepsilon^{3}\mathrm{g}\eta\eta^{\prime},

and

(4.3.10)

\mathfrak{f}_{4}(R,\varepsilon;\lambda,\eta)=-Q(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)+\upmu\boldsymbol{\zeta}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)\boldsymbol{\zeta}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)^{\prime}U(\lambda)^{\prime\prime}+\upmu\mathbb{D}^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)}}W(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon\boldsymbol{\zeta}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)}\cdot e_{2}-\varepsilon^{-1}\upsigma\mathcal{H}(\varepsilon\boldsymbol{\zeta}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)).

The codomains of the various maps (
4.3.5
) in the previous definition are determined by the data space ((
3.2.9
) and (
3.2.10
)) associated with the well-posedness theory for the thin domain Stokes equations with stress boundary conditions, which we develop in Section
3.2
. Our next result studies the mapping properties of the residual forcing terms introduced in the previous definition in these spaces. We recall the adapted boundary norms introduced in Lemma
3.2
. We also emphasize that the
\mathfrak{f}_{0}
, given by (
4.3.6
), estimate in the following is the key place where the parameter tuning (
1.4.5
) is required; it is exploited in order to force
\mathfrak{f}_{0}
to vanish at infinity and be square summable.

Proposition 4.14

(Mapping properties of the residual forcing terms)
.

Assume the hypotheses of Definition
4.13
. There exists functions
\varepsilon_{1}:(1,\infty)\to(0,1)
and
C_{1}:(1,\infty)\to(1,\infty)
with
\varepsilon_{1}\leqslant\varepsilon_{0}
such that the following hold. First, we have the lower bound

(4.3.11)

\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}1<R<\infty\\
0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}(R)\end{subarray}}\inf_{(\lambda,\eta)\in E^{R}_{\varepsilon}}\inf_{\mathbb{R}}(H(\lambda)+\varepsilon^{2}\eta)\geqslant\mathrm{H}_{-}/2.

Second, we have the uniform range estimates:

(4.3.12)

\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}1<R<\infty\\
0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{1}(R)\end{subarray}}\sup_{(\lambda,\eta)\in E^{R}_{\varepsilon}}\big[{\lVert\mathfrak{f}_{0}(R,\varepsilon;\lambda,\eta)\rVert_{H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})}+\lVert\mathfrak{f}_{3}(R,\varepsilon;\lambda,\eta)\rVert^{(\varepsilon,+)}_{H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})}+\lVert\mathfrak{f}_{4}(R,\varepsilon;\lambda,\eta)\rVert^{(\varepsilon,-)}_{H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})}}\big]<\infty\text{ and }\\
\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}1<R<\infty\\
0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{1}(R)\end{subarray}}\sup_{(\lambda,\eta,u)\in F^{R}_{\varepsilon}}\big[{\lVert\mathfrak{f}_{1}(R,\varepsilon;\lambda,\eta,u)\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}+\lVert\mathfrak{f}_{2}(R,\varepsilon;\lambda,\eta,u)\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}}\big]<\infty.

Third, we have the uniform Lipschitz estimates:

(4.3.13)

\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}1<R<\infty\\
0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{1}(R)\end{subarray}}\sup_{(\lambda,\eta)\neq(\widetilde{\lambda},\widetilde{\eta})}\frac{\lVert\mathfrak{f}_{0}(R,\varepsilon;\lambda,\eta)-\mathfrak{f}_{0}(R,\varepsilon;\widetilde{\lambda},\widetilde{\eta})\rVert_{H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})}}{\lVert\lambda-\widetilde{\lambda}\rVert_{\Lambda}+C_{1}(R)\varepsilon\lVert\eta-\widetilde{\eta}\rVert^{(\varepsilon)}_{H^{5/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})}}<\infty;

writing
s_{3}=+
and
s_{4}=-
,

(4.3.14)

\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}1<R<\infty\\
0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{1}(R)\end{subarray}}\sup_{(\lambda,\eta)\neq(\widetilde{\lambda},\widetilde{\eta})}\frac{\lVert\mathfrak{f}_{j}(R,\varepsilon;\lambda,\eta)-\mathfrak{f}_{j}(R,\varepsilon;\widetilde{\lambda},\widetilde{\eta})\rVert^{(\varepsilon,s_{j})}_{H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})}}{\lVert\lambda-\widetilde{\lambda}\rVert_{\Lambda}+C_{1}(R)\varepsilon\lVert\eta-\widetilde{\eta}\rVert^{(\varepsilon)}_{H^{5/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})}}<\infty\text{ for }j\in\{3,4\};

and for
j\in\{1,2\}
,

(4.3.15)

\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}1<R<\infty\\
0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{1}(R)\end{subarray}}\sup_{(\lambda,\eta,u)\neq(\widetilde{\lambda},\widetilde{\eta},\widetilde{u})}\frac{\lVert\mathfrak{f}_{j}(R,\varepsilon;\lambda,\eta,u)-\mathfrak{f}_{j}(R,\varepsilon;\widetilde{\lambda},\widetilde{\eta},\widetilde{u})\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}}{\lVert\lambda-\widetilde{\lambda}\rVert_{\Lambda}+C_{1}(R)\varepsilon^{2}\lVert\eta-\widetilde{\eta}\rVert_{W^{1,\infty}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})}+C_{1}(R)\varepsilon^{3/2}\lVert u-\widetilde{\eta}\rVert_{H^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}}<\infty,

where
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\Lambda}
is as in (
4.2.20
).

Proof.

For the most part the stated estimates follow from straightforward computations and estimates, so for the sake of brevity we will only highlight the pieces that require some delicate care and omit details for the rest. We begin by noting that estimate (
4.3.11
) is a direct consequence of the discussion preceding Definition
4.13
.

We now handle each term in order.

Step 0 – The term
\mathfrak{f}_{0}
:
Estimate of (
4.3.13
) is a straightforward consequence of estimate (
4.2.30
) from Corollary (
4.12
) along with the fact that
H^{5/2}(\mathbb{R})
is an algebra. In view of this Lipschitz estimate and the assumed bounds on
\lambda
and
\eta
, it then suffices to establish the
\mathfrak{f}_{0}
-bound of (
4.3.12
) when
\lambda=0
and
\eta=0
, which is equivalent to establishing the inclusion

(4.3.16)

(1-\partial_{1}^{2})^{-1}\mathfrak{f}_{0}(\varepsilon,0,0)=\overline{\upgamma}\mathsf{H}-\mathsf{H}^{2}\mathsf{U}_{1}/2-\mathsf{H}^{3}\mathsf{U}_{2}/6-\mathrm{A}\in H^{5/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon}),

where
\mathsf{H}=H(0)
and
\mathsf{U}_{1}
, and
\mathsf{U}_{2}
are defined in (
4.2.26
). We invoke the identities

(4.3.17)

\mathsf{U}=4-\mathrm{A}/\mathsf{H},\quad\mathsf{U}_{1}=(\mathrm{a}/\upmu)\mathsf{U},\text{ and }\mathsf{U}_{2}=-\mathrm{a}\mathsf{U}/\upmu\mathsf{H}+\mathsf{U}^{\prime\prime}-4\mathsf{H}^{\prime}\mathsf{U}^{\prime}/\mathsf{H}

to rewrite the function in (
4.3.16
) as

(4.3.18)

(1-\partial_{1}^{2})^{-1}\mathfrak{f}_{0}(\varepsilon,0,0)=\overline{\upgamma}\mathsf{H}-\overline{\mathrm{A}}-(4\mathrm{a}/3\upmu)\mathsf{H}^{3}-(\mathrm{a}/3\upmu)\mathsf{H}(4\mathsf{H}-4\mathsf{H}^{2}-\mathrm{A})-\mathsf{H}^{3}(\mathsf{U}^{\prime\prime}-4\mathsf{H}^{\prime}\mathsf{U}^{\prime}/\mathsf{H})/6.

Due to the exponential decay of derivatives provided by the second item of Proposition
4.10
, the third term together with the derivatives of the first two terms belong to
H^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
and, in particular

(4.3.19)

\lVert(\overline{\upgamma}\mathsf{H}-\overline{\mathrm{A}}-(4\mathrm{a}/3\upmu)\mathsf{H}^{3})^{\prime}\rVert_{H^{3/2}}+\lVert(4\mathsf{H}-4\mathsf{H}^{2}-\mathrm{A})^{\prime}\rVert_{H^{3/2}}+\lVert\mathsf{H}^{3}(\mathsf{U}^{\prime\prime}-4\mathsf{H}^{\prime}\mathsf{U}^{\prime}/\mathsf{H})/6\rVert_{H^{5/2}}\lesssim 1.

Thus,

(4.3.20)

\lVert(1-\partial_{1}^{2})^{-1}\mathfrak{f}_{0}(\varepsilon,0,0)\rVert_{H^{5/2}}\lesssim\lVert\overline{\upgamma}\mathsf{H}-\overline{\mathrm{A}}-(4\mathrm{a}/3\upmu)\mathsf{H}^{3}\rVert_{L^{2}}+\lVert 4\mathsf{H}-4\mathsf{H}^{2}-\mathrm{A}\rVert_{L^{2}}+1.

In (
4.3.19
) and (
4.3.20
) the implicit constants depend only on the physical parameters initialized by Definition
4.13
and the bore map (
4.3.2
). So it only remains to verify that each of the above polynomials-in-
\mathsf{H}
belongs to
L^{2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})
. While
\mathsf{H}
itself does not belong to
L^{2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})
, we do know that its limits at
\pm\infty
, namely
\mathrm{H}_{\pm}
, satisfy the relations (
1.4.4
) and (
1.4.5
). Hence the polynomials-in-
\mathsf{H}
from (
4.3.20
) vanish at
\pm\infty
, and we may invoke the fundamental theorem of calculus and the aforementioned exponential decay of derivatives to deduce that these functions vanish exponentially quickly at
\pm\infty
, and thus belong to
L^{2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})
.

Step 1 – The term
\mathfrak{f}_{1}
:
The
\mathfrak{f}_{1}
-parts of the bounds (
4.3.12
) and (
4.3.15
) are mostly straightforward, so we only point out the terms requiring some subtlety.

Firstly, we observe that
V
,
W
, and
Q
of (
4.3.3
) can be thought of as variable coefficient polynomials in
y/\varepsilon
. Since
y\in(0,\varepsilon)
, this ratio
y/\varepsilon
is bounded independently of
\varepsilon
. However, this structure shows that the application of
\partial_{2}^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}
to any one of these introduces a factor of
1/\varepsilon
. This is not a problem because each of these
e_{2}
-type derivatives is paired with either
V_{2}
or
W_{2}
, which have an additional factor of either
y
or
\varepsilon
to compensate. On the other hand, the derivatives
\partial_{1}^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}
are essentially harmless, as the following computation elucidates:

(4.3.21)

V(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)\cdot\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)}}W_{1}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)=U(\lambda)\partial_{1}^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)}}W_{1}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)-(y/\varepsilon)\boldsymbol{\zeta}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)U(\lambda)^{\prime}(U_{1}(\lambda)+(y/\varepsilon)\boldsymbol{\zeta}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)U_{2}(\lambda)).

Secondly, we note that
U
,
V_{1}
,
W_{1}
, and
X_{1}
do not belong to square summable Sobolev spaces, but after application of
\partial_{1}^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}
they do become square summable, as a consequence of estimate (
4.2.29
) from Corollary
4.12
. On the other hand the second components
V_{2}
,
W_{2}
,
X_{2}
do belong to square summable spaces. As a consequence of these two facts, we deduce that each of the advective style derivatives in (
4.3.7
), i.e. the first, second, and third grouping of terms, must map into square summable spaces.

Next, except possibly for the penultimate term in (
4.3.7
), no term involves more than one derivative of
\boldsymbol{\zeta}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)
, which means that control on
\eta
in
W^{1,\infty}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})
is sufficient for these. In fact, this is also true for the penultimate term, as can be see by writing it out:

(4.3.22)

(\partial_{1}^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)}})^{2}W_{1}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)=(y/\varepsilon)\boldsymbol{\zeta}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)U_{1}(\lambda)^{\prime\prime}+((y/\varepsilon)\boldsymbol{\zeta}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta))^{2}U_{2}(\lambda)^{\prime\prime}/2.

Finally, the coefficient
\varepsilon^{3/2}
present in the velocity contributions on the right hand side of (
4.3.15
) can be explained through the thin domain Sobolev embedding of the third item of Lemma
3.4
. For example, we have the estimate

(4.3.23)

\lVert\varepsilon^{2}u\cdot\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{{\boldsymbol{\zeta}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)}}}u_{1}\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}\lesssim\varepsilon^{2}\lVert u\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}\lVert u\rVert_{H^{1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}\lesssim\varepsilon^{3/2}\lVert u\rVert_{H^{1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})},

where the implicit constants depend only the physical parameters initialized by Definition
4.13
, the bore map (
4.3.2
), and
R
.

Step 2 – The term
\mathfrak{f}_{2}
:
The same arguments used to handle
\mathfrak{f}_{1}
in the previous step work here as well, so we omit further detail.

Step 3 – The term
\mathfrak{f}_{3}
:
Again we only highlight the key observations. The norm
\lVert\cdot\rVert^{(\varepsilon,+)}_{H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})}
is the sum of the
H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})
norm with
\varepsilon^{-1/2}
times the
L^{2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})
norm, so we may consider each of these separately. The first three terms in (
4.3.9
) are no trouble to bound thanks to Corollary
4.12
, each term having an additional factor of
\varepsilon
to offset the
\varepsilon^{-1/2}
built into the norm (for see
W_{2}
in (
4.3.3
)), and the fact that
Q
,
\partial_{1}^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}W_{1}
, and
W_{2}
are all square summable.

To handle the capillary contribution in (
4.3.9
) we expand

(4.3.24)

\varepsilon^{-1}\mathcal{H}(\varepsilon\boldsymbol{\zeta}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta))=\boldsymbol{\zeta}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)^{\prime\prime}\cdot(1+\varepsilon^{2}\char 12\relax{\boldsymbol{\zeta}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)^{\prime}}^{2})^{-3/2}.

The bound of the norm in
L^{2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})
is then straightforward, whereas for the
H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})
-norm we use the continuity of the product map
H^{1/2}(\mathbb{R}\Sigma_{\varepsilon})\times H^{1}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})\to H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})
and put the second factor in (
4.3.24
) in
H^{1}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})
. For the final term in (
4.3.9
) we write
2\eta\eta^{\prime}=(\eta^{2})^{\prime}
and use the fact that
H^{1}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})
and
H^{3/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})
are algebras.

Step 4 – The term
\mathfrak{f}_{4}
:
The norm employed for
\mathfrak{f}_{4}
satisfies
\lVert\cdot\rVert^{(\varepsilon,-)}_{H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})}\leqslant\lVert\cdot\rVert_{H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})}
, so it suffices to prove the
\mathfrak{f}_{4}
-part of the claimed estimates (
4.3.12
) and (
4.3.14
) for the stronger
H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})
norm, and it turns out there is no need to take advantage of any gains given by the smaller norm.

As in the previous step, handling the first and second terms of (
4.3.10
) is straightforward and the final capillary term may also be felled with inspection of (
4.3.24
). To see that the third term also does not require any new ideas, we can write it out fully as

(4.3.25)

\mathbb{D}^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)}}W(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon\boldsymbol{\zeta}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)}\cdot e_{2}=-\varepsilon\boldsymbol{\zeta}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)^{\prime}(\partial_{1}^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)}}W_{2}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)+\partial_{2}^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)}}W_{1}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta))+2\partial_{2}^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)}}W_{2}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta).

Firstly, since
W_{2}
and
\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{\prime}
are square summable the entire expression inherits this property. As we discussed in Step 1 above, the components of
\partial_{2}^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}W
come with a factor of
\varepsilon^{-1}
(from the differentiation) but the first grouping of terms in (
4.3.25
) has an explicit extra
\varepsilon
and the final term with
W_{2}
has an implicit one. On balance (
4.3.25
) comes with a net coefficient of
\varepsilon^{0}
or smaller and so it can be harmlessly bounded.
∎

At this point we have analyzed the forcing type residuals
\mathfrak{f}_{0}
,
\mathfrak{f}_{1}
,
\mathfrak{f}_{2}
,
\mathfrak{f}_{3}
, and
\mathfrak{f}_{4}
appearing in system (
4.1.14
). However, there are a few more discrepancies between this system and the linear Stokes system (
3.2.1
) that have yet to be analyzed. These are terms of linear driving type, which we define now.

Definition 4.15

(Residual driving terms)
.

Assume the hypotheses of Definition
4.13
. We further build upon the maps (
4.3.2
) and (
4.3.3
) and the sets (
4.3.4
) by defining the following functions for
\Xi,R\in(1,\infty)
and
0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}(R)
:

(4.3.26)

\mathfrak{d}^{\Xi}_{1}(R,\varepsilon;\cdot),\;\mathfrak{d}^{\Xi}_{2}(R,\varepsilon;\cdot):F^{R}_{\varepsilon}\to L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})\text{ and }\mathfrak{d}_{3}^{\Xi}(R,\varepsilon;\cdot),\;\mathfrak{d}_{4}^{\Xi}(R,\varepsilon;\cdot):E^{R}_{\varepsilon}\to H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})

given by

(4.3.27)

\mathfrak{d}^{\Xi}_{1}(R,\varepsilon;\lambda,\eta,u)=(X(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)-(4+\varepsilon^{2}\overline{\upgamma})e_{1})\cdot\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)}}u_{1}\\
+u\cdot\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)}}X_{1}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)-\Big((U(\lambda)-4)\uppi_{\Xi}\Big(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{\varepsilon}u_{1}(\cdot,y)\;\mathrm{d}y\Big)\Big)^{\prime},

(4.3.28)

\mathfrak{d}^{\Xi}_{2}(R,\varepsilon;\lambda,\eta,u)=(X(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)-(4+\varepsilon^{2}\overline{\upgamma}))\cdot\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)}}u_{2}+u\cdot\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)}}X_{2}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta),

(4.3.29)

\mathfrak{d}^{\Xi}_{3}(R,\varepsilon;\lambda,\eta)=\varepsilon(P(\lambda)-\mathrm{g}H(\lambda)-2\upmu U^{\prime}(\lambda))(\eta-\uppi_{\Xi}\eta)^{\prime}+\varepsilon\upmu(U_{2}(\lambda)-U(\lambda)^{\prime\prime})(\eta-\uppi_{\Xi}\eta)-\varepsilon\mathrm{g}H(\lambda)^{\prime}(\eta-\uppi_{\Xi}\eta),

and

(4.3.30)

\mathfrak{d}_{4}^{\Xi}(R,\varepsilon;\lambda,\eta)=\mathrm{g}(\eta-\uppi_{\Xi}\eta).

Our next result studies the mapping properties of the residual driving terms from the previous definition. Here the key point is that the
\Xi
parameter allows us to make certain constants appearing in the estimates small, even when the parameter
R
is large. This will play an essential role later in a fixed point argument.

Proposition 4.16

(Mapping properties of the residual driving terms)
.

Assume the hypotheses of Definition
4.13
. There exists functions
\varepsilon_{2}:(1,\infty)\to(0,1)
and
C_{2}:(1,\infty)\to(1,\infty)
, with
\varepsilon_{2}\leqslant\varepsilon_{0}
, such that the following hold. First, we again have the positivity (
4.3.11
). Second, we have the uniform range estimates:

(4.3.31)

\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}1<R<\infty\\
0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{2}(R)\end{subarray}}\sup_{1<\Xi<\infty}\sup_{(\lambda,\eta,u)\in F^{R}_{\varepsilon}}(1+R/\Xi)^{-1}\big[{\lVert\mathfrak{d}_{1}^{\Xi}(R,\varepsilon;\lambda,\eta,u)\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}+\lVert\mathfrak{d}^{\Xi}_{2}(R,\varepsilon;\lambda,\eta,u)\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}}\big]<\infty,\\
\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}1<R<\infty\\
0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{2}(R)\end{subarray}}\sup_{1<\Xi<\infty}\sup_{(\lambda,\eta)\in E^{R}_{\varepsilon}}(1+R/\Xi)^{-1}\big[{\lVert\mathfrak{d}_{3}^{\Xi}(R,\varepsilon;\lambda,\eta)\rVert^{(\varepsilon,+)}_{H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})}+\lVert\mathfrak{d}_{4}^{\Xi}(R,\varepsilon;\lambda,\eta)\rVert^{(\varepsilon,-)}_{H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})}}\big]<\infty.

Third, we have the uniform Lipschitz estimates for
j\in\{{1,2}\}

(4.3.32)

\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}1<R<\infty\\
0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{2}(R)\end{subarray}}\sup_{1<\Xi<\infty}\sup_{(\lambda,\eta,u)\neq(\widetilde{\lambda},\widetilde{\eta},\widetilde{u})}\frac{\lVert\mathfrak{d}_{j}^{\Xi}(R,\varepsilon;\lambda,\eta,u)-\mathfrak{d}_{j}^{\Xi}(R,\varepsilon;\widetilde{\lambda},\widetilde{\eta},\widetilde{u})\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}}{\lVert\lambda-\widetilde{\lambda}\rVert_{\Lambda}+C_{2}(R)\varepsilon^{2}\lVert\eta-\widetilde{\eta}\rVert_{W^{1,\infty}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})}+(C_{2}(R)\varepsilon+1/\Xi)\lVert u-\widetilde{u}\rVert_{H^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}}<\infty

and for
j\in\{{3,4}\}
, with the understanding that
s_{3}=+
and
s_{4}=-
,

(4.3.33)

\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}1<R<\infty\\
0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{2}(R)\end{subarray}}\sup_{1<\Xi<\infty}\sup_{(\lambda,\eta)\neq(\widetilde{\lambda},\widetilde{\eta})}\frac{\lVert\mathfrak{d}^{\Xi}_{j}(R,\varepsilon;\lambda,\eta)-\mathfrak{d}^{\Xi}_{j}(R,\varepsilon;\widetilde{\lambda},\widetilde{\eta})\rVert^{(\varepsilon,s_{j})}_{H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})}}{\lVert\lambda-\widetilde{\lambda}\rVert_{\Lambda}+(1/\Xi)\lVert\eta-\widetilde{\eta}\rVert^{(\varepsilon)}_{H^{5/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})}}<\infty,

where
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\Lambda}
is as in (
4.2.20
).

Proof.

We follow the same strategy used in the proof of Proposition
4.14
and elect to only highlight the parts requiring some more delicate care and suppress the remaining details. Again, we handle the terms in order.

Step 1 – The term
\mathfrak{d}_{1}
:
We expand (
4.3.27
) into the sum of two expressions, one that is made small by taking
\varepsilon
sufficiently small and the other that is made small by taking
\Xi
sufficiently large; more precisely, we split

(4.3.34)

\mathfrak{d}^{\Xi}_{1}(R,\varepsilon;\lambda,\eta,u)={{}_{1}}\mathfrak{d}_{1}(R,\varepsilon;\lambda,\eta,u)+{{}_{2}}\mathfrak{d}^{\Xi}_{1}(R,\varepsilon;\lambda,u),

where

(4.3.35)

{{}_{1}}\mathfrak{d}_{1}(R,\varepsilon;\lambda,\eta,u)=\Big((U(\lambda)-4)\Big(u_{1}-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{\varepsilon}u_{1}(\cdot,y)\;\mathrm{d}y\Big)\Big)^{\prime}\\
-y((U(\lambda)-4)\boldsymbol{\zeta}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta))^{\prime}\partial_{2}^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)}}u_{1}+u_{1}\Big(\varepsilon y\boldsymbol{\zeta}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)U_{1}(\lambda)^{\prime}+\frac{1}{2}(y\boldsymbol{\zeta}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta))^{2}U_{2}(\lambda)^{\prime}\Big)\\
+u_{2}(\varepsilon U_{1}(\lambda)+y\boldsymbol{\zeta}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)U_{2}(\lambda))+\varepsilon^{2}(W(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)-\overline{\upgamma}e_{1})\cdot\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)}}u_{1}

and

(4.3.36)

{{}_{2}}\mathfrak{d}^{\Xi}_{1}(R,\varepsilon;\lambda,u)=\Big((U(\lambda)-4)\Big(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{\varepsilon}u_{1}(\cdot,y)\;\mathrm{d}y-\uppi_{\Xi}\Big(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{\varepsilon}u_{1}(\cdot,y)\;\mathrm{d}y\Big)\Big)\Big)^{\prime}.

To establish the
\mathfrak{d}_{1}
-parts of the claimed estimates (
4.3.31
) and (
4.3.32
), it is sufficient to prove that the maps in the decomposition (
4.3.34
) satisfy the following. There exists
N\in(1,\infty)
with the property that for
R\in(1,\infty)
there exists
0<\varepsilon_{R}\leqslant\varepsilon_{1}(R)
and there exists
C_{R}\geqslant C_{1}(R)
such that for all
0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{R}
, for all
\Xi\in(1,\infty)
, and for all
(\lambda,u,\eta),(\widetilde{\lambda},\widetilde{u},\widetilde{\eta})\in F^{R}_{\varepsilon}
we have the bounds

(4.3.37)

\lVert{{}_{1}}\mathfrak{d}_{1}(R,\varepsilon;\lambda,\eta,u)\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}\leqslant N,\quad\lVert{{}_{2}}\mathfrak{d}^{\Xi}_{1}(\varepsilon,R;\lambda,u)\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}\leqslant N(R/\Xi),

(4.3.38)

\lVert{{}_{1}}\mathfrak{d}_{1}(R,\varepsilon;\lambda,\eta,u)-{{}_{1}}\mathfrak{d}_{1}(R,\varepsilon;\widetilde{\lambda},\widetilde{\eta},\widetilde{u})\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}\leqslant N(\lVert\lambda-\widetilde{\lambda}\rVert_{\Lambda}+C_{R}\varepsilon^{2}\lVert\eta-\widetilde{\eta}\rVert_{W^{1,\infty}}+C_{R}\varepsilon\lVert u-\widetilde{u}\rVert_{H^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}),

and

(4.3.39)

\lVert{{}_{2}}\mathfrak{d}^{\Xi}_{1}(R,\varepsilon;\lambda,u)-{{}_{2}}\mathfrak{d}^{\Xi}_{1}(R,\varepsilon;\widetilde{\lambda},\widetilde{u})\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}\leqslant N(\lVert\lambda-\widetilde{\lambda}\rVert_{\Lambda}+(1/\Xi)\lVert u-\widetilde{u}\rVert_{H^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}).

Now, the estimates (
4.3.37
), (
4.3.38
), and (
4.3.39
) are mostly direct and only require minor commentary. For the first term in (
4.3.35
) the key is to expand with the product rule and apply the average-deviation estimate from the first item of Lemma
3.4
(with
q=2
) to both
u_{1}
and
\partial_{1}u_{1}
. The remaining terms in
{{}_{1}}\mathfrak{d}_{1}
are small due to the presence of either an
\varepsilon
or a
y
. On the other hand, the key for
{{}_{2}}\mathfrak{d}_{1}^{\Xi}
is to take advantage of the low-frequency vanishing through the bounds

(4.3.40)

\lVert f-\uppi_{\Xi}f\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}\leqslant(10/\Xi)\lVert f\rVert_{H^{1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}\text{ and }\lVert\uppi_{\Xi}f\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}\leqslant\lVert f\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}\text{ for }f\in H^{1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon}),

which we apply to
f\in\{{\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{\varepsilon}u_{1}(\cdot,y)\;\mathrm{d}y,\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{\varepsilon}\partial_{1}u_{1}(\cdot,y)\;\mathrm{d}y}\}
.

Step 2 – The term
\mathfrak{d}_{2}
:
As in the proof of the previous step, we begin by expanding the expression (
4.3.28
) more carefully:

(4.3.41)

\mathfrak{d}_{2}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta,u)=-U^{\prime}(\lambda)u_{2}+(U(\lambda)-4)\partial_{1}u_{2}-y((U(\lambda-4)\boldsymbol{\zeta}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta))^{\prime}\partial^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)}}_{2}u_{2}+\boldsymbol{\zeta}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)U(\lambda)^{\prime\prime}u_{1})\\
+\varepsilon^{2}((W(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)-\overline{\upgamma}e_{1})\cdot\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)}}u_{2}+u\cdot\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)}}W_{2}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)).

Due to the inclusion
u\in H^{2}_{\mathrm{tan}}(\Omega_{\varepsilon};\mathbb{R}^{2})
, we have that
\mathrm{Tr}_{\Sigma_{0}}u_{2}=\mathrm{Tr}_{\Sigma_{0}}\partial_{1}u_{2}=0
; consequently, the first two terms in (
4.3.41
) are
\varepsilon
-small according to the first item of Lemma
3.4
. The third grouping of terms is similarly small due to the
y
-coefficient. Finally, the last grouping of terms is small due to the coefficient
\varepsilon^{2}
and the observation that
\partial_{2}^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}W_{2}
is an order one contribution (see (
4.3.3
)). These comments and straightforward computations then yield the desired bounds.

Step 3 – The term
\mathfrak{d}_{3}
:
Recall that from Lemma
3.2
we have
\lVert\cdot\rVert^{(\varepsilon,+)}_{H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})}=\lVert\cdot\rVert_{H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})}+\varepsilon^{-1/2}\lVert\cdot\rVert_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})}
. The strategy here is the same as the one used on the
{{}_{2}}\mathfrak{d}_{1}^{\Xi}
-term in the proof of the first step: low-frequency vanishing provides the bounds

(4.3.42)

\varepsilon^{-1/2}\lVert\varepsilon(\eta-\uppi_{\Xi}\eta)\rVert_{H^{1}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})}\leqslant(10/\Xi)(\varepsilon^{1/2}\lVert\eta\rVert_{H^{2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})})\text{ and }\lVert\varepsilon(\eta-\uppi_{\Xi}\eta)\rVert_{H^{3/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})}\leqslant(10/\Xi)(\varepsilon\left\lVert\eta\right\rVert_{H^{5/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})})

which leads to

(4.3.43)

\lVert\varepsilon(\eta-\uppi_{\Xi}\eta)\rVert_{H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})}^{(\varepsilon,+)}+\lVert\varepsilon(\eta-\uppi_{\Xi}\eta)^{\prime}\rVert_{H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})}^{(\varepsilon,+)}\leqslant(20/\Xi)\lVert\eta\rVert_{H^{5/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})}^{(\varepsilon)}.

The
\mathfrak{d}_{3}
-parts of the claimed estimates (
4.3.31
) and (
4.3.33
) now follow by routine arguments.

Step 4 – The term
\mathfrak{d}_{4}
:
By the definition of the boundary norm
\lVert\cdot\rVert^{(\varepsilon,-)}_{H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})}
from Lemma
3.2
we read off the bound
\lVert\cdot\rVert^{(\varepsilon,-)}_{H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})}\leqslant\varepsilon^{1/2}\lVert\cdot\rVert_{H^{1}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})}
. In turn, it suffices to prove the
\mathfrak{d}_{4}
part of estimates (
4.3.31
) and (
4.3.33
) in this stronger norm. The result now follows by vanishing low mode estimate

(4.3.44)

\varepsilon^{1/2}\lVert\eta-\uppi_{\Xi}\eta\rVert_{H^{1}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})}\leqslant(10/\Xi)\lVert\eta\rVert^{(\varepsilon)}_{H^{5/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})}.

∎

5.
Analysis of bore waves

5.1.
Existence

In this subsection we prove the existence of bore wave solutions to the free boundary Navier-Stokes system (
1.4.8
). The general strategy is to go though the solution ansatz introduced in Theorem
4.1
, which reformulates the problem in terms of solving a system of ODEs, whose solutions describe the leading order behavior, coupled to a system of PDEs for the residuals.

The careful estimates of Sections
3.2
,
4.2
, and
4.3
get us well along the way to finding the desired solutions via a contraction mapping argument. This is the ultimate goal of this subsection, but we first require a preliminary study of the interaction between the linear part of the residual PDEs (
4.1.14
) and the forcing and driving terms already studied in Section
4.3
.

As is done in Section
4.3
, we will take the physical parameters and functionalization of the ansatz constituents, residual forcing terms, and residual driving terms to be instantiated by Definitions
4.13
and
4.15
. In particular,
\beta\in(0,1)
is the size of the domain ball in (
4.3.2
), and
\varepsilon_{0}:(1,\infty)\to(0,1)
is the initial smallness threshold defined prior to Definition
4.13
.

Our first result considers all but the final equation in (
4.1.14
). We use the thin domain Stokes solution operator constructed in Section
3.2
to reformulate these equations as a fixed point identity for the velocity. We then record estimates associated with this new formulation. This is one of the key places where the extra half of a derivative gain coming from the reformulation of the conservation of relative velocity flux equation is used (compare the final equations in systems (
1.3.7
) and (
1.4.8
)). Recall the definition of the norms (
4.3.1
) and the space (
3.2.5
).

Proposition 5.1

(Velocity-pressure fixed point reformulation maps)
.

Assume the hypotheses of Definition
4.13
. For
R,\Xi\in(1,\infty)
and
0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}(R)
there exist functions
\mathfrak{R}^{\Xi}_{1}(R,\varepsilon;\cdot):F^{R}_{\varepsilon}\to H^{2}_{\mathrm{tan}}(\Omega_{\varepsilon};\mathbb{R}^{2})
,
\mathfrak{R}^{\Xi}_{2}(R,\varepsilon;\cdot):F^{R}_{\varepsilon}\to H^{1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})
,
\varepsilon_{3}:(1,\infty)\to(0,1)
, and
C_{3}:(1,\infty)\to(1,\infty)
, with
\varepsilon_{3}\leqslant\min\{{\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}}\}
, where
F^{R}_{\varepsilon}
is defined in (
4.3.4
) and the pair of functions
\varepsilon_{1}
and
\varepsilon_{2}
are from Propositions
4.14
and
4.16
(respectively), such that the following are satisfied.

(1)

For any
(\lambda,\eta,u)\in F^{R}_{\varepsilon}
upon setting

(5.1.1)

\widehat{u}=\mathfrak{R}^{\Xi}_{1}(R,\varepsilon;\lambda,\eta,u),\quad\widehat{p}=\mathfrak{R}_{2}^{\Xi}(R,\varepsilon;\lambda,\eta,u),\quad h=\boldsymbol{\zeta}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta),\\
\varphi^{1}=-(\mathfrak{f}_{1}(R,\varepsilon;\lambda,\eta,u)+\mathfrak{d}^{\Xi}_{1}(R,\varepsilon;\lambda,\eta,u),\mathfrak{f}_{2}(R,\varepsilon;\lambda,\eta,u)+\mathfrak{d}_{2}^{\Xi}(R,\varepsilon;\lambda,\eta,u)),\text{ and }\\
\psi^{1}=-(\mathfrak{f}_{3}(R,\varepsilon;\lambda,\eta)+\mathfrak{d}_{3}^{\Xi}(R,\varepsilon;\lambda,\eta),\mathfrak{f}_{4}(R,\varepsilon;\lambda,\eta)+\mathfrak{d}_{4}^{\Xi}(R,\varepsilon;\lambda,\eta))

we have that
L^{\varepsilon}_{h}(\widehat{u},\widehat{p})=(\varphi^{1},0,\psi^{1},0)
, where
L^{\varepsilon}_{h}
is the operator from Proposition
3.8
and
\boldsymbol{\zeta}
,
\mathfrak{f}_{1}
,
\mathfrak{f}_{2}
,
\mathfrak{f}_{3}
,
\mathfrak{f}_{4}
,
\mathfrak{d}_{1}^{\Xi}
,
\mathfrak{d}_{2}^{\Xi}
,
\mathfrak{d}_{3}^{\Xi}
, and
\mathfrak{d}_{4}^{\Xi}
are from Definitions
4.13
and
4.15
.

(2)

We have the uniform range estimates:

(5.1.2)

\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}1<R<\infty\\
0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{3}(R)\end{subarray}}\sup_{1<\Xi<\infty}\sup_{(\lambda,\eta,u)\in F^{R}_{\varepsilon}}(1+R/\Xi)^{-1}\lVert(\mathfrak{R}^{\Xi}_{1},\mathfrak{R}^{\Xi}_{2})(R,\varepsilon;\lambda,\eta,u)\rVert_{\mathbf{X}^{1}_{\varepsilon}}<\infty,

and the uniform Lipschitz estimates:

(5.1.3)

\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}1<R<\infty\\
0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{3}(R)\end{subarray}}\sup_{1<\Xi<\infty}\sup_{(\lambda,\eta,u)\neq(\widetilde{\lambda},\widetilde{\eta},\widetilde{u})}\frac{\lVert(\mathfrak{R}^{\Xi}_{1},\mathfrak{R}^{\Xi}_{2})(R,\varepsilon;\lambda,\eta,u)-(\mathfrak{R}^{\Xi}_{1},\mathfrak{R}^{\Xi}_{2})(R,\varepsilon;\widetilde{\lambda},\widetilde{\eta},\widetilde{u})\rVert_{\mathbf{X}^{1}_{\varepsilon}}}{\lVert\lambda-\widetilde{\lambda}\rVert_{\Lambda}+(C_{3}(R)\varepsilon+1/\Xi)(\lVert\eta-\widetilde{\eta}\rVert^{(\varepsilon)}_{H^{5/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})}+\lVert u-\widetilde{u}\rVert_{H^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})})}<\infty,

where
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\Lambda}
is as in (
4.2.20
).

Proof.

Equation (
5.1.1
) tells us how to define
\mathfrak{R}^{\Xi}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{R}^{\Xi}_{2}
, provided we can invert
L^{\varepsilon}_{h}
, namely
(\widehat{u},\widehat{p})=(L^{\varepsilon}_{h})^{-1}(\varphi^{1},0,\psi^{1},0)
. To check invertibility we note that Corollary
4.12
, paired with the definition of
\varepsilon_{0}:(1,\infty)\to(0,1)
, provide a constant
\delta_{0}>0
, depending only on
\mathrm{H}_{-}
and the number
M
from the aforementioned corollary, such that for all
0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}(R)
, we have
h=\boldsymbol{\zeta}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)\in Z_{\delta_{0}}
, where the latter set is defined in (
3.2.4
); Theorem
3.9
thus applies, allowing us to work in conjunction with Definitions
4.13
and
4.15
to define the maps in this way.

The operator bound (
5.1.2
) is then a consequence of the left hand estimate in (
3.2.73
) coupled to the bounds (
4.3.12
) and (
4.3.31
) of Propositions
4.14
and
4.16
.

The Lipschitz continuity bound (
5.1.3
) is a result of the right hand estimate in (
3.2.73
) paired with the difference estimates (
4.3.14
), (
4.3.15
), (
4.3.32
), and (
4.3.33
) coming from the aforementioned propositions.
∎

Our second result now studies the final equation in system (
4.1.14
). By using the first term, we may solve for the residual free surface in terms of the velocity up to some remainder. In what follows we implement this idea and prove some important estimates.

Proposition 5.2

(Free surface fixed point reformulation map)
.

Assume the hypotheses of Definition
4.13
. For
R,\Xi\in(1,\infty)
and
0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}(R)
there exists functions
\mathfrak{R}_{0}(R,\varepsilon;\cdot):F^{R}_{\varepsilon}\to H^{5/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})
,
\varepsilon_{4}:(1,\infty)\to(0,1)
, and
C_{4}:(1,\infty)\to(1,\infty)
, with
\varepsilon_{4}\leqslant\varepsilon_{1}
, where
F^{R}_{\varepsilon}
is defined in (
4.3.4
) and
\varepsilon_{1}
is from Proposition
4.14
, such that the following are satisfied.

(1)

For any
(\lambda,\eta,u)\in F^{R}_{\varepsilon}
, upon setting
\widehat{\eta}=\mathfrak{R}_{0}(R,\varepsilon;\lambda,\eta,u)
we have the identity

(5.1.4)

(1-\partial_{1}^{2})((4+\varepsilon^{2}\overline{\upgamma}-U(\lambda))\widehat{\eta})=\frac{\boldsymbol{\zeta}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)}{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{\varepsilon}u_{1}(\cdot,y)\;\mathrm{d}y+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\partial_{1}(u\cdot\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon\boldsymbol{\zeta}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)})-\mathfrak{f}_{0}(R,\varepsilon;\lambda,\eta)

on
\Sigma_{\varepsilon}
, where
\mathfrak{f}_{0}
is from (
4.3.6
).

(2)

We have the uniform range estimates:

(5.1.5)

\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}1<R<\infty\\
0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{4}(R)\end{subarray}}\sup_{(\lambda,\eta,u)\in F^{R}_{\varepsilon}}(1+\lVert u\rVert_{H^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})})^{-1}\lVert\mathfrak{R}_{0}(R,\varepsilon;\lambda,\eta,u)\rVert_{H^{5/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})}^{(\varepsilon)}<\infty

and the uniform Lipschitz estimates:

(5.1.6)

\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}1<R<\infty\\
0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{4}(R)\end{subarray}}\sup_{(\lambda,\eta,u)\neq(\widetilde{\lambda},\widetilde{\eta},\widetilde{u})}\frac{\lVert\mathfrak{R}_{0}(R,\varepsilon;\lambda,\eta,u)-\mathfrak{R}_{0}(R,\varepsilon;\widetilde{\lambda},\widetilde{\eta},\widetilde{u})\rVert_{H^{5/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})}^{(\varepsilon)}}{\lVert\lambda-\widetilde{\lambda}\rVert_{\Lambda}+C_{4}(R)\varepsilon\lVert\eta-\widetilde{\eta}\rVert^{(\varepsilon)}_{H^{5/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})}+\lVert u-\widetilde{u}\rVert_{H^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}}<\infty,

where
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\Lambda}
is as in (
4.2.20
).

Proof.

We begin with the observation that the number
\overline{\upgamma}
defined in (
1.4.5
) is strictly positive, so the fourth item of Proposition
4.10
provides a constant
1<M<\infty
for which

(5.1.7)

\inf_{0<\varepsilon<1}\inf_{\lambda\in\overline{B(0,\beta)}}\inf_{\mathbb{R}}(4+\varepsilon^{2}\overline{\upgamma}-U(\lambda))\geqslant 1/M.

With this bound in mind, the expression (
5.1.4
) suggests how we must define the map
\mathfrak{R}_{0}
; namely, for
1<R<\infty
,
0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}(R)
, and
(\lambda,\eta,u)\in F^{R}_{\varepsilon}
we set

(5.1.8)

\mathfrak{R}_{0}(R,\varepsilon;\lambda,\eta,u)=\frac{1}{4+\varepsilon^{2}\overline{\upgamma}-U(\lambda)}(1-\partial_{1}^{2})^{-1}\Big(\frac{\boldsymbol{\zeta}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)}{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{\varepsilon}u_{1}(\cdot,y)\;\mathrm{d}y+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\partial_{1}(u\cdot\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon\boldsymbol{\zeta}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)})-\mathfrak{f}_{0}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)\Big).

Now the proof of the estimates (
5.1.5
) and (
5.1.6
) follows from routine computations, as soon as we point out the key observations. Firstly, we recall that the norm in question,
\lVert\cdot\rVert^{(\varepsilon)}_{H^{5/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})}
, is defined in (
4.3.1
). It asks that we take the sum of
\varepsilon^{1/2}
multiplying the
H^{2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})
-norm and
\varepsilon
multiplying the
H^{5/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})
norm. In either case (thanks in part to (
5.1.7
)), we can handle the
(4+\varepsilon^{2}\overline{\upgamma}-U(\lambda))^{-1}
as a multiplier and take it in
W^{3,\infty}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})
while implementing Corollary
4.12
.

In this way we can reduce to studying the argument of
(1-\partial_{1}^{2})^{-1}
in (
5.1.8
) in the norm
\varepsilon^{1/2}\lVert\cdot\rVert_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})}+\varepsilon\lVert\cdot\rVert_{H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})}
. For the
\mathfrak{f}_{0}
term we can crudely go up with the
H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})
-norm and employ estimates (
4.3.12
) and (
4.3.13
) from Proposition
4.14
. Therefore, we can reduce to studying the
u
-dependent terms of (
5.1.8
). For the vertical average term, we only need to note the bound

(5.1.9)

\varepsilon^{1/2}\Big\lVert\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{\varepsilon}u_{1}(\cdot,y)\;\mathrm{d}y\Big\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}+\varepsilon\Big\lVert\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{\varepsilon}u_{1}(\cdot,y)\;\mathrm{d}y\Big\rVert_{H^{1/2}(\mathbb{R})}\lesssim\lVert u\rVert_{H^{1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})},

which follows directly from Cauchy-Schwarz. For the remaining term we note that it expands as

(5.1.10)

\partial_{1}(u\cdot\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon\boldsymbol{\zeta}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)})/\varepsilon=(\partial_{1}u_{2})/\varepsilon-\partial_{1}(\boldsymbol{\zeta}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)u_{1}).

The second term in this decomposition is straightforward to estimate using the second item of Lemma
3.3
. For the first term on the right hand side of (
5.1.10
), we use the estimates

(5.1.11)

\varepsilon^{1/2}\Big\lVert\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\partial_{1}u_{2}\Big\rVert_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})}+\varepsilon\Big\lVert\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\partial_{1}u_{2}\Big\rVert_{H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})}\lesssim\lVert u\rVert_{H^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})},

which are a consequence of the first item of Lemma
3.3
and
\mathrm{Tr}_{\Sigma_{0}}\partial_{1}u_{2}=0
. The implicit constants in (
5.1.9
) and (
5.1.11
) are independent of
u
and
\varepsilon
.
∎

We are, at long last, ready to produce solution to the coupled ODE - PDE system from the fifth and sixth items of Theorem
4.1
via a contraction mapping argument. The Banach space in which we will run the argument is given by

(5.1.12)

\mathbf{W}_{\varepsilon}=H^{5/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})\times H^{2}_{\mathrm{tan}}(\Omega_{\varepsilon};\mathbb{R}^{2})\text{ with norm }\lVert\eta,u\rVert_{\mathbf{W}_{\varepsilon}}=\max\{{\lVert\eta\rVert^{(\varepsilon)}_{H^{5/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})},\lVert u\rVert_{H^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}}\}.

Also, recall the definition of the mollification operators
\uppi_{\Xi}
given before Proposition
4.9
. Notice that in the following result we are taking the smoothing rate
\Xi=\char 12\relax{\log\varepsilon}
. We are also finally linking the parameter
\lambda\in\overline{B(0,\beta)}\subset[{(C^{10}\cap W^{10,\infty}\cap H^{10})(\mathbb{R})}]^{2}
back to the residual in the form promised by Proposition
4.9
.

Proposition 5.3

(Existence of a fixed point)
.

There exists
R_{\star}\in(1,\infty)
and
0<\epsilon_{\star}<\min\{{1/3,\varepsilon_{0}(R_{\star})}\}
for which the following hold for all
\varepsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{\star})
.

(1)

The following maps are well-defined: the coupling function
\boldsymbol{\uplambda}(\varepsilon;\cdot):\overline{B_{\mathbf{W}_{\varepsilon}}(0,R_{\star})}\to\overline{B(0,\beta)}
defined via

(5.1.13)

\boldsymbol{\uplambda}(\varepsilon;\eta,u)=\varepsilon^{2}\Big(\uppi_{\char 12\relax{\log\varepsilon}}\eta,\uppi_{\char 12\relax{\log\varepsilon}}\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{\varepsilon}u_{1}(\cdot,y)\;\mathrm{d}y\Big),

the velocity update function
\boldsymbol{\mathsf{u}}(\varepsilon;\cdot):\overline{B_{\mathbf{W}_{\varepsilon}}(0,R_{\star})}\to\{{u\in H^{2}_{\mathrm{tan}}(\Omega_{\varepsilon};\mathbb{R}^{2})\;:\;\lVert u\rVert_{H^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}\leqslant\sqrt{R_{\star}/2}}\}
defined via

(5.1.14)

\boldsymbol{\mathsf{u}}(\varepsilon;\eta,u)=\mathfrak{R}^{\char 12\relax{\log\varepsilon}}_{1}(R_{\star},\varepsilon;\boldsymbol{\uplambda}(\varepsilon;\eta,u),\eta,u),

where
\mathfrak{R}_{1}
is defined in Propositions
5.1
;
and the free surface update function
\boldsymbol{\upeta}(\varepsilon;\cdot):\overline{B_{\mathbf{W}_{\varepsilon}}(0,R_{\star})}\to\{{\eta\in H^{5/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})\;:\;\lVert\eta\rVert^{(\varepsilon)}_{H^{5/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})}\leqslant R_{\star}/2}\}
defined via

(5.1.15)

\boldsymbol{\upeta}(\varepsilon;\eta,u)=\mathfrak{R}_{0}(R_{\star},\varepsilon;\boldsymbol{\uplambda}(\varepsilon;\eta,u),\eta,\boldsymbol{\mathsf{u}}(\varepsilon;\eta,u)),

where
\mathfrak{R}_{0}
is defined in Proposition
5.2
. In particular, the update map
\boldsymbol{\mathsf{F}}(\varepsilon;\cdot):\overline{B_{\mathbf{W}_{\varepsilon}}(0,R_{\star})}\to\overline{B_{\mathbf{W}_{\varepsilon}}(0,R_{\star}/2)}
defined via

(5.1.16)

\boldsymbol{\mathsf{F}}(\varepsilon;\eta,u)=(\boldsymbol{\upeta}(\varepsilon;\eta,u),\boldsymbol{\mathsf{u}}(\varepsilon;\eta,u))

is well-defined.

(2)

There exists a unique
(\eta,u)\in\overline{B_{\mathbf{W}_{\varepsilon}}(0,R_{\star})}
obeying the fixed point identity
(\eta,u)=\boldsymbol{\mathsf{F}}(\varepsilon;\eta,u)
.

(3)

Using the fixed point
(\eta,u)\in\overline{B_{\mathbf{W}_{\varepsilon}}(0,R_{\star})}
from the previous item, define
\lambda=\boldsymbol{\uplambda}(\varepsilon;\eta,u)\in\overline{B(0,\beta)}
and
p=\mathfrak{R}_{2}^{\char 12\relax{\log\varepsilon}}(R_{\star},\varepsilon;\lambda,\eta,u)\in H^{1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})
, with
\mathfrak{R}_{2}
as in Proposition
5.1
. Then
(\eta,u,p)
