along with the following evaluations of the bore map constituents (
4.3.2
)

(5.1.17)

H=H(\lambda),\;U=U(\lambda),\;U_{1}=U_{1}(\lambda),\;U_{2}=U_{2}(\lambda),\;P=P(\lambda),\;P_{1}=P_{1}(\lambda),\;P_{2}=P_{2}(\lambda),\\
\mathfrak{r}_{1}=\Big((U-4)\uppi_{\char 12\relax{\log\varepsilon}}\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{\varepsilon}u_{1}(\cdot,y)\;\mathrm{d}y\Big)^{\prime},\;\mathfrak{r}_{3}=\varepsilon\mathfrak{L}\uppi_{\char 12\relax{\log\varepsilon}}\eta,\;\mathfrak{r}_{4}=\mathrm{g}\uppi_{\char 12\relax{\log\varepsilon}}\eta

satisfy the first and third through sixth items of Theorem
4.1
; in particular, these are a solution to the system of equations given by (
4.1.11
), (
4.1.12
), (
4.1.13
), and (
4.1.14
) with
\inf\boldsymbol{\zeta}>0
(where
\boldsymbol{\zeta}=H+\varepsilon^{2}\eta
).

Proof.

We begin with the construction of the maps (
5.1.13
), (
5.1.14
), and (
5.1.15
). First, we note that the following Bernstein-type estimate holds:

(5.1.18)

\sup_{0<\varepsilon<1/3}\sup_{(\eta,u)\in\mathbf{W}_{\varepsilon}\setminus\{{0}\}}\frac{\big\lVert\varepsilon^{2}\big(\uppi_{\char 12\relax{\log\varepsilon}}\eta,\uppi_{\char 12\relax{\log\varepsilon}}\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{\varepsilon}u_{1}(\cdot,y)\;\mathrm{d}y\big)\big\rVert_{\Lambda}}{\varepsilon^{3/2}\char 12\relax{\log\varepsilon}^{9}\lVert\eta,u\rVert_{\mathbf{W}_{\varepsilon}}}<\infty.

The exponent
9
here is perhaps suboptimal, but sufficient to deduce the existence of a function
\varepsilon_{5}:(1,\infty)\to(0,1/3)
with the property that

(5.1.19)

\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}1<R<\infty\\
0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{5}(R)\end{subarray}}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\eta,u)\in\mathbf{W}_{\varepsilon}\\
\lVert\eta,u\rVert_{\mathbf{W}_{\varepsilon}}\leqslant R\end{subarray}}\Big\lVert\varepsilon^{2}\Big(\uppi_{\char 12\relax{\log\varepsilon}}\eta,\uppi_{\char 12\relax{\log\varepsilon}}\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{\varepsilon}u_{1}(\cdot,y)\;\mathrm{d}y\Big)\Big\rVert_{\Lambda}\leqslant\frac{\beta}{2},

where
\beta>0
is as in Definition
4.13
. Estimate (
5.1.19
) then permits us to define the coupling map
\boldsymbol{\uplambda}(\varepsilon;\cdot):\overline{B_{\mathbf{W}_{\varepsilon}}(0,R)}\to\overline{B(0,\beta)}
via the formula (
5.1.13
) for any
1<R<\infty
and any
0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{5}(R)
.

The next step is to unpack estimates (
5.1.2
) and (
5.1.5
) from Propositions
5.1
and
5.2
. If we let
\varepsilon_{6}:(1,\infty)\to(0,1/3)
be defined via
\varepsilon_{6}=\min\{{\varepsilon_{3},\varepsilon_{4},\varepsilon_{5}}\}
, then the aforementioned estimates provide a constant
N\in(1,\infty)
with the property that for all
1<R<\infty
,
0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{6}(R)
, and
(\lambda,\eta,u)\in F^{R}_{\varepsilon}
(where the latter set is defined in (
4.3.4
)) we have the estimates

(5.1.20)

\lVert\mathfrak{R}_{1}^{\char 12\relax{\log\varepsilon}}(R,\varepsilon;\lambda,\eta,u)\rVert_{H^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}\leqslant N(1+R/\char 12\relax{\log\varepsilon})\text{ and }\lVert\mathfrak{R}_{0}(R,\varepsilon;\lambda,\eta,u)\rVert_{H^{5/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})}^{(\varepsilon)}\leqslant N(1+\lVert u\rVert_{H^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}).

Estimate (
5.1.20
) suggests that we define
R_{\star}=8N^{2}
and
\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{\star}\in(0,1/3)
via
\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{\star}=\min\{{\varepsilon_{6}(R_{\star}),e^{-R_{\star}}}\}
. Then for all
0<\varepsilon<\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{\star}
and all
(\lambda,\eta,u)\in F^{R_{\star}}_{\varepsilon}
we deduce the bounds

(5.1.21)

\lVert\mathfrak{R}_{1}^{\char 12\relax{\log\varepsilon}}(R_{\star},\varepsilon;\lambda,\eta,u)\rVert_{H^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}\leqslant\sqrt{R_{\star}/2}\text{ and }\lVert\mathfrak{R}_{0}(R_{\star},\varepsilon;\lambda,\eta,\mathfrak{R}^{\char 12\relax{\log\varepsilon}}_{1}(R_{\star},\varepsilon;\lambda,u,\eta))\rVert^{(\varepsilon)}_{H^{5/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})}\leqslant R_{\star}/2.

Through the combination of estimates (
5.1.19
) and (
5.1.21
) we deduce that (
5.1.14
), (
5.1.15
), and (
5.1.16
) are well-defined functions mapping between the stated spaces for
0<\varepsilon<\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{\star}
. This completes the proof of the first item.

We next prove the second item. By unpacking estimates (
5.1.3
) and (
5.1.18
) and using the linearity of
\boldsymbol{\uplambda}(\varepsilon;\cdot)
, we find a constant
M_{1}\in(1,\infty)
with the property that for all
0<\varepsilon<\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{\star}
and all
(\eta,u),(\widetilde{\eta},\widetilde{u})\in\overline{B_{\mathbf{W}_{\varepsilon}}(0,R_{\star})}
we have the velocity update map Lipschitz estimate

(5.1.22)

\lVert\boldsymbol{\mathsf{u}}(\varepsilon;\eta,u)-\boldsymbol{\mathsf{u}}(\varepsilon;\widetilde{\eta},\widetilde{u})\rVert_{H^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}\leqslant M_{1}(\varepsilon^{3/2}\char 12\relax{\log\varepsilon}^{9}+1/\char 12\relax{\log\varepsilon})\lVert\eta-\widetilde{\eta},u-\widetilde{u}\rVert_{\mathbf{W}_{\varepsilon}}.

We then combine the above considerations, estimate (
5.1.22
), and an unpacking of estimate (
5.1.6
) in order to be granted the existence of another constant
M_{0}\in(1,\infty)
with the property that for all
0<\varepsilon<\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{\star}
and all
(\eta,u),(\widetilde{\eta},\widetilde{u})\in\overline{B_{\mathbf{W}_{\varepsilon}}(0,R_{\star})}
we have the free surface update map Lipschitz estimate

(5.1.23)

\lVert\boldsymbol{\upeta}(\varepsilon;\eta,u)-\boldsymbol{\upeta}(\varepsilon;\widetilde{\eta},\widetilde{u})\rVert^{(\varepsilon)}_{H^{5/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})}\leqslant M_{0}(\varepsilon^{3/2}\char 12\relax{\log\varepsilon}^{9}+1/\char 12\relax{\log\varepsilon})\lVert\eta-\widetilde{\eta},u-\widetilde{\eta}\rVert_{\mathbf{W}_{\varepsilon}}.

Upon synthesizing estimates (
5.1.22
) and (
5.1.23
), we find the existence of
0<\epsilon_{\star}<\widetilde{\varepsilon}_{\star}
such that for all
0<\varepsilon<\epsilon_{\star}
the update map (
5.1.16
) is a contraction on the complete metric space
\overline{B_{\mathbf{W}_{\varepsilon}}(0,R_{\star})}
. The second item now follows from Banach’s fixed point theorem.

To prove the third item we must unpack the meaning of the fixed point found in the second item. Examining the second component of (
5.1.16
), we see that the velocity part
u
of the fixed point satisfies (by (
5.1.14
))

(5.1.24)

u=\boldsymbol{\mathsf{u}}(\varepsilon;\eta,u)=\mathfrak{R}^{\char 12\relax{\log\varepsilon}}_{1}(R_{\star},\varepsilon;\boldsymbol{\uplambda}(\varepsilon;\eta,u),\eta,u).

Extracting the first component of (
5.1.16
) (given by (
5.1.15
)) and substituting (
5.1.24
), we then find

(5.1.25)

\eta=\boldsymbol{\upeta}(\varepsilon;\eta,u)=\mathfrak{R}_{0}(R_{\star},\varepsilon;\boldsymbol{\uplambda}(\varepsilon;\eta,u),\eta,u).

We then define
\lambda=\boldsymbol{\uplambda}(\varepsilon;\eta,u)\in\overline{B(0,\beta)}
,
p=\mathfrak{R}^{\char 12\relax{\log\varepsilon}}_{2}(R_{\star},\varepsilon;\lambda,\eta,u)\in H^{1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})
, and
H,U,U_{1},U_{2},P,P_{1},P_{2}\in W^{\infty,\infty}(\mathbb{R})
via the evaluation of the bore maps (
4.3.2
) at
\lambda
(here we are implicitly using the smoothness assertion of the third item of Proposition
4.10
). Also set
\mathfrak{r}_{1},\mathfrak{r}_{3},\mathfrak{r}_{4}\in H^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
as in (
5.1.17
), where
\mathfrak{L}
is the linear operator defined in (
4.1.5
), and
\boldsymbol{\zeta}=H+\varepsilon^{2}\eta
. Note that by Corollary
4.12
, it follows that
\inf_{\mathbb{R}}\boldsymbol{\zeta}\geqslant\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{H}_{-}>0
.

The first item of Proposition
5.1
tells us that
L^{\varepsilon}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}(u,p)=(\varphi^{1},0,\psi^{1},0)
where
\varphi^{1}
and
\psi^{1}
are defined as in (
5.1.1
),
\lambda
is as above, and
\Xi=\char 12\relax{\log\varepsilon}
. By using the expressions (
4.3.27
), (
4.3.28
), (
4.3.29
), and (
4.3.30
), we then compute

(5.1.26)

\mathfrak{d}_{1}^{\Xi}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta,u)=(X-(4+\varepsilon^{2}\overline{\upgamma})e_{1})\cdot\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}u_{1}+u\cdot\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}X_{1}-\mathfrak{r}_{1},

(5.1.27)

\mathfrak{d}_{2}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta,u)=(X-(4+\varepsilon^{2}\overline{\upgamma})e_{1})\cdot\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}u_{2}+u\cdot\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}X_{2},

(5.1.28)

\mathfrak{d}^{\Xi}_{3}(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)=\varepsilon\mathfrak{L}\eta-\mathfrak{r}_{3},

and

(5.1.29)

\mathfrak{d}^{\Xi}_{4}(\lambda)=\mathrm{g}\eta-\mathfrak{r}_{4},

where
X=V+\varepsilon^{2}W
and
V
,
W
,
Q
are defined in terms of
\boldsymbol{\zeta}
,
U
,
U_{1}
,
U_{2}
,
P
,
P_{1}
,
P_{2}
and
\lambda
as in (
4.3.3
). Hence, from
L^{\varepsilon}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}(u,p)=(\varphi^{1},0,\psi^{1},0)
(and the definition (
3.2.23
)) we deduce that
(u,p)
solve all but the last equation in system (
4.1.14
) where
\mathfrak{f}_{i}
, for
i\in\{{1,2,3,4}\}
, are the appropriate evaluations of the maps (
4.3.7
), (
4.3.8
), (
4.3.9
), and (
4.3.10
), which coincide with the residual forcing terms (
4.1.7
), (
4.1.8
), (
4.1.9
), and (
4.1.10
) considered in the fourth item of Theorem
4.1
.

To see that
\eta
solves the final equation in system (
4.1.14
), we turn to the first item of Proposition
5.2
, which provides the desired identity thanks to (
5.1.25
). Hence, we conclude that
(\eta,u,p)\in H^{5/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})\times H^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon};\mathbb{R}^{2})\times H^{1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})
is a solution to the residual PDEs (
4.1.14
). On the other hand, the ODEs (
4.1.11
), (
4.1.12
), and (
4.1.13
) are satisfied thanks to the fifth item of Proposition
4.10
.
∎

We can recast the previous result as granting us the existence of bore wave solutions to the free boundary Navier-Stokes system (
1.4.8
). See Figure
3
for a graphical representation of the free surface component of some of these traveling these bore waves.

Theorem 5.4

(Existence of bore waves)
.

Let
\upmu,\mathrm{a}>0
,
\iota\in\{{-1,1}\}
,
(\mathrm{g},\mathrm{A})\in\mathfrak{C}_{\iota}
,
\upsigma\geqslant 0
. There exists
\mathsf{H}\in W^{\infty,\infty}(\mathbb{R})
,
C,\delta\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
, and
\epsilon_{\star}\in(0,1/3)
with the property that for all
\varepsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{\star})
, there exists
(\overline{\eta},\overline{u},\overline{p})\in H^{5/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})\times H^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon};\mathbb{R}^{2})\times H^{1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})
such that the following hold.

(1)

We have
\lVert\overline{\eta}\rVert_{H^{5/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})}+\lVert\overline{u}\rVert_{(L^{\infty}\cap H^{2})(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}+\lVert\overline{p}\rVert_{H^{1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}\leqslant C\varepsilon
.

(2)

The function
\mathsf{H}
is a solution to the one-dimensional shallow water equation (
1.5.3
) and we have
\inf\mathsf{H}=\mathrm{H}_{-}>0
,
\inf(\mathsf{H}+\overline{\eta})\geqslant\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{H}_{-}>0
, and
\lim_{x\to\pm\infty}\mathsf{H}(\iota x)=\lim_{x\to\pm\infty}(\mathsf{H}+\overline{\eta})(\iota x)=\mathrm{H}_{\pm}
.

(3)

Upon defining
\boldsymbol{\zeta}
,
\boldsymbol{u}
, and
\boldsymbol{p}
as in (
1.5.5
),
we have that
(\boldsymbol{\zeta},\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{p})\in H^{5/2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})\times H^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega_{\varepsilon};\mathbb{R}^{2})\times H^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})
is a strong solution to the free boundary Navier-Stokes system (
1.4.8
), moreover
\inf(4-\boldsymbol{u}_{1})\geqslant\delta
.

Proof.

Let
\epsilon_{\star}\in(0,1/3)
be the number granted by Proposition
5.3
,
0<\varepsilon<\epsilon_{\star}
,
(\eta,u,p)\in H^{5/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})\times H^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon};\mathbb{R}^{2})\times H^{1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})
be as in the third item of Proposition
5.3
,
\lambda=\boldsymbol{\uplambda}(\varepsilon;\eta,u)
(for the map as in (
5.1.13
)), and
H,U,U_{1},U_{2},P,P_{1},P_{2}\in W^{\infty,\infty}(\mathbb{R})
as in (
5.1.17
).

We combine the conclusion of the third item of Proposition
5.3
with Theorem
4.1
to deduce that the functions
\boldsymbol{\zeta}
,
\boldsymbol{u}
, and
\boldsymbol{p}
defined as in (
4.1.2
), (
4.1.3
), and (
4.1.4
) are a solution to the free boundary Navier-Stokes system (
1.4.8
). We then define
\overline{\eta}
,
\overline{u}
, and
\overline{p}
(which appear in (
1.5.5
)) to be the difference between
\boldsymbol{\zeta}
,
\boldsymbol{u}
, and
\boldsymbol{p}
and their respective distinguished parts. In other words,
\overline{\eta}
,
\overline{u}
, and
\overline{p}
are defined such that (
1.5.5
) is true.

According to Proposition
4.10
, specifically (
4.2.19
), we have decompositions of the type
Z=\mathsf{Z}+\mathfrak{Z}(\lambda)

for the quantities
Z\in\{H,U,U_{1},U_{2},P,P_{1},P_{2}\}
, where
\mathsf{Z}
is part of the distinguished bore solution; for instance,
Z=H
,
\mathsf{Z}=\mathsf{H}
, and
\mathfrak{Z}(\lambda)=\mathfrak{h}(\lambda)
. Because of the choice of
\lambda
in (
5.1.13
), the bounds (
5.1.18
), and the Lipschitz bounds (
4.2.17
) of the maps involved, the non-distinguished part in each decomposition is small in the sense that we have the estimate

(5.1.30)

\lVert\mathfrak{Z}(\lambda)\rVert_{W^{7,\infty}\cap H^{7}}\leqslant C\varepsilon.

Next, we aim to show that the bounds (
5.1.30
) imply the estimate claimed in the first item. The free surface term is simplest:
\overline{\eta}=\boldsymbol{\zeta}-\mathsf{H}
, and hence

(5.1.31)

\lVert\overline{\eta}\rVert_{H^{5/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})}=\lVert\mathfrak{h}(\lambda)+\varepsilon^{2}\eta\rVert_{H^{5/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})}\leqslant C\varepsilon+\varepsilon\lVert\eta\rVert^{(\varepsilon)}_{H^{5/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})}\leqslant C\varepsilon.

Next we handle the vertical velocity remainder,
\overline{u}_{2}
, which has the expression

(5.1.32)

\overline{u}_{2}=\boldsymbol{u}_{2}=-\Big((y\boldsymbol{\zeta})U^{\prime}+\varepsilon\frac{1}{2}(y\boldsymbol{\zeta})^{2}U_{1}^{\prime}+\frac{1}{6}(y\boldsymbol{\zeta})^{3}U_{2}^{\prime}\Big)+\varepsilon^{2}u_{2}.

Corollary
4.12
and the third item of Lemma
3.4
permit us to take the norm of the above expression in
(L^{\infty}\cap H^{2})(\Omega_{\varepsilon})
and verify that
\lVert\overline{u}_{2}\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}\leqslant C(\varepsilon+\varepsilon^{3}+\varepsilon^{2}\lVert u_{2}\rVert_{(L^{\infty}\cap H^{2})(\Omega_{\varepsilon})})\leqslant C\varepsilon
.

The horizontal velocity remainder,
\overline{u}_{1}
, is somewhat more involved. By rewriting the expression for
\boldsymbol{u}_{1}
, as defined in the first item of Theorem
4.1
, via

(5.1.33)

\boldsymbol{u}_{1}=\mathsf{U}+\varepsilon(y\mathsf{H})\mathsf{U}_{1}+\frac{1}{2}(y\mathsf{H})^{2}\mathsf{U}_{2}+\varepsilon[{(y\boldsymbol{\zeta})-(y\mathsf{H})}]\mathsf{u}_{1}+\frac{1}{2}[{(y\boldsymbol{\zeta})^{2}-(y\mathsf{H})^{2}}]\mathsf{U}_{2}+\mathfrak{u}(\lambda)+\varepsilon(y\boldsymbol{\zeta})\mathfrak{u}_{1}(\lambda)+\frac{1}{2}(y\boldsymbol{\zeta})^{2}\mathfrak{u}_{2}(\lambda)+\varepsilon^{2}u,

we learn that

(5.1.34)

\Big\lVert\boldsymbol{u}_{1}-\Big(\mathsf{U}+\varepsilon(y\mathsf{H})\mathsf{U}_{1}+\frac{1}{2}(y\mathsf{H})^{2}\mathsf{U}_{2}\Big)\Big\rVert_{(L^{\infty}\cap H^{2})(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}\leqslant C\varepsilon.

On the other hand, by using identities (
4.2.26
), we may further expand

(5.1.35)

\mathsf{U}+\varepsilon(y\mathsf{H})\mathsf{U}_{1}+\frac{1}{2}(y\mathsf{H})^{2}\mathsf{U}_{2}=4-\frac{\mathrm{A}}{\mathrm{H}}+\frac{4\mathrm{a}}{\upmu}\mathsf{H}^{2}\Big(\varepsilon y-\frac{1}{2}y^{2}\Big)+\Big[{\frac{\mathrm{a}}{\upmu}(4\mathsf{H}-\mathrm{A}-4\mathsf{H}^{2})\Big(\varepsilon y-\frac{1}{2}y^{2}\Big)+\frac{1}{2}(y\mathsf{H})^{2}\Big(\mathsf{U}^{\prime\prime}+4\frac{\mathsf{H}^{\prime}\mathsf{U}^{\prime}}{\mathsf{H}}\Big)}\Big].

The final term in square brackets in (
5.1.35
) has size at most
C\varepsilon
when measured in the norm
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{(L^{\infty}\cap H^{2})(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}
, thanks again to Corollary
4.12
and the bounds

(5.1.36)

\lVert\varepsilon y(4\mathsf{H}=\mathrm{A}-4\mathsf{H}^{2})\rVert_{(L^{\infty}\cap H^{2})(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}+\lVert y^{2}(4\mathsf{H}-\mathrm{A}-4\mathsf{H}^{2})\rVert_{(L^{\infty}\cap H^{2})(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}\leqslant C\varepsilon,

which follow from (
1.4.4
) and the first and second items of Proposition
4.10
. Synthesizing the above observations yields the bound

(5.1.37)

\lVert\overline{u}_{1}\rVert_{(L^{\infty}\cap H^{2})(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}=\Big\lVert\boldsymbol{u}_{1}-\Big(4-\frac{\mathrm{A}}{\mathrm{H}}+\frac{4\mathrm{a}}{\upmu}\mathsf{H}^{2}\Big(\varepsilon y-\frac{1}{2}y^{2}\Big)\Big)\Big\rVert_{(L^{\infty}\cap H^{2})(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}\leqslant C\varepsilon.

We now estimate the pressure remainder
\overline{p}
. We use (
4.2.26
) to write

(5.1.38)

\overline{p}=\boldsymbol{p}-\Big(\mathrm{g}\mathsf{H}-2\upmu\mathrm{A}\frac{\mathsf{H}^{\prime}}{\mathsf{H}^{2}}\Big)=\mathfrak{p}(\lambda)+\varepsilon(y\boldsymbol{\zeta})(\mathsf{P}_{1}+\mathsf{p}_{1}(\lambda))+\frac{1}{2}(y\boldsymbol{\zeta})^{2}(\mathsf{P}_{2}+\mathfrak{p}_{2}(\lambda)).

The estimate
\lVert\overline{p}\rVert_{H^{1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}\leqslant C\varepsilon
now follows by Proposition
4.10
. This completes the proof of the first item.

We now turn to the proof the second item. These claims are a direct consequences of the first item of Proposition
4.10
and the calculation done at the beginning of Section
2.1
, which takes us from the system of ODEs (
2.1.1
) to the ODE (
1.5.3
).

Finally, we prove the third item. We have already seen that
(\boldsymbol{\zeta},\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{p})
belongs to the stated space and solves the desired equations. In addition, since
\sup(4-\mathrm{A}/\mathsf{H})<4
we may let
0<\delta=(1/2)(\mathrm{A}/\max\mathsf{H})
and enforce the bound
\inf(4-\boldsymbol{u}_{1})\geqslant\delta
, by making
\epsilon_{\star}
smaller if necessary.
∎

5.2.
Regularity

We now take the solutions to the free boundary Navier-Stokes system produced by Theorem
5.4
and establish that they are, in fact, smooth. It is temping to think that because of the similarities between equations (
1.4.8
) and the elliptic system of Stokes equations, we should get elliptic regularity for free and hence smoothness of solutions. However, while this is true in spirit, in practice some care is needed since the system of equations is quasilinear and the free surface unknown is not defined in the bulk. Nevertheless, our presentation here follows the familiar themes of elliptic regularity and, as such, is abbreviated as much as possible.

The following definition introduces the ingredients for this regularity promotion. Note that in what follows there are some similarities with the objects of Theorem
4.1
and Definition
4.13
.

Definition 5.5

(Objects for regularity promotion theory)
.

Assume the hypotheses of Definition
4.13
and let
\mathsf{H}\in W^{\infty,\infty}(\mathbb{R})
and
\delta,\epsilon_{\star}\in(0,1/3)
be the objects produced by Theorem
5.4
. Define
\mathsf{U},\mathsf{U}_{1},\mathsf{U}_{2},\mathsf{P}
,
\mathsf{P}_{1},\mathsf{P}_{2}\in W^{\infty,\infty}(\mathbb{R})
in terms of
\mathsf{H}
as in (
4.2.26
). Given any
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\varepsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{\star})
we define the Banach spaces of functions
\mathbf{U}^{n}_{\varepsilon}
and
\mathbf{V}^{n}_{\varepsilon}
via

(5.2.1)

\mathbf{U}^{n}_{\varepsilon}=H^{n+5/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})\times H^{n+2}_{\mathrm{tan}}(\Omega_{\varepsilon};\mathbb{R}^{2})\times H^{n+1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})\text{ and}\\
\mathbf{V}^{n}_{\varepsilon}=H^{n+1/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})\times H^{n}(\Omega_{\varepsilon};\mathbb{R}^{2})\times H^{n+1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})\times H^{n+1/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon};\mathbb{R}^{2})\times H^{n+1/2}(\Sigma_{0}).

The spaces of (
5.2.1
) are endowed with the norms

(5.2.2)

\lVert\eta,u,p\rVert_{\mathbf{U}^{n}_{\varepsilon}}=\lVert\eta\rVert^{(\varepsilon)}_{H^{5/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})}+\lVert u,p\rVert_{\mathbf{X}^{1}_{\varepsilon}}+n(\lVert\eta\rVert_{H^{n+5/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})}+\lVert u\rVert_{H^{n+2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}+\lVert p\rVert_{H^{n+1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})})\text{ and}\\
\lVert\psi^{0},\varphi^{1},\varphi^{2},\psi^{1},\psi^{2}\rVert_{\mathbf{V}^{n}_{\varepsilon}}=\varepsilon^{1/2}\lVert\psi^{0}\rVert_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})}+\varepsilon\lVert\psi^{0}\rVert_{H^{1/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})}+\lVert\varphi^{1},\varphi^{2},\psi^{1},\psi^{2}\rVert_{\mathbf{Y}^{1}_{\varepsilon}}\\
+n(\lVert\psi^{0}\rVert_{H^{n+1/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})}+\lVert\varphi^{1}\rVert_{H^{n}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}+\lVert\varphi^{2}\rVert_{H^{n+1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})}+\lVert\psi^{1}\rVert_{H^{n+1/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})}+\lVert\psi^{2}\rVert_{H^{n+1/2}(\Sigma_{0})}),

for all
(\eta,u,p)\in\mathbf{U}^{n}_{\varepsilon}
and all
(\psi^{0},\varphi^{1},\varphi^{2},\psi^{1},\psi^{2})\in\mathbf{V}^{n}_{\varepsilon}
. We also define the closed subsets of
\mathscr{U}^{n}_{\varepsilon}\subset\mathbf{U}^{n}_{\varepsilon}
given by

(5.2.3)

\mathscr{U}^{n}_{\varepsilon}=\{{(\eta,u,p)\in\mathbf{U}^{n}_{\varepsilon}\;:\;\mathsf{H}+\varepsilon^{2}\eta\geqslant\mathrm{H}_{-}/4,\;4+\varepsilon^{2}u_{1}-\mathsf{U}\geqslant\delta/2,\;\lVert\eta,u,p\rVert_{\mathbf{U}^{0}_{\varepsilon}}\leqslant\char 12\relax{\log\varepsilon}^{10}}\}.

Next, we define a family of bounded linear operators acting between these function spaces and indexed by
\mathbf{u}=(\widetilde{\eta},\widetilde{u},\widetilde{p})\in\mathscr{U}^{n}_{\varepsilon}
. Indeed, we set
G^{\varepsilon}_{\mathbf{u}}:\mathbf{U}^{n}_{\varepsilon}\to\mathbf{V}^{n}_{\varepsilon}
via
G^{\varepsilon}_{\mathbf{u}}(\eta,u,p)=(\psi^{0},\varphi^{1},\varphi^{2},\psi^{1},\psi^{2})
where

(5.2.4)

(\varphi^{1},\varphi^{2},\psi^{1},\psi^{2})=L^{\varepsilon}_{\mathsf{H}+\varepsilon^{2}\widetilde{\eta}}(u,p)-(0,0,\upsigma\varepsilon^{2}(1+\varepsilon^{2}\char 12\relax{\mathsf{H}+\varepsilon^{2}\widetilde{\eta}}^{2})^{-3/2}\eta^{\prime\prime}\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon(\mathsf{H}+\varepsilon^{2}\widetilde{\eta})},0),\text{ and }\\
\psi^{0}=(4+\varepsilon^{2}(\overline{\upgamma}+\widetilde{u}_{1})-\mathsf{U})(1-\partial_{1}^{2})\eta-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\partial_{1}u\cdot\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon(\mathsf{H}+\varepsilon^{2}\widetilde{\eta})}.

where the action of
L
is defined in (
3.2.23
).

Finally, we introduce the lower order term conglomerate map
F^{\varepsilon}:\mathscr{U}^{n}_{\varepsilon}\to\mathbf{V}^{n+1}_{\varepsilon}
, whose components
F^{\varepsilon}=(F^{\varepsilon}_{0},(F^{\varepsilon}_{1},F^{\varepsilon}_{2}),0,(F^{\varepsilon}_{3},F^{\varepsilon}_{4}),0)
are given by

(5.2.5)

F^{\varepsilon}_{0}(\eta,u,p)=(1-\partial_{1}^{2})(\overline{\upgamma}\mathsf{H}-(\mathsf{H}+\varepsilon^{2}\eta)^{2}\mathsf{U}_{1}/2-(\mathsf{H}+\varepsilon^{3}\eta)\mathsf{U}_{2}/6-\overline{\mathrm{A}})-\frac{\mathsf{H}+\varepsilon^{2}\eta}{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{\varepsilon}u_{1}(\cdot,y)\;\mathrm{d}y-\mathsf{H}^{\prime\prime}u_{1}+2\mathsf{U}^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}+\mathsf{U}^{\prime\prime}\eta,

(5.2.6)

F^{\varepsilon}_{1}(\eta,u,p)=[{(\mathsf{X}(\mathsf{H}+\varepsilon^{2}\eta)-(4+\varepsilon^{2}\overline{\upgamma})e_{1})\cdot\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{H}+\varepsilon^{2}\eta}}u+u\cdot\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{H}+\varepsilon^{2}\eta}}\mathsf{X}(\mathsf{H}+\varepsilon^{2}\eta)}]\cdot e_{1}-\overline{\upgamma}\mathsf{U}^{\prime}\\
+(\mathsf{V}(\mathsf{H}+\varepsilon^{2}\eta)-(4+\varepsilon^{2}\overline{\upgamma})e_{1})\cdot\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{H}+\varepsilon^{2}\eta}}\mathsf{W}_{1}(\mathsf{H}+\varepsilon^{2}\eta)+\mathsf{W}(\mathsf{H}+\varepsilon^{2}\eta)\cdot\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{H}+\varepsilon^{2}\eta}}\mathsf{X}_{1}(\mathsf{H}+\varepsilon^{2}\eta)\\
+\varepsilon^{2}u\cdot\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{H}+\varepsilon^{2}\eta}}u_{1}+\partial_{1}^{\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{H}+\varepsilon^{2}\eta}}\mathsf{Q}(\mathsf{H}+\varepsilon^{2}\eta)-\upmu(\partial_{1}^{\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{H}+\varepsilon^{2}\eta}})^{2}\mathsf{W}_{1}(\mathsf{H}+\varepsilon^{2}\eta),

(5.2.7)

F^{\varepsilon}_{2}(\eta,u,p)=[{(\mathsf{X}(\mathsf{H}+\varepsilon^{2}\eta)-(4+\varepsilon^{2}\overline{\upgamma})e_{1})\cdot\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{H}+\varepsilon^{2}\eta}}u+u\cdot\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{H}+\varepsilon^{2}\eta}}\mathsf{X}(\mathsf{H}+\varepsilon^{2}\eta)}]\cdot e_{2}+\overline{\upgamma}y(\mathsf{H}+\varepsilon^{2}\eta)\mathsf{U}^{\prime\prime}\\
+(\mathsf{V}(\mathsf{H}+\varepsilon^{2}\eta)-(4+\varepsilon^{2}\overline{\upgamma})e_{1})\cdot\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{H}+\varepsilon^{2}\eta}}\mathsf{W}_{2}(\mathsf{H}+\varepsilon^{2}\eta)+\mathsf{W}(\mathsf{H}+\varepsilon^{2}\eta)\cdot\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{H}+\varepsilon^{2}\eta}}\mathsf{X}_{2}(\mathsf{H}+\varepsilon^{2}\eta)\\
+\varepsilon^{2}u\cdot\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{H}+\varepsilon^{2}\eta}}u_{2}-\upmu(\partial_{1}^{\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{H}+\varepsilon^{2}\eta}})^{2}\mathsf{W}_{2}(\mathsf{H}+\varepsilon^{2}\eta),

(5.2.8)

F^{\varepsilon}_{3}(\eta,u,p)=\varepsilon\upsigma(\mathsf{H}+\varepsilon^{2}\eta)^{\prime}\mathsf{H}^{\prime\prime}(1+\varepsilon^{2}\char 12\relax{(\mathsf{H}+\varepsilon^{2}\eta)^{\prime}}^{2})^{-3/2}+\varepsilon(\mathsf{P}-\mathrm{g}\mathsf{H}-2\upmu\mathsf{U}^{\prime})\eta^{\prime}+\varepsilon\upmu(\mathsf{U}_{2}-\mathsf{U}^{\prime\prime})\eta-\varepsilon\mathrm{g}\mathsf{H}^{\prime}\eta\\
+\varepsilon\mathsf{Q}(\mathsf{H}+\varepsilon^{2}\eta)(\mathsf{H}+\varepsilon^{2}\eta)^{\prime}-2\varepsilon\upmu\partial_{1}^{\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{H}+\varepsilon^{2}\eta}}\mathsf{W}_{1}(\mathsf{H}+\varepsilon^{2}\eta)(\mathsf{H}+\varepsilon^{2}\eta)^{\prime}+\upmu\partial_{1}^{\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{H}+\varepsilon^{2}\eta}}\mathsf{W}_{2}(\mathsf{H}+\varepsilon^{2}\eta)-\varepsilon^{3}\mathrm{g}\eta\eta^{\prime},

and

(5.2.9)

F^{\varepsilon}_{4}(\eta,u,p)=-\upsigma\mathsf{H}^{\prime\prime}(1+\varepsilon^{2}\char 12\relax{(\mathsf{H}+\varepsilon^{2}\eta)^{\prime}}^{2})^{-3/2}+\mathrm{g}\eta\\
-\mathsf{Q}(\mathsf{H}+\varepsilon^{2}\eta)+\upmu(\mathsf{H}+\varepsilon^{2}\eta)(\mathsf{H}+\varepsilon^{2}\eta)^{\prime}\mathsf{U}^{\prime\prime}+\upmu\mathbb{D}^{\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{H}+\varepsilon^{2}\eta}}\mathsf{W}(\mathsf{H}+\varepsilon^{2}\eta)\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon(\mathsf{H}+\varepsilon^{2}\eta)}\cdot e_{2},

where the auxiliary functions
\mathsf{V}
,
\mathsf{W}
,
\mathsf{X}
, and
\mathsf{Q}
are given by

(5.2.10)

\mathsf{V}(\zeta)=\mathsf{U}e_{1}-y\zeta\mathsf{U}^{\prime}e_{2},\quad\mathsf{W}(\zeta)=\Big(\frac{y\zeta}{\varepsilon}\mathsf{U}_{1}=\frac{1}{2}\Big(\frac{y\zeta}{\varepsilon}\Big)^{2}\mathsf{U}_{2}\Big)e_{1}-\varepsilon\Big(\frac{1}{2}\Big(\frac{y\zeta}{\varepsilon}\Big)^{2}\mathsf{U}_{1}^{\prime}+\frac{1}{6}\Big(\frac{y\zeta}{\varepsilon}\Big)^{3}\mathsf{U}_{2}^{\prime}\Big)e_{2},\\
\mathsf{X}(\zeta)=\mathsf{V}(\zeta)+\varepsilon^{2}\mathsf{W}(\zeta),\text{ and }\mathsf{Q}(\zeta)=\frac{y\zeta}{\varepsilon}\mathsf{P}_{1}+\frac{1}{2}\Big(\frac{y\zeta}{\varepsilon}\Big)^{2}\mathsf{P}_{2}

for
\zeta=\mathsf{H}+\varepsilon^{2}\eta
.

We remark that the precise choice of the norms (
5.2.2
) (and how they depend on
\varepsilon
) is only really relevant in the case
n=0
. For
n\geqslant 1
, any choice of equivalent norms will be fine for the proofs that follow.

Definition
5.5
takes for granted that the map
F^{\varepsilon}:\mathscr{U}^{n}_{\varepsilon}\to\mathbf{V}^{n+1}_{\varepsilon}
is well-defined; this can be verified by elementary but tedious calculations, which we omit here for the sake of brevity. We note, though, that Section
4.3
establishes a related and much sharper version of the
n=0
case of the claimed mapping properties, and the same considerations will work for general
n
.

The following lemma reveals how the solutions produced by Theorem
5.4
interact with the objects of Definition
5.5
.

Lemma 5.6

(Reformulation for regularity promotion)
.

Assume the hypotheses of Definition
5.5
. For
0<\varepsilon<\epsilon_{\star}
let
(\eta,u,p)\in\mathbf{U}^{0}_{\varepsilon}
and
\lambda\in\overline{B(0,\beta)}
denote the fixed point granted by the second and third items of Proposition
5.3
. Define
(h,v,q)\in\mathbf{U}^{0}_{\varepsilon}
via

(5.2.11)

h=\eta+\varepsilon^{-2}\mathfrak{h}(\lambda),\;v=u+\varepsilon^{-2}(X(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)-\mathsf{X}(\mathsf{H}+\varepsilon^{2}h)),\text{ and }q=p+Q(\varepsilon,\lambda,\eta)-\mathsf{Q}(\mathsf{H}+\varepsilon^{2}h)+\varepsilon^{-2}(\mathsf{P}-P(\lambda)),

where
\mathfrak{h}(\lambda)
is defined in Proposition
4.10
; the functions
\mathsf{X}
,
\mathsf{H}
,
\mathsf{Q}
, and
\mathsf{P}
are from Definition
5.5
; and
X
,
Q
, and
P
are from Definition
4.13
.

There exists
\epsilon_{{\hbox to4.95pt{\vbox to4.95pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\>\lower-2.47614pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{

such that the following hold for all

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 3.15 0 L 0 0.79 L -3.15 0 L 0 -0.79 Z}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

.

(1)

We have the inclusion
(h,v,q)\in\mathrm{int}\mathscr{U}^{0}_{\varepsilon}
, where
\mathrm{int}
denotes the topological interior.

(2)

If
(\boldsymbol{\zeta},\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{p})\in H^{5/2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})\times H^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega_{\varepsilon};\mathbb{R}^{2})\times H^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})
denotes the solution to (
1.4.8
) produced by Theorem
5.4
, then we have the identities

(5.2.12)

\boldsymbol{\zeta}=\mathsf{H}+\varepsilon^{2}h,\;\boldsymbol{u}=\mathsf{X}(\mathsf{H}+\varepsilon^{2}h)+\varepsilon^{2}v,\text{ and }\boldsymbol{p}=\mathsf{P}+\varepsilon^{2}\mathsf{Q}(\mathsf{H}+\varepsilon^{2}h)+\varepsilon^{2}q.

In particular, if we know that
(h,v,q)\in\mathbf{U}^{n}_{\varepsilon}
for some
n\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
, then we have the higher regularity inclusions
\boldsymbol{\zeta}\in(W^{\infty,\infty}+H^{n+5/2})(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})
,
\boldsymbol{u}\in(W^{\infty,\infty}+H^{n+2})(\Omega_{\varepsilon};\mathbb{R}^{2})
, and
\boldsymbol{p}\in(W^{\infty,\infty}+H^{n+1})(\Omega_{\varepsilon})
.

(3)

The triple
(h,v,q)
obeys the identity

(5.2.13)

G^{\varepsilon}_{(h,v,q)}(h,v,q)=-F^{\varepsilon}(h,v,q),

where
G^{\varepsilon}
and
F^{\varepsilon}
are the family of linear operators and lower order terms, respectively, from Definition
5.5
.

Proof.

We begin by establishing the second item, as it actually holds over the full range of
0<\varepsilon<\epsilon_{\star}
. Thanks to the proof of Theorem
5.4
we know that
\boldsymbol{\zeta}
,
\boldsymbol{u}
, and
\boldsymbol{p}
are defined in terms of
\eta
,
u
,
p
, and
\lambda
via identities (
4.1.2
), (
4.1.3
), and (
4.1.4
). It is then immediate that
h
,
v
, and
q
as defined in (
5.2.11
) yield the identities in (
5.2.12
). The remainder of the second item follows readily.

Next, we establish the first item. Since
\boldsymbol{\zeta}=\mathsf{H}+\varepsilon^{2}h
we know by Theorem
5.4
that
\boldsymbol{\zeta}\geqslant\mathrm{H}_{-}/2>\mathrm{H}_{-}/4
. By the proof of the same result, we know that
\inf(4-\mathsf{U})\geqslant\delta
and so one can check that
4+\varepsilon^{2}v_{1}-\mathsf{U}>\delta/2
for
0<\varepsilon<\epsilon_{\hbox to4.95pt{\vbox to4.95pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\>\lower-2.47614pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 3.15 L 0.79 0 L 0 -3.15 L -0.79 0 Z}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 3.15 0 L 0 0.79 L -3.15 0 L 0 -0.79 Z}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}
small enough by direct computation from the definition (
5.2.11
). The claimed upper bound
\lVert h,v,q\rVert_{\mathbf{U}^{0}_{\varepsilon}}<\char 12\relax{\log\varepsilon}^{10}
also follows from direct computation from (
5.2.11
), using the fact that
\lVert\eta,u\rVert_{\mathbf{W}_{\varepsilon}}\leqslant R_{\star}
, the estimates on
\lambda
provided by (
5.1.18
), the estimates on
p
provided by Proposition
5.1
, and taking
\epsilon_{\hbox to4.95pt{\vbox to4.95pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\>\lower-2.47614pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{}}{}

{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 3.15 L 0.79 0 L 0 -3.15 L -0.79 0 Z}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 3.15 0 L 0 0.79 L -3.15 0 L 0 -0.79 Z}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}

sufficiently small.

We now turn our attention to the third item, which amounts to a computation and packing the resulting terms into the expressions
G^{\varepsilon}_{(h,v,q)}
and
F^{\varepsilon}
appearing in (
5.2.13
). The first step is to note that Definition
5.5
requires that
(\boldsymbol{\zeta},\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{p})
is a solution to (
1.4.8
) and that the tuple
(\mathsf{H},\mathsf{U},\mathsf{U}_{1},\mathsf{U}_{2},\mathsf{P},\mathsf{P}_{1},\mathsf{P}_{2})
satisfies the ODEs from (
4.2.26
). Consequently, the first through fifth items of Theorem
4.1
are satisfied under the replacements
(\eta,u,p)\mapsto(h,v,q)
and
(H,U,U_{1},U_{2},P,P_{1},P_{2})\mapsto(\mathsf{H},\mathsf{U},\mathsf{U}_{1},\mathsf{U}_{2},\mathsf{P},\mathsf{P}_{1},\mathsf{P}_{2})
with
\mathfrak{r}_{1}=\mathfrak{r}_{3}=\mathfrak{r}_{4}=0
. This allows us to invoke its converse statement to learn that the triple
(h,v,q)
is a solution to system (
4.1.14
) under the aforementioned replacements. To arrive at (
5.2.13
), we take the lower order terms on the left hand side of these equations (such as the residual advective derivative) and place them into
F^{\varepsilon}
, and we also take the higher order capillarity terms hiding in
\mathfrak{f}_{3}
and
\mathfrak{f}_{4}
and place them into
G^{\varepsilon}
.
∎

The second item of the previous lemma reduces establishing smoothness of
(\boldsymbol{\zeta},\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{p})
to establishing that of
(h,v,q)
. To take the next step in achieving the latter, we embark on a further study of the left hand side of the operator equation (
5.2.13
) from the third item of the lemma.

Proposition 5.7

(Main engine for regularity promotion)
.

Assume the hypotheses of Lemma
5.6
and let the parameter
\epsilon_{\hbox to4.95pt{\vbox to4.95pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\>\lower-2.47614pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 3.15 L 0.79 0 L 0 -3.15 L -0.79 0 Z}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 3.15 0 L 0 0.79 L -3.15 0 L 0 -0.79 Z}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\in(0,1/3)
be as in the lemma. There exist
C\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
and
\epsilon_{\hbox to4.95pt{\vbox to4.95pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\>\lower-2.47614pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 3.15 L 0.79 0 L 0 -3.15 L -0.79 0 Z}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 3.15 0 L 0 0.79 L -3.15 0 L 0 -0.79 Z}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}^{1}\in(0,\epsilon_{\hbox to4.95pt{\vbox to4.95pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\>\lower-2.47614pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 3.15 L 0.79 0 L 0 -3.15 L -0.79 0 Z}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 3.15 0 L 0 0.79 L -3.15 0 L 0 -0.79 Z}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}})
such that the following hold for all
0<\varepsilon<\epsilon^{1}_{\hbox to4.95pt{\vbox to4.95pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\>\lower-2.47614pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 3.15 L 0.79 0 L 0 -3.15 L -0.79 0 Z}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 3.15 0 L 0 0.79 L -3.15 0 L 0 -0.79 Z}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}
and all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

(1)

The map
E^{\varepsilon}:\mathrm{int}\mathscr{U}^{n}_{\varepsilon}\to\mathbf{V}^{n}_{\varepsilon}
, defined via
E^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{u})=G^{\varepsilon}_{\mathbf{u}}\mathbf{u}
for all
\mathbf{u}=(\eta,u,p)\in\mathscr{U}^{n}_{\varepsilon}
, is continuously differentiable.

(2)

There exists a function
R^{\varepsilon}:\mathrm{int}\mathscr{U}^{n}_{\varepsilon}\to\mathbf{V}^{n}_{\varepsilon}
such that for all
\mathbf{u}\in\mathrm{int}\mathscr{U}^{1}_{\varepsilon}
we have the identity

(5.2.14)

\partial_{1}[{E^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{u})}]=DE^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{u})[\partial_{1}\mathbf{u}]+R^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{u}).

(3)

If
\mathbf{u}\in\mathrm{int}\mathscr{U}^{n}_{\varepsilon}
is such that
\partial_{1}\mathbf{u}\in\mathbf{U}^{n}_{\varepsilon}
and
E^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{u})\in\mathbf{V}^{n+1}_{\varepsilon}
, then
\mathbf{u}\in\mathrm{int}\mathscr{U}^{n+1}_{\varepsilon}
.

(4)

For all
\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}\in\mathrm{int}\mathscr{U}^{n}_{\varepsilon}
the derivative
DE^{\varepsilon}(\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}):\mathbf{U}^{n}_{\varepsilon}\to\mathbf{V}^{n}_{\varepsilon}
is a linear isomorphism.

(5)

If
\mathbf{u}\in\mathrm{int}\mathscr{U}^{0}_{\varepsilon}
is such that
E^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{u})\in\mathbf{V}^{n}_{\varepsilon}
, then
\mathbf{u}\in\mathrm{int}\mathscr{U}^{n}_{\varepsilon}
.

Proof.

The first item follows from standard computations and estimates for products and compositions in Sobolev spaces, which we omit for brevity.

For the second item we take
\mathbf{u}=(\eta,u,p)\in\mathrm{int}\mathscr{U}^{1}_{\varepsilon}
and define
R^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{u})=\partial_{1}[{E^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{u})}]-DE^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{u})[{\partial_{1}\mathbf{u}}]
. A straightforward computation, which we again mostly omit, will show the mapping property
R^{\varepsilon}:\mathrm{int}\mathscr{U}^{n}_{\varepsilon}\to\mathbf{V}^{n}_{\varepsilon}
holds. To illustrate the flavor of the terms in
R^{\varepsilon}
, consider just the
e_{2}
-momentum pressure contribution:

(5.2.15)

\partial_{1}[{\partial_{2}^{\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{H}+\varepsilon^{2}\eta}}p}]-\Big(\partial_{2}^{\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{H}+\varepsilon^{2}\eta}}[{\partial_{1}p}]-\varepsilon^{2}\frac{[{\partial_{1}\eta}]}{(\mathsf{H}+\varepsilon^{2}\eta)^{2}}\partial_{2}p\Big)=-\frac{\partial_{1}\mathsf{H}}{(\mathsf{H}+\varepsilon^{2}\eta)^{2}}\partial_{2}p.

As in the right side above, generically the summands in
R^{\varepsilon}
occur when the derivative
\partial_{1}
falls onto the smooth background terms.

We now prove the third item. Let
\mathbf{u}=(\eta,u,p)\in\mathscr{U}^{n}_{\varepsilon}
satisfy
\partial_{1}\mathbf{u}\in\mathbf{U}^{n}_{\varepsilon}
and
E^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{u})\in\mathbf{V}^{n+1}_{\varepsilon}
. The former two of these inclusions directly imply higher regularity of the free surface:
\eta\in H^{n+7/2}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})
. In turn, we find from the definition (
5.2.4
) that

(5.2.16)

\varphi^{1}=\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{H}+\varepsilon^{2}\eta}}p-\upmu\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{H}+\varepsilon^{2}\eta}}\cdot\mathbb{D}^{\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{H}+\varepsilon^{2}\eta}}u\in H^{n+1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon};\mathbb{R}^{2})\text{ and }\varphi^{2}=\nabla^{\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{H}+\varepsilon^{2}\eta}}\cdot u\in H^{n+2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon}).

Now it remains to show that
\partial_{2}p\in H^{n+1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})
and
\partial_{2}^{2}u\in H^{n+1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon};\mathbb{R}^{2})
. For this we argue as in the later part of the third step of the proof of Proposition
3.8
and use identities (
5.2.16
) to establish tangential to normal regularity promotion. In fact, with
h=\mathsf{H}+\varepsilon^{2}\eta
, we compute that identities (
3.2.66
) and (
3.2.68
) remain true. The desired inclusions and the third item follow.

Next, consider the fourth item. We begin by establishing that when
0<\varepsilon<\epsilon^{1}_{\hbox to4.95pt{\vbox to4.95pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\>\lower-2.47614pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 3.15 L 0.79 0 L 0 -3.15 L -0.79 0 Z}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 3.15 0 L 0 0.79 L -3.15 0 L 0 -0.79 Z}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}
is sufficiently small the derivative
DE^{\varepsilon}(\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}):\mathbf{U}^{0}_{\varepsilon}\to\mathbf{V}^{0}_{\varepsilon}
is an isomorphism whenever
\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}\in\mathrm{int}\mathscr{U}^{0}_{\varepsilon}
. A direct computation shows that this derivative has the form

(5.2.17)

DE^{\varepsilon}(\widetilde{\mathbf{u}})\mathbf{u}=M^{\varepsilon}\mathbf{u}+\varepsilon^{2}N^{\varepsilon}_{\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}}\mathbf{u}

where for
(\eta,u,p)=\mathbf{u}
we have

(5.2.18)

M^{\varepsilon}\mathbf{u}=((4-\mathsf{U})(1-\partial_{1}^{2})\eta-(\partial_{1}u\cdot\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon\mathsf{H}})/\varepsilon,L^{\varepsilon}_{\mathsf{H}}(u,p))\text{ and }N^{\varepsilon}_{\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}}\mathbf{u}=\varepsilon^{-2}(DE^{\varepsilon}(\widetilde{\mathbf{u}})\mathbf{u}-M^{\varepsilon}\mathbf{u}).

The main part
M^{\varepsilon}:\mathbf{U}^{0}_{\varepsilon}\to\mathbf{V}^{0}_{\varepsilon}
is invertible due to Theorem
3.9
, its block structure, the lower bound
\inf(4-\mathsf{U})>0
, and the invertibility of
(1-\partial_{1}^{2})
. In fact, we deduce from the definition of the norm (
5.2.2
) (when
n=0
) and estimate (
3.2.73
) that the corresponding operator norms satisfy

(5.2.19)

\sup_{0<\varepsilon<\epsilon_{\hbox to4.95pt{\vbox to4.95pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\>\lower-2.47614pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 3.15 L 0.79 0 L 0 -3.15 L -0.79 0 Z}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 3.15 0 L 0 0.79 L -3.15 0 L 0 -0.79 Z}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}\max\{{\lVert M^{\varepsilon}\rVert_{\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{U}^{0}_{\varepsilon};\mathbf{V}^{0}_{\varepsilon})},\lVert(M^{\varepsilon})^{-1}\rVert_{\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{V}^{0}_{\varepsilon};\mathbf{U}^{0}_{\varepsilon})}}\}<\infty.

By using the inequality
\lVert\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}\rVert_{\mathbf{U}^{0}_{\varepsilon}}\leqslant\char 12\relax{\log\varepsilon}^{10}
built into the definition
5.2.3
, the remaining piece

can be tamed, under a conservative argument, with only a factor of
\varepsilon^{3/2}
. More precisely, we compute (in the style of Section
4.3
) that we have

(5.2.20)

\sup_{0<\varepsilon<\epsilon_{\hbox to4.95pt{\vbox to4.95pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\>\lower-2.47614pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 3.15 L 0.79 0 L 0 -3.15 L -0.79 0 Z}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 3.15 0 L 0 0.79 L -3.15 0 L 0 -0.79 Z}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}\sup_{\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}\in\mathrm{int}\mathscr{U}^{0}_{\varepsilon}}\varepsilon^{3/2}\lVert N^{\varepsilon}_{\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}}\rVert_{\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{U}^{0}_{\varepsilon};\mathbf{V}^{0}_{\varepsilon})}<\infty.

Combining the estimates (
5.2.19
) and (
5.2.20
) with the decomposition
5.2.17
and recalling that the set of invertible operators is open, we deduce the existence of
0<\epsilon_{\hbox to4.95pt{\vbox to4.95pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\>\lower-2.47614pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{}}{}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 3.15 L 0.79 0 L 0 -3.15 L -0.79 0 Z}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}

{}{}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 3.15 0 L 0 0.79 L -3.15 0 L 0 -0.79 Z}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}^{1}<\epsilon_{\hbox to4.95pt{\vbox to4.95pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\>\lower-2.47614pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 3.15 L 0.79 0 L 0 -3.15 L -0.79 0 Z}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 3.15 0 L 0 0.79 L -3.15 0 L 0 -0.79 Z}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}
such that whenever
0<\varepsilon<\epsilon^{1}_{\hbox to4.95pt{\vbox to4.95pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\>\lower-2.47614pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 3.15 L 0.79 0 L 0 -3.15 L -0.79 0 Z}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 3.15 0 L 0 0.79 L -3.15 0 L 0 -0.79 Z}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}
and
\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}\in\mathrm{int}\mathscr{U}^{0}_{\varepsilon}
the operator
DE^{\varepsilon}(\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}):\mathbf{U}^{0}_{\varepsilon}\to\mathbf{V}^{0}_{\varepsilon}
is an isomorphism.

The ‘base case’ regularity
n=0
of the fourth item is now established. Notice that we immediately get that the maps
DE^{\varepsilon}(\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}):\mathbf{U}^{n}_{\varepsilon}\to\mathbf{V}^{n}_{\varepsilon}
are injective for all
n
. Therefore, we only need to establish surjectivity in the higher regularity cases, which can be viewed as regularity promotion. The key to this is the linear problem’s analog of the tangential to normal regularity promotion mechanism, which we discuss next.

We claim that for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
, if
\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}\in\mathrm{int}\mathscr{U}^{n+1}_{\varepsilon}
and
\mathbf{u},\partial_{1}\mathbf{u}\in\mathbf{U}^{n}_{\varepsilon}
with
DE^{\varepsilon}(\widetilde{\mathbf{u}})\mathbf{u}\in\mathbf{V}^{n+1}_{\varepsilon}
, then we have the inclusion
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbf{U}^{n+1}_{\varepsilon}
. If we write
\mathbf{u}=(\eta,u,p)
then the assumed tangential regularity inclusion
\partial_{1}\mathbf{u}\in\mathbf{U}^{n}_{\varepsilon}
forces
\eta\in H^{7/2+n}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})
. We then return to the bulk (i.e. momentum and divergence equations) components of the decomposition
5.2.17
and see that the
N^{\varepsilon}_{\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}}
contribution depends only on
\eta
, and hence it can be thrown on the higher regularity right hand side of the equation. Doing this leaves us with an inclusion of the form (
5.2.16
), but replacing the argument of the geometry matrices, namely
\mathsf{H}+\varepsilon^{2}\eta
, with the term
\mathsf{H}+\varepsilon^{2}\widetilde{\eta}
where
\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}=(\widetilde{\eta},\widetilde{u},\widetilde{p})
. We can then argue exactly as before to deduce that
\partial_{2}p\in H^{n+1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})
and
\partial^{2}_{2}u\in H^{n+1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon};\mathbb{R}^{2})
, and hence
(\eta,u,p)=\mathbf{u}\in\mathbf{U}^{n+1}_{\varepsilon}
. This completes the proof of the claim.

The above claim reduces the higher regularity promotion (and thus surjectivity) question to proving only tangential regularity promotion. We aim to do this with an inductive argument; however, we need first deal with the second base case,
n=1
, before we can properly differentiate the equation. Suppose that
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbf{V}^{1}_{\varepsilon}
,
\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}\in\mathrm{int}\mathscr{U}^{1}_{\varepsilon}
, and
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbf{U}^{0}_{\varepsilon}
are related by the equation
DE^{\varepsilon}(\widetilde{\mathbf{u}})\mathbf{u}=\mathbf{v}
. Instead of differentiating the equation in the
e_{1}
-direction, we use finite forward difference quotients of step-size
t\in(0,1)
, denoted by
\updelta_{t}
. We schematically write

(5.2.21)

\updelta_{t}[{DE^{\varepsilon}(\widetilde{\mathbf{u}})\mathbf{u}}]={{}_{0}}L^{t}_{\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}}\updelta_{t}\mathbf{u}+{{}_{1}}L_{\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}}^{t}\mathbf{u}=\updelta_{t}\mathbf{v}

where
{{}_{0}}L^{t}_{\mathbf{\widetilde{u}}}\approx DE^{\varepsilon}(\widetilde{\mathbf{u}})
(in fact
{{}_{0}}L_{\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}}^{t}\to DE^{\varepsilon}(\widetilde{\mathbf{u}})
in the operator norm as
t\to 0
) and
{{}_{1}}L_{\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}}^{t}
and
\updelta_{t}\mathbf{v}
have the correct order in the sense that
\sup_{0<t<1}\lVert{{}_{1}}L_{\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}}^{t}\mathbf{u}\rVert_{\mathbf{V}^{0}_{\varepsilon}}<\infty
and
\sup_{0<t<1}\lVert\updelta_{t}\mathbf{v}\rVert_{\mathbf{V}^{0}_{\varepsilon}}<\infty
. The same argument that established the invertibility of
DE^{\varepsilon}(\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}):\mathbf{U}^{0}_{\varepsilon}\to\mathbf{V}^{0}_{\varepsilon}
applies for the ‘translated’ operator
{{}_{0}}L^{t}_{\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}}
for small enough
t>0
, and so from (
5.2.21
) we deduce that

(5.2.22)

\updelta_{t}\mathbf{u}=({{}_{0}}L^{t}_{\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}})^{-1}(\updelta_{t}\mathbf{v}-{{}_{1}}L_{\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}}^{t}\mathbf{u})\text{ and hence }\limsup_{t\to 0}\lVert\updelta_{t}\mathbf{u}\rVert_{\mathbf{U}^{0}_{\varepsilon}}<\infty.

The above implies that
\partial_{1}\mathbf{u}\in\mathbf{U}^{0}_{\varepsilon}
. Feeding this information into the linear problem’s tangential to normal regularity promotion mechanism yields then
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbf{U}^{1}_{\varepsilon}
. Thus, the
n=1
case of the fourth item is now established.

The cases
n\geqslant 2
can now be established inductively, as we have enough regularity to differentiate the equation. Indeed, if we suppose that
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbf{V}^{n}_{\varepsilon}
,
\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}\in\mathrm{int}\mathscr{U}^{n}_{\varepsilon}
, and
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbf{U}^{n-1}_{\varepsilon}
satisfy
DE^{\varepsilon}(\widetilde{\mathbf{u}})\mathbf{u}=\mathbf{v}
, then an application of
\partial_{1}
to this identity produces the more favorable form of (
5.2.21
):

(5.2.23)

\partial_{1}[{DE^{\varepsilon}(\widetilde{\mathbf{u}})\mathbf{u}}]=DE^{\varepsilon}(\widetilde{\mathbf{u}})\partial_{1}\mathbf{u}+{{}_{1}}L_{\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}}^{0}\mathbf{u}=\partial_{1}\mathbf{v}.

The induction hypothesis is that
DE^{\varepsilon}(\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}):\mathbf{U}^{n-1}_{\varepsilon}\to\mathbf{V}^{n-1}_{\varepsilon}
is an isomorphism, and we have that
{{}_{1}}L^{0}_{\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}}\mathbf{u},\partial_{1}\mathbf{v}\in\mathbf{V}^{n-1}_{\varepsilon}
, so by arguing as in (
5.2.22
), we find that
\partial_{1}\mathbf{u}\in\mathbf{U}^{n-1}_{\varepsilon}
. The tangential-to-normal engine kicks in again and we deduce further that
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbf{U}^{n}_{\varepsilon}
. This completes the induction argument and the proof of the fourth item as well.

Finally, we prove the fifth item. The case
n=0
is trivial, while the case
n=1
suffers from the same ‘base case’ differentiability woes as above. Suppose that
\mathbf{u}\in\mathrm{int}\mathscr{U}^{0}_{\varepsilon}
,
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbf{V}^{1}_{\varepsilon}
are related by the equation
E^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{u})=\mathbf{v}
. We apply, for
t\in(0,1)
, the difference quotient operator
\updelta_{t}
to this identity and derive the following equation, which is similar to (
5.2.14
):

(5.2.24)

\updelta_{t}[{E^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{u})}]={{}_{0}}L^{t}_{\mathbf{u}}\updelta_{t}\mathbf{u}+R^{\varepsilon}_{t}(\mathbf{u})=\updelta_{t}\mathbf{v},

where again
{{}_{0}}L^{t}_{\mathbf{u}}\to DE^{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{u})
as
t\to 0
in
\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{U}^{0}_{\varepsilon};\mathbf{V}^{0}_{\varepsilon})
and we have the estimates
\sup_{0<t<1}\lVert R^{\varepsilon}_{t}(\mathbf{u})\rVert_{\mathbf{V}^{0}_{\varepsilon}}<\infty
and
\sup_{0<t<1}\lVert\updelta_{t}\mathbf{v}\rVert_{\mathbf{V}^{0}_{\varepsilon}}<\infty
. Therefore, we may apply
({{}_{0}}L^{t}_{\mathbf{u}})^{-1}
to identity (
5.2.24
) for
t
sufficiently small and deduce that
\limsup_{t\to 0}\lVert\updelta_{t}\mathbf{u}\rVert_{\mathbf{U}^{0}_{\varepsilon}}<\infty
. This implies that
\partial_{1}\mathbf{u}\in\mathbf{U}^{0}_{\varepsilon}
, and feeding this into the third item then yields
\mathbf{u}\in\mathrm{int}\mathscr{U}^{1}_{\varepsilon}
. The base case
n=1
of the fifth item is now proved.

The remaining cases of
n\geqslant 2
for the fifth item now follow from a simple induction argument, as we have enough regularity to differentiate the equation with
\partial_{1}
and use the second and third items. The strategy is very similar to that used in the proof of the fourth item, so we omit the remaining details. This completes the proof of the fifth item.
∎

The results of this subsection are combined to give the following theorem.

Theorem 5.8

(Smoothness of bore waves)
.

Assume the hypotheses of Theorem
5.4
and Definition
5.5
while letting
0<\varepsilon<\epsilon^{1}_{\hbox to4.95pt{\vbox to4.95pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\>\lower-2.47614pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 3.15 L 0.79 0 L 0 -3.15 L -0.79 0 Z}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 3.15 0 L 0 0.79 L -3.15 0 L 0 -0.79 Z}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}
(where the latter smallness threshold is from Proposition
5.7
). The remainders and solutions produced by the aforementioned theorem for such
\varepsilon
obey the higher regularity inclusions

(5.2.25)

(\overline{\eta},\overline{u},\overline{p})\in H^{\infty}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})\times H^{\infty}(\Omega_{\varepsilon};\mathbb{R}^{2})\times H^{\infty}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})\text{ and }(\boldsymbol{\zeta},\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{p})\in W^{\infty,\infty}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})\times W^{\infty,\infty}(\Omega_{\varepsilon};\mathbb{R}^{2})\times W^{\infty,\infty}(\Omega_{\varepsilon}).

Proof.

Let
(h,v,q)\in\mathrm{int}\mathscr{U}^{0}_{\varepsilon}
be the variables instantiated by Lemma
5.6
. Due to the third item of this result, we know that
E^{\varepsilon}(h,v,q)=-F^{\varepsilon}(h,v,q)
where
F^{\varepsilon}:\mathrm{int}\mathscr{U}_{\varepsilon}^{n}\to\mathbf{V}^{n+1}_{\varepsilon}
(for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
) is the function from Definition
5.5
. The operator
E^{\varepsilon}
obeys the regularity promotion property of the fifth item of Proposition
5.7
. Therefore, an induction argument applies and we find that
(h,v,q)\in H^{\infty}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon})\times H^{\infty}(\Omega_{\varepsilon};\mathbb{R}^{2})\times H^{\infty}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})
. In turn, thanks to the identity (
5.2.12
), we find that the right inclusion of (
5.2.25
) must hold.

The validity of the left inclusion in (
5.2.25
) follows by writing

(5.2.26)

\mathsf{H}+\overline{\eta}=\boldsymbol{\zeta}=\mathsf{H}+\varepsilon^{2}h,\quad\Big(4-\frac{\mathrm{A}}{\mathsf{H}}+\frac{4\mathrm{a}}{\upmu}\mathsf{H}^{2}\Big(\varepsilon y-\frac{1}{2}y^{2}\Big)\Big)e_{1}+\overline{u}=\boldsymbol{u}=\mathsf{X}(\mathsf{H}+\varepsilon^{2}h)+\varepsilon^{2}v,\\
\text{ and }\mathrm{g}\mathsf{H}-2\upmu\mathrm{A}\frac{\mathsf{H}^{\prime}}{\mathsf{H}^{2}}+\overline{p}=\boldsymbol{p}=\mathsf{P}+\varepsilon^{2}\mathsf{Q}(\mathsf{H}+\varepsilon^{2}h)+\varepsilon^{2}q,

and solving for
\overline{\eta}
,
\overline{u}
, and
\overline{p}
.
∎

5.3.
Conclusions

This subsection is devoted to the proofs of the main theorem and corollaries of Section
1.5
.

Proof of Theorem
1
.

This result nearly follows by combining Theorems
5.4
and
5.8
and taking
\epsilon_{\star}=\epsilon^{1}_{\hbox to4.95pt{\vbox to4.95pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\>\lower-2.47614pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 3.15 L 0.79 0 L 0 -3.15 L -0.79 0 Z}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 3.15 0 L 0 0.79 L -3.15 0 L 0 -0.79 Z}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}
. We only need to check that the solutions produced by these results satisfy the requirements to be traveling bore waves in the sense of Definition
0
. The first item of this definition is manifestly satisfied by Theorem
5.4
and the equivalence between systems (
1.3.7
) and (
1.4.8
). The second item is satisfied by
\hat{\mathrm{A}}=\mathrm{A}=\varepsilon^{2}\overline{\mathrm{A}}
and the definition of
\overline{\upgamma}
,
\overline{\mathrm{A}}
in the parameter tuning identities (
1.4.5
). For the third item, we can use the identity (
4.2.15
) to check that the limits (
1.4.3
) hold. Indeed, we compute initially that

(5.3.1)

\lim_{x\to\pm\infty}\boldsymbol{u}_{1}(\iota x,y)=\Big(4-\frac{\mathrm{A}}{\mathrm{H}_{\pm}}\Big)+\frac{4\mathrm{a}}{\upmu}\mathrm{H}^{2}_{\pm}\Big(\varepsilon y-\frac{1}{2}y^{2}\Big).

Now we substitute the identity
4-\mathrm{A}/\mathrm{H}_{\pm}=4\mathrm{H}_{\pm}
, which is a consequence of (
1.4.4
), into (
5.3.1
) to find that the limit claimed for
\boldsymbol{u}_{1}
in (
1.4.3
) holds. The remaining limits are clear.
∎

Proof of Corollary
2
.

Fix
\alpha\in(0,1)
and
\iota\in\{{-1,1}\}
. Thanks to (
1.4.4
), we know that
\lim_{\mathrm{A}\to 1}(\mathrm{H}_{+}(\mathrm{A})-\mathrm{H}_{-}(\mathrm{A}))=1
, so for some
\mathrm{A}\in(0,1)
we have
(\mathrm{H}_{+}(\mathrm{A})-\mathrm{H}_{-}(\mathrm{A}))>1-\alpha
. The existence of a
\mathrm{g}
such that
(\mathrm{g},\mathrm{A})\in\mathfrak{C}_{\iota}
is assured by (
2.1.6
) from Lemma
2.2
. We are now in a position to invoke the third item of Theorem
1
to obtain
\boldsymbol{\zeta}
,
\boldsymbol{u}
, and
\boldsymbol{p}
that are a smooth bore wave solution to system (
1.3.7
) in the sense of Definition
0
. The formulas for the solution (
1.5.5
), paired with the end state identities (
1.5.4
) and (
1.4.4
), permit us to readily compute the jumps (
1.5.6
).
∎

Proof of Corollary
3
.

The computation done at the beginning of Section
2.1
shows that the equations (
1.5.9
) are satisfied when (
1.5.3
) holds with
(4-\mathsf{U})\mathsf{H}=\mathrm{A}
,
\mathsf{H}>0
, and
\mathsf{U}<4
. Thus, the result follows by Theorem
1
and using the estimate from the first item with the expressions (
1.5.5
) to bound the differences (
1.5.8
).
∎

Proof of Corollary
4
.

We only prove the case
\iota=-1
, as the case
\iota=1
follows by similar arguments. Since
0<\gamma<2ga/\kappa
we have that
\mathrm{g}=16ga/\kappa\gamma>8
. Then the third item of Lemma
2.2
gives the existence of
\mathrm{A}\in(0,1)
such that
(\mathrm{g},\mathrm{A})\in\mathfrak{C}_{-1}
. Once we define the nondimensional parameters as in (
1.3.3
), Theorem
1
applies and, for sufficiently small
\varepsilon>0
, we obtain a nondimensional smooth solution
(\boldsymbol{\zeta},\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{p})
to system (
1.4.8
).

Recall that the flattening diffeomorphism
\mathfrak{F}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}:\overline{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}\to\overline{\Omega_{\varepsilon\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}
is defined in Section
1.3
. Now define
\boldsymbol{v}=\boldsymbol{u}\circ\mathfrak{F}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}^{-1}
and
\boldsymbol{q}=\boldsymbol{p}\circ\mathfrak{F}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}^{-1}
with
\mathfrak{F}_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}^{-1}(x,z)=(x,z/\boldsymbol{\zeta}(x))
being the inverse flattening diffeomorphism
\overline{\Omega_{\varepsilon\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}\to\overline{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}
. Then
\boldsymbol{v}\in W^{\infty,\infty}(\Omega_{\varepsilon\boldsymbol{\zeta}};\mathbb{R}^{2})
and
\boldsymbol{q}\in W^{\infty,\infty}(\Omega_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}})
, and the triple
(\boldsymbol{\zeta},\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{q})
is a solution to system (
1.3.4
).

As in Section
1.3
, we define the length scale
\mathrm{L}=a\gamma/\kappa
and the velocity scale
\mathrm{U}=\gamma/4
and then determine the dimensional variables
\zeta
,
v
, and
q
as in (
1.3.2
). These are the sought after solution to the Eulerian formulation, system (
1.1.3
). The limits (
1.5.11
) and (
1.5.12
) are a direct consequence of (
1.5.4
), (
1.5.5
), and dimensional unpacking. Finally, the expression for the relative velocity flux
\Phi
given by (
1.5.13
) can by proved by directly inserting either the left or right equilibrium height into equation (
1.1.7
) and using the parameter tuning identities (
1.4.5
) to simplify.
∎
